due to the symmetries of the Riemann tensor. We find that 6 of the components are not present, this leaves the 10 equations in Einstein’s equations. Solutions to
eq.

3.41
define an Einstein metric
eq.

3.16
that is a solution to Einstein’s equation
eq.

3.9
.

3.2.4
Einstein-Cartan Action

The differential equations of motion, in the frame formulation of gravity, that form Einstein’s equations are

\displaystyle de^{I}+A^{I}{}_{J}\wedge e^{J}=0,\quad R^{I}-\left(\Lambda+\frac{R}{2}\right)e^{I}=0.

(3.43)

As with the metric formulation these can be efficiently stated as an action principle

\displaystyle S_{EC}=\frac{1}{32\pi G}\int\epsilon_{IJKL}e^{I}\wedge e^{J}\wedge\left(F^{KL}-\frac{\Lambda}{6}e^{K}\wedge e^{L}\right)

(3.44)

where
\epsilon_{IJKL}\in\Lambda^{4}V^{*}
is the totally antisymmetric symbol with values in
\pm 1
. It is possible to check that this action is invariant under
SO(1,3)
transformations of the frame and spin connection. As all the objects are differential forms or built from the exterior algebra of forms, the action is also diffeomorphism invariant. To compute the first equation in
eq.

3.43
we vary the action with respect to
A^{IJ}
. The variation of the curvature in this direction gives

\displaystyle\delta F^{IJ}=d^{A}\delta A^{IJ}=dA^{IJ}+A^{I}{}_{K}\wedge\delta A^{KJ}+A^{J}{}_{K}\wedge\delta A^{IK}.

(3.45)

Applying this to the action and integrating by parts, ignoring the boundary terms, we obtain the equation of motion

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S_{EC}}{\delta A^{IJ}}=\epsilon_{IJKL}d^{A}\left(e^{K}\wedge e^{L}\right)=0

(3.46)

By defining a new form
T^{I}=d^{A}e^{I}\in\Lambda^{2}(M)
we can see that this condition is equivalent too

\displaystyle\epsilon_{IJKL}e^{K}\wedge T^{L}=0.

(3.47)

Using the Hodge star,
\star:\Lambda^{q}\rightarrow\Lambda^{4-q}
, we find, in components that

\displaystyle(\star\epsilon_{IJKL}e^{K}\wedge T^{L})_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{IJKL}\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}e^{K}_{\nu}T^{L}_{\rho\sigma}=0.

(3.48)

The frame can then be used to convert the spacetime to internal indices, allowing us to write the above condition with only internal indices

\displaystyle\epsilon_{IJLM}\epsilon^{KLNP}T^{M}_{NP}=0

(3.49)

where
T^{I}_{JK}=T^{I}_{\mu\nu}e^{\mu}_{J}e^{\nu}_{K}
. Expanding the product of two Levi-Civita tensors into sum of Kronecker delta’s and expanding we find the result is

\displaystyle T^{K}_{IJ}+\delta_{I}^{K}T^{L}_{JL}-\delta_{J}^{K}T^{L}_{IL}=0

(3.50)

Taking the trace with respect to
J,K
we find that
T^{J}_{IJ}=0
and therefore,

\displaystyle T^{I}_{JK}=0.

(3.51)

Moving back to spacetime indices and using the definition of
T^{I}
we see that this equation of motion implies

\displaystyle d^{A}e^{I}=0.

(3.52)

It is clear that this equation of motion defines
A^{I}{}_{J}
as the spin connection for the frame
e^{I}
. Next, we vary with respect to the frame to find

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S_{EC}}{\delta e^{I}}=\epsilon_{IJKL}e^{J}\wedge\left(F^{KL}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}e^{K}\wedge e^{L}\right)=0.

(3.53)

By raising the index
I
the above equation is a vector valued 3-form. By employing the Hodge star map,
\star
, we can map this onto a 1-form, in components this is

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}\epsilon^{I}{}_{JKL}e^{J}_{\nu}\left(F_{\rho\sigma}{}^{KL}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}e^{K}_{\rho}e^{L}_{\sigma}\right)=0.

(3.54)

Taking advantage of the frame to translate internal and spacetime indices we can use the product of epsilon tensors to find the above is proportional to

\displaystyle F^{IJ}_{\mu\nu}e^{\nu}_{J}-\frac{1}{2}F^{KL}_{\rho\sigma}e^{\rho}_{K}e^{\sigma}_{L}e^{I}_{\mu}-\Lambda e^{I}_{\mu}=0.

(3.55)

Which is equivalent to
eq.

3.41
, which are themselves equivalent to Einstein’s equations on the metric.

We have seen that Einstein’s equations can be written as exterior differential equations on the frame and connection. The Lagrangian in this language is polynomial which is simpler than the metric case, allowing the action to be computed more easily. Another advantage is that the Einstein-Cartan formulation allows for the coupling to fermionic matter, the Dirac matrices on
M
are the pullback of the “flat” internal Dirac matrices, that is
\gamma_{\mu}=e^{I}_{\mu}\gamma_{I}
where
\gamma_{I}
are the gamma matrices on the Minkowski spacetime. We can also see the storage efficiency of this formalism, the frame and connection consist of
16+24=40
components whereas the metric and its Christoffel symbols constitute
10+40=50
components. Therefore, the total number of components one has to compute is reduced at the price of
4
more initial components in the frame. However, the one disadvantage to this formulation is that its Hamiltonian form contains second-class constraints
[
61
]
, which are not present in the metric formulations, this is due to the
24
connection momentum variables. This makes the Hamiltonian tetrad formulation more difficult deal with as one has to solve the second-class constraints manually and this is a difficult task but not impossible see
[
62
]
. A formulation that keeps all the advantages of the frame formulation and that does not contain second-class constraints, is the Plebański formulation of gravity.

Chapter 4
Plebański’s Formulation of general relativity

Plebański’s formulation is at heart of this Thesis, we use it to aid calculations in various areas of differential geometry and general relativity. This section describes the details of this formalism and how it relates to more standard formalisms. In short, it is the description of geometry in dimension
4
through the language of self-dual 2-forms and is a chiral projection of the frame formulation. As with the metric and frame formalisms, the main fields in Plebański’s formulation can also be described as a
G
-structure packed into differential forms, this time it is a triple of 2-forms. Einstein’s equations are then simple algebraic conditions on the curvature of the torsion free connection defined by the
G
-structure. We begin with the reverse construction, in that we assume a metric exists on the spacetime and this defines a triple of 2-forms that encodes said metric. Then we go on to show that the inverse is also possible and the Einstein’s equations can be imposed in either case.

4.1
Historical Remarks

One of the first uses of self-duality in general relativity (GR) came from Petrov’s classification of Einstein spacetimes
[
11
]
, where self-dual bivectors were used to decompose the Weyl curvature into a complex
3\times 3
matrix. Self-dual 2-forms, in the context GR, appeared in
[
13
]
. In this paper the link was made between the self-dual 2-forms and the null tetrad formalism of Newmann-Penrose
[
63
,
12
]
. It also introduces the self-dual connection defined through exterior derivatives of the self-dual 2-forms. This way the 12 complex spin coefficients of the Newmann-Penrose formalism are neatly encoded into the 3 complex 1-forms that are the self-dual connections. Lastly, this paper made clear that Einstein’s equations are equivalent to the statement that the curvature of the self-dual connection is self-dual. This statement is also noted in
[
64
]
, along with further applications of self-dual 2-forms to chiral Yang-Mills theory.

An action principle that imposes Einstein’s equations using the self-dual techniques was discovered by Jerzy Plebański in
[
19
]
. There the triple of self-dual 2-forms are taken as the main variable instead of the metric, given an algebraic condition on those 2-forms that they must satisfy it is possible to show that they can be written as a product of the frame and hence encode the metric. Importantly, no metric is needed to impose Einstein’s equations in this formalism, only the 2-forms need to be introduced. Plebański’s paper uses spinor indices to denote the triple of 2-forms, here we use an
\mathfrak{so}(3,\mathbb{C})
index which was also done in the reference
[
65
]
. This is possible due to the isomorphism between
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
and
\mathfrak{so}(3,\mathbb{C})
and allows us to ignore the more involved spinor algebra.

4.2
Metric and Self-Dual 2-forms

Although the theory of interest does not need to involve the metric for pedagogical reasons it is useful to introduce the metric and show that the self-dual 2-forms appear as a decomposition on the space of 2-forms. In this section we assume that the signature of the spacetime is
(-+++)
, the Euclidean signature version of what follow can be recovered by taking every object to be real valued and replace the imaginary unit with 1.

Let
M
be a
4
dimensional Lorentzian manifold with tangent bundle
TM
and cotangent bundle
T^{*}M
. Let
g\in S^{2}T^{*}M
be the nondegenerate bilinear form we call the Lorentzian metric. Using the metric one can define the Hodge star operator
\star:\Lambda^{n}\rightarrow\Lambda^{4-n}
, between n-forms and
(4-n)
-forms. In this particular dimension the Hodge star is an endomorphism on the space of 2-forms,

\displaystyle\star:\Lambda^{2}\rightarrow\Lambda^{2}.

(4.1)

Applying the Hodge star twice to any 2-form reveals that it is an anti-involution,
\star^{2}=-\mathbb{I}
. The space of 2-forms can then be split into

\displaystyle\Lambda^{2}=\Lambda^{+}\oplus\Lambda^{-}

(4.2)

where
\Lambda^{+}
are the self-dual (SD) and
\Lambda^{-}
the anti-self-dual (ASD) spaces for eigenvalues
+i
and
-i
respectively. As the eigenvalues are complex, this means that each subspace contains complex 2-forms.

We can now consider the wedge product with respect to the decomposition in
eq.

4.2
. It is then known that the restriction of the wedge product metric, that is
\wedge:\Lambda^{2}\otimes\Lambda^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, on
\Lambda^{+}
(or
\Lambda^{-}
) is nondegenerate. Let
\Sigma^{i}\in\Lambda^{+}
be a basis for the SD 2-forms, where
i=1,2,3
, it can then be chosen such that

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{j}\sim\delta^{ij}.

(4.3)

We call this the metricity condition for a triple of 2-forms. The tensor
\delta^{ij}
we call the internal metric as it lives in a “flat” internal space. The group
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
acts on these 2-forms and leaves the metric
\delta^{ij}
invariant under its transformation. As we are in Lorentzian signature the basis of the ASD 2-forms is related to the complex conjugate of the SD basis,

\displaystyle\overline{\Sigma}^{i}=-{(\Sigma^{i})}^{*}\in\Lambda^{-}.

(4.4)

Here
∗
denotes complex conjugation. In this way one can generate the entire space of 2-forms from only the self-dual 2-forms. Self-dual 2-forms in Euclidean signature do not have such a property as they are real valued and no standard transformation exists between the SD and ASD 2-forms. A consequence of this is that instanton solutions to various theories do not exist in Lorentzian signature. In any signature the SD and ASD 2-forms are orthogonal with respect to the wedge product,

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}\wedge\overline{\Sigma}^{j}=0.

(4.5)

Given an arbitrary 2-form
B\in\Lambda^{2}
it is not true that the wedge product of itself and its complex conjugate is zero, this is only true for SD or ASD 2-forms.

This completes the construction of self-dual 2-forms starting from a metric on the manifold. Next, we take the construction in the opposite direction. We show that given a triple of 2-forms, and imposing some algebraic condition, a real valued metric is uniquely defined.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
M
be a 4-dimensional manifold and
Q\subset\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}
be a rank 3 subspace of the complexified 2-forms that is nondegenerate with respect to the wedge product. When
Q\oplus Q^{*}=\Lambda^{2}
, with
Q^{*}
being the complex conjugate of
Q
, then
Q
defines a conformal metric on
M
.

Proof.

We present a proof given in
[
66
]
but modified for Lorentzian signature. First, we take a basis for the space of
Q
,

\displaystyle B^{i}\in Q\in\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{C}},\quad i=1,2,3.

(4.6)

We choose the notation

\displaystyle B^{i}=\frac{1}{2}B^{i}_{\mu\nu}dx^{\mu}\wedge dx^{\nu}

(4.7)

where
B^{i}_{\mu\nu}
are the components of the 2-form. By using the totally antisymmetric symbol
\tilde{\epsilon}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=\pm 1
we can define the bivector dual to
B^{i}
as

\displaystyle\tilde{B}^{i\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\epsilon}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}B^{i}_{\rho\sigma}.

(4.8)

We define a spacetime metric, built from the
B^{i}
’s, using Urbantke’s formula
[
67
]

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{i}{12}\epsilon_{ijk}\tilde{\epsilon}^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}B^{i}_{\mu\rho}B^{j}_{\nu\sigma}B^{k}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{i}{6}\epsilon_{ijk}B^{i}_{\mu\rho}\tilde{B}^{j\rho\sigma}B^{k}_{\sigma\nu}.

(4.9)

As well as an internal metric

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{ij}=\frac{1}{4i}B^{i}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{B}^{j\mu\nu}.

(4.10)

When this metric is positive-definite we say that the same is true of the wedge product metric, the factor of
i
appears in the definition due to the Lorentzian signature. As this metric is also symmetric this means that it is a real-valued matrix. Each of the above metrics are used to raise and lower the indices of their type. One can then show that

\displaystyle B^{(i}_{\mu\rho}\tilde{B}^{j)\rho\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}B^{(i}_{\mu[\rho}B^{j)}_{\alpha\beta]}\tilde{\epsilon}^{\rho\nu\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}B^{i}_{[\mu\rho}B^{j}_{\alpha\beta]}\tilde{\epsilon}^{\rho\nu\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}B^{i}_{[\mu\rho}B^{j}_{\alpha\beta]}\tilde{\epsilon}^{\rho\nu\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}B^{i}_{\mu^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}}B^{j}_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\frac{1}{4!}\delta^{[\mu^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}]}_{\mu\rho\alpha\beta}\tilde{\epsilon}^{\rho\nu\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}B^{i}_{\mu^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}}B^{j}_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\frac{1}{4!}\left(-\tilde{\epsilon}^{\mu^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\mu\rho\alpha\beta}\right)\tilde{\epsilon}^{\rho\nu\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{3!}\left(\frac{1}{2i}\frac{1}{4}B^{i}_{\mu^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}}B^{j}_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\tilde{\epsilon}^{\mu^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\right)\left(-\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\mu\rho\alpha\beta}\tilde{\epsilon}^{\nu\rho\alpha\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=-i\tilde{h}^{ij}\delta_{\mu}^{\nu}

(4.11)

where we have introduced
\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=\pm 1
as the totally antisymmetric tensor with indices down and
(ij)
indicates the indices are symmetrised. The second equality in the first line uses a known result in any dimension that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(A_{\mu[\nu}B_{\rho\sigma]}+B_{\mu[\nu}A_{\rho\sigma]}\right)=A_{[\mu\nu}B_{\rho\sigma]},\quad A,B\in\Lambda^{2}

(4.12)

which can be checked by expanding the symmetries and comparing. A similar argument can also be used to show

\displaystyle\tilde{B}^{(i|\mu\rho}B^{|j)}_{\rho\nu}=-i\tilde{h}^{ij}\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}.

(4.13)

This allows us to show that

\displaystyle B^{i}_{\mu\rho}\tilde{B}^{j\rho\sigma}B^{k}_{\sigma\nu}-B^{k}_{\mu\rho}\tilde{B}^{j\rho\sigma}B^{i}_{\sigma\nu}=-2i\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}.

(4.14)

To see this we first note that by symmetrising
ij
we have

\displaystyle B^{(i}_{\mu\rho}\tilde{B}^{j)\rho\sigma}B^{k}_{\sigma\nu}-B^{k}_{\mu\rho}\tilde{B}^{(j\rho\sigma}B^{i)}_{\sigma\nu}=-i\tilde{h}^{ij}B^{k}_{\mu\nu}+iB^{k}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{h}^{ji}=0

(4.15)

where we have used that
\tilde{h}^{[ij]}=0
. Using the same arguments we can also see that
eq.

4.14
is also antisymmetric in
jk
and as it is antisymmetric in
ik
by construction we can see that it is totally antisymmetric and hence proportional to
\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{g}
. By inverting the internal metric we can define

\displaystyle\underaccent{\tilde}{B}_{i\mu\nu}=\underaccent{\tilde}{h}_{ij}B^{j}_{\mu\nu},\quad\textrm{and}\quad B_{i}^{\mu\nu}=\underaccent{\tilde}{h}_{ij}\tilde{B}^{j\mu\nu}

(4.16)

with
\underaccent{\tilde}{h}_{ij}
being the inverse of
\tilde{h}^{ij}
. From which we get the following identities

\displaystyle B_{i}^{\mu\rho}B^{i}_{\rho\nu}=\underaccent{\tilde}{B}_{i\mu\rho}\tilde{B}^{i\rho\nu}

\displaystyle=-3i\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}

(4.17)

\displaystyle B_{j}^{\mu\alpha}B^{i}_{\alpha\rho}\tilde{B}^{j\rho\nu}

\displaystyle=i\tilde{B}^{i\mu\nu}

(4.18)

\displaystyle B^{j}_{\mu\rho}\tilde{B}^{i\rho\sigma}\underaccent{\tilde}{B}_{j\sigma\nu}

\displaystyle=iB^{i}_{\mu\nu}

(4.19)

Using this and contracting on the left side of
eq.

4.14
we find

\displaystyle\tilde{B}^{[i\mu\rho}B^{j]}_{\rho\nu}=\epsilon^{ijk}B_{k}^{\mu\rho}\tilde{g}_{\rho\nu}

(4.20)

such that

\displaystyle\tilde{B}^{i\mu\rho}B^{j}_{\rho\nu}=-i\tilde{h}^{ij}\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}+\epsilon^{ijk}B_{k}^{\mu\rho}\tilde{g}_{\rho\nu}.

(4.21)

Now defining an a priori unrelated inverse metric

\displaystyle\underaccent{\tilde}{g}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{6\det(\tilde{h})}\epsilon_{ijk}\tilde{B}^{i\mu\rho}B^{j}_{\rho\sigma}\tilde{B}^{k\sigma\nu}

(4.22)

and reperforming the previous steps we arrive at the result

\displaystyle\tilde{B}^{[i\mu\rho}B^{j]}_{\rho\nu}=i\det(\tilde{h})\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{g}^{\mu\rho}\underaccent{\tilde}{B}_{k\rho\nu}.

(4.23)

Therefore we conclude that

\displaystyle\tilde{B}^{i\mu\rho}\tilde{g}_{\rho\nu}=i\det(\tilde{h})\underaccent{\tilde}{g}^{\mu\rho}B^{i}_{\rho\nu}

(4.24)

Next we can verify that
\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}
and
\underaccent{\tilde}{g}^{\mu\nu}
are inverses of each other. This is checked by computing

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{\mu\rho}\underaccent{\tilde}{g}^{\rho\nu}=\frac{i}{36\det(\tilde{h})}\epsilon_{ijk}\epsilon_{lmn}B^{i}_{\mu\alpha}\tilde{B}^{j\alpha\beta}B^{k}_{\beta\rho}\tilde{B}^{l\rho\sigma}B^{m}_{\sigma\gamma}\tilde{B}^{n\gamma\nu}

(4.25)

then using

\displaystyle\epsilon_{ijk}\epsilon_{lmn}=\epsilon_{ijk}\epsilon^{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}\det(\tilde{h})\underaccent{\tilde}{h}_{ll^{\prime}}\underaccent{\tilde}{h}_{mm^{\prime}}\underaccent{\tilde}{h}_{nn^{\prime}}

(4.26)

and expanding the antisymmetric tensors into Kronecker delta’s and lowering indices with
\underaccent{\tilde}{h}_{ij}
we are able to use the previous algebra on
B
’s to show

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{\mu\rho}\underaccent{\tilde}{g}^{\rho\nu}=\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}.

(4.27)

From this we immediately see that

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{\mu\rho}\tilde{B}^{i\rho\sigma}\tilde{g}_{\sigma\nu}=i\det(\tilde{h})B^{i}_{\mu\nu}

(4.28)

Applying this relation to itself again reveals that

\displaystyle\det(\tilde{g})=-\det(\tilde{h})^{2}.

(4.29)

Therefore, it is convenient to introduce a physical metric
g_{\mu\nu}=\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}/\sqrt{-g}
and define
\tilde{h}^{ij}=\sqrt{-g}h^{ij}
such that the above becomes

\displaystyle\det(h)^{2}=1.

(4.30)

The soon-to-be self-duality relation becomes

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho\sigma}B^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=i\det(h)B^{i}_{\mu\nu}

(4.31)

from which it is easy to see that
\det(h)=1
is required for
B^{i}
to be self-dual, and we have used
\tilde{\epsilon}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=\sqrt{-g}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
from the definition of the Levi-Civita tensor. Finally, we see that 2-forms
B^{i}
define the metric
g_{\mu\nu}
through Urbantke’s formula that makes them self-dual. This metric only defines a conformal class as it can be multiplied by an arbitrary function and the above proof does not change.
∎

The metric in the above proof can be complex valued, this does not represent a physical situation and as such some reality conditions need to be imposed. If the physical metric defined in
eq.

4.9
is real-valued and the internal metric
eq.

4.10
is positive-definite metric, then
\bar{B}^{i}
are anti-self-dual with respect to the real-valued physical metric, that is

\displaystyle\star\overline{B}^{i}=-i\overline{B}^{i}

(4.32)

where
\bar{B}^{i}=-(B^{*})^{i}
are the anti-self-dual 2-forms. In this case it is easy to show that the reality condition,
eq.

4.5
, is automatically satisfied. The argument in the other direction is more involved and leads to the following proposition.

Proposition 4.2
.

A triple of complex 2-forms
B^{i}
, that satisfy the weak metricity and reality conditions

\displaystyle B^{i}\wedge B^{j}=2ih^{ij}\nu_{B}\quad B^{i}\wedge\overline{B}^{j}=0

(4.33)

where
h^{ij}
is now a real positive-definite matrix and
\nu_{B}=\frac{1}{6i}B_{i}\wedge B^{i}
is the top form, define a real metric given by

\displaystyle g(u,v)\nu_{B}=\frac{i}{6}\epsilon_{ijk}\iota_{u}B^{i}\wedge\iota_{v}B^{j}\wedge B^{k}.

(4.34)

With which the 2-forms
B^{i}
are self-dual and
\overline{B}^{i}
are anti-self-dual.

Proof.

We have already seen that the metric defined by
eq.

4.9
makes
B^{i}
self-dual, we have left to check that the condition
B^{i}\wedge\overline{B}^{j}=0
implies the metric is real-valued. We being by decomposing the 2-form
B^{i}
into real and imaginary parts

\displaystyle B^{i}=S^{i}+iP^{i}

(4.35)

where
S^{i},P^{i}\in\Lambda^{2}
. Which gives a similar decomposition for
\overline{B}^{i}
,

\displaystyle\overline{B}^{i}=-S^{i}+iP^{i}

(4.36)

The reality and metricity conditions then imply

\displaystyle S^{i}\wedge S^{j}=0,\quad P^{i}\wedge P^{j}=0,\quad S^{i}\wedge P^{j}=h^{ij}\nu_{B}.

(4.37)

Solutions to this can be found by introducing a frame
e^{0},e^{i}\in\Lambda^{1}
, see 5.4.4 of
[
68
]
. The solutions are then

\displaystyle P^{i}=e^{0}\wedge e^{i},\quad S^{i}=-\frac{1}{2}h^{ij}\epsilon_{jkl}e^{k}\wedge e^{l}

(4.38)

The solutions for
P^{i}
and
S^{i}
can swap or change sign, this amounts to a change of sign for the top form or making
B^{i}
anti-self-dual. The orientation chosen here matches the rest of the formulas in the Thesis.
We choose this orientation and take
B^{i}
to be the combination of
S^{i},P^{i}
above. The top form is given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{6i}h_{ij}B^{i}\wedge B^{j}=\frac{1}{6}\epsilon_{ijk}e^{i}\wedge e^{j}\wedge e^{k}\wedge e^{0}=\nu_{B}.

(4.39)

As
e^{i}\wedge e^{j}\wedge e^{k}
is totally antisymmetric we can write
e^{i}\wedge e^{j}\wedge e^{k}\wedge e^{0}=\epsilon^{ijk}\nu_{B}
. Computing the metric we find that in terms of the frame we have

\displaystyle g(u,v)=-\iota_{u}e^{0}\iota_{v}e^{0}+h_{ij}\iota_{u}e^{i}\iota_{v}e^{j}.

(4.40)

As the metric
h^{ij}
is positive-definite so is its inverse, it being symmetric also implies that it is real-valued and therefore the metric is real-valued.

∎

We now relate the arbitrary triple
B^{i}
to the Plebański triple
\Sigma^{i}
. We restrict ourselves to the case where the weak metricity and reality conditions are satisfied, as such the matrix
h_{ij}
is positive-definite and there exists a transformation matrix
b_{a}^{i}
such that

\displaystyle\delta_{ab}=b_{a}^{i}b_{b}^{j}h_{ij},\quad\textrm{or}\quad h_{ij}=b_{i}^{a}b_{j}^{b}\delta_{ab}

(4.41)

where
a,b,\ldots=1,2,3
and as
h_{ij}
is positive-definite this is always possible. Moreover, as
\det(h)=1
this implies that
\det(b)=\pm 1
for which we take the positive branch
\det(b)=1
. This choice does not fully determine
b
and there remains an
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
rotation freedom, that is
b^{a}_{i}
and
m^{a}{}_{b}b^{b}_{i}
, where
m\in SO(3,\mathbb{C})
, produce the same desired result. The transformation between the two triples of two forms is then

\displaystyle\Sigma^{a}=b^{a}_{i}B^{i}.

(4.42)

This is equivalent to a change of 3-frame
e^{i}=b^{i}_{a}e^{a}
with

\displaystyle\Sigma^{a}=ie^{0}\wedge e^{a}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{abc}e^{b}\wedge e^{c}

(4.43)

where the raised and lowered indices
a
do not change their meaning as their metric is
\delta^{ab}
. By choosing the positive determinant for
b
we find that the volume forms for the two triples have the same sign, indeed computing the transformation we find

\displaystyle 6\nu_{B}=\epsilon_{ijk}e^{i}\wedge e^{j}\wedge e^{k}\wedge e^{0}=\epsilon_{ijk}b^{i}_{a}b^{j}_{b}b^{k}_{c}e^{a}\wedge e^{b}\wedge e^{c}\wedge e^{0}=\epsilon_{abc}e^{a}\wedge e^{b}\wedge e^{c}\wedge e^{0}=6\nu_{\Sigma}.

(4.44)

We see that the metricity condition becomes

\displaystyle\Sigma^{a}\wedge\Sigma^{b}=b^{a}_{i}b^{b}_{j}B^{i}\wedge B^{j}=2ib^{a}_{i}b^{b}_{j}h^{ij}\nu_{B}=2i\delta^{ab}\nu_{\Sigma}.

(4.45)

If we relabel the indices
a,b,c,\ldots\rightarrow i,j,k,\ldots
, we can “forget” that the metric
h^{ij}
ever existed and work with the flat internal metric
\delta^{ij}
. In this way we recover the triple
\Sigma^{i}
that is self-dual and satisfies the metricity and reality condition
eqs.

4.3
and
4.5
. Much like the freedom in choosing the volume form for the physical metric we are also free to choose which triple of 2-forms we wish to make fundamental, the choice here is to make the following algebra much simpler. Seeing as this choice is always possible we define the internal metric to be orthonormal throughout the rest of the Thesis. With the internal metric being the identity matrix we have reduced the group of transformations on the 2-forms from
GL(3,\mathbb{C})
to
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
. The fact that there is a group transformation on the 2-forms that leaves the metric they describe invariant suggests that these objects also arise from
G
-structure on a principal bundle.

4.3
Plebański 2-forms as
G
-structures

In the frame formalism, we have seen that the metric is encoded using a collection of differential forms. Similarly, here we find it is a triple of complex 2-forms instead of a 4 real valued 1-forms that encode the metric. We also know that frames arise naturally as soldering forms on a principal bundle. It is then interesting to consider a similar construction for self-dual 2-forms.

To this end we introduce the rank 3 biframe bundle
QM
over a manifold
M
,

\displaystyle QM=\coprod_{x\in M}Q_{x},\quad Q_{x}=\{\left.f_{x}\right|f_{x}:Q\subset\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}|_{x}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{3}\}.

(4.46)

Where
\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}|_{x}
is the space of complexified 2-forms at the point
x
and
Q
is a rank 3 subspace of
\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}|_{x}
. The maps
f_{x}
are isomorphisms from this subspace to
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, any such map we call a biframe. The group
GL(3,\mathbb{C})
acts naturally on
QM
by identifying two biframes. This identification allows the
QM
to be realised as a
GL(3,\mathbb{C})
principal bundle
P
,
GL(3,\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow P\overset{\pi_{P}}{\rightarrow}M
. The soldering form is then defined as

\displaystyle\varSigma(X,Y)=f^{-1}(d\pi_{P}(X)\wedge d\pi_{P}(Y)|_{Q})\in\Lambda^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{3}),\quad X,Y\in T_{p}P.

(4.47)

It is simple to check that this soldering form is
G
-equivariant by computing
R^{*}_{g}\varSigma=g^{-1}\varSigma
and that it is Horizontal as vertical vectors are in the kernel of
d\pi_{P}
. This means that
\varSigma
is a tensorial differential form just like the usual soldering form. We now fix the internal metric on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
to be
\delta^{ij}
and restrict our biframe bundle to contain only the biframes that are isometries between the internal metric and the wedge product metric. That is they satisfy

\displaystyle\varSigma(X,Y)\wedge\varSigma(V,W)\sim(d\pi_{P}(X)\wedge d\pi_{P}(Y)\wedge d\pi_{P}(V)\wedge d\pi_{P}(W))\delta

(4.48)

where
\delta\in S^{2}\mathbb{C}^{3}
is the internal metric on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. This restricts the structure group to be
SO(3,\mathbb{C})\subset GL(3,\mathbb{C})
and therefore we are considering
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
-structures.
This defines the wedge product metric in the same way as the usual metric appears in the frame formulations. To recover the metric on the tangent space one needs to evaluate Urbantke’s formula, see
eq.

4.97
. A connection on the principal bundle is then a Lie algebra valued 1-form

\displaystyle A\in\Lambda^{1}(P,\mathfrak{so}(3,\mathbb{C})).

(4.49)

From the general theory in
chapter

2
we know this connection defines a covariant derivative on tensorial q-forms. Indeed, the torsion of the soldering form is

\displaystyle\Theta=D\varSigma=d\varSigma+A\wedge\varSigma.

(4.50)

The curvature of the connection is also defined as usual

\displaystyle R=DA\in\Lambda^{2}(P,\mathfrak{so}(3,\mathbb{C})).

(4.51)

We have seen that the triple of self-dual 2-forms has an interpretation of soldering forms on a principal
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
-bundle. In the frame bundle it was shown that the curvature of the connection encodes the correct components of the Riemann curvature tensor needed to impose Einstein’s equations. The upcoming sections explain how the same is true for the triple of self-dual 2-forms, and it is shown how Einstein’s equations can be imposed elegantly in this formulation.

4.4
Local Soldering form, Connection and Curvature

In this section we summarise the move to the local versions of the objects on the principal bundle
P
. Associated to this bundle is the vector bundle
E=P\times_{SO(3,\mathbb{C})}\mathbb{C}^{3}
and by introducing a local section
\sigma:U\subset M\rightarrow P
we induce a trivialisation
E|_{U}=U\times\mathbb{C}^{3}
. The pullback of the soldering forms under this section are

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}\in Q=\Lambda^{+}\subset\Lambda^{2}(M)

(4.52)

where the index
i=1,2,3
labels the components in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
which we call the internal indices, Equivalently it is an
\mathfrak{so}(3,\mathbb{C})
Lie algebra index. Pulling back the isometry condition results the metricity condition on the triple of 2-forms

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{j}\sim\delta^{ij}.

(4.53)

For a real valued metric, as we have seen before, it is required that
\Sigma^{i}\wedge\overline{\Sigma}^{j}=0
. The Lie algebra of
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
has its matrix representation in
3\times 3
antisymmetric matrices and as such we can view the connection as the following 1-form on
M
,

\displaystyle A^{ij}\in\Lambda^{1}(M,\mathfrak{so}(3,\mathbb{C}))

(4.54)

where
A^{ij}=A^{[ij]}
is antisymmetric. The curvature of this connection is

\displaystyle F^{ij}=dA^{ij}+A^{ik}\wedge A^{kj}.

(4.55)

The metric on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
is flat and the position of the indices carries no meaning, therefore, we keep all internal indices in the raised position. In dimension 3 there is an isomorphism between vectors and antisymmetric matrices, explicitly it is captured by the map

\displaystyle\phi(m)^{i}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}m^{jk}

(4.56)

and its inverse

\displaystyle\phi^{-1}(v)^{ij}=\epsilon^{ijk}v^{k}.

(4.57)

We take advantage of this map to introduce the connection 1-form with values in
\mathbb{C}^{3}

\displaystyle A^{i}=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}A^{jk}\in\Lambda^{1}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{3}.

(4.58)

The exterior covariant derivative on the soldering form is then

\displaystyle d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=d\Sigma^{i}+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}.

(4.59)

On a tensor with an arbitrary number of internal indices the exterior covariant derivative is

\displaystyle d^{A}\Psi^{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}=d\Psi^{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}+\epsilon^{i_{1}jk}A^{j}\Psi^{ki_{2}\ldots i_{n}}+\epsilon^{i_{2}jk}A^{j}\Psi^{i_{1}ki_{3}\ldots i_{n}}+\cdots+\epsilon^{i_{n}\ldots jk}A^{j}\Psi^{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots k}.

(4.60)

The curvature in this representation is given by

\displaystyle F^{i}=dA^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\wedge A^{k}.

(4.61)

This means we can view the 2-form frame, connection 1-form and curvature form as
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued differential forms.

4.5
Decomposition of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued Differential forms

As we have seen,
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued differential forms make up most of the objects of interest in the study of
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
-structures. It is then meaningful to study the irreducible representations of these objects with respect to
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
. This section is the Lorentzian version of the calculation done in
[
15
]
, however, most of the results are identical. We have the following result for the Lorentz group that
SO(1,3)=SL(2,\mathbb{C})/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, and since each element of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
is complex it has a complex conjugate forming the group
\overline{SL(2,\mathbb{C})}
which both act on separate chiral halves. As such we can denote the decomposition of the Lorentz group as
SO(1,3)=SL(2,\mathbb{C})\times\overline{SL(2,\mathbb{C})}/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. One of the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
act on
\Sigma^{i}
with an
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
transformation, the other leaves
\Sigma^{i}
invariant. We call the first
SL{(2,\mathbb{C})}_{+}
for which we denote the
{\rm spin}(k/2)
representations as
S_{+}^{k}
and the second
SL{(2,\mathbb{C})}_{-}
with
S_{-}^{k}
. They are of dimension
\dim(S_{\pm}^{k})=k+1
and decomposes the following interesting spaces into spinors representations

\displaystyle\Lambda^{1}=S_{+}\otimes S_{-},\quad\Lambda^{2}=S_{+}^{2}\oplus S_{-}^{2},\quad\mathbb{C}^{3}=S_{+}^{2}.

(4.62)

First we introduce the components for the triple of 2-forms

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}dx^{\mu}\wedge dx^{\nu}

(4.63)

where
\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}
are the components of the 2-forms. By raising a spacetime index we can create a triple of endomorphisms of
\Lambda^{1}
,
\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}:\Lambda^{1}\rightarrow\Lambda^{1}
.

4.5.1
Algebra and Identities

The self-dual (SD) endomorphism,
\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}
, can be shown to satisfy a quaternion algebra,

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\Sigma^{j}_{\rho}{}^{\nu}=-\delta^{ij}\delta_{\mu}{}^{\nu}+\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{k}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}.

(4.64)

There are other important identities

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=12i\delta_{\alpha}^{[\mu}\delta_{\beta}^{\nu}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma]}.

(4.65)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=g_{\mu\rho}g_{\nu\sigma}-g_{\mu\sigma}g_{\nu\rho}-i\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}

(4.66)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=-2\Sigma^{i}_{[\mu|\rho|}g_{\nu]\sigma}+2\Sigma^{i}_{[\mu|\sigma|}g_{\nu]\rho}

(4.67)

The first identity can be derived by employing the self-duality relation to replace
\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha\beta}
with
\frac{-i}{2}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\gamma\pi}\Sigma^{i}_{\gamma\pi}
. Then by expanding the product of two Levi-Civita tensors we find the desired result. The second result can be found by noting that the symmetries restrict the form to be the sum of
g_{\mu\rho}g_{\nu\sigma}-g_{\mu\sigma}g_{\nu\rho}
and
\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
, the coefficients can then be found by performing contractions with the metric and Levi-Civita symbol. The last identity follows in a similar way, in that the r.h.s is the only object with the correct indices and symmetries. The overall factor is determined by comparing both sides after contractions and using the quaternion algebra. The corresponding algebra for the anti-self-dual (ASD) 2-forms
\overline{\Sigma}^{i}=-(\Sigma^{i})^{*}
are relatively simple to derive using complex conjugate and the definition of the ASD 2-forms. Their identities are then

\displaystyle\overline{\Sigma}^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\overline{\Sigma}^{j}_{\rho}{}^{\nu}

\displaystyle=-\delta^{ij}\delta_{\mu}{}^{\nu}-\epsilon^{ijk}\overline{\Sigma}^{k}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}

(4.68)

\displaystyle\overline{\Sigma}^{i}_{\mu\nu}\overline{\Sigma}^{i}_{\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=g_{\mu\rho}g_{\nu\sigma}-g_{\mu\sigma}g_{\nu\rho}+i\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\overline{\Sigma}^{j}_{\mu\nu}\overline{\Sigma}^{k}_{\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=2\overline{\Sigma}^{i}_{[\mu|\rho|}g_{\nu]\sigma}-2\overline{\Sigma}^{i}_{[\mu|\sigma|}g_{\nu]\rho}

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\overline{\Sigma}^{i}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-12i\delta_{\alpha}^{[\mu}\delta_{\beta}^{\nu}\overline{\Sigma}^{i\rho\sigma]}.

The orthogonality of the SD and ASD 2-forms is equivalent to the condition

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\overline{\Sigma}^{j\mu\nu}=0.

(4.69)

Which is another way of writing the reality condition,
\Sigma^{i}\wedge\overline{\Sigma}^{j}=0
. Another condition involving both the SD and ASD 2-forms is

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\rho}\overline{\Sigma}^{j\rho}{}_{\nu}=\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu|\rho|}\overline{\Sigma}^{j\rho}{}_{\nu\rangle}.

(4.70)

Where
A_{\langle\mu\nu\rangle}=\frac{1}{2}(A_{\mu\nu}+A_{\nu\mu}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}g^{\rho\sigma}A_{\rho\sigma})
denotes the symmetric tracefree components of the tensor
A_{\mu\nu}
. This identity can be checked by contracting the free indices,
\mu\nu
, with
\Sigma^{k\mu\nu}
and
\overline{\Sigma}^{k\mu\nu}
to find that it vanishes. Next, by contracting with the metric
g^{\mu\nu}
we find the result is also zero, hence
eq.

4.70
has only its symmetric and traceless components. Any symmetric tensor can be decomposed into

\displaystyle S_{(\mu\nu)}=Sg_{\mu\nu}+S^{ij}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\overline{\Sigma}^{j}_{\rho\nu}

(4.71)

as
eq.

4.70
and the metric form a basis for the symmetric
(0,2)
-tensors. With the algebra at hand we can now start deriving the decompositions of the spaces of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued differential forms.

Proposition 4.3
.

Given a triple of endomorphisms
\Sigma^{i}
that satisfy the reality, metricity conditions and the quaternion algebra, there is an operator
J_{1}:\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}

that decomposes the space
\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}
into a 4 dimensional and 8 dimensional part

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}=(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}){}_{4}\oplus(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}){}_{8}=(S_{+}\otimes S_{-})\oplus(S_{+}^{3}\otimes S_{-})

(4.72)

with eigenvalues
2,-1
respectively.

Proof.

We begin by introducing the operator on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued 1-forms

\displaystyle J_{1}(A)^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}A^{k}_{\nu}.

(4.73)

Squaring this and using the quaternion algebra we find

\displaystyle J_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{ikl}\Sigma^{k}\epsilon^{lmj}\Sigma^{m}=2\delta^{ij}\mathbb{I}+\epsilon^{ikj}\Sigma^{k}

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{I}+J_{1}.

(4.74)

Factorising the above reveals

\displaystyle(J_{1}-2)(J_{1}+1)=0

(4.75)

therefore, the eigenvalues are
+2,-1
. The 4-dimensional eigenspace of the operator
J_{1}
is parametrised by a 1-form
\xi_{\mu}\in\Lambda^{1}=S_{+}\otimes S_{-}
and the corresponding
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued 1-form is

\displaystyle\theta^{i}_{\mu}=\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\xi_{\nu}\in(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}){}_{4}.

(4.76)

We see by computing

\displaystyle J_{1}(\theta)^{i}_{\mu}=\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\xi_{\rho}=2\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\xi_{\nu}=2\theta^{i}_{\mu}

(4.77)

that it has an eigenvalue of
+2
. The 8 dimensional space is then the compliment of the 12 dimensional
\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}
and this 4-dimensional part. That is given a
\theta\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}
we extract the 4-dimensional 1-forms components through

\displaystyle\theta_{\mu}=\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\theta^{i}_{\nu}.

(4.78)

Using the ansatz for the
(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}){}_{4}
we obtain

\displaystyle(\theta^{i}_{\mu}){}_{4}=\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\theta_{\nu}=\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\theta^{j}_{\rho}=-\theta^{i}_{\mu}-\epsilon^{ikj}\Sigma^{k}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\theta^{j}_{\nu}=-(\mathbb{I}+J_{1})(\theta)^{i}_{\mu}.

(4.79)

The compliment of this in
\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}
is

\displaystyle(\theta^{i}_{\mu}){}_{8}=\theta^{i}_{\mu}-(\theta^{i}_{\mu}){}_{4}=(2-J_{1})(\theta)^{i}_{\mu}\in(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}){}_{8}=(S_{+}^{3}\otimes S_{-}).

(4.80)

By acting with
J_{1}
we recover

\displaystyle J_{1}(\theta_{8})^{i}_{\mu}=-(\theta_{8})^{i}_{\mu}

(4.81)

the eigenvalue to be
-1
. As such
J_{1}
defines the decomposition stated.
∎

Next we have a similar result for
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued 2-forms.

Proposition 4.4
.

Given a triple of endomorphisms
\Sigma^{i}
that satisfy the reality, metricity conditions and the quaternion algebra, we define an operator
J_{2}:\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}
that decomposition the space of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued 2-forms into

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}){}_{1}\oplus(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}){}_{3}\oplus(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}){}_{5}\oplus(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}){}_{9}

(4.82)

\displaystyle=C^{\infty}(M)\oplus S_{+}^{2}\oplus S_{+}^{4}\oplus(S_{+}^{2}\otimes S_{-}^{2})

(4.83)

with eigenvalues
0,1,-1,2
respectively.

Proof.

First, let us define the operator
J_{2}
,

\displaystyle J_{2}(B)^{i}_{\mu\nu}=\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{[\mu|}{}^{\rho}B^{k}_{\rho|\nu]},\quad B^{i}_{\mu\nu}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}.

(4.84)

A lengthy computation gives us

\displaystyle J_{2}^{2}(B)^{i}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}B^{i}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{i}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho\sigma}B^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}J_{2}(B)^{i}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{[\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu]}{}^{\beta}B^{j}_{\alpha\beta}

(4.85)

\displaystyle J_{2}^{3}(B)^{i}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho\sigma}B^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+2J_{2}(B)^{i}_{\mu\nu}+\Sigma^{i}_{[\mu}{}^{\rho}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu]}{}^{\sigma}B^{j}_{\rho\sigma}

(4.86)

\displaystyle J_{2}^{4}(B)^{i}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}B^{i}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{3i}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho\sigma}B^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+\frac{5}{2}J_{2}(B)^{i}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{5}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{[\mu}{}^{\rho}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu]}{}^{\sigma}B^{j}_{\rho\sigma}.

(4.87)

Taking the appropriate combinations we find

\displaystyle J_{2}^{4}-2J_{2}^{3}-J_{2}^{2}+2J_{2}=0,\quad\Rightarrow J_{2}(J_{2}-2)(J_{2}-1)(J_{2}+1)=0

(4.88)

which identifies the correct eigenvalues. As in the 1-form case we can look for parametrisations of the eigenspaces. First we compute the action of
J_{2}
on
M^{ij}\Sigma^{j}
where
M^{ij}=\frac{1}{3}\Tr(M)\delta^{ij}+M_{stf}^{\langle ij\rangle}+M_{a}^{[ij]}
to find that

\displaystyle J_{2}(M^{ij}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu})

\displaystyle=\Tr(M)\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}-M^{ji}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{2}{3}\Tr(M)\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}-M_{stf}^{ij}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}+M_{a}^{ij}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}.

(4.89)

From which we read off that multiples of
\Sigma^{i}
span the
+2
eigenspace,
-1
is spanned by
M_{stf}^{ij}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}
and
+1
is spanned by
M_{a}^{ij}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}
. For the final eigenspace we parametrise using a tracefree tensor
h_{\mu}{}^{\nu}
such that
h_{[\mu}{}^{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{|\rho|\nu]}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}
. Before acting with
J_{2}
we note that
h_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{4}hg_{\mu\nu}+h_{[\mu\nu]}+h_{(\mu\nu)}
, the trace part has already been considered previously. The antisymmetric part can be decomposed into self-dual and anti-self-dual parts
h_{[\mu\nu]}=h^{i}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}+\bar{h}^{i}\overline{\Sigma}^{i}_{\mu\nu}
. It can then be seen that
h^{i}
is equivalent to
M_{a}^{ij}
and can be ignored. For
\bar{h}^{i}
, the contribution vanishes due to
eq.

4.70
. We are left with
h_{\mu\nu}=h_{\langle\mu\nu\rangle}
being the remaining 9 dimensional symmetric tracefree components that are not already considered in the previous components. The action of
J_{2}
on these components gives

\displaystyle J_{2}(h_{[\mu}{}^{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{|\rho|\nu]})=\frac{1}{2}h_{\rho}{}^{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}=0.

(4.90)

Therefore, we see that
h_{[\mu}{}^{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{|\rho|\nu]}
, with
h_{\mu\nu}=h_{\langle\mu\nu\rangle}
parametrises the 9 dimensional eigenspace with eigenvalue 0.
∎

The maps in the proof above are from the irreducible spaces into
\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}
, we can define the maps going in the opposite direction. Given an object
B^{i}_{\mu\nu}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}
we have irreducible components

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}B^{i}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\in C^{\infty}(

\displaystyle M)

(4.91)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}B^{k}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{C}^{3}

\displaystyle=S_{+}^{2}

(4.92)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\langle j\mu\nu}B^{i\rangle}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\in S^{2}_{0}\mathbb{C}^{3}

\displaystyle=S_{+}^{4}

(4.93)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\rho}B^{i}_{|\rho|\nu\rangle}

\displaystyle\in S^{2}_{0}\Lambda^{1}

\displaystyle=S_{+}^{2}\otimes S_{-}^{2}

(4.94)

for the 1,3,5 and 9 dimensional components respectively. Here we have used that
S^{2}_{0}
denotes the symmetric tracefree part. Any higher degree differential forms are isomorphic to lower degree forms through the Hodge star operator, therefore, our analysis up to
\Lambda^{2}
completes the decomposition for
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued differential forms. A corollary for the previous proposition is

Corollary 4.1
.

The space
(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}){}_{9}=\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{-}
is the space of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued anti-self-dual 2-forms.

Proof.

Given an arbitrary symmetric tracefree
h_{\mu\nu}
we have that

\displaystyle h_{[\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\nu]}\in(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}){}_{9}

(4.95)

We then compute the result of the Hodge star, using
eq.

4.65
, to find

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho\sigma}h_{[\rho}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\sigma]}=-ih_{[\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\nu]}\in\Lambda^{-}

(4.96)

and is therefore anti-self-dual.
∎

4.6
Connection, Intrinsic Torsion and Curvature

Employing the established algebra in the previous section we are able to compare, as was done with the
SO(1,3)
frame formalism, with the standard metric definitions of curvature. We fix a representative metric in the conformal class that we choose to be our physical metric,

\displaystyle\sqrt{-g}g_{\mu\nu}=\frac{i}{12}\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{\epsilon}^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\rho}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu\sigma}\Sigma^{k}_{\alpha\beta}.

(4.97)

There is then a unique metric covariant derivative
\nabla_{\mu}
that is compatible with the metric
\nabla_{\mu}g_{\rho\sigma}=0
. The metric covariant derivative can be expanded as the usual partial derivative plus the Christoffel symbols
\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}
, when assume no torsion for the metric we can uniquely solve for the Christoffel symbols in terms of partial derivatives of the metric. In this case the Christoffel symbols are symmetric in the lower two indices
\Gamma^{\rho}_{[\mu\nu]}=0
. Using this metric covariant derivative we define the torsion of the 2-forms
\Sigma^{i}
to be

\displaystyle T_{\mu\rho\sigma}^{i}=\nabla_{\mu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}.

(4.98)

For which we have the following result.

Proposition 4.5
.

The intrinsic torsion of a triple self-dual 2-form
\Sigma^{i}
, that satisfies the quaternion algebra
eq.

4.64
, can be encoded into a
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued 1-form.

Proof.

Given an arbitrary vector field
X^{\mu}\in TM
we can define the following
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued 2-form from the torsion

\displaystyle\nabla_{X}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}=X^{\rho}\nabla_{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}=T_{X}^{i}{}_{\mu\nu}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}.

(4.99)

Taking the projection to the irreducible parts reveals

\displaystyle\Sigma^{(j\mu\nu}\nabla_{X}\Sigma^{i)}_{\mu\nu}=\nabla_{X}(\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu})=4\nabla_{X}\delta^{ij}=0

(4.100)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\nabla_{X}\Sigma^{k}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{8}T^{i}_{X}

(4.101)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\rho}\nabla_{X}\Sigma^{i}_{|\rho|\nu)}=\nabla_{X}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{|\rho|\nu)}=-3\nabla_{X}g_{\mu\nu}=0.

(4.102)

The corresponding irreducible components of the torsion are

\displaystyle\Sigma^{(j\mu\nu}\nabla_{X}\Sigma^{i)}_{\mu\nu}=\Sigma^{(j\mu\nu}T_{X}^{i)}{}_{\mu\nu}=T_{X}^{(ij)}

(4.103)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\nabla_{X}\Sigma^{k}_{\mu\nu}=\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}T_{X}^{k}{}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{8}T_{X}^{i}

(4.104)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\rho}\nabla_{X}\Sigma^{i}_{|\rho|\nu)}=\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\rho}T_{X}^{i}{}_{|\rho|\nu)}=T_{X\mu\nu}

(4.105)

Therefore, we see that
T^{i}_{X}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
is the only non-zero component. As this was computed with an arbitrary
X^{\mu}
we find the

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\rho\sigma}\nabla_{\mu}\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}=\frac{1}{8}T^{i}_{\mu}

(4.106)

parametrises all the active components of intrinsic torsion. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=\epsilon^{ijk}T^{j}_{\mu}\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}

(4.107)

where the intrinsic torsion is
T^{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}
.
∎

We are now able to see that the intrinsic torsion is directly related to the torsion free connection 1-form.

Proposition 4.6
.

Given a self-dual triple
\Sigma^{i}
there exists a unique torsion free
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued connection 1-form
A^{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}
. This connection is then minus the intrinsic torsion.

Proof.

We start with the definition of torsion free

\displaystyle d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=d\Sigma^{i}+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}=0

(4.108)

which is a
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued 3-form. To bring this condition into a more useful form we apply the Hodge star to both sides, in components this is

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\nu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+\epsilon^{ijk}\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}A^{j}_{\nu}\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}=0

(4.109)

using the self-duality of
\Sigma^{i}
we see that

\displaystyle 2i\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}A^{k}_{\nu}=\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\nu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}

(4.110)

In operator notation we have

\displaystyle J_{1}(A)^{i}=-i\star d\Sigma^{i}.

(4.111)

From
J_{1}^{2}-J_{2}-2=0
we see that
J_{1}^{-1}=\frac{1}{2}(J_{1}-1)
is well-defined, therefore the connection is

\displaystyle A^{i}=-iJ_{1}^{-1}(\star d\Sigma)^{i}.

(4.112)

Which uniquely defines the torsion free connection from
\Sigma^{i}
. By antisymmetrising the torsion definition we find

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}T^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}=\nabla\Sigma^{i}=d\Sigma^{i}=-\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\Sigma^{k}

(4.113)

therefore we obtain the final statement that
A^{i}=-T^{i}
.
∎

We check that the definition of the torsion free connection in the above way is compatible with the reality conditions. Indeed, by taking the complex conjugate of the torsion free equation we find the anti-self-dual connection

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}\overline{\Sigma}^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+\epsilon^{ijk}\bar{A}^{j}_{\mu}\overline{\Sigma}^{k}_{\rho\sigma}=0.

(4.114)

We have used that the metric is real valued so that
\nabla^{*}=\nabla
. It is clear then that no additional conditions are imposed by taking the derivatives of the reality condition. Applying
\nabla_{\mu}
to
eq.

4.69
we see that it is zero automatically, hence no conditions need to be imposed on the connection to satisfy the reality conditions. This is perhaps unsurprising as the connections are computed using a triple of 2-forms that satisfy the reality condition.

We are now in a position to compare the curvature of the connection to that of the metric. The components of the curvature of the torsion free connection are then

\displaystyle F^{i}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{[\mu}A^{i}_{\nu]}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}_{[\mu}A^{k}_{\nu]}

(4.115)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{[\mu}A^{i}_{\nu]}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}_{[\mu}A^{k}_{\nu]}

(4.116)

By applying another covariant derivative to the definition of the intrinsic torsion and antisymmetrising we find

\displaystyle\nabla_{[\mu}\nabla_{\nu]}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=R_{\mu\nu[\rho}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\sigma]}=-\epsilon^{ijk}\left(\nabla_{[\mu}A^{j}_{\nu]}+\epsilon^{jlm}A^{l}_{[\mu}A^{m}_{\nu]}\right)\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}=-\epsilon^{ijk}F^{j}_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}

(4.117)

By using that the commutator of two metric covariant derivatives is the curvature, we find that

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu[\rho}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\sigma]}=R_{\mu\nu}{}_{[\rho}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\sigma]}=-\epsilon^{ijk}F^{j}_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}.

(4.118)

By contracting with arbitrary vectors
X^{\mu}
and
Y^{\nu}
we find

\displaystyle R_{XY}{}_{[\rho}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\sigma]}=-\epsilon^{ijk}F^{j}_{XY}\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}.

(4.119)

Using the knowledge of the decomposition of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-valued 2-forms we know that this implies
R_{XY}{}_{[\rho}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\sigma]}\in(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2}){}_{3}
. Therefore, it can be encoded into a
\mathbb{C}^{3}
vector, the 3 components can be extracted by multiplying by with
\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\rho\sigma}
, which gives

\displaystyle R_{XY}{}^{\rho\sigma}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=2F^{i}_{XY}.

(4.120)

As
X,Y
were arbitrary we can drop them and return the indices to find that the Riemann curvature is related to the self-dual curvature by

\displaystyle F^{i}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}.

(4.121)

It is known that the Riemann curvature, as it is valued in
S^{2}\Lambda^{2}
, can be decomposed into a
2\times 2
block form of self-dual and anti-self-dual components. We see that
F^{i}_{\mu\nu}
is exactly a self-dual row of this
2\times 2
block decomposition.

The Ricci tensor can be extracted through

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}R_{\alpha\nu\rho\sigma}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}=(\delta_{\mu}^{\rho}g^{\alpha\sigma}-\delta_{\mu}^{\sigma}g^{\alpha\rho}-i\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\alpha\rho\sigma})R_{\alpha\nu\rho\sigma}=-2R_{\mu\nu}

(4.122)

or equivalently

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}=\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}.

(4.123)

To check that this tensor is symmetric we contract it with
\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}
and
\overline{\Sigma}^{i\mu\nu}
, which form a basis for the antisymmetric (0,2)-tensors, and we find it vanishes. Therefore, the Ricci tensor is symmetric as is expected. The Ricci scalar is then

\displaystyle R=\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}F^{i}_{\mu\nu}.

(4.124)

The
\mathbb{C}
-valued 2-form
F^{i}
has 18 components, we have seen that the 9 and 1 irreducible components are contained in the Ricci tensor, by computing
d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=0
we see that

\displaystyle d^{A}d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}F^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}\sim\epsilon^{ijk}F^{j}_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{k\mu\nu}=0

(4.125)

which means that the
3
dimensional irreducible components vanish. Only
5
components remain, and they can be encoded into
\Psi^{ij}=\Psi^{\langle ij\rangle}
, a symmetric tracefree internal matrix that represents the self-dual half of the Weyl curvature. We therefore have the following decomposition of the curvature

\displaystyle F^{i}_{\mu\nu}=\Psi^{ij}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{R}{6}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}+R_{[\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\nu]}.

(4.126)

Introducing the tracefree Ricci curvature as
\tilde{R}_{\mu\nu}=R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}R
we can rewrite the decomposition as

\displaystyle F^{i}_{\mu\nu}=\Psi^{ij}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{R}{12}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}+\tilde{R}_{[\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\nu]}

(4.127)

where the first two terms are self-dual and the last is anti-self-dual (as
(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}){}_{9}=\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{-}
). The leads to the known block decomposition of the Riemann curvature; when viewed as a
\Lambda^{2}\otimes\Lambda^{2}
tensor it has the following self-dual anti-self-dual decomposition

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=\begin{pmatrix}\Psi+\frac{R}{12}&&\tilde{R}\\
\tilde{\bar{R}}&&\bar{\Psi}+\frac{R}{12}\end{pmatrix}.

(4.128)

With
\bar{\Psi}^{ij}
being the anti-self-dual half of the Weyl curvature. We then have the interpretation that
F^{i}
computes the top self-dual row of this block decomposition, in spirit computing half of the total Riemann curvature. However, as we are in Lorentzian signature the other half of the Riemann curvature is available through complex conjugation. Indeed,
\bar{\Psi}^{ij}=-(\Psi^{*})^{ij}
gives the relation between the two chiral halves of the Weyl curvature. In Euclidean signature it becomes a true halving of the number of components as the self-dual and ant-self-dual spaces are real-valued.

Imposing Einstein’s equations is then an algebraic condition on the curvature,

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}F^{i}_{\rho\sigma}g_{\mu\nu}+\Lambda g_{\mu\nu}=T_{\mu\nu}

(4.129)

or in its trace reversed form

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}=T_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}Tg_{\mu\nu}+\Lambda g_{\mu\nu}.

(4.130)

This condition can also be written as

\displaystyle F^{i}_{\mu\nu}=\left(\Psi^{ij}-\frac{4\Lambda-T}{6}\delta^{ij}\right)\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}+T_{[\mu}{}^{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{|\rho|\nu]}.

(4.131)

When no matter is present,
T_{\mu\nu}=0
, Einstein’s equations simplify to be

\displaystyle F^{i}\in\Lambda^{+},\quad\textrm{and}\quad F^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{i}\sim\Lambda.

(4.132)

This is significantly easier to compute, especially when done by hand.

4.7
Plebański as a Chiral Projection of the Frame Formulation

Here we compare the frame formalism to Plebański’s formulation and write all the objects appearing in the latter as chiral projections of the former. First, we compare the 2-form frame and the tetrad frame. Let
e^{I}\in\Lambda^{1}
be the frame for the tetrads, we have that the space of 2-forms is spanned by

\displaystyle B^{IJ}=e^{I}\wedge e^{J}\in\Lambda^{2}.

(4.133)

B^{(IJ)}=0
is antisymmetric with respect to its internal indices and is therefore
\mathfrak{so}(1,3)
-valued, in the sense that
B^{I}{}_{J}=B^{IK}\eta_{KJ}\in\mathfrak{so}(1,3)
. We can then use the self-dual and anti-self-dual ’t Hooft symbols
[
69
]
, which are projectors from
\mathfrak{so}(1,3)
to
\mathfrak{so}(3,\mathbb{C})
and
\overline{\mathfrak{so}(3,\mathbb{C})}
. In components, they are

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}

\displaystyle=i\delta^{0}_{I}\delta^{i}_{J}-i\delta^{0}_{J}\delta^{i}_{I}-\epsilon^{ijk}\delta^{j}_{I}\delta^{k}_{J}

(4.134)

\displaystyle\overline{\Sigma}^{i}_{IJ}

\displaystyle=i\delta^{0}_{I}\delta^{i}_{J}-i\delta^{0}_{J}\delta^{i}_{I}+\epsilon^{ijk}\delta^{j}_{I}\delta^{k}_{J}.

(4.135)

Where
i=1,2,3
indexes
\mathbb{C}^{3}=\mathfrak{so}(3,\mathbb{C})=\overline{\mathfrak{so}}(3,\mathbb{C})
. By raising and lowering the internal index with
\eta_{IJ}={\rm diag}(-1,1,1,1)
it can be shown that the ’t Hooft symbols satisfy quaternion algebras

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{I}{}^{K}\Sigma^{j}_{K}{}^{J}=-\delta^{ij}\delta_{I}^{J}+\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{k}_{I}{}^{J}

(4.136)

\displaystyle\overline{\Sigma}^{i}_{I}{}^{K}\overline{\Sigma}^{j}_{K}{}^{J}=-\delta^{ij}\delta_{I}^{J}-\epsilon^{ijk}\overline{\Sigma}^{k}_{I}{}^{J}.

(4.137)

This offers the interpretation that
\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}
are self-dual 2-forms on the internal space. We can then see the self-dual 2-forms arise as the projection of
B^{IJ}
to
\mathfrak{so}{(3,\mathbb{C})}
, that is

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}B^{IJ}=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}e^{I}\wedge e^{J}=ie^{0}\wedge e^{i}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}e^{j}\wedge e^{k}.

(4.138)

The anti-self-dual 2-forms are defined similarly,

\displaystyle\overline{\Sigma}^{i}=\frac{1}{2}\overline{\Sigma}^{i}_{IJ}e^{I}\wedge e^{J}=ie^{0}\wedge e^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}e^{j}\wedge e^{k}.

(4.139)

It is clear then that any element in
\Lambda^{2}
can be reached by a complex combination of
\Sigma^{i}
and
\overline{\Sigma}^{i}
. In particular the decomposition of
B^{IJ}
is

\displaystyle B^{IJ}=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{iIJ}\Sigma^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\overline{\Sigma}^{iIJ}\overline{\Sigma}^{i}.

(4.140)

Next we consider the self-dual connection,
A^{i}
, as compared with the spin connection,
\omega^{IJ}
. Indeed, by taking the exterior derivative of both sides of
\Sigma^{i}=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}B^{IJ}
we find that

\displaystyle-\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}

\displaystyle=d\Sigma^{i}=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}d(e^{I}\wedge e^{J})

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}de^{I}\wedge e^{J}=-\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}\omega^{I}{}_{K}\wedge e^{K}\wedge e^{J}=-\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}\omega^{I}{}_{K}\wedge B^{KJ}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}\omega^{I}{}_{K}\left(\Sigma^{jKJ}\Sigma^{j}+\overline{\Sigma}^{jKJ}\overline{\Sigma}^{j}\right)=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{jIJ}\omega_{IJ}\wedge\Sigma^{k}

(4.141)

where we have used
eq.

4.136
,
\Sigma^{i}_{[I|K|}\overline{\Sigma}^{jK}{}_{J]}=0
and that
de^{I}+\omega^{I}{}_{J}\wedge e^{J}=0
. We can read off from the above that

\displaystyle A^{i}=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}\omega^{IJ}.

(4.142)

Therefore, we have that the self-dual 2-forms and their connections are the self-dual projections of the 2-forms
B^{IJ}
and the spin connection. In this sense Plebański’s formulation is encoding the metric into the chiral half of the tetrad formulation.

4.8
Infinitesimal Transformations

It is useful to see how infinitesimal actions of both diffeomorphism and gauge transformations affect the fields
\Sigma^{i}
in
A^{i}
. The transformation resulting from the linearised action of the group
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
is parametrised by a Lie algebra element
\mathfrak{so}(3,\mathbb{C})
which are
3\times 3
complex antisymmetric matrices, using the isomorphism
eq.

4.56
these are equivalent to vectors
\phi^{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
. The infinitesimal
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
action is then

\displaystyle\delta_{\phi}\Sigma^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}\phi^{k}\Sigma^{j}.

(4.143)

To compute the infinitesimal action on the connection we use the torsion free definition
d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=0
and act the transformation on it to find

\displaystyle\delta_{\phi}d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=d(\delta_{\phi}\Sigma^{i})+\epsilon^{ijk}\delta_{\phi}A^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\wedge\delta_{\phi}\Sigma^{k}=0

(4.144)

Using
eq.

4.143
and expanding everything gives

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}d\phi^{k}\wedge\Sigma^{j}+\phi^{j}A^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{j}-\phi^{i}A^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{j}+\epsilon^{ijk}\delta_{\phi}A^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}=0

(4.145)

Which by using the definition of the exterior covariant derivative we have

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}d^{A}\phi^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}=\epsilon^{ijk}\delta_{\phi}A^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}.

(4.146)

Taking the Hodge star and applying
J_{1}^{-1}
to both sides we obtain

\displaystyle\delta_{\phi}A^{i}=d^{A}\phi^{i}

(4.147)

which is the infinitesimal transformation on the gauge. Finally, we can compute its action on the curvature of
A^{i}
,

\displaystyle\delta_{\phi}F^{i}=d^{A}\delta_{\phi}\phi^{i}=d^{A}d^{A}\phi^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}F^{j}\phi^{k}.

(4.148)

The infinitesimal action of gauge transformations can be summarised as

\displaystyle\delta_{\phi}\Sigma^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}\phi^{k}\Sigma^{j},\quad\delta_{\phi}A^{i}=d^{A}\phi^{i},\quad\delta_{\phi}F^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}F^{j}\phi^{k}.

(4.149)

For the action of linearised diffeomorphisms we can make use of Cartan’s magic formula for the Lie derivative to see that along a vector
X\in TM
, which represents the infinitesimal coordinate change, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X}\Sigma^{i}=\iota_{X}d\Sigma^{i}+d\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i}=\iota d^{A}\Sigma^{i}-\epsilon^{ijk}\iota_{X}A^{j}\Sigma^{k}+d^{A}\iota_{\Sigma}^{i}=d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i}-\epsilon^{ijk}\iota_{X}A^{j}\Sigma^{k}

(4.150)

We can see that this action induces an infinitesimal gauge transformation with gauge parameter
\phi^{i}=-\iota_{X}A^{i}
. To counteract this gauge transformation and extract the diffeomorphism only transformation we instead compute

\displaystyle\delta_{X}\Sigma^{i}=(\mathcal{L}_{X}+\delta_{\iota_{X}A})(\Sigma)^{i}=d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i}

(4.151)

where
\delta_{X}
is defined to be the action of the diffeomorphism only. The linearised diffeomorphism transformation effects the connection as

\displaystyle\delta_{X}(d^{A}\Sigma^{i})=d^{A}\delta_{X}\Sigma^{i}+\epsilon^{ijk}\delta_{X}A^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}=\epsilon^{ijk}F^{j}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{k}+\epsilon^{ijk}\delta_{X}A^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}=0.

(4.152)

Applying the Hodge star to both sides and using that
d^{A}d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}F^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}=0
we find that

\displaystyle\delta_{X}A^{i}=\iota_{X}F^{i}.

(4.153)

The operation
\delta_{X}
does not produce a gauge transformation for the connection, as is expected. Lastly, we have the action on the curvature

\displaystyle\delta_{X}F^{i}=d^{A}\iota_{X}F^{i}.

(4.154)

Concluding the action of infinitesimal diffeomorphisms we have

\displaystyle\delta_{X}\Sigma^{i}=d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i},\quad\delta_{X}A^{i}=\iota_{X}F^{i},\quad\delta_{X}F^{i}=d^{A}\iota_{X}F^{i}.

(4.155)

An arbitrary infinitesimal action,
\delta
, on the metric can be written as a transformation on the 2-forms given by

\displaystyle\delta g_{\mu\nu}=\delta\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu|\rho|}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu)}{}^{\rho}-\frac{1}{6}g_{\mu\nu}\delta\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}.

(4.156)

This is derived knowing that
g_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{3}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\rho}g^{\rho\sigma}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu\sigma}
and then using the standard linear algebra results
\delta g^{-1}=-g^{-1}\delta gg^{-1}
to find the above equation. Computing the gauge transformation on the metric gives

\displaystyle\delta_{\phi}g_{\mu\nu}=2\phi^{i}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu\nu)}=0

(4.157)

which is expected as the metric is invariant under rotation of the frame. For diffeomorphisms we first write its action on
\Sigma^{i}
in components

\displaystyle\delta_{X}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}=\partial^{A}_{[\mu}\left(X^{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho|\nu]}\right)=\Sigma^{i}_{[\mu}{}^{\rho}\nabla^{A}_{\nu]}X_{\rho}=\Sigma^{i}_{[\mu}{}^{\rho}\nabla_{\nu]}X_{\rho}

(4.158)

where we have used that
d^{A}=\nabla^{A}
when all the indices are antisymmetrised. Substituting this into the metric formula, and using the algebra of
\Sigma
’s, we find that

\displaystyle\delta_{X}g_{\mu\nu}=\nabla_{(\mu}X_{\nu)}=\mathcal{L}_{X}g_{\mu\nu}

(4.159)

which matches the standard infinitesimal action on the metric.

4.9
Plebański Action

In
[
19
]
Plebański first wrote down his action principal for general relativity based on a triple of self-dual 2-forms
\Sigma^{i}
. His idea was to write a BF-type action with
\Sigma^{i}
and
A^{i}
as the fundamental fields. B
F theories are form of topological gauge theories with with actions
S_{BF}=\int B\wedge dA
for some differential forms
B
and
A
, with
F=dA
. For more details on BF actions see
[
70
]
. Certain constraints need to be imposed on the self-dual 2-forms such that they encode a real valued metric, these are the metricity and reality conditions. The later reality condition involves both the self-dual and anti-self-dual 2-forms which destroys the holomorphic nature of the action, also imposing it at the level of the action is more complicated. Instead of adding the reality conditions to the action we instead impose them separately or assume they are already satisfied. In which case the Plebański action, without matter terms, is

\displaystyle S_{Pleb}[\Sigma,A,\Psi]=\frac{1}{8\pi Gi}\int\Sigma^{i}\wedge F^{i}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\Psi^{ij}+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\delta^{ij}\right)\Sigma^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{j}

(4.160)

where
F(A)^{i}=dA^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\wedge A^{k}
and
\Psi^{ij}=\Psi^{\langle ij\rangle}
are Lagrange multipliers that can be interpreted as the self-dual part of the Weyl curvature. Both
\Sigma^{i}
and
A^{i}
are complex fields, so this Lagrangian is complex-valued in general. When supplemented with the reality conditions

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}\wedge\overline{\Sigma}^{j}=0,\quad\textrm{and}\quad\Real(\Sigma^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{i})=0

(4.161)

it can be shown that the imaginary part of the action is the topological Holst term
[
71
]
, which is given by

\displaystyle S_{Holst}=\int e_{I}\wedge e_{J}\wedge F^{IJ}.

(4.162)

It can be shown that this is the integration by parts of the torsion squared term
d^{\omega}e^{I}\wedge d^{\omega}e_{I}
, which itself vanishes as we choose the torsion to be zero. Adding this term to the Einstein-Cartan actions, with a factor of the imaginary unit, produces the internal self-dual projection operator

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}\Sigma^{iKL}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta_{I}^{K}\delta_{J}^{L}-\delta_{I}^{L}\delta_{J}^{K}-i\epsilon_{IJ}{}^{KL}\right),

that is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}S_{EH}+iS_{Holst}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{IJKL}e^{I}\wedge e^{J}\wedge F^{KL}+i\frac{1}{2}\left(\eta_{IK}\eta_{JL}-\eta_{IL}\eta_{JK}\right)e^{I}\wedge e^{J}\wedge F^{KL}

(4.163)

\displaystyle=\int i\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}\Sigma^{i}_{JK}e^{I}e^{J}\wedge F^{KL}=i\int\Sigma^{i}\wedge F^{i}.

(4.164)

The result is the Plebański action. Since torsion is assumed to vanish this modification is equivalent to the Einstein-Cartan action, and as such does not affect the equations of motion. The real part is equivalent to the Einstein-Hilbert action
eq.

3.44
.

Taking the variation of
eq.

4.160
with respect to
\Psi^{ij}
implies

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S}{\delta\Psi^{ij}}=\Sigma^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{j}\sim\delta^{ij}

(4.165)

which recovers the metricity condition. The physical metric, which makes
\Sigma^{i}
self-dual, can then be recovered using Urbantke’s formula
eq.

4.97
. Taking the variation with respect to
A^{i}
gives

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S}{\delta A^{i}}=d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=d\Sigma^{i}+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}=0

(4.166)

which is the torsion free condition. Finally, variation with
\Sigma^{i}
reveals Einstein’s equations

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S}{\delta\Sigma^{i}}=F^{i}-\left(\Psi^{ij}+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\delta^{ij}\right)\Sigma^{j}=0.

(4.167)

As we have seen the tracefree part of the Ricci tensor appears in the anti-self-dual components of
F^{i}
, and the Ricci scalar appears as a factor in front of the self-dual 2-forms. The above equation then imposes that the Ricci tensor or anti-self-dual components vanish and that the Ricci scalar is a constant,
\Lambda
.

A clear advantage of this formulation is that the action is cubic in fields even with
\Lambda\neq 0
, this is the only known formulation where this occurs. Counting the number of components in the self-dual 2-forms we find that in general they have
6\times 3=18\mathbb{C}
components, the metricity condition removes
5\mathbb{C}
of these which leaves us with the
10\mathbb{C}+3\mathbb{C}
of a complex metric and
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
frame. The reality conditions are then
10\mathbb{R}=5\mathbb{C}
conditions that remove the 10 imaginary components form the metric leaving us with
10\mathbb{R}+3\mathbb{C}
components for a real metric and frame. In comparison, we have
16\mathbb{R}
components in the frame
e^{I}
which is equal the number of real components in
\Sigma^{i}
, we see that the self-dual 2-forms are a special repackaging of the full frame in Lorentzian signature. This clever repackaging brings with it the powerful algebra satisfied by
\Sigma
’s which simplifies many calculations, importantly the action is a holomorphic complex projection of the Einstein-Cartan action. The holomorphic nature allows us to treat the
3\mathbb{C}

SO(3,\mathbb{C})
components as
3
real components which simplifies certain computations. We also have that
A^{i}
contains
12\mathbb{C}
components which equals the
24\mathbb{R}
in the spin connection
\omega^{IJ}
, indeed the self-dual connection
A^{i}
is the self-dual projection of spin connection into the chiral half. Again we can treat the connection holomorphically and use the connection as if it had
12
real components. If one is interested in coupling fermions to this theory, without explicit use of the metric, certain Lagrange multipliers need to be introduced which complicated the theory. While it is possible we do not consider this matter content here.

A useful point to make clear is that all the solutions to the Einstein’s equations have a corresponding solution in the case of Plebański’s formulation, assuming the solutions are orientable. That is no information is lost during the chiral projection and using self-dual objects. One way to view this is that for Lorentzian signature everything is complex, so once the constraints are solved it is actually the real Einstein-Cartan formulation in disguise as it contains the same number of real components. However, this is not the preferred way one should interpret this projection. In the case of Euclidean signature the objects take real values and no real and imaginary split exists to recover the lost components. To resolve this we make the assumption that the metric defined by the triple of 2-forms, with the proportionality constant fixed, is the physical metric. In this case, as we have seen in section, all the Ricci tensor components are encoded into the self-dual part of the Riemann curvature tensor. This allows us to impose all of Einstein’s equations on the metric but through the use of self-dual or chiral objects. Therefore, the number of component in the Euclidean version of Plebański’s formulation is truly less than the number that appears in the Einstein-Cartan formalism. The Lorentzian signature version of Plebański’s formalism should be thought of in the same way, in that it is a projection of the Einstein-Cartan theory, but it is holomorphic and so there are fewer components even if they are complex. A similar argument can be made about the metric the 2-forms encode being solutions to the full Einstein’s equations due to the same structure of the Riemann curvature tensor. Meaning that the chiral formulation of gravity does indeed capture all of Einstein’s equations and its solutions.

In summary the action for the metric for general relativity can be encoded into a tripe of self-dual 2-form
\Sigma^{i}
that satisfy certain metricity and reality conditions. The intrinsic torsion of the 2-forms uniquely defines a torsion free
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
connection 1-form the curvature of which encodes the Ricci tensor and the self-dual half of the Weyl curvature. Einstein’s equations are then algebraic conditions on the curvature of the connection. Plebański’s action,
eq.

4.160
, is an action principal that encodes the metricity constraints and related equations of motion to impose Einstein’s equations. To make the metric real-valued one must also impose reality conditions.

4.10
Pure Connection Formalisms

This section will detail a related first-order description of gravity where the main fields are the self-dual connection instead of the self-dual 2-forms. This is obtained by integrating out certain fields from the action. For example, the equation
d^{A}\Sigma^{i}
can be solved for
A^{i}
, this can then be substituted back into the action to recover a second-order version of Plebański’s action
eq.

4.160
only in terms of
\Sigma^{i}
and its derivatives. However, in this case the solution for
A^{i}
is nonpolynomial and the resulting second-order action is unwieldy. Instead, we consider integrating out the metric to obtain the so-called pure connection formalisms.

4.10.1
First-Order Pure Connection

Here we consider integrating out the self-dual 2-forms
\Sigma^{i}
, to obtain a pure connection description
[
72
,
22
]
. We will show that it results in a simple first-order description of gravity. Beginning with
eq.

4.160
and taking the equation of motion from varying with respect to
\Sigma^{i}
we find

\displaystyle F^{i}=(\Psi+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\delta){}^{ij}\Sigma^{j}

(4.168)

then assuming the matrix
M^{ij}=(\Psi+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\delta){}^{ij}
is invertible we can solve for
\Sigma^{i}
,

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}=\frac{1}{(\Psi+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\delta){}^{ij}}F^{j}.

(4.169)

Substituting this back into the action we find

\displaystyle S_{Pleb}[A,\Psi]=\frac{1}{16\pi Gi}\int\frac{1}{M^{ij}}F^{i}\wedge F^{j}

(4.170)

which is the pure connection action for general relativity. This is a diffeomorphism invariant gauge theory, where
M^{ij}
is now a Lagrange multiplier field. Starting from the action we show its equations of motion are first-order and equivalent to those Einstein’s equations in Plebański formalism. Taking the variation with respect to
A^{i}
gives

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S}{\delta A^{i}}=d^{A}\left(\frac{1}{M^{ij}}F^{j}\right)=0.

(4.171)

Using the Bianchi identity
d^{A}F^{i}=0
we can also write this as

\displaystyle d^{A}M^{ij}\wedge\frac{1}{M^{jk}}F^{k}=0.

(4.172)

This equation of motion is first-order in derivatives, schematically it is
\partial\Psi\partial A=0
. Taking the variation with respect to
\Psi^{ij}
we find

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S}{\delta\Psi^{ij}}=\frac{1}{M^{ik}}\frac{1}{M^{jl}}F^{k}\wedge F^{l}\sim\delta^{ij}

(4.173)

or by multiplying with
M^{2}
on both sides

\displaystyle F^{i}\wedge F^{j}\sim\left(M^{2}\right){}^{ij}.

(4.174)

Again we see that the equation of motion is first-order in its derivatives on
A
, schematically this equation appears as
\partial A\partial A\sim M^{2}
. Therefore, we have a pair of first order equations of motion from the action
eq.

4.170
.

To compute the metric we have to invert the steps taken to construct this action. We first introduce a 2-form

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{F}:=\frac{1}{M^{ij}}F^{j}

(4.175)

built from
A^{i}
and
\Psi^{ij}
. The equations of motion then imply that

\displaystyle d^{A}\Sigma^{i}_{F}=0,\quad\Sigma^{i}_{F}\wedge\Sigma^{j}_{F}\sim\delta^{ij}.

(4.176)

It is clear that this 2-form correspond to the triple of self-dual 2-forms seen in Plebański’s formulation. The metric can be recovered using the Urbantke formula

\displaystyle g(u,v){}_{F}\nu_{\Sigma_{F}}=\frac{i}{6}\epsilon^{ijk}\iota_{u}\Sigma^{i}_{F}\wedge\iota_{v}\Sigma^{j}_{F}\wedge\Sigma^{k}_{F}.

(4.177)

Using the definition of
\Sigma_{F}
in terms of
M,F
we find that

\displaystyle g(u,v){}_{F}\nu_{\Sigma_{F}}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{6}\epsilon^{ijk}\frac{1}{M^{il}}\frac{1}{M^{jm}}\frac{1}{M^{kn}}\iota_{u}F^{i}\wedge\iota_{v}F^{j}\wedge F^{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{6\det(M)}\epsilon^{ijk}\iota_{u}F^{i}\wedge\iota_{v}F^{j}\wedge F^{k}.

(4.178)

By changing the definition of the volume form for the connection formalism to be
\nu_{F}:=\det(M)\nu_{\Sigma}=\frac{i\det(M)}{6}\frac{1}{M^{ij}}\frac{1}{M^{ik}}F^{j}\wedge F^{k}
we fix physical metric in the conformal class to be

\displaystyle g(u,v)\nu_{F}=\frac{i}{6}\epsilon^{ijk}\iota_{u}F^{i}\wedge\iota_{v}F^{j}\wedge F^{k}.

(4.179)

In which case we can compute the metric using only
A^{i}
and
\Psi^{ij}
. The equation defining
\Sigma^{i}_{F}
then becomes

\displaystyle F^{i}=\left(\Psi^{ij}+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\delta^{ij}\right)\Sigma^{j}_{F},

(4.180)

and we can identify
\Psi^{ij}
with the self-dual Weyl curvature and
\Lambda
with the cosmological constant. These are Einstein’s equations, and we find that the metric recovered is therefore Einstein. It is worth comparing the number of variables in this theory to that of the action
eq.

4.160
. For the connection formulation one needs
12\mathbb{C}+5\mathbb{C}=18\mathbb{C}
components for the self-dual connection and Weyl curvature. Whereas, for Plebański’s formulation the number of components one starts with in general is
18\mathbb{C}+12\mathbb{C}=30\mathbb{C}
for a triple of complex 2-form and the connection. Usually the constraints are applied which leaves
10\mathbb{R}+3\mathbb{C}+12\mathbb{C}=20\mathbb{C}
components for the real metric, frame and connection. In either case the number of components is more than in the connection formulation.

It should be mentioned that a major drawback to the connection formulation is that one loses the polynomial structure of the action. Importantly, this means that when
\Lambda=0
this theory fails to describe Minkowski space as there
\Psi=0
and the
1/\Psi
term is ill-defined. Perturbation around Minkowski have to be performed more carefully, although it is still possible to compute in this formulation. There is also the issue of reality conditions, in Plebański’s formulation they are algebraic conditions on
\Sigma^{i}
and its complex conjugate. In these connection formulations they appear as non-holomorphic differential equations, that is

\displaystyle F^{i}\wedge\bar{F}^{j}=0,

(4.181)

these are much harder to solve in practice.

4.10.2
Pure Connection Action

Another first order pure connection action was discovered in
[
22
]
by noticing that when
\Lambda\neq 0
one can integrate out the Weyl curvature and cosmological constant
M^{ij}=\Psi^{ij}+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\delta^{ij}
. Indeed, by introducing a matrix

\displaystyle X^{ij}=F^{i}\wedge F^{j}/d^{4}x,

(4.182)

where we have divide by a top form, the equation of motion
eq.

4.174
can be solved for
M^{ij}

\displaystyle M^{ij}=\sqrt{X}^{ij}.
