(4.183)

The resulting action is

\displaystyle S[A]=\int\Tr(\sqrt{F^{i}\wedge F^{j}}).

(4.184)

To recover the usual formalism we notice that the combination

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{X}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{X^{ij}}}F^{j}

(4.185)

satisfies

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{X}\wedge\Sigma^{j}_{X}\sim\delta^{ij}

(4.186)

and is therefore metric. Taking the variation of
eq.

4.184
with respect to
A^{i}
we find

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S}{\delta A^{i}}=\frac{1}{2}d^{A}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{X^{ij}}}F^{j}\right)=d^{A}\Sigma^{i}_{X}=0.

(4.187)

Therefore, the connection
A^{i}
is the intrinsic torsion for the 2-forms
\Sigma^{i}_{X}
. Einstein’s equations are then hidden in the definition of the self-dual 2-forms

\displaystyle F^{i}=X^{ij}\Sigma^{j}_{X}.

(4.188)

The self-dual Weyl tensor is then encoded into
\Psi^{ij}=X^{\langle ij\rangle}
and the Ricci scalar is its trace
R=Tr(X)
. An interesting family of modified theories also arises from this formulation, the action
eq.

4.184
can be written as an arbitrary scalar function on the matrix
X
,

\displaystyle S[A]=\int f(F^{i}\wedge F^{j}).

(4.189)

The modified theories all propagate the same number of degrees of freedom
[
73
]
, however, the metricity condition is lost and an understanding of how the metric related to the 2-forms has not yet been developed. Some developments for the linearised version of these modified theories can be found in
[
66
]
.

Chapter 5
Chiral Yang-Mills

The goal of this chapter is to introduce the chiral formulation of Yang-Mills. We further introduce its first-order version whose action and equations of motion closely resemble those appearing in the chiral formulations of gravity. Specifically chiral Maxwell’s theory, the simplest of the Yang-Mills theories, will be used to compare with Plebański’s formulation throughout the Thesis.

5.1
Yang-Mills Lagrangian

The standard second order formulation of Yang-Mills starts with the same spacetime manifold
M
, over which we attach Lie groups
G
as fibres to form a principal bundle
P
. The vector potential
A^{a}_{\mu}
is then the pullback of a connection 1-form
\omega^{a}\in\Lambda^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})
to
M
in a local trivialisation, where
a
is an index for vector representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. As such,
A^{a}\in\Lambda^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
, the vector potential is a Lie algebra valued 1-form on
M
. In this representation we label define the basis for the Lie algebra as
T_{a}\in\mathfrak{g}
, for the purposes here we consider
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{su}(n)
such that the basis satisfies

\displaystyle[T_{a},T_{b}]=f_{abc}T^{c},\quad\Tr(T_{a}T_{b})=\delta^{ab}

(5.1)

where
f^{abc}
are the structure constants and
[\cdot,\cdot]
is the Lie bracket. The field strength of the vector potential then corresponds with the pullback of the curvature of the connection 1-form,

\displaystyle F^{a}=dA^{a}+\frac{1}{2}f^{abc}A^{b}\wedge A^{c}\in\Lambda^{2}(M,\mathfrak{g}).

(5.2)

The Lagrangian for Yang-Mills is given by

\displaystyle L=F^{a}\wedge\star F^{a}.

(5.3)

Taking the variation with respect to
A^{a}
recovers

\displaystyle\frac{\delta L}{\delta A^{a}}=D\star F^{a}=0

(5.4)

where
D\phi^{a}=d\phi^{a}+f^{abc}A^{b}\wedge\phi^{c}
is the exterior covariant derivative. By the definition of
F^{a}
we can also see that the Bianchi identity holds

\displaystyle DF^{a}=0.

(5.5)

The two equations above constitute all the second order equations for Yang-Mills. As we have seen in the general description of principal bundles, the curvature transforms via the adjoint under the group action of
G
. This leaves the Lagrangian invariant under the group actions. Another important action is the infinitesimal gauge transformations, given a Lie algebra element
\phi^{a}\in\mathfrak{g}
we define the infinitesimal action of this on the connection as

\displaystyle\delta_{\phi}A^{a}=D\phi^{a}.

(5.6)

Acting on the curvature this becomes

\displaystyle\delta_{\phi}F^{a}=DD\phi^{a}=f^{abc}F^{b}\phi^{c}

(5.7)

which allows for the direct computation of its action on the Lagrangian

\displaystyle\delta_{\phi}L=2\delta_{\phi}F^{a}\wedge\star F^{a}=2f^{abc}\star F^{a}\wedge F^{b}\phi^{c}.

(5.8)

By definition the structure constants are antisymmetric in the first two indices, that is
f^{abc}T^{c}=[T^{a},T^{b}]
. Therefore, as
F^{a}\wedge\star F^{b}
is symmetric in
ab
the infinitesimal gauge transformation leaves the Lagrangian invariant. Gauge theories all share the same property that a gauge transformation does not affect the physical degrees of freedom, as such it is expected that the gauge action should leave the Lagrangian invariant. Furthermore, the equations of motion are also invariant under this action. Now we turn to infinitesimal diffeomorphism transformations, these are generated by a vector field
X^{\mu}
. We expect that these transformations should also leave the Lagrangian invariant as they amount to small changes in the coordinate description. The Lie derivative along
X^{\mu}
computes the infinitesimal change of tensor along integral curves of
X^{\mu}
and hence how they transform. For the connection, using Cartan’s magic formula, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X}A^{a}=\iota_{X}dA^{a}+d\iota_{X}A^{a}=\iota_{X}F^{a}+D\iota_{X}A^{a}.

(5.9)

Similarly to the case for Plebański’s formulation we find the diffeomorphisms also cause a gauge transformation. We can subtract this to leave only the diffeomorphism contribution

\displaystyle\delta_{X}A^{a}=(\mathcal{L}_{X}-\delta_{\iota_{X}A^{a}})A^{a}=\iota_{X}F^{a}.

(5.10)

For the curvature we have

\displaystyle\delta_{X}F^{a}=D\delta_{X}A^{a}=D\iota_{X}F^{a}.

(5.11)

The Lagrangian is then automatically invariant under diffeomorphisms as

\displaystyle\delta_{X}L=\mathcal{L}_{X}L=D\iota_{X}L=d\iota_{X}L

(5.12)

which is a total derivative and hence constant up to boundary terms which we can safely ignore. We have made use of the fact that
L
is not Lie algebra valued and so the Lie algebra terms are zero when applied to it.

5.2
Chiral Lagrangians

Another Lagrangian exists, that is quadratic in the curvature, for the case of Yang-Mills, this is the Pontryagin Lagrangian

\displaystyle L_{top}=F^{a}\wedge F^{a}=d(A^{a}\wedge dA^{a}+\frac{1}{3}f^{abc}A^{a}\wedge A^{b}\wedge A^{c}).

(5.13)

It is a total divergence and does not affect the equations of motion. The variation with respect to
A^{a}
vanishes identically due to the Bianchi identity. We are free to add this Lagrangian to the original, and by choosing a specific combination we can construct

\displaystyle L=F^{a}\wedge(F^{a}-i\star F^{a})=F^{a}\wedge F^{a}_{+}

(5.14)

where
F^{a}_{+}\in\Lambda^{+}
is the self-dual part of the curvature. We are then able to use the result that
\Lambda^{2}=\Lambda^{-}\oplus\Lambda^{+}
splits orthogonally with respect to the wedge product and write the Lagrangian as

\displaystyle L=F^{a}_{+}\wedge F^{a}_{+}

(5.15)

purely in terms of self-dual curvatures. This is the chiral Yang-Mills action, also called the Chalmers-Siegel action
[
74
]
. We can now introduce a first-order field
B^{a}=\phi^{ai}\Sigma^{i}\in\Lambda^{+}
, where
\Sigma^{i}
are background self-dual 2-forms for Minkowski, such that the Lagrangian is

\displaystyle L=\phi^{ai}\Sigma^{i}\wedge F^{a}-\frac{1}{2}\phi^{ai}\phi^{aj}\Sigma^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{j}.

(5.16)

Using that
\Sigma^{i}
satisfies the metricity condition we find the above action in coordinates becomes, up to an overall constant,

\displaystyle L=\phi^{ai}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}F^{a}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\phi^{ai}\phi^{ai}.

(5.17)

If one performs the same first-order reduction without the self-dual projection, we find that twice as many first-order fields are required (i.e.
\phi^{ai}
and
\overline{\phi}^{ai}
for
\Lambda^{+}
and
\Lambda^{-}
). Defining
n=\dim(\mathfrak{g})
as the dimension of the Lie group, we have that
3n+4n=7n
components instead of
10n
in the full case. As the only difference between the two action is the addition of a total divergence the equations of motion remain unchanged, and the same theory is described with both Lagrangians. The first-order chiral version of Yang-Mills is then a good starting theory for first-order perturbation theory, due to the reduced number of fields. Taking the variation with respect to
\phi^{ai}
and
A^{a}
give

\displaystyle\phi^{ai}=\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}F^{a}_{\mu\nu},\quad\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial^{A}_{\nu}\phi^{ai}=0

(5.18)

or equivalently as differential forms we have

\displaystyle B^{a}=F{(A)}^{a}_{+},\quad D^{*}B^{a}=0.

(5.19)

Where
D^{*}=-\star D\star
is the gauge covariant codifferential. It may seem like solutions to the chiral theory somehow represent “half” of a solution to the non-chiral theory. This is the incorrect interpretation. To see this let
F^{a}
be the curvature 2-form of the connection
A^{a}
, then we can project
F^{a}
onto its chiral half using the Hodge star of the background metric,

\displaystyle F{(A)}^{a}_{+}=(i+\star)F^{a}

(5.20)

it is easy to check using
\star^{2}=-\mathbb{I}
that this is indeed self-dual. Substituting this into the chiral equations of motion
eq.

5.19
we find,

\displaystyle D^{\star}F^{a}_{+}=-\star D\star(i+\star)F^{a}=\star D(\star i-\mathbb{I})F^{a}=-iD^{\star}F^{a}

(5.21)

where we have used the Bianchi identity,
DF^{a}=0
. This is exactly the second-order equation on the connection that corresponds to the full Yang-Mills solutions. Therefore, no information is lost in the projection to the self-dual theory. The similarities of the chiral Yang-Mills and gravity theories is worth noting and are discussed in the next section.

5.3
Self-Dual Yang-Mills and Self-Dual Gravity

Self-dual theories are important truncations of the chiral theories for Yang-Mills (YM) and general relativity (GR). They are known to be integrable as seen from many perspectives, e.g.
[
75
,
76
]
. They are also useful in computing quantum scattering amplitudes as the calculations take on a simple form
[
77
,
78
,
79
,
80
,
81
]
and in some cases they can be used to compute amplitudes for the full theory as well
[
82
]
. For these reasons we are motivated to show how self-dual theories are obtained from their chiral Lagrangians.

5.3.1
Self-dual Yang-Mills

For self-dual theories there solutions are instantons, these only exist in Euclidean signature as they require the self-dual and anti-self-dual components to be independent whereas in Lorentzian signature they are complex conjugates. Self-dual Yang-Mills solutions are characterised by connections whose curvatures are anti-self-dual,

\displaystyle F{(A)}_{+}=0.

(5.22)

A Lagrangian description of instantons is obtained from the Chalmers-Siegel Lagrangian
eq.

5.16
by dropping the terms containing
\phi^{2}
,

\displaystyle L_{SDYM}=\phi^{ai}\Sigma^{i}\wedge F^{a}.

(5.23)

Varying with respect to
\phi^{ai}
reveals the instanton equations,

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}\wedge F^{a}=0,\quad\Rightarrow\quad F^{a}\in\Lambda^{-}.

(5.24)

These remain solutions to the full Yang-Mills equations, indeed we see that

\displaystyle DF^{a}=0,\quad D\star F^{a}=-DF^{a}=0.

(5.25)

5.3.2
Self-dual Gravity

Chiral gravity has a similar truncation whose solutions are self-dual instantons. Similarly to self-dual YM, self-dual gravity solutions are Einstein metrics with vanishing self-dual Weyl curvature and as such are only non-trivial, meaning that they are not maximally symmetric, in Euclidean signature. The first-order self-dual gravity action can be obtained by removing
\Psi^{ij}
in
eq.

4.160
,

\displaystyle L=\Sigma^{i}\wedge F^{i}+\frac{\Lambda}{6}\Sigma^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{i}.

(5.26)

The equations of motion obtained by varying with
A^{i}
and
\Sigma^{i}
are

\displaystyle d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=0,\quad F^{i}=-\frac{\Lambda}{3}\Sigma^{i}.

(5.27)

These have to be supplemented with
\Sigma^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{j}\sim\delta^{ij}
to make sure that only the metric and
SO(3)
rotation components are in the 2-forms. Solutions of this type are called instantons, and it is clear that they are also solutions to the full theory. We have seen that the 2-forms can be integrated out to leave a theory where the metric is encoded into a triple of 1-forms,
A^{i}
, which is the starting point of the connection formalism
section

4.10
. Recall that the first-order pure connection Lagrangian is

\displaystyle L=\frac{1}{{(\Psi+\frac{\Lambda}{3})}^{ij}}F^{i}\wedge F^{j}.

(5.28)

The self-dual Lagrangian cannot be derived in the same way as before, by removing the self-dual Weyl curvature,
\Psi^{ij}=0
, the only term remaining is the topological Holst term
F^{i}\wedge F^{i}
. Instead, the correct method is to expand in powers of
\Psi^{ij}
and truncate at the first order
[
81
,
22
]
, for which the Lagrangian is

\displaystyle L_{SDGR}=\Psi^{ij}F^{i}\wedge F^{j}.

(5.29)

Here
\Psi^{ij}
is a symmetric tracefree tensor and is not associated with Weyl curvature any more. The variation with respect to
\Psi^{ij}
gives

\displaystyle F^{i}\wedge F^{j}\sim\delta^{ij}.

(5.30)

To obtain the metric solution we define a 2-form
\Sigma^{i}:=F^{i}
and see that it satisfies the equations of motion for the full theory,

\displaystyle d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=0,\quad\Sigma^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{j}\sim\delta^{ij}.

(5.31)

The final realisation is that
F^{i}=\Sigma^{i}
implies that the curvature of the metric defined by
\Sigma^{i}
has no self-dual Weyl curvature. Note that this only has solutions where
\Lambda\neq 0
.

It was realised in
[
83
]
that there exists a self-dual action that exists on “flat” spacetimes, that is spacetimes where
F^{+}{(A)}^{i}=0
. The Lagrangian for these solutions is obtained by dropping the
A\wedge A
terms in the curvatures from the Lagrangian
eq.

5.29
, in doing so one finds

\displaystyle L_{fSDGR}=\Psi^{ij}dA^{i}\wedge dA^{j}

(5.32)

which is called the “flat” SDGR (fSDGR) Lagrangian. The equations of motion are given by

\displaystyle dA^{i}\wedge dA^{j}\sim\delta^{ij},\qquad{\rm and}\qquad d(\Psi^{ij}dA^{j})=0.

(5.33)

To see that this gives solution where the self-dual curvature vanishes we first define the self-dual 2-forms as
\Sigma^{i}=dA^{i}
, the
A^{i}
here should not be identified with the self-dual connections, they are simply an arbitrary triple of 1-forms. From this definition of the
\Sigma^{i}
we see that

\displaystyle d\Sigma^{i}=0,\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\Sigma^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{j}\sim\delta^{ij}

(5.34)

which implies that the self-dual connections are zero and therefore
F^{i}=0
. As for the second equation in
eq.

5.33
this can be solved by imposing that the Lagrange multipliers
\Psi^{ij}
are constant.

This Lagrangian is also suitable for expanding around flat space,
dA^{i}\rightarrow\Sigma^{i}+da^{i}
, where
\Sigma^{i}
are now the Minkowski self-dual 2-forms. Computing the linearised Lagrangian we find

\displaystyle\delta^{2}L_{fSDGR}=\Psi^{ij}(\Sigma^{i}da^{j}+\frac{1}{2}da^{i}\wedge da^{j}).

(5.35)

From here the comparison with
eq.

5.23
is made explicit. To convert from YM to GR one replaces the colour index
a
with the kinetic index
i
, this gives a realisation of the double copy
[
84
]
and the colour/kinematic duality
[
85
]
. Moreover, if an ansatz for the critical points of
eqs.

5.23
and
5.35
are parametrised by a scalar as in
[
27
]
then the double copy structure is realised more explicitly.

Overall, the similarities between chiral Yang-Mills and gravity give good reason to compare the two throughout the Thesis.

5.4
Chiral Maxwell’s Theory

By choosing
G=U(1)
for the group in Yang-Mills we recover Maxwell’s theory of electromagnetism. In this case the Lie algebra is simply
\mathfrak{g}=\mathbb{R}
, therefore, the connection is
A\in\Lambda^{1}(M)
a normal 1-form. It is also known that the structure constants vanish,
f^{abc}=0
. The curvature in this case is simply

\displaystyle F(A)=dA.

(5.36)

The first order 2-forms then expand into a basis of self-dual 2-forms,
B=\phi^{i}\Sigma^{i}
, and the equations of motion simplify to be

\displaystyle\phi^{i}=\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu},\quad\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\phi^{i}=0.

(5.37)

The spacetime indices are raised and lowered with a non-dynamical metric
g_{\mu\nu}
defined by the non-dynamical 2-forms
\Sigma^{i}
. To see how this related to Maxwell’s equations we introduce a foliation on the manifold
M
such that
M\cong\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{S}
where
\mathcal{S}
is our spatial hypersurface. To do so we introduce a distinguished coordinate
t:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
which we call the time coordinate. The remaining coordinates we give arbitrary symbols
x^{i}
for
i=1,2,3
, this induces a basis of 1-forms,

\displaystyle dt,dx^{i}\in\Lambda^{1}.

(5.38)

In this basis the Minkowski self-dual 2-forms become

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}=idt\wedge dx^{i}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}dx^{j}\wedge dx^{k}

(5.39)

or in components

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{0j}=i\delta^{i}_{j},\quad\Sigma^{i}_{jk}=-\epsilon^{i}{}_{jk}.

(5.40)

By fixing the self-dual 2-forms to those of Minkowski the metric is then also the Minkowski one
g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}={\rm diag}(-1,1,1,1)
. The Yang-Mills connection admits a similar decomposition under this foliation

\displaystyle A=\theta dt+A^{i}dx^{i}.

(5.41)

Decomposing all the equations of motion into this foliation gives the following equations of motion,

\displaystyle\phi^{i}=-i(\partial_{t}A^{i}-\partial^{i}\theta)-\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{j}A^{k},\quad\partial_{t}\phi^{i}=-i\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{j}\phi^{k},\quad\partial^{i}\phi^{i}=0.

(5.42)

Where
\partial_{t}=\partial/\partial t
and
\partial^{i}=\partial/\partial x^{i}
. To make the connection with Maxwell’s equations we now equate the curvature with the Faraday 2-form, specifically the timelike and spacelike parts of the curvature are identified with the electric and magnetic field

\displaystyle E^{i}=F_{i0}=-\partial_{t}A^{i}+\partial^{i}\theta,\quad B^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}F_{jk}=\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{j}A^{k}.

(5.43)

Now we see that the first field equation defines
\phi^{i}
as a complex combination of the electric and magnetic field

\displaystyle\phi^{i}=iE^{i}-B^{i}

(5.44)

which is related to the Riemann-Silberstein vector
[
86
]
. The real and imaginary parts of the second equation in
eq.

5.42
become

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{j}B^{k},\quad\partial_{t}B^{i}=-\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{j}E^{k}

(5.45)

which are the correct evolution equations for the electric and magnetic fields. Lastly, the final equation of motion gives

\displaystyle\partial^{i}E^{i}=0,\quad\partial^{i}B^{i}=0

(5.46)

which are the Gauss constraints for the electric and magnetic field. We see that we have recovered all of Maxwell’s equations in a vacuum. The chiral theory halves the number of first order variables needed to write down the equations of motion, in this case
\phi^{i}
has 3 instead of
B\in\Lambda^{2}
having 6. Although the number of fields is reduced we see that the full theory is recovered, this remains true even with the addition of source terms. One could argue that the fields introduced are complex and the extra degrees of freedom are hidden in the imaginary components, however, much like in the chiral gravity theories the fields appear holomorphically and can therefore be treated as 3 components. We then see that the chiral formulations give an efficient way to write down the equations of motion, and locally it is exactly equivalent to the usual theory.

Part II
Linearised Gravity

Chapter 6
Linearised Gravity

One important topic in the study of gravity is its linearisation, in general this a perturbative technique that allows one to compute approximations to nonlinear partial differential equations around a known background solution. It is a powerful technique and is used on a wide range of problems, for example a large effort is being put into studying perturbations around black hole spacetimes in order to model the gravitational waves emitted. Studying the structure of the linearised equation of motion gives an insight to the highest order structure of the nonlinear equations, and for properties such as hyperbolicity these are the only important terms. In this chapter we compute the perturbation of the Plebański 2-form with respect to the metric perturbation, and go on to derive the linearised Einstein’s equations. It is then a simple computation to check that there are 2 propagating degrees of freedom, meaning that there are only two undetermined components in the theory once all the equations of motion have been solved. The last part of this chapter develops a hyperbolic gauge fixing for the Plebański formalism, this is an extension of the Harmonic gauge and implies that despite the change in the description the equations of motion remain hyperbolic and therefore well-posed. A well-posed set of differential equations has a unique solution that depends continuously on its initial data, a criterion initially proposed by Jacques Hadamard
[
87
]
. Hyperbolicity is a property of first-order partial differential equations that is useful in analysing their well-posedness
[
88
]
. Both are useful properties for our gauge-fixed equations of motion to have when we start considering numerical schemes. Before showing the linearised structure and gauge fixing for Gravity we first present the story for chiral
U(1)
Yang-Mills. It has many similarities with linearised gravity and the gauge fixing procedure is very similar to our strategy for gravity, however, it is easier to understand for
U(1)
Yang-Mills.

6.1
Chiral Maxwell First-Order Gauge Fixing

We recall the first-order Lagrangian for
U(1)
Yang-Mills on a Minkowski background

\displaystyle L=\phi^{i}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\phi^{i}\phi^{i}

(6.1)

where
\phi^{i}
is the Riemann-Silberstein vector,
A_{\mu}
is the
U(1)
connection 1-form and
\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}
are the Minkowski self-dual 2-forms. Here spacetime indices are raised and lowered with the Minkowski metric
g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}
. The equations of motion for this Lagrangian are

\displaystyle\phi^{i}=\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu},\quad\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\phi^{i}=0.

(6.2)

By substituting the first equation into the second find, after using the algebra of
\Sigma
’s that

\displaystyle\partial^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}-\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\nu}A_{\nu}=0.

(6.3)

Clearly the term
\partial^{\mu}A_{\mu}
is obstructing wave operator from being the leading symbol of this second order equation. We can then impose a gauge fixing to remove this, the standard approach is to impose the Lorenz gauge
\partial^{\mu}A_{\mu}=0
. As we are interested in the first-order structure we impose gauge by adding the following gauge fixing Lagrangian

\displaystyle L_{g.f}=\theta\partial^{\mu}A_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{2}

(6.4)

where
\theta\in C^{\infty}(M)
is a new scalar field. The total Lagrangian is then

\displaystyle L+L_{g.f.}=\phi^{i}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\phi^{i}\phi^{i}+\theta\partial^{\mu}A_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{2}.

(6.5)

Varying with respect to
\phi,\theta
and
A_{\mu}
gives

\displaystyle\phi^{i}=\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu},\quad\theta=\partial^{\mu}A_{\mu}

(6.6)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\phi^{i}-\partial_{\mu}\theta=0.

(6.7)

By substituting the first line of equations into the second reveals that

\displaystyle\partial^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}=0.

(6.8)

Therefore, the solutions are solutions to wave equations. By applying the wave operator to the equations defining
\phi^{i}
and
\theta
we can see that they also follow the wave equation. We find that this gauge fixing causes every field to satisfy the wave equation. Since the wave equation is the typical example of a well-posed second order differential equation we know that this setup of PDEs is also well-posed up to gauge.

It is not clear from the equations in
eqs.

6.6
and
6.7
that this is in fact a gauge fixing, the equations of motion have been changed, and it appears that there are solutions that do not satisfy the original equations of motion. To show that the evolution under these equations is equivalent to Maxwell’s equations we first decompose into the 3+1 language. By separating the coordinates into
x^{\mu}=t,x^{i}
we find that the equations of motion become

\displaystyle\phi^{i}

\displaystyle=-i(\partial_{t}A^{i}-\partial^{i}A_{0})-\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{j}A^{k},\quad

\displaystyle\theta=-\partial_{t}A_{0}+\partial^{i}A^{i}

(6.9)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi^{i}=-i\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{j}\phi^{k}-i\partial^{i}\theta,\quad

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\theta=i\partial^{i}\phi^{i}.

(6.10)

We can see that every equation has become an evolution equation for one of
A_{0},A^{i},\phi^{i},\theta
. For consistency with the original system of equations we require that the Gauss constraint is satisfied at all times,

\displaystyle C_{\phi}=\partial^{i}\phi^{i}=0.

(6.11)

A physical solution to the new system will satisfy this condition. Suppose then that we prepare the system at
t=0
such that it is a solution to the original Maxwell equations, that is

\displaystyle\partial^{i}\phi^{i}|_{t=0}=0=\partial_{t}\theta|_{t=0},\quad\left(\partial_{t}\phi^{i}+i\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{j}\phi^{k}\right)|_{t=0}=0=\partial^{i}\theta|_{t=0}.

(6.12)

This implies that
\partial_{\mu}\theta|_{t=0}=0
or that
\theta|_{t=0}=\kappa
is a constant. As
\theta
also satisfies the wave equation we can use the result that if
\theta=\kappa
and
\partial_{\mu}\theta=0
at
t=0
then
\theta=\kappa
for all
t
. Importantly this suggests that

\displaystyle C_{\phi}=-i\partial_{t}\theta=0

(6.13)

for the entire evolution. The time component of the connection,
A_{0}
, acts as a Lagrange multiplier imposing the Gauss constraint, as such we are free to fix it to any value we choose. The Lorenz gauge then gives an evolution equation for
A_{0}
allowing it to change during evolution without disrupting the physical degrees of freedom. We then see that given we initialise the system such that the constraint is zero then the evolution of the system remains on the constraint surface and hence corresponds to a physical Maxwell solution. Another important question to ask is why one should leave
\theta
in the equations rather than removing it if is zero anyway? To answer this we consider our intended application, numerical study, where at each time step one needs to require that
C_{\phi}=0
is satisfied up to a tolerance. For Maxwell’s equations this constraint is relatively simple to compute, however, for gravity the constraints are more complicated nonlinear differential equations. Numerical errors cause the constraint to be violated and to enforce it at each time step, by solving the constraint for a solution
\phi^{i}
, can require a lot of computation. Instead, a standard solution is to evolve the system in such a way that the numerical solutions approaches the constraint surface (i.e. the surface on which the constraint vanishes). The gauge fixing we present here gives a method of constructing the highest order terms for such equations, by imposing the correct damping terms one can force the system to the constraint surface. Damping terms are modification to equations of motion that enforce a given particular constraint by forcing the solution to decay to the desired result. These have been historically used in numerical relativity
[
89
]
, hence we also consider them here. The inclusion of
\theta
allows us to measure the amount of constraint violation and modify the equations accordingly such that it is then removed. More details on this are given in
section

10.2
and a numerical showcase for this gauge fixing can be found in
section

10.6
.

6.2
Frame Perturbation of
\Sigma

We now consider the perturbation of Plebański’s formulation of gravity with respect to the frame and metric perturbations. To begin we consider derive a convenient parametrisation of the linearised self-dual 2-forms
\Sigma^{i}
in terms of the perturbation of the frame. The easiest route to this is through its definition in terms of the frame
eq.

4.138
, that is

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}e^{I}_{\mu}e^{J}_{\nu}.

(6.14)

To linearise we then fix a background frame
e^{I}_{\mu}
and write the first-order perturbation as
\delta e^{I}_{\mu}
where the perturbations are assumed to be
\ll 1
and hence higher than linear terms are ignored. The background,
e^{I}_{\mu}
, is chosen to be that of Minkowski, and using the background we have a way to convert internal and spacetime indices. The linearisation of the metric is obtained from its definition in terms of the frame,

\displaystyle{(\delta g)}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\delta(\eta_{IJ}e^{I}_{\mu}e^{J}_{\nu})=2\eta_{IJ}\delta e^{I}_{(\mu}e^{J}_{\nu)}=2\delta e_{(\mu\nu)}

(6.15)

in the last line we have used the background to convert the internal index to a spacetime index,
\delta e_{\mu\nu}=e_{I\mu}\delta e^{I}_{\nu}
. We see that the metric perturbation is captured by the symmetric part of
\delta e_{\mu\nu}
, the remaining antisymmetric components make up the
6
components of the perturbation of the Lorentz frame. The antisymmetric part is a 2-form
\delta e_{[\mu\nu]}\in\Lambda^{2}
and as such we can decompose it into a self-dual and anti-self-dual basis

\displaystyle\delta e_{[\mu\nu]}=\frac{1}{2}h^{i}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{h}^{i}\overline{\Sigma}^{i}_{\mu\nu}

(6.16)

where we see the
\mathfrak{so}(3,\mathbb{C})
and
\overline{\mathfrak{so}(3,\mathbb{C})}
infinitesimal gauge transformations appearing with gauge parameters
h^{i}
and
\bar{h}^{i}
respectively. We relabel the symmetric part of the perturbation as
h_{\mu\nu}=\delta e_{(\mu\nu)}
which leaves us with the parametrisation

\displaystyle\delta e_{\mu\nu}=h_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}h^{i}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{h}^{i}\overline{\Sigma}^{i}_{\mu\nu}.

(6.17)

In this way we have parametrised the perturbation of the frame into its irreducible components. With this parametrisation in mind we can compute the linearisation of the 2-forms
\Sigma^{i}
,

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}\delta(e^{I}_{\mu}e^{J}_{\nu})=\Sigma^{i}_{IJ}\delta e^{I}_{[\mu}e^{J}_{\nu]}=\delta e_{[\mu}{}^{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{|\rho|\nu]}.

(6.18)

Then using
eq.

6.17
we find

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}=h_{[\mu}{}^{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{|\rho|\nu]}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}h^{k}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}

(6.19)

We see that
\bar{h}^{i}
does not enter the linearisation, this is because only one of the chiral halves is present in the theory and the other does not act on this space of 2-forms. The
\mathfrak{so}{(3,\mathbb{C})}
gauge parameter can be identified with
h^{i}
, comparing it with
eq.

4.143
we see it acts as an infinitesimal gauge transformation. Note that the metric perturbations appear in the
(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2})_{1}\oplus(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2})_{9}
irreducible components and the gauge transformation in
(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2})_{3}
. One can show that conformal transformations change only the trace of the metric perturbation and as such they perturb 2-forms in the space
(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2})_{1}
. All other metric transformations cause an anti-self-dual perturbation, due to the realisation
(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{2})_{9}=\Lambda^{-}
. This parametrisation for the perturbation is also a solution of the linearised metricity and reality conditions,

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}\wedge\sigma^{j}\sim\delta^{ij},\qquad\Sigma^{i}\wedge\bar{\sigma}^{j}+\sigma^{i}\wedge\overline{\Sigma}^{j}=0.

(6.20)

Where therefore have that the data
h_{\mu\nu},h^{i}
defines a perturbation of the Plebański 2-forms. Next we can calculate the linearisation of the connection

\displaystyle a^{i}=-iJ_{1}^{-1}(\star d\sigma^{i})

(6.21)

where
J_{1}
is the operator defined in
eq.

4.73
and
\star
is the Hodge star valued on the Minkowski background. We tackle the above computation by first computing

\displaystyle(\star d\sigma)^{i}_{\mu}=\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\nu}h_{[\rho}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\sigma]}=i\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h+i\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}h_{\mu\sigma}-i\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}h_{\rho\sigma}+i\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h^{k}.

(6.22)

Applying
J_{1}
to this we find

\displaystyle J_{1}(\star d\sigma)^{i}_{\mu}=i\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h-i\Sigma^{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}h_{\mu\sigma}-i\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}h_{\rho\sigma}+i\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h^{k}+2i\partial_{\mu}h^{i}.

(6.23)

Therefore, we have the perturbation of the connection 1-form in terms of the metric perturbation

\displaystyle a^{i}_{\mu}=-\frac{i}{2}\left(J_{1}-1\right)(\star d\sigma)^{i}_{\mu}=-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}h_{\mu\sigma}+\partial_{\mu}h^{i}.

(6.24)

The gauge parameter appears as an infinitesimal gauge transformation on the connection. Immediately from this we can see that the linearisation of the curvature of the connection is

\displaystyle\delta F^{i}_{\mu\nu}=-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho[\mu}h_{\nu]\sigma}.

(6.25)

Where we see that the gauge parameter does not enter the curvature as it is a gauge equivariant 2-form, this can also be argued as the choice of gauge should not affect the physical curvature. The Ricci tensor can then be computed as

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}=\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}=-\partial^{2}h_{\mu\nu}+2\partial_{(\mu}\left(\partial^{\rho}h_{\nu)\rho}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu)}h\right).

(6.26)

This reproduces the standard linearisation result found in the metric formulation, this is to be expected as Plebański’s formulation is only a different way of writing the same equations. To make this even clearer we can compute the degrees of freedom for Einstein’s equation with
T_{\mu\nu}=0=\Lambda
, for which the second order linearisation is

\displaystyle\partial^{2}h_{\mu\nu}-2\partial_{(\mu}\left(\partial^{\rho}h_{\nu)\rho}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu)}h\right)=0.

(6.27)

By performing a Fourier transformation,
\partial_{\mu}\rightarrow k_{\mu}
where
k_{\mu}
is the wave-covector, and moving to frequency space the above equation becomes algebraic instead of differential,

\displaystyle k^{2}h_{\mu\nu}-2k_{(\mu}\left(k^{\rho}h_{\nu)\rho}-\frac{1}{2}k_{\nu)}h\right)=0.

(6.28)

To solve this we split the solution into two cases
k^{2}=0
and
k^{2}\neq 0
. First we consider
k^{2}\neq 0
for which we have

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}=2\frac{k_{(\mu}}{k^{2}}\left(k^{\rho}h_{\rho)\rho}-\frac{1}{2}k_{\nu)}h\right)

(6.29)

therefore it is of the form
h_{\mu\nu}=k_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}
. Recalling the linear action of diffeomorphism,
\delta_{\xi}g_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}
we see that this solution is not physical as it can be removed by performing a diffeomorphism transformation. These linear solutions are sums of gravitational waves with wave 4-vectors
k_{\mu}
, it is known that gravitational waves travel at the speed of light and hence
k^{2}=0
. Therefore, we expect that solutions with
k^{2}\neq 0
are purely gauge artefacts which corroborates the solution being a diffeomorphism transformation. Next, we consider
k^{2}=0
for which we have

\displaystyle k_{(\mu}\left(k^{\rho}h_{\nu)\rho}-\frac{1}{2}k_{\nu)}h\right)=0

(6.30)

which implies

\displaystyle k^{\rho}h_{\mu\rho}-\frac{1}{2}k_{\mu}h=0

(6.31)

We introduce a basis on
\Lambda^{1}
adapted to
k^{\mu}
, that is
k_{\mu},l_{\nu},m^{A}_{\mu}
where
A=2,3
. The basis is chosen the orthonormal in the sense that

\displaystyle k^{2}=0,\quad k^{\mu}l_{\mu}=1,\quad k^{\mu}m^{A}_{\mu}=l^{\mu}m^{A}_{\mu}=0,\quad m^{A}_{\mu}m^{B\mu}=\delta^{AB}.

(6.32)

Using this basis we expand the metric perturbation

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}=k_{(\mu}\xi_{\mu)}+al_{\mu}l_{\nu}+b_{A}l_{(\mu}c^{A}_{\nu)}+C_{AB}m^{A}_{\mu}m^{B}_{\nu}

(6.33)

where
\xi_{\mu}
is an arbitrary 1-form and
C_{AB}=C_{(AB)}
. Substituting this in we find that

\displaystyle k^{\nu}h_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}k_{\mu}k^{\nu}\xi_{\nu}+\frac{1}{2}l_{\mu}a+\frac{1}{2}b_{A}m^{A}_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}k_{\mu}h

(6.34)

therefore
\xi_{\mu}=hl_{\mu}
,
a=b_{A}=0
. Computing the trace we obtain
h=h+C_{AB}\delta^{AB}
which shows that
C_{AB}=C_{\langle AB\rangle}
should be symmetric tracefree. The solution for the metric perturbation is then

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}=k_{(\mu}l_{\nu)}h+C_{\langle AB\rangle}m^{A}_{\mu}m^{B}_{\nu}.

(6.35)

The first term is a diffeomorphism, leaving us with the two components in the symmetric tracefree matrix
C_{\langle AB\rangle}
. These are the two physical degrees of freedom in the metric perturbation, and they only appear in the symmetric tracefree part of the perturbation. This result is consistent with usual metric formulation. Returning to the parametrisation of the linearised 2-forms in terms of the metric we see that the physical degrees of freedom are found in the anti-self-dual part of the perturbation. This is one of our first hints that the conformal components in gravity should be treated separately from the remaining components.

6.3
Hyperbolic First-Order Gauge Fixing

Here we consider the problem of gauge fixing the first-order Einstein’s equations (EE) using the Plebański formulation. We have already seen that the second order EE, when written using the metric perturbation, is equivalent to the original metric formulation. It is known that by imposing the de Donder gauge for the linearised metric, EE becomes the wave equation at highest order. Solutions to the linearised EEs can then be constructed from plane wave solutions to the wave equation. In fact the nonlinear version of this gauge fixing has been used to prove that EE’s can be put in a hyperbolic and well-posed form
[
47
]
. Much like in the chiral Maxwell case we aim to find gauge fixing that causes all the fields to satisfy the wave equation, this is a different computation to that in the metric formulation as we also account for the
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
frame perturbation.

6.3.1
First-Order Linearised Einstein’s Equation

We begin by constructing the linearised first-order version of Einstein’s equations, the gauge fixing will then be added to this in order to obtain the hyperbolic set of equations. The perturbation is done around the Minkowski spacetime, this means the background 2-forms and metric are constant, and the background connections are zero

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=0=\partial_{\mu}g_{\rho\sigma},\quad A^{i}_{\mu}=0.

(6.36)

In the previous section we saw that the conformal perturbation appears in the trace part of the metric perturbation, and that no physical dynamics are contained in there at the linear level. For this reason we consider separating out the trace of the metric perturbation,

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}=\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}h,\qquad g^{\mu\nu}\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}=0.

(6.37)

The prescription
\hat{h}_{\mu\nu},h,h^{i}
and
h_{\mu\nu},h^{i}
are equivalent, the perturbation of
\Sigma^{i}
in this conformally separated parametrisation is

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{4}h\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}h^{k}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}+\hat{h}_{[\mu}{}^{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{|\rho|\nu]}.

(6.38)

Similarly, for the connection we can separate the conformal term

\displaystyle a^{i}_{\mu}=\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h+\partial_{\mu}h^{i}-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\hat{h}_{\mu\sigma}.

(6.39)

The first-order Einstein condition on the curvature of the connection,
\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}=0
, linearises to become

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu}a^{i}_{\rho}-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}a^{i}_{\nu}=0.

(6.40)

Collecting the results we have a first-order system of equations that imposes the linearised Einstein’s equations

\displaystyle a^{i}_{\mu}=\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h+\partial_{\mu}h^{i}-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\hat{h}_{\mu\sigma}.

(6.41)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu}a^{i}_{\rho}-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}a^{i}_{\nu}=0.

(6.42)

Repeating the previous calculation of substituting in the equation defining
a^{i}_{\mu}
in terms of the frame perturbation, we find that in terms of the data
\hat{h}_{\mu\nu},h,h^{i}
the second-order Einstein’s equations are

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu}a^{i}_{\rho}-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}a^{i}_{\nu}=-\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}h-\partial^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}+\partial_{(\mu}\left(2\partial^{\rho}\hat{h}_{\nu)\rho}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu)}h\right).

(6.43)

The first two terms here are hyperbolic as they’re proportional to the linear wave equation
\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\phi=0
. The last term in the brackets is not hyperbolic, the strategy then becomes finding a first-order gauge fixing that removes this unwanted term but keeps the same physical solutions. It also should be noted that the perturbation of the
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
frame,
h^{i}
, is not present and is therefore not hyperbolic. The gauge fixing developed should also give
h^{i}
a hyperbolic second-order equation of motion.

6.3.2
de Donder Gauge Fixing

In the usual metric formalism it is known that the linearised version of the Harmonic condition, also called the de Donder gauge, is imposed to make Einstein’s equation hyperbolic. We impose the de Donder gauge in its first-order form, which fixes the linear diffeomorphism freedom, by introducing the 1-form

\displaystyle\Lambda^{1}\ni\xi_{\mu}=-2\partial^{\nu}\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}h.

(6.44)

This term has exactly the same form as the non-hyperbolic term in
eq.

6.43
. It follows that to remove it from Einstein’s equations we can simply modify
eq.

6.42
to include

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu}a^{i}_{\rho}-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}a^{i}_{\nu}+\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}=0.

(6.45)

We interpret
\xi_{\mu}
as being 4 extra connection component, along with the original connection
a^{i}_{\mu}
. This is because connections are, in general, derivatives of the metric variables. In fact
\xi_{\mu}
can be seen as the linearisation of the 4 components of the Levi-Civita connection
\Gamma^{\mu}=\Gamma^{\mu}_{\rho\sigma}g^{\rho\sigma}
, this will help motivate the nonlinear version of this gauge in later sections. Substituting
\xi_{\mu}
and
a^{i}_{\mu}
with their definitions in terms of the metric perturbations we find

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu}a^{i}_{\rho}-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}a^{i}_{\nu}+\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}=-\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}h-\partial^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}=-\partial^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}h_{\mu\nu}=0

(6.46)

is then hyperbolic as intended. To recover the usual de Donder gauge we take
\xi_{\mu}=0
in which case
eq.

6.44
is the de Donder condition and
eq.

6.45
loses its modification and returns to the usual linearised Einstein equations. The factor chosen in the modification in
eq.

6.45
is fixed by the definition of the 1-form
\xi_{\mu}
, this part of the gauge fixing is fixed and there is no freedom if one wants to obtain the wave equation.

6.3.3
Modified Lorenz Gauge

There still remains the
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
freedom in the theory. A common choice, and the choice used in the previous section, is to remove this gauge explicitly by setting
h^{i}=0
. Here, we leave it dynamical and impose an equation of motion on
a^{i}
which propagates the frame with the wave equation. The gauge choice here must be
3
conditions on the connection, a natural choice is then the Lorenz gauge

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}a^{i}_{\mu}=0.

(6.47)

Substituting in
eq.

6.41
for
a^{i}
, we find

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}\left(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h-\partial_{\mu}h^{i}-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\hat{h}_{\mu\sigma}\right)=-\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\partial^{\mu}\hat{h}_{\mu\sigma}=0

(6.48)

We see that the first term is hyperbolic in
h^{i}
, which is the term we wish to keep, however, the second term destroys the overall hyperbolicity. To fix this we notice that the second term in the above can be written using the new connection 1-form,

\displaystyle-\Sigma^{i\rho\mu}\partial_{\rho}\partial^{\nu}\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}=\Sigma^{i\rho\mu}\partial_{\rho}\left(-\partial^{\nu}\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\mu}h\right)=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\rho\mu}\partial_{\rho}\xi_{\nu}.

(6.49)

The term involving
h
is identically zero, therefore we can change its factor from
-\frac{1}{4}
to
+\frac{1}{2}
so that the definition of
\xi_{\mu}
appears, and realising the last equality. Clearly this produces the following modification to the Lorenz gauge

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}a^{i}_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}=-2\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}=0.

(6.50)

We call this the modified Lorenz gauge and its second order equation is exactly the wave equation on the perturbation of the
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
frame,
h^{i}
.

6.3.4
Modified de Donder and Connections

The previous two gauge fixings for the diffeomorphism and
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
gauges are enough to ensure hyperbolicity for the frame sector. However, the number of components in the frame and connection sectors do not match. There are
4+12=16
connection fields and
1+3+9=13
in the self-dual frame. We treat real and complex components equally in this context as it is purely the number of fields that determines the structure, another way of thinking about it is that
\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{C}
and hence all fields can be taken as
\mathbb{C}
valued (given some extra reality constraints). When the number of fields matches in each sector this allows one to interpret the first-order equation of motion as a Dirac operator and its adjoint (with respect to some norm) acting on these fields. As we will see in the nonlinear story adding the missing
3
fields allows for the Gauss constraint to propagate which directly effects hyperbolicity.
Therefore, we introduce another internal vector
\chi^{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
, this is taken to be part of the frame sector making
16
fields in total. This allows us to modify the equations defining the connections,
eqs.

6.41
and
6.44
, like so

\displaystyle a^{i}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h-\partial_{\mu}h^{i}-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\hat{h}_{\mu\sigma}+c_{1}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}

(6.51)

\displaystyle\xi_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-2\partial^{\nu}\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}h+c_{3}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}

(6.52)

where
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
are constants. These are all the extra terms one can construct using derivatives of
\chi^{i}
, this can be explicitly checked using representation theory, which we will not spell out here. Similar to before we wish for Einstein’s equations to be hyperbolic. Since the linearised equations are linear in their arguments and the modifications to the connection are linear, to compute the hyperbolicity of the new fields we only need to consider

\displaystyle a^{i}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=c_{1}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}

(6.53)

\displaystyle\xi_{\mu}

\displaystyle=c_{3}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}.

(6.54)

Substituting these into the modified Einstein’s equations we find

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu}a^{i}_{\rho}-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}a^{i}_{\nu}+

\displaystyle\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}=\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu}\left(c_{1}\partial_{\rho}\chi^{i}+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\rho}{}^{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}\chi^{k}\right)+c_{3}\partial_{(\mu}\left(\Sigma^{i}_{\nu)}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\chi^{i}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\left(c_{1}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}\chi^{k}\right)

\displaystyle=c_{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\chi^{i}+c_{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\chi^{i}-(c_{1}+c_{2})\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\chi^{i}

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+(c_{1}+2c_{2})\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\chi^{i}+c_{3}\partial_{(\mu}\left(\Sigma^{i}_{\nu)}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\chi^{i}\right)

\displaystyle=c_{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\chi^{i}+(c_{3}+2c_{2})\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu)}\partial_{\rho}\chi^{i}.

(6.55)

It is then clear that
c_{3}=-2c_{2}
will cause Einstein’s equations to be hyperbolic in
\chi^{i}
.
We also have to check that this modification keeps the hyperbolicity of the Lorenz gauge,

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}a^{i}_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}=c_{1}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}-\frac{c_{3}}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\left(\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{j}\right)

(6.56)

\displaystyle=\left(c_{1}+\frac{c_{3}}{2}\right)\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}=(c_{1}-c_{2})\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}.

(6.57)

No new conditions are imposed by the requirement that the Lorenz gauge implies second-order hyperbolic equations. This results in a 2 parameter family of hyperbolic gauge fixing for linearised Plebański, assuming that both
c_{1},c_{2}
are not zero at the same time which would remove the gauge fixing. If
c_{1}=c_{2}
then it appears that the equation of motion for
\chi^{i}
is removed, however, it is still provided a dynamical equation of motion through the modification to the Einstein condition.

6.3.5
Linearised Gauge Fixed Plebański

Starting with the linearised self-dual frame
(h,h^{i},\hat{h}_{\mu\nu})
we add to this sector an internal vector
\chi^{i}
. Then we define the modified linearised self-dual connection
a^{i}_{\mu}
along with another connection 1-form
\xi_{\mu}
as derivatives of the frame and internal vector

\displaystyle a^{i}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h-\partial_{\mu}h^{i}-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\hat{h}_{\mu\sigma}+c_{1}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}

(6.58)

\displaystyle\xi_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-2\partial^{\nu}\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}h-2c_{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}.

(6.59)

Such that the modified linearised Einstein equation and Lorenz condition form a hyperbolic system of equations. The modified Einstein’s equations and Lorenz condition are

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu}a^{i}_{\rho}-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}a^{i}_{\nu}+\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}=0

(6.60)

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}a^{i}_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}=0.

(6.61)

On the removal of the gauge variables
\xi_{\mu}=\chi^{i}=0
this returns the usual Einstein’s equations on a linearised metric
(\delta g)_{\mu\nu}=2\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}h
. The second order equations imposed by the above conditions are

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}h-\partial^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}+c_{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\chi^{i}

\displaystyle=0

(6.62)

\displaystyle-\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+(c_{1}-c_{2})\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}

\displaystyle=0.

(6.63)

We see the highest order symbols are the wave operators
\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
for all the components. The equations (
6.58
,
6.59
) and (
6.60
,
6.61
) form our linearised gauged fixed Plebański system.

6.3.6
Linear Separation

There is a coincidence, in Plebański’s formalism, that allows the gauge fixed Einstein’s equation to be separated into two sectors. This is possible because of maps between
\Lambda^{1}
and
\mathbb{C}^{3}\times\Lambda^{1}
which are the projection and embedding of
(\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1})_{4}
into and out of
\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}
detailed in
eqs.

4.79
and
4.78
. To show this separation we first introduce new connection fields as linear combinations of the old connections

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=a^{i}_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\xi_{\nu}

(6.64)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\xi_{\mu}+2\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}a^{i}_{\nu}

(6.65)

along with their inverse

\displaystyle a^{i}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{i}_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\Omega^{k}_{\nu}-\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\omega_{\nu}

(6.66)

\displaystyle\xi_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{\mu}.

(6.67)

For our new connection fields we can look at their implied definitions, through
a^{i}_{\mu}
and
\xi_{\mu}
, in terms of derivatives of the frame perturbation

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{\mu}\left(h^{i}-(c_{1}-c_{2})\chi^{i}\right)+\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\hat{h}_{\rho\sigma}-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\hat{h}_{\mu\sigma}

(6.68)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{\mu}h-2\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\left(h^{i}-(c_{1}+c_{2})\chi^{i}\right).

(6.69)

If we introduce two linear combinations of the frame

\displaystyle\hat{h}^{i}

\displaystyle=h^{i}-(c_{1}-c_{2})\chi^{i}

(6.70)

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}^{i}

\displaystyle=h^{i}-(c_{1}+c_{2})\chi^{i}

(6.71)

we find the connections become

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{\mu}\hat{h}^{i}+\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\hat{h}_{\rho\sigma}-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\hat{h}_{\mu\sigma}

(6.72)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{\mu}h-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\hat{\chi}^{i}.

(6.73)

Note that
\Omega^{i}_{\mu}
only depends on
\hat{h}^{i},\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}
and
\omega_{\mu}
only depends on
h,\hat{\chi}^{i}
. The dimension of each sector is
12
and
4
. So far this split holds at the level of the connection definitions, but as we will see it extends to the first-order connection equations of motion. To check this we can substitute
eqs.

6.66
and
6.67
into
eqs.

6.60
and
6.61
.
In doing so we find

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}+\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\rho}\omega_{\rho}-\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\left(\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\omega_{\sigma}+2\partial^{\rho}\Omega^{i}_{\rho}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu\rangle}\Omega^{i}_{\rho}-\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu|}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\Omega^{i}_{|\nu\rangle}=0

(6.74)

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}a^{i}_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\partial^{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\mu}=0

(6.75)

where
T_{\langle\mu\nu\rangle}=\frac{1}{2}\left(T_{\mu\nu}+T_{\nu\mu}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}g^{\rho\sigma}T_{\rho\sigma}\right)
is the symmetric tracefree part. Clearly, the second equation is already separated as it only depends on
\Omega^{i}_{\mu}
. Now we can use the decomposition
\Lambda^{1}\otimes\Lambda^{1}=C^{\infty}(M)\oplus\Lambda^{1}\otimes_{stf}\Lambda^{1}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{3}\oplus\overline{\mathbb{C}^{3}}
and compute the irreducible parts through

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}g^{\mu\nu}\in C^{\infty}(M),\quad T_{\langle\mu\nu\rangle}\in\textrm{Sym}_{0}^{2}(\Lambda^{1}),\quad T_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\in\mathbb{C}^{3},\quad T_{\mu\nu}\overline{\Sigma}^{i\mu\nu}\in\overline{\mathbb{C}^{3}}.

(6.76)

Where
\textrm{Sym}_{0}^{2}(\Lambda^{1})
is the space of symmetric tracefree tensors. For which we find the irreducible components of
eq.

6.74
are

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}\omega_{\mu}=0,\quad\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}+2\partial^{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\mu}=0,\quad\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu\rangle}\Omega^{i}_{\rho}-\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu|}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\Omega^{i}_{|\nu\rangle}=0.

(6.77)

The trace and symmetric tracefree component spaces are clearly only depend on one of the two connection sectors, as for the self-dual component by taking the appropriate linear combination with modified Lorenz condition we can see that

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\mu}=0,\quad\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}=0

(6.78)

are separate conditions. Therefore, the linear Einstein’s equations and modified Lorenz condition together are equivalent to the separate system of equations,

\displaystyle\omega_{\mu}=-\partial_{\mu}h-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\hat{\chi}^{i},\quad\partial^{\mu}\omega_{\mu}=0,\quad\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}=0

(6.79)

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}_{\mu}=-\partial_{\mu}\hat{h}^{i}+\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\hat{h}_{\rho\sigma}-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\hat{h}_{\mu\sigma},\quad\partial^{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\mu}=0,\quad\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu\rangle}\Omega^{i}_{\rho}-\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu|}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\Omega^{i}_{|\nu\rangle}=0.

(6.80)

This separation is quite remarkable, it is the two parameter generalisation of the separation appearing in
[
26
,
49
]
and
chapter

7
. For example the splitting in
[
49
]
and
chapter

7
is obtained when taking
c_{1}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
and
c_{2}=0
, then after rescaling some fields we find the maps are equivalent. The equivalence to
[
26
]
is less obvious as one would need to take care when converting from spinor indices to spacetime indices. From the metric perturbation analysis earlier in this section we found that the physical degrees of freedom are in the tracefree part of the metric perturbation
\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}
, this only appears in the 12 component sector
eq.

6.80
. One can then ignore the 4 component system in
eq.

6.79
as they do not propagate physical degrees of freedom. This offers a more efficient route to imposing linearised Einstein’s equations.

6.3.7
Separated Lagrangian

We note that the equations of motion for the separated system can each be obtained from a Lagrangian

\displaystyle L_{4}

\displaystyle=h\partial^{\mu}\omega_{\mu}-\hat{\chi}^{i}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\omega^{\mu}\omega_{\mu}

(6.81)

\displaystyle L_{12}

\displaystyle=\hat{h}^{i}\partial^{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\mu}-2\hat{h}^{[\mu}{}_{\rho}\Sigma^{i\nu]\rho}\partial_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{i\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\mu}.

(6.82)

By varying with respect to each field we obtain the equations
eqs.

6.79
and
6.80
.
There is a 1-parameter family of combined Lagrangians that describes both the 4 and 12, however, the combination that is proportional to linearised Plebański is given by

\displaystyle L_{pleb}=\frac{1}{4}L_{4}-L_{12}.

(6.83)

Using the formulas for
L_{4}
and
L_{12}
and taking the transformation back to the original variables we find the total Lagrangian becomes

\displaystyle L_{pleb}=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{4}h\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}+\epsilon^{ijk}h^{j}\Sigma^{k\mu\nu}-2\hat{h}^{[\mu|}{}_{\rho}\Sigma^{i\rho|\nu]}\right)\partial_{\mu}a^{i}_{\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a^{j}_{\mu}a^{k}_{\nu}

\displaystyle-\left(\hat{h}^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}h\right)\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}-2c_{2}\chi^{i}\partial^{\mu}a^{i}_{\mu}-(c_{1}+c_{2})\chi^{i}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a^{k}_{\nu}

\displaystyle\hskip 200.0pt+c_{2}\chi^{i}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+\frac{1}{4}\xi^{\mu}\xi_{\mu}.

(6.84)

The first line in the above is the linearisation of the Plebański Lagrangian,
L=\Sigma^{i}\wedge F^{i}
and the second is the Lagrangian corresponding to the modified gauge choices. Again we see the 2 parameter family of gauge choices that produces the separation property as well as the hyperbolicity. The action in
[
49
]
is also equivalent given the correct choice of
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
. The reason any linear combination of
L_{4}
and
L_{12}
can be chosen is because
L_{4}
does not propagate any physical degrees of freedom therefore the action can always be written as
L=L_{pleb}+\alpha L_{4}
and as the physical theory is independent of
L_{4}
the choice of
\alpha
is irrelevant.

6.4
Dirac Operators

Since the linearised Einstein’s equation are first-order and their square produces the wave operator, it should be possible to write this system as two Dirac operators on the frame and connection fields. First, we recall some basic factors about the Dirac-Kähler operator on differential forms. The Dirac-Kähler operator is a formal square root of the wave operator on the space of differential forms, and is given by

\displaystyle D=-(d+d^{*}),\quad D:\Lambda^{n}\rightarrow\Lambda^{n-1}\oplus\Lambda^{n+1}.

(6.85)

The operators
d^{*}
is the codifferential and its action is defined as

\displaystyle(d^{*}\theta)_{\mu_{1}...\mu_{n}}=-\partial^{\mu}\theta_{\mu\mu_{1}...\mu_{n}}\in\Lambda^{n},\quad\theta\in\Lambda^{n+1}.

(6.86)

It can then be shown that the Dirac squares to the wave operator
D^{2}=dd^{*}+d^{*}d=\Box
. This construction is due to a correspondence between spinors and differential forms
[
90
]
. The actions
eqs.

6.81
and
6.82
can then be expressed using differential forms.

6.4.1
4 Component Dirac Operator

For the 4 component sector we only need to identify each field with an appropriate differential form, a natural choice is

\displaystyle h\in\Lambda^{0},\quad\omega\in\Lambda^{1},\quad\hat{\chi}=\hat{\chi}^{i}\Sigma^{i}\in\Lambda^{+}.

(6.87)

Since the Dirac-Kähler operator swaps between odd and even polyforms is makes sense to introduce the odd and even combinations of these fields,

\displaystyle H=h+\hat{\chi}\in\Lambda^{0}\oplus\Lambda^{+},\qquad\omega\in\Lambda^{1}.

(6.88)

The equations of motion in
eq.

6.79
are then

\displaystyle\omega=D|_{\Lambda^{1}}H,\quad D_{+}\omega=0

(6.89)

where
D|_{\Lambda^{1}}
is
D
followed by the projection onto
\Lambda^{1}
and
D_{+}
is
D
followed by the projection of
\Lambda^{2}\rightarrow\Lambda^{+}
. Despite the projections we find that the composition of the two still gives the wave operator. Explicitly the individual operators are

\displaystyle D|_{\Lambda^{1}}H=dh+d^{*}\hat{\chi},\qquad D_{+}\omega=\frac{1}{2}(1-i\star)d\omega+d^{*}\omega

(6.90)

where
P_{+}=\frac{1}{2}(1-i\star):\Lambda^{2}\rightarrow\Lambda^{+}
is the projector onto self-dual 2-forms. To recover the third equation in
eq.

6.79
from the above system one can make use of the definition
\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\sim(P_{+})_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho\sigma}
. The composition of the two operators is then

\displaystyle D_{+}D|_{\Lambda^{1}}H=\frac{1}{2}(1-i\star)d(dh+d^{*}\hat{\chi})+d^{*}(dh+d^{*}\hat{\chi})=\frac{1}{2}(1-i\star)dd^{*}\hat{\chi}+d^{*}dh

(6.91)

We can then make use of the definition of the codifferential and self-duality to show

\displaystyle\star dd^{*}\hat{\chi}=\star d\star d\star\hat{\chi}=id^{*}d\hat{\chi}

(6.92)

such that the result of the composition is

\displaystyle D_{+}D|_{\Lambda^{1}}H=\frac{1}{2}(dd^{*}+d^{*}d)\hat{\chi}+d^{*}dh=\frac{1}{2}\Box\hat{\chi}+\Box h.

(6.93)

From which we can easily recover the result that the second order equations on the frame are wave operators

\displaystyle D_{+}D|_{\Lambda^{1}}(h+\hat{\chi})=0,\quad\Rightarrow\quad\Box h=0,\quad\Box\hat{\chi}=0.

(6.94)

This sector is related to the truncated de Rham complex

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\Lambda^{0}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\Lambda^{1}\overset{d_{+}}{\longrightarrow}\Lambda^{+}\longrightarrow 0

(6.95)

where
d_{+}=P_{+}\circ d
. This also plays an important role in chiral Yang-Mills theories
[
64
]
. Each space has an inner product defined through metric pairing, the Dirac-Kähler operator is then obtained by combining the adjoint operators into the maps

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-9.61627pt}{-11.2614pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -13.31 -15.58)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(6.96)

Part of Einstein’s equations are then equivalent to the vanishing of the composition of the top arrow followed by the bottom one.

6.4.2
12 Component Dirac Operator

For the 12 component sector some more work is needed in order to represent the fields as differential forms. First we tackle
\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}
and make use of
corollary

4.1
to map
Sym^{2}_{0}(\Lambda^{1})
into
\Lambda^{-}
,

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\mu\nu}=2\hat{h}_{[\mu}{}^{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu]\rho}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{-}

(6.97)

Under this map the first equation in (
6.80
) becomes

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}_{\mu}=-\partial_{\mu}\hat{h}^{i}+\partial^{\nu}H^{i}_{\nu\mu}.

(6.98)

For the last equation in (
6.80
) we use the map between
Sym^{2}_{0}(\Lambda^{1})
and
3\times 3
Hermitian matrices,
eq.

4.70
to find

\displaystyle\Sigma^{k}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{[\nu}\Omega^{k}_{\rho]}\Sigma^{i\mu\sigma}\overline{\Sigma}^{j\nu}{}_{\sigma}=(\delta^{ik}g^{\rho\sigma}-\epsilon^{ikl}\Sigma^{l\rho\sigma})\partial_{[\nu}\Omega^{k}_{\rho]}\overline{\Sigma}^{j\nu}{}_{\sigma}=\overline{\Sigma}^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}=0.

(6.99)

The above condition can then be interpreted as the anti-self-dual projection of
d\Omega^{i}
.
Now the fields all have a differential form interpretation,

\displaystyle\hat{h}^{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{0},\quad\Omega^{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1},\quad H^{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{-}.

(6.100)

Using this representation the equations of motion in
eq.

6.80
become

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}=D|_{\Lambda^{1}}(\hat{h}^{i}+H^{i}),\quad D_{-}\Omega^{i}=0.

(6.101)

Where, similar to the 4 component case,
D_{-}
is the usual Dirac-Kähler operator followed by projection onto
\Lambda^{-}
. The composition of the two operators can be computed similarly to before and results in

\displaystyle D_{-}D|_{\Lambda^{1}}(\hat{h}^{i}+H^{i})=0,\quad\Rightarrow\quad\Box\hat{h}^{i}=0,\quad\Box H^{i}=0.

(6.102)

This sector can be placed into another truncated de Rham complex,

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{0}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}\overset{d_{-}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{-}\longrightarrow 0

(6.103)

this time all the fields are multiplied with
\mathbb{C}^{3}
and the anti-self-dual 2-forms are present instead of the self-dual. As before the Dirac-Kähler operators are recovered using the metric inner product and constructing the following operators

\hbox to191.13pt{\vbox to30.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 95.56245pt\lower-14.41417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{1.1824pt}{-11.2614pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 1.64 -15.58)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(6.104)

Their composition leads to the remaining part of Einstein’s equations. We see that by parametrising the fields correctly, gauge fixed linearised gravity, through the lens of Plebański’s formulation, has a relatively simple interpretation using Dirac-Kähler operators. This is due to fact that linearised gravity around Minkowski can be thought of as a collection of truncated de Rham complexes and the gauge fixing that occurs here becomes the adjoint of the maps on the spaces of the elliptic operator. This leads to the work in the next chapter on further realisations of gravity as an elliptic complex.

Chapter 7
Linear Plebański Elliptic Complex

In this chapter we explore a mathematical result available through the use of self-dual 2-form formulation of differential geometry in dimension 4. Yang-Mills (YM) instantons are important equations in both physical and mathematical literature, their solutions corresponding to physical configurations of gauge fields. The YM instanton equation is

\displaystyle F(A)=-\star F(A),\quad\textrm{or}\quad F_{-}=0

(7.1)

where
F(A)
is the curvature of a connection
A
and
F_{-}
is its projection onto
\Lambda^{-}
. Instantons are critical points of the YM’s action in dimension 4. The YM action, when evaluated on an instanton, is a topological invariant on the principal bundle that the connection appears on. To study the solution space of the nonlinear instanton equation one linearises around an instanton background. When also introducing the correct gauge fixing these equations form an elliptic complex. Using a result from Atiyah-Singer
[
91
]
one can relate the dimension of the space of solutions to topological data. Then through the pioneering work by Atiyah, Hitchin and Singer
[
64
]
, Donaldson
[
92
]
was able to obtain novel results on differential structures in dimension 4.

In this spirit we aim to enable a similar story in the case of self-dual instantons for gravity. Instantons, in the case of self-dual 2-forms, only exists in Euclidean or split signature. In any signature we have the decomposition of the self-dual curvature into self-dual and anti-self-dual 2-forms,

\displaystyle F^{i}=\Psi^{ij}\Sigma^{j}+\frac{R}{36}\Sigma^{i}+R^{ij}\overline{\Sigma}^{j}.

(7.2)

Where
\Psi^{ij}
is the self-dual Weyl curvature,
R^{ij}
and
R
are the tracefree Ricci tensor and Ricci scalar. Einstein’s equations, in a vacuum and with zero cosmological constant, imply,

\displaystyle F^{i}=\Psi^{ij}\Sigma^{j}

(7.3)

such that
R^{ij}=0=R
. That is the Ricci tensor vanishes. Instanton solutions to Einstein’s equations are then characterised by

\displaystyle\Psi^{ij}=0,\quad\textrm{or}\quad F^{i}=0.

(7.4)

In Lorentzian signature we have that
-\overline{\Psi^{ij}}^{*}=\Psi^{ij}
and so the instanton condition implies that the entire of the Riemann curvature vanishes which corresponds to Minkowski space. No interesting conditions are imposed in this case. We therefore choose to focus on Euclidean signature instantons. It should be noted that a certain subset of solutions to the Euclidean instantons will correspond to Lorentzian solutions analytically continued into Euclidean ones, therefore the Euclidean case may remain of interest in physics. This chapter is heavily based on the author’s paper
[
49
]
.

7.1
Elliptic Complex

Definition 7.1
.

Let
E_{0},E_{1},\ldots,E_{k}
be a sequence of vector bundles over a manifold
M
. An elliptic complex is the given by the corresponding sequence of operators
d_{i}
,

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow E_{0}\overset{d_{1}}{\longrightarrow}E_{1}\overset{d_{2}}{\longrightarrow}\ldots\overset{d_{k}}{\longrightarrow}E_{k}\longrightarrow 0.

(7.5)

Such that the operators satisfy

1.

d_{i+1}\circ d_{i}=0
, which implies that
\textrm{Im}(d_{i})\subseteq\textrm{Ker}(d_{i+1})
.

2.

The associated symbols
\sigma(d_{i})
are exact, that is
\textrm{Im}(\sigma(d_{i},\xi))=\textrm{Ker}(\sigma(d_{i+1},\xi))
, when
\xi\neq 0\in\Lambda^{1}
.

The symbol of a differential operator,

\displaystyle d=\sum_{k=0}^{N}a^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}\partial_{\mu_{1}}\ldots\partial_{\mu_{k}}

(7.6)

for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
, is given by

\displaystyle\sigma(d,\xi)=a^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}\xi_{\mu_{1}}\ldots\xi_{\mu_{N}}.

(7.7)

In a sense it is the Fourier transform of the highest order term of the differential operator
d
. The prototypical example of an Elliptic Complex is the de Rham complex of differential forms

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\Lambda^{0}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\Lambda^{1}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\ldots\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\Lambda^{n}

(7.8)

where the maps
d_{i}=d
are all the exterior derivative. The symbol of the operator is then

\displaystyle\sigma(d_{i},\xi)=\sigma(d,\xi)=\xi\wedge:\Lambda^{k}\rightarrow\Lambda^{k+1},\quad\xi\in\Lambda^{1}.

(7.9)

Using a standard result of linear algebra we have that for a differential form
\theta\in\Lambda^{k}
,

\displaystyle\xi\wedge\theta=0,\quad\Rightarrow\quad\theta=\xi\wedge\alpha,\quad\alpha\in\Lambda^{k-1}.

(7.10)

Which proves that the complex is elliptic since the image of any operator is the kernel of the next.

7.2
Yang-Mills Instantons

We now show how the Euclidean Yang-Mills instanton equations appear in the context of elliptic complexes. Starting from the instanton condition
eq.

7.1
, we linearise the instanton equations around an instanton background and find the following elliptic complex of differential operators

\displaystyle\Lambda^{0}(\mathfrak{g})\overset{d^{A}}{\longrightarrow}\Lambda^{1}(\mathfrak{g})\overset{d^{A}_{+}}{\longrightarrow}\Lambda^{+}(\mathfrak{g})

(7.11)

Here
d^{A}
is the exterior covariant derivative with respect to the background gauge field
A
, and the second operator is the restriction of the exterior covariant derivative (of a Lie algebra valued 1-form) to the space of self-dual Lie algebra valued 2-forms. The composition of the two operators appearing above vanishes when the background gauge field is an instanton
F(A){}_{+}=0
. The above complex can be checked to be elliptic, this follows from the Hodge complex as the symbols are the same. Also, the full YM equations
\star d^{A}\star F(A)=0
are equivalent to
\star d^{A}\star F_{+}(A)=0
, and the linearisation of the latter is then the statement that

\displaystyle(d^{A}_{+}){}^{*}d^{A}_{+}a=0,

(7.12)

where
(d^{A}_{+}){}^{*}
is the adjoint of
d^{A}_{+}a
with respect to the metric pairing inner product. So, the complex in
eq.

7.11
can be said to encode the full second-order YM field equations. Adding the adjoint operators, one gets an elliptic operator

\displaystyle\Lambda^{1}(\mathfrak{g})\overset{d^{A}_{+}+{(d^{A})}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}\Lambda^{0}(\mathfrak{g})\oplus\Lambda^{+}(\mathfrak{g})

(7.13)

This operator is known to be the (twisted by
A
) Dirac operator
D:S_{+}\otimes S_{-}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to S_{+}\otimes S_{+}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
, where
S_{\pm}
are the two spinor representations of
{\rm Spin}(4)
. The instanton complex plays the key role in understanding the (local) properties of the moduli space of YM instantons, see
[
93
]
.

7.3
Plebański Complex

As mentioned the aim of this chapter is to build an analogous elliptic differential complex, but this time relevant for Einstein equations rather than YM equations. A similar construction in the metric language is also considered in
[
94
]
. Our construction became possible due to the recent re-interpretation
[
15
]
of the Plebański description
[
19
]
of Einstein equations.

We have seen in
chapter

4
that Einstein’s equation can be encoded into a triple of complex 2-forms. Here we give a brief description of the changes that occur when working in Euclidean signature. Firstly, the Hodge star squares to
\star^{2}=+\mathbb{I}
, this means that (anti-) self-dual 2-forms are real valued and no extension to complex numbers is needed. As such the reality conditions can also be dropped due to the metric being automatically real-valued. Let
\Sigma^{i},i=1,2,3\in\Lambda^{2}
be a triple of 2-forms satisfying the metricity condition
\Sigma^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{j}\sim\delta^{ij}
, note the proportionality factor here should be real-valued unlike in the Lorentzian case. Some literature refers to such a set of 2-forms as perfect triples. The
{\rm GL}(4,\mathbb{R})
stabiliser of a perfect triple can be shown to be
{\rm SU}(2)
, and so perfect triples give an example of a
G
-structure, with
G={\rm SU}(2)
. Given an
{\rm SU}(2)
-structure
\Sigma^{i}
, there exists a triple of 1-forms
A^{i}
satisfying the equation

\displaystyle d\Sigma^{i}+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}=0.

(7.14)

This triple of 1-forms can be shown
[
15
]
to coincide with the so-called intrinsic torsion of the
{\rm SU}(2)
-structure
\Sigma^{i}
. When the intrinsic torsion vanishes the
{\rm SU}(2)
-structure is integrable. It can be shown that in this case the metric defined by
\Sigma^{i}
is Ricci-flat and there are 3 integrable complex structures satisfying the algebra of the imaginary quaternions. So, when
d\Sigma^{i}=0
the metric defined by
\Sigma^{i}
is hyper-Kähler. For more information about this description of hyper-Kähler metrics see e.g.
[
95
]
, Section 4.1. More generally, Einstein equations
R_{\mu\nu}=0
can also be encoded in the same language. Indeed, as is well-known from the general context of
G
-structures, the intrinsic torsion of a
G
-structure, when
G\subset{\rm SO}(n)
, where
\dim(M)=n
, together with its first derivatives, encode some part of the Riemann curvature tensor of the Riemannian metric defined by the
G
-structure. This is why it may be possible to impose some equations on the curvature, for example Einstein equations, as a first-order in derivatives condition on the intrinsic torsion. In the case at hand, one shows that Einstein equations
R_{\mu\nu}=0
become the statement that

\displaystyle F^{i}(A)=\Psi^{ij}\Sigma^{j},\qquad F^{i}(A)=dA^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\wedge A^{k}.

(7.15)

Here
\Psi^{ij}
is an arbitrary symmetric tracefree
3\times 3
matrix. The equations (
7.14
), (
7.15
) are the two equations appearing in the first-order Plebański formalism for general relativity. Their linearisation around the hyper-Kähler background
d\Sigma^{i}=0
, and thus
A^{i}=0
, is what gives rise to the Plebański complex. Define
E=\mathfrak{su}(2)=\mathfrak{so}(3)=\mathbb{R}^{3}
and let
S\subset E\otimes\Lambda^{2}
be the tangent space to the space of perfect triples at
\Sigma^{i}
. We will characterise
S
more explicitly below. Similarly, let
E\otimes\Lambda^{1}
be the tangent space to the space of triples of 1-forms
A^{i}
. The linearisation of the equation (
7.14
) at
A^{i}=0
is then

\displaystyle d\sigma^{i}+\epsilon^{ijk}a^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}=0,\qquad\sigma^{i}\in S,\quad a^{i}\in E\otimes\Lambda^{1}.

(7.16)

The solution of this equation can be stated in terms of a certain linear operator
J_{1}:E\otimes\Lambda^{1}\to E\otimes\Lambda^{1}
defined in (
7.38
). This operator has two eigenspaces in
E\otimes\Lambda^{1}
, decomposing it
E\otimes\Lambda^{1}=(E\otimes\Lambda^{1}){}_{4}\oplus(E\otimes\Lambda^{1}){}_{8}
. The equation (
7.16
) becomes a linear equation for
a^{i}
, whose solution is given by
a^{i}=d_{2}\sigma^{i}
, where

\displaystyle d_{2}:S\to E\otimes\Lambda^{1},\qquad d_{2}\sigma^{i}=\frac{1}{2}J_{1}^{-1}(\star d\sigma^{i}).

(7.17)

Here
\star
is the Hodge star with respect to the background metric defined by
\Sigma^{i}
. This is the operator that appears as the second arrow of our to-be-constructed complex. To construct the first arrow we note that the objects
a^{i}
that appear as the result of the map
d_{2}:S\to E\otimes\Lambda^{1}
are diffeomorphism invariant. Linearised diffeomorphisms act on the space
S
via

\displaystyle d_{1}:TM\to S,\qquad d_{1}\xi=d(\iota_{\xi}\Sigma^{i}).

(7.18)

We will see in the main exposition that the object
d(\iota_{\xi}\Sigma^{i})
is in
S
