, the tangent space to the space of perfect triples. It is then immediate to see that the composition
d_{2}d_{1}=0
. The final arrow of the Plebański complex arises by considering the map

\displaystyle d_{3}:E\otimes\Lambda^{1}\to E,\qquad d_{3}a^{i}=da^{i}\Big|_{3}\equiv\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}\wedge da^{k}/v_{\Sigma}.

(7.19)

Here
da^{i}\in E\otimes\Lambda^{2}
is the exterior derivative of
a^{i}
, and the projection is taken onto the copy of
E
inside
E\otimes\Lambda^{2}
. The last equality describes this projection explicitly, here
v_{\Sigma}
is the volume form for the metric defined by
\Sigma^{i}
, which we can take to be
v_{\Sigma}=(1/6)\Sigma^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{i}
. The vanishing of the composition
d_{3}d_{2}=0
is the consequence of the equation (
7.16
). Indeed, taking the exterior derivative of this equation one gets
d_{3}a^{i}=0
whenever
a^{i}
satisfies (
7.16
).

Assembling the spaces and the differential operators we get what we propose to call the Plebański complex

\displaystyle TM\overset{d_{1}}{\longrightarrow}S\overset{d_{2}}{\longrightarrow}E\otimes\Lambda^{1}\overset{d_{3}}{\longrightarrow}E

(7.20)

We then have the following

Theorem 7.1
.

The complex (
7.20
) is an elliptic complex of differential operators, as in
definition

7.1
.

We remark that the dimensions of the spaces appearing in (
7.20
) are

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 4}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 13}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 12}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}}

(7.21)

and thus the alternating sum of the dimensions vanishes, as it should for an elliptic complex.

Our second statement gives an Einstein analogue of the characterisation (
7.12
) of the linearised YM equations.

Theorem 7.2
.

Define the inner product on
E\otimes\Lambda^{1}
to be given by

\displaystyle\langle a,a\rangle=\int_{M}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i}\wedge a^{j}\wedge a^{k}.

(7.22)

The linearisation of the Einstein’s equations (
7.15
) is the statement

\displaystyle d_{2}^{*}d_{2}\sigma^{i}=0.

(7.23)

The operator
d_{2}^{*}
also depends on the inner product in
S
, and this is not unique. But the above theorem holds for any choice of the inner product in
S
.

Our final statement is related to a construction of an elliptic operator for (
7.20
) that squares to the Laplacian, giving a gravity analogue of (
7.13
). The most natural such construction would consist in simply adding the adjoints. Indeed, let us consider the operator

\displaystyle D:S\oplus E\to TM\oplus E\otimes\Lambda^{1},\qquad D(\sigma,\chi)=(d_{1}^{*}\sigma,d_{2}\sigma+d_{3}^{*}\chi).

(7.24)

The operator so constructed is guaranteed to be an elliptic operator. However, somewhat to our surprise, we have the following negative result:
D^{*}D
is not a multiple of the Laplacian on
S\oplus E
, if we demand that the inner product on
E\otimes\Lambda^{1}
is given by (
7.22
). However,
D^{*}D
is a multiple of the Laplacian if instead one uses the following inner product

\displaystyle\langle a,a\rangle^{\prime}=\int_{M}(a^{i},a^{i}),

(7.25)

where
(\cdot,\cdot)
is the metric pairing on
\Lambda^{1}
, defined with respect to the metric determine by
\Sigma^{i}
. While this choice of the inner product leads to the desired result for
D^{*}D
, this is not the choice that leads to
d_{2}^{*}
being the operator appearing in the linearised Einstein equations. This makes us look for a construction of
D
that has the property
D^{*}D\sim\Delta
and that will involve the operators
d_{2},d_{2}^{*}
, the latter being defined with respect to the inner product (
7.22
). Such a construction exists, but is non-trivial.

Let us consider the more general elliptic differential operator

\displaystyle\tilde{D}:S\oplus E\to TM\oplus E\otimes\Lambda^{1},\qquad\tilde{D}(\sigma,\chi)=(\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}\sigma+\tilde{d}_{4}\chi,d_{2}\sigma+\tilde{d}_{3}^{*}\chi),

(7.26)

where we introduced
\tilde{d}_{4}:E\to TM
. We require that
d_{2}
that appears here is the one of the Plebański complex (
7.20
), and that the inner product on
E\otimes\Lambda^{1}
is as in (
7.22
) so that
d_{2}^{*}
is the one that appears in the linearised Einstein equations. All other operators are allowed to be completely general first order differential operators as allowed by the relevant representation theory. In particular, we do not require that
\tilde{d}_{1},d_{2},\tilde{d}_{3},\tilde{d}_{4}
form a differential complex (i.e. there is no demand that the compositions are zero). We have the following

Theorem 7.3
.

There is a choice of the inner product on
S
, and there is a choice of the operators
\tilde{d}_{1},\tilde{d}_{3},\tilde{d}_{4}
such that all the following hold:

•

\tilde{D}^{*}\tilde{D}=\pm\Delta
on
S\oplus E

•

The operators
\tilde{d}_{1},\tilde{d}_{3},\tilde{d}_{4}
are linear combinations of
d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}
of the Plebański complex.

•

There exist linear maps

\displaystyle T_{1}:S\oplus E\to S\oplus E,\qquad T_{2}:TM\oplus E\otimes\Lambda^{1}\to TM\oplus E\otimes\Lambda^{1}

(7.27)

such that

\displaystyle T_{2}\tilde{D}T_{1}=D_{4}\oplus D_{12},

(7.28)

with
D_{4},D_{12}
being operators

\displaystyle D_{4}:\Lambda^{0}\oplus E\to\Lambda^{1},\qquad D_{12}:E\oplus{\rm Sym}^{2}_{0}(\Lambda^{1})\to E\otimes\Lambda^{1}

(7.29)

explicitly given by (
7.304
). Both of these operators are versions of the Dirac operator

\displaystyle D_{4}:S_{+}\otimes S_{+}\to S_{-}\otimes S_{+},\qquad D_{12}:S_{-}\otimes S_{-}\otimes S_{+}^{2}\to S_{+}\otimes S_{-}\otimes S_{+}^{2}.

(7.30)

Moreover, the choices we need to make for the above to hold are essentially unique, modulo signs that can be absorbed into field redefinitions. The operator
\tilde{D}
that is the result of construction in this theorem is explicitly given by

\displaystyle\tilde{D}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(d^{*}_{1}-\Phi d_{2})&&-\Phi d^{*}_{3}\\
d_{2}&&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}J_{1}d^{*}_{3}\end{pmatrix}.

(7.31)

Here all operators are those of the Plebański complex, the linear map
\Phi:E\otimes\Lambda^{1}\to\Lambda^{1}
is given by (
7.205
) and
J_{1}:E\otimes\Lambda^{1}\to E\otimes\Lambda^{1}
is the linear map given by (
7.38
). This gives us a non-trivial gauge-fixing of the Plebański complex, by appropriately assembling the operators
d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}
and their adjoints into an elliptic operator that can be written as (twisted by
T_{1},T_{2}
) direct sum of two Dirac operators.

If one does not demand that
\tilde{d}_{1},\tilde{d}_{3},\tilde{d}_{4}
can be expressed in terms of
d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}
of the Plebański complex then there is some more freedom in the choice of
\tilde{d}_{1},\tilde{d}_{3},\tilde{d}_{4}
that leads to
\tilde{D}^{*}\tilde{D}=\pm\Delta
. The operators
D_{4},D_{12}
already appeared in the work
[
26
]
, but this reference was using spinor techniques heavily, which makes it difficult to establish any uniqueness statement. More generally, the operators are those appearing in
section

6.4
containing a 2 parameter family of Dirac operators for the linearised Einstein’s equation. However, no consideration is taken to the ellipticity of the complex formed by such operators. In contrast, the present work makes all the assumptions that go into the construction of
\tilde{D}
manifest, leading to the statement in the above theorem.

7.4
Decomposition of
E
-valued differential forms

The material in this preparatory section is from
[
15
]
and is a translation of
section

4.5
in Euclidean signature. The rotation group in four dimensions is
{\rm SO}(4)={\rm SU}(2)\times{\rm SU}(2)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. One of the two
{\rm SU}(2)
factors stabilises the 2-forms
\Sigma^{i}
. The other
{\rm SU}(2)
factor rotates the triple
\Sigma^{i}
by an
{\rm SO}(3)
orthogonal transformation. This means that it is natural to treat
\Sigma^{i}
as a map
\Sigma:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\Lambda^{2}
. The image of this map is the space
\Lambda^{2}_{+}
of self-dual 2-forms. The map
\Sigma
thus identifies
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\Lambda^{2}_{+}
. This makes it natural to consider how the spaces of
E
-valued differential forms on
M
transform with respect to all of the rotation group
{\rm SO}(4)={\rm SU}(2)\times{\rm SU}(2)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

Thus, we need to understand the decomposition of the spaces
\Lambda^{1}(M)\otimes E,\Lambda^{2}(M)\otimes E
, into irreducible representations of
{\rm SU}(2)\times{\rm SU}(2)/Z_{2}
. Irreducible representations of
{\rm SU}(2)
are the spin
k/2
representations that we denote by
S^{k}
. They are of dimension
\dim(S^{k})=k+1
. As we have already discussed, there are two different
{\rm SU}(2)
’s in the game. One
{\rm SU}(2)
is the group with respect to which the 2-forms
\Sigma^{i}
are invariant. We will choose to denote this copy of
{\rm SU}(2)
by
{\rm SU}_{-}(2)
, and the corresponding representations by
S^{k}_{-}
. The other
{\rm SU}(2)
is one that acts non-trivially on
\Sigma^{i}
by mixing them, with the map
\Sigma:E\to\Lambda^{2}
being equivariant with respect to this copy of
{\rm SU}(2)
. We will denote it by
{\rm SU}_{+}(2)
, and the corresponding representations by
S_{+}^{k}
. We then have

\displaystyle\Lambda^{1}(M)=S_{+}\otimes S_{-},\qquad\Lambda^{2}(M)=S_{+}^{2}\oplus S_{-}^{2},\qquad E=S_{+}^{2},

(7.32)

and the decomposition of
\Lambda^{1}(M)\otimes E,\Lambda^{2}(M)\otimes E
into irreducibles is

\displaystyle\Lambda^{1}(M)\otimes E=(S_{+}\otimes S_{-})\oplus(S_{+}^{3}\otimes S_{-}),

(7.33)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{2}(M)\otimes E=C^{\infty}(M)\oplus S_{+}^{2}\oplus S_{+}^{4}\oplus(S_{+}^{2}\otimes S_{-}^{2}).

We now go on to state the algebra for the Euclidean
\Sigma^{i}
’s and the component formulae for the irreducible components.

7.4.1
Algebra of
\Sigma
’s

The components of the 2-forms we call
\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}
, where
\mu,\nu=1,2,3,4
. The Riemannian metric defined by
\Sigma^{i}
can be written explicitly as

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}v_{g}=\frac{1}{6}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\alpha}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu\beta}\Sigma^{k}_{\gamma\delta}\tilde{\epsilon}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}.

(7.34)

Here
\tilde{\epsilon}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
is the densitiesed completely antisymmetric tensor, which has components
\pm 1
in any coordinate system and does not need a metric for its definition. Both sides of this formula are densitiesed
\mu\nu
symmetric tensors, and
v_{g}=\sqrt{\det(g_{\mu\nu})}
is the volume form for
g_{\mu\nu}
.

One of the two indices of
\Sigma^{i}
can be raised with the metric (
7.34
), to convert these objects into those in
{\rm End}(TM)
. We then have a triple of such endomorphisms of the tangent bundle, satisfying the same algebra of the imaginary quaternions

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{j}_{\alpha}{}^{\nu}=-\delta^{ij}\delta_{\mu}{}^{\nu}+\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{k}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}.

(7.35)

It can be shown that the 2-forms also satisfy the following algebra

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=g_{\mu\rho}g_{\nu\sigma}-g_{\mu\sigma}g_{\nu\rho}+\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma},

(7.36)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}=-2\Sigma^{i}_{[\mu|\rho|}g_{\nu]\sigma}+2\Sigma^{i}_{[\mu|\sigma|}g_{\nu]\rho},

(7.37)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\sigma\alpha}=\delta_{\sigma}^{\rho}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}+\delta_{\sigma}^{\mu}\Sigma^{i\nu\rho}+\delta_{\sigma}^{\nu}\Sigma^{i\rho\mu}.

These relations, as well as
eq.

7.35
, will be used on numerous occasions below, without explicit mention.

7.4.2
Decomposition of
\Lambda^{1}(M)\otimes E

Given an
{\rm SU}(2)
structure
\Sigma^{i}
, we have the following operator acting on
\Lambda^{1}(M)\otimes E

\displaystyle\Lambda^{1}(M)\otimes E\ni a^{i}_{\mu}\to J_{1}(A){}_{\mu}^{i}:=\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}a^{k}_{\alpha}.

(7.38)

As the algebra,
eq.

7.35
, is signature independent we recover the same polynomial identity for
J_{1}
,

\displaystyle J_{1}^{2}=2\mathbb{I}+J_{1}.

(7.39)

This means that the eigenvalues of
J_{1}
are
2,-1
. It is easy to check that

\displaystyle E\otimes\Lambda^{1}(M)=(\Lambda^{1}(M)\otimes E){}_{4}\oplus(\Lambda^{1}(M)\otimes E){}_{8}

(7.40)

and that

\displaystyle\xi^{\nu}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu\mu}\in(\Lambda^{1}(M)\otimes E){}_{4}=(S_{+}\otimes S_{-})

(7.41)

and that
J_{1}
has eigenvalue
+2
on such forms. The space

\displaystyle(\Lambda^{1}(M)\otimes E){}_{8}=(S^{3}_{+}\otimes S_{-})

(7.42)

can then be characterised as the orthogonal complement of (
7.41
) in
\Lambda^{1}(M)\otimes E
.

7.4.3
Decomposition of
\Lambda^{2}(M)\otimes E

To characterise some of the spaces appearing in the decomposition of
\Lambda^{2}(M)\otimes E
we first consider the
{\rm GL}(4)
orbit of the 2-forms
\Sigma^{i}
. The tangent space to this orbit is precisely the space
S
of tangent vectors to perfect triples that we introduced above. This tangent space is the space of
E
-valued 2-forms of the form
h_{[\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\nu]}
. Decomposing
h_{\mu\nu}\in{\rm GL}(4)
into its symmetric and antisymmetric parts, and noting that the antisymmetric part is valued in
\Lambda^{2}(M)=S_{+}^{2}\oplus S_{-}^{2}
, we get the following list of irreducibles appearing

\displaystyle h_{[\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\nu]}\in S=C^{\infty}(M)\oplus S_{+}^{2}\oplus(S_{+}^{2}\otimes S_{-}^{2})\subset\Lambda^{2}(M)\otimes E,

(7.43)

which is all spaces in the second line of (
7.33
) apart from
S_{+}^{4}
. These irreducibles in
\Lambda^{2}(M)\otimes E
can then be characterised as the images of the map
h_{\mu\nu}\to h_{[\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\nu]}
.

One can also act on the index
i
of
\Sigma^{i}
2-forms with a
{\rm GL}(3)
transformation, i.e., consider the orbit of
E
-valued 2-forms of the form
h^{ij}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}
. Decomposing the matrix
h^{ij}
into symmetric and antisymmetric parts, one finds the following list of irreducibles

\displaystyle h^{ij}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}\in C^{\infty}(M)\oplus S_{+}^{2}\oplus S_{+}^{4}

(7.44)

where
C^{\infty}(M),S_{+}^{2},S_{+}^{4}
correspond to the trace, antisymmetric and symmetric tracefree parts respectively. In the opposite direction, given an object
B^{i}_{\mu\nu}\in\Lambda^{2}(M)\otimes E
, its irreducible parts can be extracted as follows

\displaystyle B^{k}_{\alpha\beta}\Sigma^{k\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\in C^{\infty}(M),

(7.45)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}B^{j}_{\alpha\beta}\Sigma^{k\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\in S_{+}^{2},

\displaystyle B^{\langle i}_{\alpha\beta}\Sigma^{j\rangle\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\in S_{+}^{4},

\displaystyle B^{i}_{\langle\mu|\alpha|}\Sigma^{i\alpha}{}_{\nu\rangle}

\displaystyle\in S_{+}^{2}\otimes S_{-}^{2}.

Here
T_{\langle\mu\nu\rangle}=(1/2)(T_{\mu\nu}+T_{\nu\mu}-(1/4)g_{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}T_{\alpha\beta})
, which denotes the symmetric tracefree part.

As in the Lorentzian case let us introduce the following operator

\displaystyle J_{2}:\Lambda^{2}\otimes E\to\Lambda^{2}\otimes E,\qquad J_{2}(B){}_{\mu\nu}^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{[\mu}{}^{\alpha}B_{|\alpha|\nu]}^{k},\qquad B_{\mu\nu}^{i}\in\Lambda^{2}\otimes E.

(7.46)

For which we find the following polynomial relation

\displaystyle J_{2}^{4}-2J_{2}^{3}-J_{2}^{2}+2J_{2}=0\qquad\text{or}\qquad J_{2}(J_{2}-2)(J_{2}-1)(J_{2}+1)=0,

(7.47)

which implies that the eigenvalues of
J_{2}
are
0,1,-1,+2
respectively for the second line in
eq.

7.33
. We are able to identify
(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E){}_{9}=\Lambda^{-}\otimes E
. To obtain the parametrisation that we will use through this section we split the
GL(4)
orbit partially into its irreducible parts,

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}\rightarrow h_{(\mu\nu)}+\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}h^{i}+\overline{\Sigma}^{i}_{\mu\nu}\bar{h}^{i}.

(7.48)

Where we have used
h_{[\mu\nu]}\in\Lambda^{2}=\Lambda^{+}\oplus\Lambda^{-}
to separate, the antisymmetric part into its self-dual and anti-self-dual components, each of these are decomposed into a basis using
\Sigma^{i}
and
\overline{\Sigma}^{i}
. Substituting this into the
GL(4)
actions, and making use of the quaternion algebra, we find

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}=2h_{[\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\nu]}\rightarrow 2h_{[\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\nu]}+2\epsilon^{ijk}h^{k}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}+2\bar{h}^{j}\overline{\Sigma}^{j}_{[\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\nu]}.

(7.49)

The last term in the above is zero as
\overline{\Sigma}^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha\nu}\in\rm Sym^{2}(\Lambda^{1})
is a basis for symmetric tracefree tensors. Next we need to check that no components of
h_{(\mu\nu)}
are in the kernel of the above parametrisation. To check this we introduce a basis
e^{0}_{\mu},e^{i}_{\mu}\in\Lambda^{1}
and decompose the following objects in this basis,

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=h_{00}e^{0}_{\mu}e^{0}_{\nu}+h_{0i}(e^{0}_{\mu}e^{i}_{\nu}+e^{0}_{\nu}e^{i}_{\mu})+h_{ij}e^{i}_{\mu}e^{j}_{\nu}

(7.50)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=ie^{0}_{\mu}e^{i}_{\nu}-ie^{0}_{\nu}e^{i}_{\mu}-\epsilon^{ijk}e^{j}_{\mu}e^{k}_{\nu}

(7.51)

where
h_{ij}=h_{ji}
. Substituting this into the definitions of
\sigma^{i}
we find

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}=\left(i\delta^{ij}h_{00}-\epsilon^{ijk}(2ih^{k}+h^{k}_{0})-ih^{ij}\right)e^{0}_{[\mu}e^{j}_{\nu]}+\left(h^{ij}-\delta^{ij}h^{kk}-\epsilon^{ijk}(ih^{k}_{0}+2h^{k})\right)\epsilon^{jlm}e^{l}_{\mu}e^{m}_{\nu}.

(7.52)

For
\sigma^{i}=0
we find that
h_{\mu\nu}=0
and
h^{i}=0
, therefore the kernel is trivial. The parametrisation of
S
that we will use throughout this chapter is then

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}=2\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}h^{k}+2h_{[\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\nu]}\in S

(7.53)

here
h_{\mu\nu}
is a symmetric tensor and
h^{i}
is an internal vector. Another notation that we will frequently use below is to represent objects in
S
as the following two lists

\displaystyle\left(h^{i},\ h_{\mu\nu}\right)\in

\displaystyle E\oplus\rm Sym^{2}(\Lambda^{1})

(7.54)

\displaystyle\left(h,\ h^{i},\ \tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}\right)\in

\displaystyle C^{\infty}\oplus E\oplus\rm Sym^{2}_{0}(\Lambda^{1})

(7.55)

where
\rm Sym^{2}_{0}(\Lambda^{1})
denotes the tracefree part and
h_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}h+\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}
. So, the second representation splits the trace and the tracefree parts.

7.5
Proof of ellipticity

We are now equipped to study the complex (
7.20
) in more detail. We prove
theorem

7.1
by showing that the sequence of symbols

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\otimes\Lambda^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma(d_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma(d_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma(d_{3})}

(7.56)

is exact, which means that Plebański complex is an elliptic complex of differential operators.

7.5.1
Proof of the
theorem

7.1

We consider the symbols of all the differential operators. Symbols are obtained by replacing the operators of partial derivative
\partial_{\mu}
with a factor of an arbitrary 1-form, which we denote by
k_{\mu}
. The symbol of the operator in the first arrow (
7.18
) is

\displaystyle\xi^{\mu}\to k_{[\mu}\xi^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\alpha|\nu]}.

(7.57)

To see that this has zero kernel we use the fact that if two 1-forms wedge to zero then they necessarily point in the same direction. The kernel of the above map is then

\displaystyle\xi^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha\nu}=k_{\nu}\phi^{i}

(7.58)

where
\phi^{i}
is an arbitrary internal vector. We would like to show that
\phi^{i}
must vanish.
We can solve this to find
\xi_{\mu}
by contracting with
\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}
,

\displaystyle\xi_{\mu}=\frac{1}{3}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha\nu}\xi^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{3}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}k_{\nu}\phi^{i}.

(7.59)

Substituting this
\xi_{\mu}
back into the left-hand side of (
7.58
) and using the algebra of
\Sigma^{i}
’s we find

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\Sigma^{j\alpha\nu}k_{\nu}\phi^{j}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha\mu}=\frac{1}{3}k_{\mu}\phi^{i}+\frac{1}{3}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}k_{\nu}\phi^{k}.

(7.60)

Comparing with the right-hand side of (
7.58
) we find

\displaystyle 2k_{\mu}\phi^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}k_{\nu}\phi^{k}.

(7.61)

Contracting the above with
k^{\mu}
we find

\displaystyle k^{2}\phi^{i}=0.

(7.62)

As
k^{2}=k^{\mu}k_{\mu}\neq 0
then this requires that
\phi^{i}=0
.
Which implies that kernel of the first map (
7.18
) is trivial, and
shows the exactness of the first arrow in (
7.56
).

The symbol of the second arrow (
7.17
) is

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}\to\frac{1}{2}J_{1}^{-1}(\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}k_{\alpha}\sigma^{i}_{\beta\gamma}).

(7.63)

We know that
J_{1}^{-1}
is invertible, so we can safely ignore it and understand the kernel of

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}\to\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}k_{\alpha}\sigma^{i}_{\beta\gamma}

(7.64)

Using the same linear algebra fact as before we know that
k_{[\alpha}\sigma^{i}_{\beta\gamma]}
is zero when
\sigma^{i}_{\beta\gamma}=k_{[\beta}b^{i}_{\gamma]}
for any
b^{i}_{\mu}\in E\otimes\Lambda^{1}
. This may make it seem that the kernel of the second map is all of
E\otimes\Lambda^{1}
. However, in general
k\wedge b^{i}\in E\otimes\Lambda^{2}
, which is bigger than
S
. Let us determine one-forms
b^{i}
such that
k\wedge b^{i}\in S
.
A 2-form from
E\otimes\Lambda^{2}
belongs to
S
if and only if

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\langle j|\mu\nu}\sigma^{i\rangle}_{\mu\nu}=\Sigma^{\langle j|\mu\nu}k_{\mu}b^{|i\rangle}_{\nu}=0.

(7.65)

To find solutions of this equation, we decompose
b^{i}
into its irreducible parts

\displaystyle b^{i}_{\mu}=\xi^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha\mu}+(b_{8}){}^{i}_{\mu}

(7.66)

where
\xi^{\mu}
is the 4-vector part and
b^{i}_{8}\in(E\otimes\Lambda^{1}){}_{8}
.
Using the algebra of
\Sigma^{i}
’s it is easy to see that the 4-vector part satisfies (
7.65
) automatically. Let us consider the
b^{i}_{8}
part. To analyse this further we can choose a basis for
\Sigma^{i}
,

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{k_{\mu}}{|k|}e^{i}_{\nu}-\frac{k_{\nu}}{|k|}e^{i}_{\mu}-\epsilon^{ijk}e^{j}_{\mu}e^{k}_{\nu}

(7.67)

where
e^{i}_{\mu}
are orthonormal and orthogonal to
k_{\mu}
, i.e.
e^{i}_{\mu}k^{\mu}=0
and
e^{i}_{\mu}e^{j\mu}=\delta^{ij}
.
This allows the decomposition

\displaystyle(b_{8}){}^{i}_{\mu}=k_{\mu}b^{i}+b^{ij}e^{j}_{\mu}.

(7.68)

The eigenvalue of
b_{8}^{i}
under the map
J_{1}
is
-1
, which restricts the components of
b^{ij}
. Acting with
J_{1}
gives

\displaystyle J_{1}(b_{8}){}^{i}_{\mu}=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\left(\frac{k_{\mu}}{|k|}e^{j\nu}-\frac{k^{\nu}}{|k|}e^{j}_{\mu}-\epsilon^{jmn}e^{m}_{\mu}e^{n\nu}\right)(k_{\nu}b^{k}+b^{kl}e^{l}_{\nu})

(7.69)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\mu}}{|k|}\left(\epsilon^{ijk}b^{kj}\right)+\left(-b^{ji}+\delta^{ij}b^{kk}-|k|\epsilon^{ijk}b^{k}\right)e^{j}_{\mu}.

(7.70)

Therefore,
J_{1}(b_{8}){}^{i}=-b^{i}_{8}
implies

\displaystyle b^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}\frac{b^{jk}}{|k|}\quad\textrm{and}\quad b^{ij}=b^{ji}-\delta^{ij}b^{kk}+|k|\epsilon^{ijk}b^{k}.

(7.71)

The second equality implies
b^{ii}=0
. We have thus parameterised
b^{i}_{8}
by a tracefree matrix
b^{ij}
(and is not necessarily symmetric). We can now determine which part of
b^{i}_{8}
is actually contributing to
k\wedge b^{i}_{8}\in S
. It is clear that the
k_{\mu}b^{i}
part does not survive in
k\wedge b^{i}_{8}
, so let us consider the other term. We want to determine if any part of
k\wedge b^{ij}e^{j}
is in
S
. Substituting this into (
7.65
) we find

\displaystyle\left(\frac{k^{\mu}}{|k|}e^{\langle i|\nu}-\frac{k^{\nu}}{|k|}e^{\langle i|\mu}-\epsilon^{\langle i|lm}e^{l\mu}e^{m\nu}\right)k_{\mu}b^{|j\rangle n}e^{n}_{\nu}=|k|b^{\langle ij\rangle}.

(7.72)

This must vanish in order for
k\wedge b^{i}_{8}\in S
. This means that
k\wedge b^{i}_{8}
is either zero or not in
S
. It thus cannot contribute to the non-trivial kernel of (
7.63
). This means that
b^{i}_{\mu}=\xi^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha\mu}
generates all the non-trivial kernel, where elements in the kernel are given by
\sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}=k_{[\mu|}\xi^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha|\nu]}
and its dimension is
4
.
It is also clear then that the image of
\sigma(d_{1})
and the kernel of
\sigma(d_{2})
exactly coincide.
As the dimension of the kernel of
\sigma(d_{2})
is
4
and the dimension of the domain is
13
then the image will have dimension
13-4=9
.

We now look at the final map

\displaystyle a^{i}_{\mu}\to\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}k_{\mu}a^{k}_{\nu}=k^{\mu}J_{1}(a){}^{i}_{\mu}.

(7.73)

To find its kernel we decompose
a^{i}_{\mu}

\displaystyle a^{i}_{\mu}=k_{\mu}a^{i}+a^{ij}e^{j}_{\mu}

(7.74)

where
a^{i}
and
a^{ij}
are an arbitrary internal vector and matrix.
Computing the action of
J_{1}
is the same computation as (
7.69
), which gives

\displaystyle J_{1}(a){}^{i}_{\mu}=\frac{k_{\mu}}{|k|}(-\epsilon^{ijk}a^{jk})+\left(\delta^{ij}a^{kk}-a^{ji}-\epsilon^{ijk}|k|a^{k}\right)e^{j}_{\mu}.

(7.75)

This implies

\displaystyle k^{\mu}J_{1}(a){}^{i}_{\mu}=-|k|\epsilon^{ijk}a^{jk}.

(7.76)

This shows that the kernel of
\sigma(d_{3})
is all of
a^{i}_{\mu}
apart from
a^{[ij]}
. So, the kernel is
12-3=9
dimensional, and the image is
E
. We can also compute the image of
\sigma(d_{2})
explicitly, by using the form (
7.96
) of this operator. In terms of symbols we have

\displaystyle(h^{i},h_{\mu\nu})\to-\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}k_{\alpha}h_{\mu\beta}+2k_{\mu}h^{i}.

(7.77)

We now decompose
h_{\mu\nu}
into its longitudinal and transverse parts

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}=\psi k_{\mu}k_{\nu}+k_{(\mu}X_{\nu)}+\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}.

(7.78)

Here both
X_{\mu},\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}
are transverse
k^{\mu}X_{\mu}=0,k^{\mu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}=0
. We now want to compute the image of various part of the map (
7.77
) and check that it coincides with the described above kernel of
\sigma(d_{3})
. The last term in (
7.77
) is precisely the first term in (
7.74
). The image of the first term in (
7.78
) is trivial. The image of the second term is

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}k_{\alpha}X_{\beta}k_{\mu},

(7.79)

which is also of the form of the first term in (
7.74
). To compute the image of the last term in (
7.78
) we use the explicit form of
\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}
. We have

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{k^{\alpha}}{|k|}e^{i\beta}-\frac{k^{\beta}}{|k|}e^{i\alpha}-\epsilon^{ijk}e^{j\alpha}e^{k\beta}\right)k_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\beta}=-|k|e^{i\beta}\tilde{h}_{\mu\beta}.

(7.80)

We can further decompose
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}
into the basis of vectors
e^{i}_{\mu}

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}=h^{ij}e^{i}_{\mu}e^{j}_{\nu},

(7.81)

where
h^{ij}=h^{(ij)}
is a symmetric
3\times 3
matrix. This gives for the right-hand-side of (
7.80
)

\displaystyle-|k|h^{ij}e^{j}_{\mu}.

(7.82)

This is precisely the part of the second term in (
7.74
) that is in the kernel of
\sigma(d_{3})
. Thus, we have explicitly checked that the image of
\sigma(d_{2})
coincides with the kernel of
\sigma(d_{3})
. This finishes the proof of ellipticity of the complex.

7.6
Characterisation of Einstein equations

The purpose of this section is to show that there exists a choice of the inner products on
E\otimes\Lambda^{1}
and
S
such that the linearisation of the Einstein equations
\delta R_{\mu\nu}=0
becomes the statement
d_{2}^{*}d_{2}\sigma^{i}=0
.

7.6.1
Linearisation of the Einstein condition

As we have reviewed in the Introduction, in Plebański formalism the Einstein condition becomes the statement (
7.15
). Its linearisation around
A^{i}=0
is the statement

\displaystyle da^{i}=\psi^{ij}\Sigma^{j},

(7.83)

with again
\psi^{ij}
being an arbitrary symmetric tracefree
3\times 3
matrix. From the discussion in
section

7.4.3
we know that the right-hand side of (
7.83
) is an arbitrary vector in
(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E){}_{5}\subset\Lambda^{2}\otimes E
. This means that (
7.83
) can be stated as
(da^{i})\Big|_{1+3+9}=0
, i.e. as the statement that the projection of the exterior derivative of
a^{i}
on the representations of dimensions
1,3,9
in
\Lambda^{2}\otimes E
vanishes. All these components can be recovered by computing

\displaystyle(\partial_{\mu}a^{i}_{\alpha}-\partial_{\alpha}a^{i}_{\mu})\Sigma^{i\alpha}{}_{\nu}\in\Lambda^{1}\otimes\Lambda^{1}.

(7.84)

The symmetric part of this tensor computes the
(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E){}_{1+9}
parts, and the antisymmetric part only contains the
\Lambda^{2}_{+}=(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E){}_{3}
part. So, linearised Einstein equations can be written as the statement that (
7.84
) vanishes. Projecting (
7.84
) onto
\Sigma^{k\mu\nu}
it is easy to see that the
(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E){}_{3}
part of this condition reads

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k}=0

(7.85)

The symmetric part of (
7.84
), on the other hand, gives

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}(\partial_{\nu)}a^{i}_{\alpha}-\partial_{\alpha}a^{i}_{\nu)})=0.

(7.86)

As we already discussed, the condition (
7.85
) is an automatic consequence of the equation (
7.16
), and so holds automatically. The equations (
7.86
) are the ten linearised Einstein equations.

7.6.2
The adjoint of
d_{2}

We now compute the adjoint of
d_{2}
given by (
7.17
), with the inner product

\displaystyle\langle a,a\rangle=\int\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a_{\mu}^{j}a_{\nu}^{k}=-\int(a,J_{1}(a)),

(7.87)

where
J_{1}
is the operator (
7.38
) and
(a,b)=g^{\mu\nu}a_{\mu}^{i}b_{\nu}^{i}
is the metric pairing of two objects in
\Lambda^{1}\otimes E
. We have

\displaystyle\langle a,d_{2}\sigma\rangle=

\displaystyle\langle a,\frac{1}{2}J_{1}^{-1}(\star d\sigma)\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}\int a_{\mu}^{i}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\nu}\sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=-\frac{1}{2}\int\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}a_{\sigma}^{i}

(7.88)

Here we have used that
J_{1}^{-1}J_{1}=\mathbb{I}
. We already see that the adjoint of
d_{2}
is related to the
da^{i}
. Given that
\sigma^{i}\in(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E){}_{1+3+9}
, the operator
d_{2}^{*}
will have components in all these spaces. It is clear that these will be multiples of the left-hand sides in (
7.85
) and (
7.86
). This proves
theorem

7.2
. We will present a more detailed computation of
d_{2}^{*}
below, after the form of the inner product on
S
is fixed.

7.7
The Plebański complex case

We will now describe the Plebański complex in more details, including all the adjoint maps. We will be using the parametrisation (
7.53
) of the space
S
, so that
S
is the sum of the following irreducible components

\displaystyle S=\Lambda^{0}\oplus E\oplus{\rm Sym}_{0}^{2}(\Lambda^{1}),

(7.89)

where
{\rm Sym}_{0}^{2}(\Lambda^{1})
denotes the tracefree part. Now we establish the form of the operators
d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}
in this parametrisation.

7.7.1
Plebański complex in detail

In the parametrisation (
7.53
) of
S
the first map of the Plebański complex takes the following form

\displaystyle d_{1}\xi=(\partial^{\mu}\xi_{\mu},\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu},\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+\partial_{\nu}\xi_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\alpha}\xi_{\alpha})).

(7.90)

Here the notation is that we list the irreducible components in
S
in brackets, in the order they appear in (
7.89
). The second operator is given by

\displaystyle d_{2}\sigma=\frac{1}{2}J_{1}^{-1}(\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\partial_{\alpha}\sigma^{i}_{\beta\gamma}).

(7.91)

Let us compute this in the parametrisation (
7.53
). We have

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\partial_{\alpha}\sigma^{i}_{\beta\gamma}=2(\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\mu\beta}-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}h_{\alpha\beta}+\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h)+4\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}.

(7.92)

Using
J_{1}^{-1}=(1/2)(J_{1}-\mathbb{I})
we have

\displaystyle d_{2}\sigma=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}(\Sigma^{k\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\nu\beta}-\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}h_{\alpha\beta}+\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h)-\frac{1}{2}(\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\mu\beta}-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}h_{\alpha\beta}+\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h)

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{m}-\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}.

The first term here is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\Sigma^{k\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}h_{\nu\sigma}=\frac{1}{2}(-g^{\mu\rho}\Sigma^{i\nu\sigma}+g^{\nu\rho}\Sigma^{i\mu\sigma}+g^{\mu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\nu\rho}-g^{\nu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\mu\rho})\partial_{\rho}h_{\nu\sigma}=

(7.93)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\sigma}\partial^{\nu}h_{\nu\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\nu\rho}\partial_{\rho}h_{\nu\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\rho}\partial_{\rho}h.

The second term

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}h_{\alpha\beta}=-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}h_{\alpha\beta}.

(7.94)

The third is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h=\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h.

(7.95)

The first term in the second line is

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{m}=(\delta^{il}\delta^{jm}-\delta^{im}\delta^{jl})(-\delta^{jl}\delta_{\mu}^{\alpha}+\epsilon^{jls}\Sigma^{s}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha})\partial_{\alpha}h^{m}=2\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}.

Combining everything we observe multiple cancellations, with the final result being

\displaystyle d_{2}\sigma=-\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\mu\beta}+2\partial_{\mu}h^{i}.

(7.96)

Let us now exhibit the tracefree part and the trace of
h_{\mu\nu}
explicitly. We parametrise

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}=\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}h.

(7.97)

In this parametrisation

\displaystyle d_{2}\sigma=\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+2\partial_{\mu}h^{i}-\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\beta}.

(7.98)

The last operator of the complex is

\displaystyle d_{3}a=\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k}.

(7.99)

7.7.2
Inner products

We will now write the general form of the inner products in all the spaces. We will be identifying
TM\sim\Lambda^{1}
using the metric. The inner products on
\Lambda^{1}
and
E
are unique (up to an overall multiple which we choose to be unity). The spaces
S,E\otimes\Lambda^{1}
each contains several irreducible components, and the inner product is no longer unique. We will introduce arbitrary (at this stage) constants to parametrise the arising inner products. The inner product in
\Lambda^{1}
is

\displaystyle\langle\xi,\xi\rangle=\int_{M}(\xi_{\mu})^{2}

(7.100)

where
(\xi_{\mu})^{2}=\xi^{\mu}\xi_{\mu}
denotes the metric contraction. The inner product in
S
an arbitrary linear combination of inner products on all 3 irreducible pieces composing
S

\displaystyle\langle\sigma,\sigma\rangle=\int_{M}\beta_{1}h^{2}+\beta_{2}(h^{i})^{2}+\beta_{3}(\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})^{2}.

(7.101)

The inner product in
E\otimes\Lambda^{1}
is similarly a linear combination of two terms

\displaystyle\langle a,a\rangle=\int_{M}\gamma_{1}(a_{\mu}^{i})^{2}+\gamma_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a^{j}_{\mu}a^{k}_{\nu}.

(7.102)

The inner product in
E
is

\displaystyle\langle\chi,\chi\rangle=\int_{M}(\chi^{i})^{2}.

(7.103)

7.7.3
General form of the adjoints

We will first write down the most general form that the adjoint operators can take (based on the relevant representation theory). For the adjoint of the first operator we have

\displaystyle d_{1}^{*}\sigma=a_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\mu}h+a_{2}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h^{i}+a_{3}^{\prime}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}.

(7.104)

For the second operator we have

\displaystyle d_{2}^{*}a=(b_{1}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{i},\ b_{2}^{\prime}\partial^{\mu}a_{\mu}^{i}+b_{3}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k},\ 2b_{4}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu\rangle}a_{\alpha}^{i}+2b_{5}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}a_{\nu\rangle}^{i}).

(7.105)

The third adjoint has the form

\displaystyle d_{3}^{*}\chi=c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{k}.

(7.106)

7.7.4
Plebański case adjoints

We now compute the Plebański complex adjoints. For
\langle\sigma,d_{1}\xi\rangle=\langle d_{1}^{*}\sigma,\xi\rangle
we get

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{1}h\partial^{\mu}\xi_{\mu}+\frac{\beta_{2}}{4}h^{i}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+\beta_{3}\tilde{h}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}=

(7.107)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\xi^{\mu}(a_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\mu}h+a_{2}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h^{i}+a_{3}^{\prime}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}).

So, we have

\displaystyle a_{1}^{\prime}=-\beta_{1},\qquad a_{2}^{\prime}=\frac{\beta_{2}}{4},\qquad a_{3}^{\prime}=-\beta_{3}.

(7.108)

For
\langle a,d_{2}\sigma\rangle=\langle d_{2}^{*}a,\sigma\rangle
we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\gamma_{1}a^{i\mu}(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+2\partial_{\mu}h^{i}-\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\beta})

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+\gamma_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a^{j}_{\mu}(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+2\partial_{\nu}h^{k}-\Sigma^{k\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\nu\beta})

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\beta_{1}b_{1}^{\prime}h\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{i}+\beta_{2}h^{i}(b_{2}^{\prime}\partial^{\mu}a_{\mu}^{i}

\displaystyle\vskip 100.0pt+b_{3}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k})+\beta_{3}\tilde{h}^{\mu\nu}(2b_{4}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu)}a_{\alpha}^{i}+2b_{5}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}a_{\nu)}^{i}).

The last term in the first line gives

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\Sigma^{k\rho\sigma}a^{j}_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}

\displaystyle=(g^{\mu\rho}\Sigma^{i\nu\sigma}-g^{\nu\rho}\Sigma^{i\mu\sigma}-g^{\mu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\nu\rho}+g^{\nu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\mu\rho})a^{i}_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}

(7.109)

\displaystyle=-\Sigma^{i\mu\sigma}a^{i}_{\mu}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}-\Sigma^{i\nu\rho}a^{i\mu}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\mu},

where we used the fact that
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}
is traceless. This means we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\frac{\gamma_{1}-2\gamma_{2}}{4}\Sigma^{i\mu\rho}a^{i}_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}h+2\gamma_{1}a^{i\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+2\gamma_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a^{j}_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}h^{k}

\displaystyle-(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2})a^{i\mu}\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\beta}+\gamma_{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\sigma}a^{i}_{\mu}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\beta_{1}b_{1}^{\prime}h\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{i}+\beta_{2}h^{i}(b_{2}^{\prime}\partial^{\mu}a_{\mu}^{i}+b_{3}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k})

\displaystyle+\beta_{3}\tilde{h}^{\mu\nu}(2b_{4}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu)}a_{\alpha}^{i}+2b_{5}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}a_{\nu)}^{i}).

This gives

\displaystyle b_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{\gamma_{1}-2\gamma_{2}}{4\beta_{1}},\quad b_{2}^{\prime}=-\frac{2\gamma_{1}}{\beta_{2}},\quad b_{3}^{\prime}=\frac{2\gamma_{2}}{\beta_{2}},\quad b_{4}^{\prime}=\frac{\gamma_{2}}{2\beta_{3}},\quad b_{5}^{\prime}=-\frac{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}}{2\beta_{3}}.

(7.110)

For
\langle\chi,d_{3}a\rangle=\langle d_{3}^{*}\chi,a\rangle
we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\chi^{i}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k}

(7.111)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\gamma_{1}a^{i\mu}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{k})+\gamma_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a_{\mu}^{j}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{m}),

or

\displaystyle\int_{M}\chi^{i}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k}

(7.112)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}(\gamma_{1}c_{1}^{\prime}-2\gamma_{2}c_{2}^{\prime})a^{i\mu}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+(\gamma_{1}c_{2}^{\prime}-\gamma_{2}c_{1}^{\prime}-\gamma_{2}c_{2}^{\prime})\epsilon^{ijk}a^{i\mu}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{k},

and so

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}c_{1}^{\prime}-2\gamma_{2}c_{2}^{\prime}=0,\qquad 1=-\gamma_{1}c_{2}^{\prime}+\gamma_{2}c_{1}^{\prime}+\gamma_{2}c_{2}^{\prime}.

(7.113)

This gives

\displaystyle c_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{2\gamma_{2}}{\gamma_{1}(\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1})+2\gamma_{2}^{2}},\quad c_{2}^{\prime}=\frac{\gamma_{1}}{\gamma_{1}(\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1})+2\gamma_{2}^{2}}.

(7.114)

We have explicitly checked that the compositions of the adjoint operators (with these coefficients) vanish, as they should.

7.7.5
Imposing the
\Delta
condition

We now form the operators
D
and
D^{*}
, see (
7.179
) and (
7.180
). Requiring
D^{*}D
to be a multiple of the
\Delta=\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
operator, we are led to equations (
7.190
). Substituting the values of all the coefficients as they are (
7.151
) in the Plebański complex case, and expressing the coefficients of the adjoint operators via those in the inner products, we can view the equations (
7.190
) as those on the inner product coefficients. The solution is as follows

\displaystyle\langle\sigma,\sigma\rangle=\beta\int_{M}\frac{1}{4}h^{2}+8(h^{i})^{2}+(\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})^{2},\qquad\langle a,a\rangle=\beta^{2}\int_{M}(a_{\mu}^{i})^{2}.

(7.115)

It also makes sense to choose
\beta=1
. We note that the relative coefficient in the
h^{2},(\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})^{2}
terms is the natural one. We can pass to treating
h_{\mu\nu}
as an object that contains the trace part. Indeed, using (
7.97
) we have

\displaystyle(h_{\mu\nu})^{2}=(\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})^{2}+\frac{1}{4}h^{2},

(7.116)

which is precisely what we have in the above inner product. The inner products that appear in this construction are then

\displaystyle\langle\xi,\xi\rangle=\int_{M}(\xi_{\mu})^{2},\quad\langle\sigma,\sigma\rangle=\int_{M}8(h^{i})^{2}+(h_{\mu\nu})^{2},\quad\langle a,a\rangle=\int_{M}(a_{\mu}^{i})^{2},\quad\langle\xi,\xi\rangle=\int_{M}(\xi^{i})^{2}.

(7.117)

7.7.6
Inner product on
S
in terms of
\sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}

It is interesting to rewrite the inner product on
S
in (
7.117
) in terms of the object
\sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\in S
. Using the parametrisation (
7.53
) we have

\displaystyle(\sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu})^{2}=2(h_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha\nu}-h_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha\mu})h^{\mu\beta}\Sigma^{i}_{\beta}{}^{\nu}+8h_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha\nu}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\chi^{k}+4\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}\chi^{k}\epsilon^{imn}\Sigma^{m\mu\nu}\chi^{n}=

\displaystyle 2(3(h_{\mu\nu})^{2}-h_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}h^{\mu\beta}(g_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{\mu}{}^{\nu}-\delta_{\alpha}{}^{\nu}g_{\mu\beta}+\epsilon_{\alpha\mu\beta}{}^{\nu}))+32(\chi^{i})^{2}=

\displaystyle 2(2(h_{\mu\nu})^{2}+h^{2})+32(\chi^{i})^{2}.

In the parametrisation (
7.97
) this becomes

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}(\sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu})^{2}=(\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})^{2}+\frac{3}{4}h^{2}+8(\chi^{i})^{2}.

(7.118)

We also have

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}=2\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}h_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha\nu}=6h.

(7.119)

This means that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}h^{2}+8(h^{i})^{2}+(\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})^{2}=\frac{1}{4}(\sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu})^{2}-\frac{1}{72}(\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu})^{2}.

(7.120)

7.7.7
Explicit form of the adjoints

We now write the Plebański complex operators without splitting the tracefree and the trace parts of
h_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle d_{1}\xi=(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu},\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)})\in(E,S^{2}T^{*}M),

(7.121)

\displaystyle d_{2}\sigma=-\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\mu\beta}+2\partial_{\mu}h^{i},

\displaystyle d_{3}a=\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k}.

The adjoints, in the same notation are given by

\displaystyle d_{1}^{*}\sigma=2\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h^{i}-\partial^{\nu}h_{\mu\nu},

(7.122)

\displaystyle d_{2}^{*}a=(-\frac{1}{4}\partial^{\mu}a_{\mu}^{i},-\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}a_{\nu)}^{i})\in(E,S^{2}T^{*}M),

\displaystyle d_{3}^{*}\chi=-\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{k}.

As a check, we recompute the operators that appear in
D^{*}D
. The operator
(d_{1}d_{1}^{*}+d_{2}^{*}d_{2})\sigma
becomes, in the
h^{i}
component

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(2\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{j}-\partial^{\alpha}h_{\nu\alpha})-\frac{1}{4}\partial^{\mu}(-\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\mu\beta}+2\partial_{\mu}h^{i})=

(7.123)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}-\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\alpha}h_{\nu\alpha}+\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\mu\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}=-\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}.

In the
h_{\mu\nu}
component we get

\displaystyle\partial_{(\mu}(2\Sigma^{i}_{\nu)}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}-\partial^{\alpha}h_{\nu)\alpha})-\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}(-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}h_{\nu)\sigma}+2\partial_{\nu)}h^{i})=

(7.124)

\displaystyle 2\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu)}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}-\partial_{(\mu}\partial^{\alpha}h_{\nu)\alpha}+(\delta_{(\mu}{}^{\rho}g^{\alpha\sigma}-\delta_{(\mu}{}^{\sigma}g^{\alpha\rho})\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\rho}h_{\nu)\sigma}-2\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\nu)}h^{i}=-\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\mu\nu}.

For the operator
d_{3}d_{3}^{*}\chi
we have

\displaystyle d_{3}d_{3}^{*}\chi=\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(-\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{m})=-\epsilon^{ijk}\epsilon^{klm}(-\delta^{jl}g^{\mu\alpha})\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{m}=-2\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i},

(7.125)

which confirms that
D^{*}D\sim\Delta
.

7.7.8
Linearised Einstein equations

As we have seen previously, see (
7.86
), the linearisation of the Einstein equations is

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}(\partial_{\nu)}a_{\alpha}^{i}-\partial_{\alpha}a_{\nu)}^{i})=0.

(7.126)

We would like to realise the operator that appears in this equation as
d_{2}^{*}
, but this is not the operator
d_{2}^{*}
that arises if the inner products are chosen as in (
7.117
) to satisfy
D^{*}D\sim\Delta
condition. We now change the inner product to obtain the desired
d_{2}^{*}
, but this will then be in conflict with the
D^{*}D\sim\Delta
condition. Still, we develop this option as the one that is relevant for the Einstein equations. The construction of an elliptic operator
\tilde{D}
with
\tilde{D}^{*}\tilde{D}\sim\Delta
will occupy us in the next sections.

7.7.9
Plebański case different inner product

We now consider the case of Plebański complex with the inner product
\gamma_{1}=0
. In this case
b_{3}=b_{4}=0
, and it is not hard to see that this gives
b_{4}^{\prime}+b_{5}^{\prime}=0,b_{2}^{\prime}=0
. The other coefficients are

\displaystyle b_{1}^{\prime}=-\frac{\gamma_{2}}{2\beta_{1}},\quad b_{3}^{\prime}=\frac{2\gamma_{2}}{\beta_{2}},\quad b_{4}^{\prime}=-b_{5}^{\prime}=\frac{\gamma_{2}}{2\beta_{3}}

(7.127)

so that

\displaystyle d_{2}^{*}a=\gamma_{2}(-\frac{1}{2\beta_{1}}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{i},\frac{2}{\beta_{2}}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k},\frac{1}{\beta_{3}}(\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu\rangle}a_{\alpha}^{i}-\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}a_{\nu\rangle}^{i})).

Note that the desired operator (
7.126
) appears here. We now choose

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}=1,\qquad\beta_{1}=\frac{1}{4},\qquad\beta_{2}=8,\qquad\beta_{3}=1,

(7.128)

which corresponds to the following inner products

\displaystyle\langle\xi,\xi\rangle=\int_{M}(\xi_{\mu})^{2},\quad\langle\sigma,\sigma\rangle=\int_{M}\frac{1}{4}h^{2}+8(h^{i})^{2}+(\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})^{2},

(7.129)

\displaystyle\langle a,a\rangle=\int_{M}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a_{\mu}^{j}a_{\nu}^{k},\quad\langle\xi,\xi\rangle=\int_{M}(\xi^{i})^{2}.

These are the same inner products in all the spaces as in (
7.117
), apart from that in
E\otimes\Lambda^{1}
, where we instead take the inner product that contains the
J_{1}
operator. This makes the inner product on
E\otimes\Lambda^{1}
not positive-definite. An important consequence of this is that the composition of maps with their adjoints will not be elliptic and therefore also not equal to
\Delta
. With these inner products, the full complex becomes

\displaystyle d_{1}\xi=(\partial^{\mu}\xi_{\mu},\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu},\partial_{\langle\mu}\xi_{\nu\rangle})\in(E,S^{2}T^{*}M),

(7.130)

\displaystyle d_{2}\sigma=\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h+2\partial_{\mu}h^{i}-\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\beta},

\displaystyle d_{3}a=\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k},

and

\displaystyle d_{1}^{*}\sigma=-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\mu}h+2\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h^{i}-\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu},

\displaystyle d_{2}^{*}a=(-2\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{i},\ \frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k},\ \Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu\rangle}a_{\alpha}^{i}-\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}a_{\nu\rangle}^{i}),

\displaystyle d_{3}^{*}\chi=\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}.

We will refer to the operators appearing above as those of the Plebański complex. Here
\langle\rangle
denotes the symmetric tracefree part.

7.7.10
Computation of
d_{2}^{*}d_{2}

As a check, we compute the operator
d_{2}^{*}d_{2}
for the operators of the Plebański complex as detailed above. We will need the result of this computation below. For the trace part we have

\displaystyle-2\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+2\partial_{\nu}h^{i}-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma})=\frac{3}{2}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h-2\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}.

(7.131)

For the vector part we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+2\partial_{\nu}h^{k}-\Sigma^{k\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma})=

(7.132)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}(g^{\mu\rho}\Sigma^{i\nu\sigma}-g^{\nu\rho}\Sigma^{i\mu\sigma}-g^{\mu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\nu\rho}+g^{\nu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\mu\rho})\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}=0.

For the tracefree part we have

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu\rangle}(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}h+2\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\sigma})-\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu\rangle}{}^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}h+2\partial_{\nu\rangle}h^{i}-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\rangle\sigma})=

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{4}\partial_{\langle\mu}\partial_{\nu\rangle}h+2\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu\rangle}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}+\partial_{\langle\mu}\partial^{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\rangle\alpha}+\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\langle\mu}\partial_{\nu\rangle}h-2\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu\rangle}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}+\partial_{\langle\mu}\partial^{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\rangle\alpha}-\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\langle\mu}\partial_{\nu\rangle}h+2\partial_{\langle\mu}\partial^{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\rangle\alpha}-\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}.

Collecting the results we have

\displaystyle d_{2}^{*}d_{2}\sigma=(\frac{3}{2}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h-2\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu},0,-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\langle\mu}\partial_{\nu\rangle}h+2\partial_{\langle\mu}\partial^{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\rangle\alpha}-\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}).

(7.133)

7.8
General complex

We now change the game, and start by defining a general set of differential operators between the same spaces as appear in the Plebański complex. We will write down the operators most general as allowed by the relevant representation theory. The representation theory exercise is straightforward, for example the first-order differential maps from
\Lambda^{1}
to
S
can be computed by first associating the spaces and operators with

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}=S_{+}\otimes S_{-},\quad\Lambda^{1}=S_{+}\otimes S_{-},\quad S=C^{\infty}\oplus S_{+}^{2}\oplus S_{-}^{2}\oplus S_{+}^{2}\otimes S_{-}^{2}.

(7.134)

Then the first-order differential operators on
\xi\in\Lambda^{1}
are decomposed into

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}

\displaystyle=S_{+}\otimes S_{-}\otimes S_{+}\otimes S_{-}=C^{\infty}\oplus S_{+}^{2}\oplus S_{-}^{2}\oplus S_{+}^{2}\otimes S^{2}_{-}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\rho}\xi_{\rho}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\xi_{\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\overline{\Sigma}^{i}_{\mu\nu}\overline{\Sigma}^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\xi_{\sigma}+\partial_{\langle\mu}\xi_{\nu\rangle}.

(7.135)

Where
\partial_{\langle\mu}\xi_{\nu\rangle}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+\partial_{\nu}\xi_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\rho}\xi_{\rho}\right)
is the symmetric tracefree part. More generally one calculates the different number of copies of one of the spaces in (
7.136
) in the tensor product of another space in (
7.136
) and the space
\Lambda^{1}
. One can then write down the relevant differential operators without difficulty.

7.8.1
Differential operators defined

We now attempt to find the most general complex of the type

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\otimes\Lambda^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}}

(7.136)

The possible maps are dictated by the representation theory. They can be parameterised as

\displaystyle d_{1}\xi=(a_{1}\partial^{\mu}\xi_{\mu},a_{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu},2a_{3}\partial_{\langle\mu}\xi_{\nu\rangle})\in(\Lambda^{0},E,{\rm Sym}_{0}^{2}T^{*}M),

(7.137)

where
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
are arbitrary coefficients and

\displaystyle\partial_{\langle\mu}\xi_{\nu\rangle}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu}\xi_{\mu}-\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\alpha}\xi_{\alpha}

(7.138)

is the symmetric tracefree part.
Here
{\rm Sym}_{0}^{2}T^{*}M
is the space of symmetric tracefree tensors. For the second map we have

\displaystyle d_{2}\sigma=b_{1}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+b_{2}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+b_{3}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}+b_{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+b_{5}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\mu\sigma}\in E\otimes\Lambda^{1}.

(7.139)

Again,
b_{1,2,3,4,5}
are arbitrary coefficients. The last map is

\displaystyle d_{3}a=c_{1}\partial^{\mu}a_{\mu}^{i}+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k}\in E.

(7.140)

7.8.2
Compositions

We want to the above to be a differential complex, so we want the compositions of the arrows to give zero. We have

\displaystyle d_{2}d_{1}\xi=\

\displaystyle b_{1}a_{1}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\xi_{\sigma}+b_{2}a_{2}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\xi_{\sigma}+b_{3}a_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\Sigma^{k\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\xi_{\sigma}

(7.141)

\displaystyle+b_{4}a_{3}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}(\partial_{\rho}\xi_{\sigma}+\partial_{\sigma}\xi_{\rho}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\rho\sigma}\partial^{\alpha}\xi_{\alpha})+b_{5}a_{3}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}(\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\sigma}+\partial_{\sigma}\xi_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\sigma}\partial^{\alpha}\xi_{\alpha}).

Next we use

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}=-g_{\mu\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu\sigma}+g_{\nu\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\sigma}+g_{\mu\sigma}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu\rho}-g_{\nu\sigma}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\rho}

(7.142)

to get

\displaystyle d_{2}d_{1}\xi=\

\displaystyle(b_{1}a_{1}+b_{4}a_{3}-b_{3}a_{2}+\frac{b_{5}a_{3}-b_{4}a_{3}}{2})\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\xi_{\sigma}+(b_{2}a_{2}+b_{5}a_{3}-b_{3}a_{2})\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\xi_{\sigma}

(7.143)

\displaystyle+(b_{4}a_{3}+b_{3}a_{2})\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}\xi_{\rho}.

This gives

\displaystyle b_{1}a_{1}+b_{4}a_{3}-b_{3}a_{2}+\frac{b_{5}a_{3}-b_{4}a_{3}}{2}=0,\qquad b_{2}a_{2}+b_{5}a_{3}-b_{3}a_{2}=0,\qquad b_{4}a_{3}+b_{3}a_{2}=0.

(7.144)

The other composition is as follows

\displaystyle d_{3}d_{2}\sigma=

\displaystyle c_{1}b_{2}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+c_{1}b_{4}\Sigma^{i\mu\rho}\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+c_{1}b_{5}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\partial^{\mu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\sigma}

(7.145)

\displaystyle+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(b_{1}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+b_{2}\partial_{\nu}h^{k}+b_{3}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{m}+b_{4}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+b_{5}\Sigma^{k\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}).

The first two terms in the second line do not contribute. The third term gives

\displaystyle c_{2}b_{3}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{m}=

\displaystyle c_{2}b_{3}(\delta^{il}\delta^{jm}-\delta^{im}\delta^{jl})(-\delta^{jl}g^{\mu\alpha}+\epsilon^{jls}\Sigma^{s\mu\alpha})\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}h^{m}

(7.146)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2c_{2}b_{3}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}.

The fourth term gives

\displaystyle c_{2}b_{4}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}=2c_{2}b_{4}\Sigma^{i\mu\rho}\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}.

(7.147)

The last term in the second line gives

\displaystyle c_{2}b_{5}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\Sigma^{k\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}=c_{2}b_{5}(-g^{\mu\rho}\Sigma^{i\nu\sigma}+g^{\nu\rho}\Sigma^{i\mu\sigma}+g^{\mu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\nu\rho}-g^{\nu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\mu\rho})\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}=0.

So, overall

\displaystyle d_{3}d_{2}\sigma=(c_{1}b_{2}+2c_{2}b_{3})\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+(c_{1}b_{4}+c_{1}b_{5}+2c_{2}b_{4})\Sigma^{i\mu\rho}\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}.

(7.148)

Requiring this to vanish gives

\displaystyle c_{1}b_{2}+2c_{2}b_{3}=0,\qquad c_{1}b_{4}+c_{1}b_{5}+2c_{2}b_{4}=0.

(7.149)

The system of equations (
7.144
), (
7.149
) can be interpreted as that for the constants
b_{1,2,3,4,5}
in terms of
a_{1,2,3},c_{1,2}
. The equations are not all independent, and not all 5
b
’s can be solved for. Taking one of them as a parameter we have

\displaystyle b_{2}=-\frac{2a_{1}b_{1}c_{2}}{a_{2}(c_{1}-c_{2})},\quad b_{3}=\frac{a_{1}b_{1}c_{1}}{a_{2}(c_{1}-c_{2})},\quad b_{4}=-\frac{a_{1}b_{1}c_{1}}{a_{3}(c_{1}-c_{2})},\quad b_{5}=\frac{a_{1}b_{1}(c_{1}+2c_{2})}{a_{3}(c_{1}-c_{2})}.

(7.150)

This means we can make the operator
d_{2}
more explicit

\displaystyle d_{2}\sigma=b(\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h-\frac{2a_{1}c_{2}}{a_{2}(c_{1}-c_{2})}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+\frac{a_{1}c_{1}}{a_{2}(c_{1}-c_{2})}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}

\displaystyle-\frac{a_{1}c_{1}}{a_{3}(c_{1}-c_{2})}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+\frac{a_{1}(c_{1}+2c_{2})}{a_{3}(c_{1}-c_{2})}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\mu\sigma}),

where
b
is an arbitrary parameter.

7.8.3
Plebański case

The Plebański case coefficients are

\displaystyle a_{1}=1,\quad a_{2}=\frac{1}{4},\quad a_{3}=\frac{1}{2},\quad c_{1}=0,\quad c_{2}=1,

(7.151)

\displaystyle b_{1}=\frac{1}{4},\quad b_{2}=2,\quad b_{3}=0,\quad b_{4}=0,\quad b_{5}=-1.

It can be checked that the compositions vanish with these coefficients, as they should.

7.8.4
Matching adjoints with inner products

We now parametrise the adjoint operators as in (
7.104
), (
7.105
), (
7.106
). We will keep the inner product to be general, as in Section 5.2.
Let us relate the constants appearing in the adjoint operators to those in the inner products. We start with
d_{1}^{*}
. We have

\displaystyle\langle\sigma,d_{1}\xi\rangle=\langle d_{1}^{*}\sigma,\xi\rangle,

(7.152)

which gives

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{1}a_{1}h\partial^{\mu}\xi_{\mu}+\beta_{2}a_{2}h^{i}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+2\beta_{3}a_{3}\tilde{h}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}=

(7.153)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\xi^{\mu}(a_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\mu}h+a_{2}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h^{i}+a_{3}^{\prime}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}).

So, we have

\displaystyle\beta_{1}a_{1}=-a_{1}^{\prime},\qquad\beta_{2}a_{2}=a_{2}^{\prime},\qquad 2\beta_{3}a_{3}=-a_{3}^{\prime}.

(7.154)

Let us proceed similarly with
d_{2}^{*}
. We have

\displaystyle\langle a,d_{2}\sigma\rangle=\langle d_{2}^{*}a,\sigma\rangle,

(7.155)

which gives

\displaystyle\int_{M}\gamma_{1}a^{i\mu}(b_{1}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+b_{2}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+b_{3}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}+b_{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+b_{5}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\mu\sigma})

\displaystyle+\gamma_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a^{j}_{\mu}(b_{1}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+b_{2}\partial_{\nu}h^{k}+b_{3}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{m}+b_{4}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+b_{5}\Sigma^{k\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma})=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{1}b_{1}^{\prime}h\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{i}+\beta_{2}h^{i}(b_{2}^{\prime}\partial^{\mu}a_{\mu}^{i}+b_{3}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k})+\beta_{3}\tilde{h}^{\mu\nu}(2b_{4}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu)}a_{\alpha}^{i}+2b_{5}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}a_{\nu)}^{i}).

We first match the
h^{i}
containing terms. Using the algebra of
\Sigma
’s gives

\displaystyle\int_{M}(\gamma_{1}b_{2}-2\gamma_{2}b_{3})a^{i\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+(\gamma_{1}b_{3}-\gamma_{2}b_{2}-\gamma_{2}b_{3})\epsilon^{ijk}a_{\mu}^{i}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}=

(7.156)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{2}h^{i}(b_{2}^{\prime}\partial^{\mu}a_{\mu}^{i}+b_{3}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k}),

which means

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}b_{2}-2\gamma_{2}b_{3}=-\beta_{2}b_{2}^{\prime},\qquad\gamma_{1}b_{3}-\gamma_{2}b_{2}-\gamma_{2}b_{3}=-\beta_{2}b_{3}^{\prime}.

(7.157)

Let us now work out the
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}
terms. The fourth term in the second line is

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a^{j}_{\mu}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}=\epsilon^{ijk}\epsilon^{iks}\Sigma^{s\mu\rho}a^{j}_{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}=-2\Sigma^{i\mu\rho}a^{i}_{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}.

(7.158)

The last term in the second line

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a^{j}_{\mu}\Sigma^{k\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}

\displaystyle=(g^{\mu\rho}\Sigma^{i\nu\sigma}-g^{\nu\rho}\Sigma^{i\mu\sigma}-g^{\mu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\nu\rho}+g^{\nu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\mu\rho})a^{i}_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}

(7.159)

\displaystyle=-\Sigma^{i\mu\sigma}a^{i}_{\mu}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}-\Sigma^{i\nu\rho}a^{i}_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\mu},

where we have used that
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}
is tracefree. This means that we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}(\gamma_{1}b_{4}-2\gamma_{2}b_{4}-\gamma_{2}b_{5})a^{i\mu}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+(\gamma_{1}b_{5}+\gamma_{2}b_{5})\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}a^{i\mu}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\mu\sigma}=

(7.160)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{3}\tilde{h}^{\mu\nu}(2b_{4}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu)}a_{\alpha}^{i}+2b_{5}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}a_{\nu)}^{i}).

This means that

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}b_{4}-2\gamma_{2}b_{4}-\gamma_{2}b_{5}=2\beta_{3}b_{4}^{\prime},\qquad\gamma_{1}b_{5}+\gamma_{2}b_{5}=2\beta_{3}b_{5}^{\prime}.

(7.161)

We now match the
h
terms

\displaystyle\int_{M}\gamma_{1}b_{1}a^{i\mu}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+\gamma_{2}b_{1}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a^{j}_{\mu}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h=\int_{M}\beta_{1}b_{1}^{\prime}h\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{i},

which gives

\displaystyle b_{1}(\gamma_{1}-2\gamma_{2})=\beta_{1}b_{1}^{\prime}.

(7.162)

The calculation for the final adjoint is

\displaystyle\langle\chi,d_{3}a\rangle=\langle d_{3}^{*}\chi,a\rangle,

(7.163)

which reads

\displaystyle\int_{M}\chi^{i}(c_{1}\partial^{\mu}a_{\mu}^{i}+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k})=

(7.164)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\gamma_{1}a^{i\mu}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{k})+\gamma_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a_{\mu}^{j}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{m}).

The last term in the second line simplifies

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a_{\mu}^{j}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{m}

\displaystyle=(\delta^{il}\delta^{jm}-\delta^{im}\delta^{jl})(-\delta^{il}g^{\mu\alpha}+\epsilon^{ils}\Sigma^{s\mu\alpha})a_{\mu}^{j}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{m}

(7.165)

\displaystyle=-2a^{i\mu}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}-\epsilon^{ijk}a_{\mu}^{i}\Sigma^{j\mu\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{k}.

This means that we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\chi^{i}(c_{1}\partial^{\mu}a_{\mu}^{i}+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k})=

(7.166)

\displaystyle\int_{M}(\gamma_{1}c_{1}^{\prime}-2\gamma_{2}c_{2}^{\prime})a^{i\mu}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+(\gamma_{1}c_{2}^{\prime}-\gamma_{2}c_{1}^{\prime}-\gamma_{2}c_{2}^{\prime})\epsilon^{ijk}a^{i\mu}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{k},

and so

\displaystyle-c_{1}=\gamma_{1}c_{1}^{\prime}-2\gamma_{2}c_{2}^{\prime},\qquad-c_{2}=\gamma_{1}c_{2}^{\prime}-\gamma_{2}c_{1}^{\prime}-\gamma_{2}c_{2}^{\prime}.

(7.167)

The equations we obtained give the coefficients appearing in the adjoints in terms of those in the operators and those in the inner products.

7.8.5
Composition of the adjoints

Another useful calculation is that of the composition of the adjoints. We will compute the equations that guarantee that the compositions vanish.
The object
d_{2}^{*}d_{3}^{*}\chi
has three irreducible parts. The
h
part equals

\displaystyle b_{1}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{k})=0.

(7.168)

The
h^{i}
part is

\displaystyle b_{2}^{\prime}\partial^{\mu}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{k})+b_{3}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{m})=

(7.169)

\displaystyle(b_{2}^{\prime}c_{1}^{\prime}+2b_{3}^{\prime}c_{2}^{\prime})\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}.

We thus want

\displaystyle b_{2}^{\prime}c_{1}^{\prime}+2b_{3}^{\prime}c_{2}^{\prime}=0.

(7.170)

The
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}
part is the tracefree part of

\displaystyle 2b_{4}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu)}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{i}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}\chi^{k})+2b_{5}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\nu)}\chi^{i}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu)}{}^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}\chi^{k}).

(7.171)

The second term is

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu)}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}\chi^{k}=2\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu)}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{i}.

(7.172)

The last term is

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu)}{}^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}\chi^{k}=(\delta^{\alpha}_{(\nu}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu)}{}^{\beta}+\delta^{\beta}_{(\mu}\Sigma^{i\alpha}{}_{\nu)})\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\chi^{k}=0.

(7.173)

So, overall we have

\displaystyle(2b_{4}^{\prime}c_{1}^{\prime}+4b_{4}^{\prime}c_{2}^{\prime}+2b_{5}^{\prime}c_{1}^{\prime})\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu)}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{i},

(7.174)

and thus we want

\displaystyle b_{4}^{\prime}c_{1}^{\prime}+2b_{4}^{\prime}c_{2}^{\prime}+b_{5}^{\prime}c_{1}^{\prime}=0.

(7.175)

We now compute the other composition. We have

\displaystyle d_{1}^{*}d_{2}^{*}a=a_{1}^{\prime}b_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\mu}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}a_{\sigma}^{i}+a_{2}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}(b_{2}^{\prime}\partial^{\alpha}a_{\alpha}^{i}+b_{3}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}a_{\sigma}^{k})

(7.176)

\displaystyle+a_{3}^{\prime}\partial^{\nu}(b_{4}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu}a_{\alpha}^{i}+b_{4}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\mu}a_{\alpha}^{i}+b_{5}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}a_{\nu}^{i}+b_{5}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}a_{\mu}^{i}-\frac{b_{4}^{\prime}-b_{5}^{\prime}}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}a^{i}_{\sigma}).

The last term in the first line gives

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\rho\sigma}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}a_{\sigma}^{k}

\displaystyle=(-g^{\mu\rho}\Sigma^{i\nu\sigma}+g^{\nu\rho}\Sigma^{i\mu\sigma}+g^{\mu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\nu\rho}-g^{\nu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\mu\rho})\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}a_{\sigma}^{i}

(7.177)

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{i\mu\sigma}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}a_{\sigma}^{i}-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\rho}a_{\sigma}^{i}-\Sigma^{i\mu\rho}\partial_{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}a_{\sigma}^{i}.

This gives

\displaystyle d_{1}^{*}d_{2}^{*}a=(a_{1}^{\prime}b_{1}^{\prime}+a_{3}^{\prime}b_{4}^{\prime}-a_{2}^{\prime}b_{3}^{\prime}-\frac{a_{3}^{\prime}(b_{4}^{\prime}-b_{5}^{\prime})}{2})\partial_{\mu}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}a_{\sigma}^{i}+(a_{2}^{\prime}b_{2}^{\prime}+a_{3}^{\prime}b_{5}^{\prime}-a_{2}^{\prime}b_{3}^{\prime})\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\partial^{\alpha}a_{\alpha}^{i}

\displaystyle+(a_{2}^{\prime}b_{3}^{\prime}+a_{3}^{\prime}b_{4}^{\prime})\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\sigma}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}a_{\sigma}^{i}.

We thus want

\displaystyle a_{1}^{\prime}b_{1}^{\prime}+a_{3}^{\prime}b_{4}^{\prime}-a_{2}^{\prime}b_{3}^{\prime}-\frac{a_{3}^{\prime}(b_{4}^{\prime}-b_{5}^{\prime})}{2}=0,\qquad a_{2}^{\prime}b_{2}^{\prime}+a_{3}^{\prime}b_{5}^{\prime}-a_{2}^{\prime}b_{3}^{\prime}=0,\qquad a_{2}^{\prime}b_{3}^{\prime}+a_{3}^{\prime}b_{4}^{\prime}=0.

(7.178)

We have checked that all these equations hold provided the primed coefficients are as for the adjoint operators, and provided that squaring relations (
7.144
), (
7.149
) hold.

7.8.6
Computation of the square

We now form an elliptic operator

\displaystyle(S,E)\ni(\sigma,\chi)\to D(\sigma,\chi)=(d_{1}^{*}\sigma,d_{2}\sigma+d_{3}^{*}\chi)\in(\Lambda^{1},E\otimes\Lambda^{1}).

(7.179)

Its adjoint is given by

\displaystyle(\Lambda^{1},E\otimes\Lambda^{1})\ni(\xi,a)\to D^{*}(\xi,a)=(d_{1}\xi+d_{2}^{*}a,d_{3}a)\in(S,E).

(7.180)

The composition of these two operators is

\displaystyle D^{*}D(\sigma,\chi)=(d_{1}d_{1}^{*}\sigma+d_{2}^{*}(d_{2}\sigma+d_{3}^{*}\chi),d_{3}(d_{2}\sigma+d_{3}^{*}\chi))=((d_{1}d_{1}^{*}+d_{2}^{*}d_{2})\sigma,d_{3}d_{3}^{*}\chi).

(7.181)

We want to compute all the operators appearing here, starting with
d_{1}d_{1}^{*}
. The
h
component of
d_{1}d_{1}^{*}\sigma
is

\displaystyle a_{1}\partial^{\mu}(a_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\mu}h+a_{2}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h^{i}+a_{3}^{\prime}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})=a_{1}a_{1}^{\prime}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h+a_{1}a_{3}^{\prime}\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}.

(7.182)

For the
h^{i}
component we have

\displaystyle a_{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(a_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\nu}h+a_{2}^{\prime}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{j}+a_{3}^{\prime}\partial^{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\nu\alpha})=-a_{2}a_{2}^{\prime}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+a_{2}a_{3}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\nu\alpha}.

(7.183)

For the
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}
component we have

\displaystyle 2a_{3}\partial_{(\mu}(a_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\nu)}h+a_{2}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu)}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}+a_{3}^{\prime}\partial^{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\nu)\alpha})=

(7.184)

\displaystyle 2a_{3}a_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}h+2a_{3}a_{2}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu)}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}+2a_{3}a_{3}^{\prime}\partial_{(\mu}\partial^{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\nu)\alpha},

where it is understood that the tracefree part of the result is taken.
We now compute
d_{2}^{*}d_{2}\sigma
. For the
h
component we have

\displaystyle b_{1}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(b_{1}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+b_{2}\partial_{\nu}h^{i}+b_{3}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}+b_{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+b_{5}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma})=

(7.185)

\displaystyle-3b_{1}b_{1}^{\prime}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h-3b_{4}b_{1}^{\prime}\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}+b_{5}b_{1}^{\prime}(g^{\mu\rho}g^{\nu\sigma}-g^{\mu\sigma}g^{\nu\rho}+\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma})\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}=

\displaystyle(-3b_{1}b_{1}^{\prime}+b_{5}b_{1}^{\prime})\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h-(3b_{4}b_{1}^{\prime}+b_{5}b_{1}^{\prime})\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}.

For the
h^{i}
component we have

\displaystyle b_{2}^{\prime}\partial^{\mu}(b_{1}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+b_{2}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+b_{3}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}+b_{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+b_{5}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\mu\sigma})

\displaystyle+b_{3}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(b_{1}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+b_{2}\partial_{\nu}h^{k}+b_{3}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{m}+b_{4}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+b_{5}\Sigma^{k\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma})=

\displaystyle b_{2}b_{2}^{\prime}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+(b_{4}b_{2}^{\prime}+b_{5}b_{2}^{\prime})\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+b_{3}b_{3}^{\prime}(\delta^{il}\delta^{jm}-\delta^{im}\delta^{jl})(-\delta^{jl}g^{\mu\alpha}+\epsilon^{jls}\Sigma^{s\mu\alpha})\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}h^{m}

\displaystyle+2b_{4}b_{3}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i\mu\rho}\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+b_{5}b_{3}^{\prime}(-g^{\mu\rho}\Sigma^{i\nu\sigma}+g^{\nu\rho}\Sigma^{i\mu\sigma}+g^{\mu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\nu\rho}-g^{\nu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\mu\rho})\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}=

\displaystyle(b_{2}b_{2}^{\prime}+2b_{3}b_{3}^{\prime})\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+(b_{4}b_{2}^{\prime}+b_{5}b_{2}^{\prime}+2b_{4}b_{3}^{\prime})\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}.

For the
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}
component we need the tracefree part of

\displaystyle 2b_{4}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu)}(b_{1}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}h+b_{2}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}+b_{3}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}h^{k}+b_{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+b_{5}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\sigma})

\displaystyle+2b_{5}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}(b_{1}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu)}{}^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}h+b_{2}\partial_{\nu)}h^{i}+b_{3}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu)}{}^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}h^{k}+b_{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu)}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+b_{5}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu)\sigma})=

\displaystyle-6b_{1}b_{4}^{\prime}\partial_{(\mu}\partial_{\nu)}h+(2b_{2}b_{4}^{\prime}+4b_{3}b_{4}^{\prime})\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu)}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}-(6b_{4}b_{4}^{\prime}+2b_{5}b_{4}^{\prime})\partial_{(\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\nu)\sigma}

\displaystyle+2b_{1}b_{5}^{\prime}(g_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h-\partial_{(\mu}\partial_{\nu)}h)+2b_{2}b_{5}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\nu)}h^{i}+2b_{3}b_{5}^{\prime}(-g_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}+g_{(\mu}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu)}{}^{\beta}-g_{(\mu}^{\beta}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu)}{}^{\alpha})\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}h^{i}

\displaystyle+2b_{4}b_{5}^{\prime}(g_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}-\partial_{(\mu}\partial^{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu)\rho})+2b_{5}b_{5}^{\prime}(\partial_{(\mu}\partial^{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu)\rho}-\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})=

\displaystyle+(-6b_{1}b_{4}^{\prime}-2b_{1}b_{5}^{\prime})\partial_{(\mu}\partial_{\nu)}h+2b_{1}b_{5}^{\prime}g_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+(2b_{2}b_{4}^{\prime}+4b_{3}b_{4}^{\prime}+2b_{2}b_{5}^{\prime})\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu)}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}

\displaystyle+(-6b_{4}b_{4}^{\prime}-2b_{5}b_{4}^{\prime}-2b_{4}b_{5}^{\prime}+2b_{5}b_{5}^{\prime})\partial_{(\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\nu)\sigma}+2b_{4}b_{5}^{\prime}g_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}-2b_{5}b_{5}^{\prime}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}.

Here we have used that
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}
is tracefree. Dropping the trace parts, and collecting all the terms, we have the following expression for the
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}
part of
(d_{1}d_{1}^{*}+d_{2}^{*}d_{2})\sigma

\displaystyle(-6b_{1}b_{4}^{\prime}-2b_{1}b_{5}^{\prime}+2a_{3}a_{1}^{\prime})\partial_{\langle\mu}\partial_{\nu\rangle}h+(2b_{2}b_{4}^{\prime}+4b_{3}b_{4}^{\prime}+2b_{2}b_{5}^{\prime}+2a_{3}a_{2}^{\prime})\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu\rangle}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}

(7.186)

\displaystyle+(-6b_{4}b_{4}^{\prime}-2b_{5}b_{4}^{\prime}-2b_{4}b_{5}^{\prime}+2b_{5}b_{5}^{\prime}+2a_{3}a_{3}^{\prime})\partial_{\langle\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\nu\rangle\sigma}-2b_{5}b_{5}^{\prime}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}.

For the
h^{i}
part we have

\displaystyle(b_{2}b_{2}^{\prime}+2b_{3}b_{3}^{\prime}-a_{2}a_{2}^{\prime})\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+(b_{4}b_{2}^{\prime}+b_{5}b_{2}^{\prime}+2b_{4}b_{3}^{\prime}+a_{2}a_{3}^{\prime})\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}.

(7.187)

For the trace part
h
we have

\displaystyle(-3b_{1}b_{1}^{\prime}+b_{5}b_{1}^{\prime}+a_{1}a_{1}^{\prime})\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h+(-3b_{4}b_{1}^{\prime}-b_{5}b_{1}^{\prime}+a_{1}a_{3}^{\prime})\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}.
