(7.188)

We also need to compute the operator
d_{3}d_{3}^{*}\chi
. We have

\displaystyle d_{3}d_{3}^{*}\chi

\displaystyle=c_{1}\partial^{\mu}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{k})+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{m})

(7.189)

\displaystyle=(c_{1}c_{1}^{\prime}+2c_{2}c_{2}^{\prime})\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}.

7.8.7
Imposing the
\Delta
Conditions

We want the
D^{*}D
operator to be a multiple of the
\Delta=\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}
operator. This means we want to have the following quantities vanishing

\displaystyle-6b_{1}b_{4}^{\prime}-2b_{1}b_{5}^{\prime}+2a_{3}a_{1}^{\prime}=0,\quad 2b_{2}b_{4}^{\prime}+4b_{3}b_{4}^{\prime}+2b_{2}b_{5}^{\prime}+2a_{3}a_{2}^{\prime}=0,

(7.190)

\displaystyle-6b_{4}b_{4}^{\prime}-2b_{5}b_{4}^{\prime}-2b_{4}b_{5}^{\prime}+2b_{5}b_{5}^{\prime}+2a_{3}a_{3}^{\prime}=0,

\displaystyle b_{4}b_{2}^{\prime}+b_{5}b_{2}^{\prime}+2b_{4}b_{3}^{\prime}+a_{2}a_{3}^{\prime}=0,\quad-3b_{4}b_{1}^{\prime}-b_{5}b_{1}^{\prime}+a_{1}a_{3}^{\prime}=0.

When these conditions are satisfied, the parts of
D^{*}D
become

\displaystyle(d_{1}d_{1}^{*}+d_{2}^{*}d_{2})\sigma

\displaystyle=(-3b_{1}b_{1}^{\prime}+b_{5}b_{1}^{\prime}+a_{1}a_{1}^{\prime})\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h+(b_{2}b_{2}^{\prime}+2b_{3}b_{3}^{\prime}-a_{2}a_{2}^{\prime})\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}-2b_{5}b_{5}^{\prime}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\mu\nu},

\displaystyle d_{3}d_{3}^{*}\chi

\displaystyle=(c_{1}c_{1}^{\prime}+2c_{2}c_{2}^{\prime})\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}.

Solving the conditions (
7.190
) for the operators
d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}
of the Plebański complex leads to the choice of the inner products as in (
7.117
). As we have already described, the resulting in this case operator
d_{2}^{*}
is not the one relevant for the linearised Einstein equations. This means we need a different operator
D
. We will build it in the next section, based on the general computations that were carried out in this section.

7.9
Twisted operator

7.9.1
Construction of a more general operator
\tilde{D}

We know that the operators
D,D^{*}
(
7.179
), (
7.180
) constructed from the Plebański complex do not satisfy
D^{*}D\sim\Delta
, due to the fact they come from an complex that is not elliptic. We would now like to use the operators of the Plebański complex as building blocks of more general operators
\tilde{D},\tilde{D}^{*}
. Thus, we consider the twisted operators

\displaystyle\tilde{D}(\sigma,\chi)=(\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}\sigma+\tilde{d}_{4}\chi,\tilde{d}_{2}\sigma+\tilde{d}_{3}^{*}\chi),

(7.191)

Here all operators are general, of the type considered in the previous section, see (
7.137
), (
7.139
), (
7.140
), and we have introduced a new operator
d_{4}:E\to\Lambda^{1}

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{4}\chi=f\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{i}.

(7.192)

Let us also introduce

\displaystyle\tilde{D}^{*}(\xi,a)=(\tilde{d}_{1}\xi+\tilde{d}_{2}^{*}a,\tilde{d}_{4}^{*}\xi+\tilde{d}_{3}a).

(7.193)

We do not yet assume that the operators appearing here are adjoints of those in (
7.191
) with respect to some inner product. For now these are just the most general operators of the type (
7.104
), (
7.105
), (
7.106
) and we introduce

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{4}^{*}\xi=f^{\prime}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}.

(7.194)

The composition
\tilde{D}^{*}\tilde{D}
is

\displaystyle\tilde{D}^{*}\tilde{D}(\sigma,\chi)=(\tilde{d}_{1}(\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}\sigma+\tilde{d}_{4}\chi)+\tilde{d}_{2}^{*}(\tilde{d}_{2}\sigma+\tilde{d}_{3}^{*}\chi),\tilde{d}_{4}^{*}(\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}\sigma+\tilde{d}_{4}\chi)+\tilde{d}_{3}(\tilde{d}_{2}\sigma+\tilde{d}_{3}^{*}\chi)).

(7.195)

Most of the operators appearing here were already evaluated in section
7.8.6
. Let us compute the parts that have not been previously computed.

7.9.2
Computation of
\tilde{d}_{4}^{*}\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}+\tilde{d}_{3}\tilde{d}_{2}

We have

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{4}^{*}\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}+\tilde{d}_{3}\tilde{d}_{2}=f^{\prime}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(a_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\nu}h+a_{2}^{\prime}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{j}+a_{3}^{\prime}\partial^{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\nu\alpha})

\displaystyle+c_{1}\partial^{\mu}(b_{1}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+b_{2}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+b_{3}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}+b_{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+b_{5}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\mu\sigma})

\displaystyle+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(b_{1}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+b_{2}\partial_{\nu}h^{k}+b_{3}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{m}+b_{4}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+b_{5}\Sigma^{k\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma})=

\displaystyle-f^{\prime}a_{2}^{\prime}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}+f^{\prime}a_{3}^{\prime}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\nu\alpha}+c_{1}b_{2}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+c_{1}(b_{4}+b_{5})\Sigma^{i\mu\rho}\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle+c_{2}b_{3}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}h^{m}+c_{2}b_{4}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}+c_{2}b_{5}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\Sigma^{k\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}.

The terms in the last line compute as follows

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}h^{m}=(\delta^{il}\delta^{jm}-\delta^{im}\delta^{jl})(-\delta^{jl}g^{\mu\alpha}+\epsilon^{jls}\Sigma^{s\mu\alpha})\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}h^{m}=2\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i},

(7.196)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma}=2\Sigma^{i\mu\rho}\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\sigma}\tilde{h}_{\rho\sigma},

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\Sigma^{k\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}=(-g^{\mu\rho}\Sigma^{i\nu\sigma}+g^{\nu\rho}\Sigma^{i\mu\sigma}+g^{\mu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\nu\rho}-g^{\nu\sigma}\Sigma^{i\mu\rho})\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}=0.

This gives

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{4}^{*}\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}+\tilde{d}_{3}\tilde{d}_{2}=(-f^{\prime}a_{2}^{\prime}+2c_{2}b_{3}+c_{1}b_{2})\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}+(f^{\prime}a_{3}^{\prime}+c_{1}(b_{4}+b_{5})+2c_{2}b_{4})\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\nu\alpha}.

In order for our operator
\tilde{D}
to square to a multiple of the box operator the second term here must vanish, and so we impose

\displaystyle f^{\prime}a_{3}^{\prime}+c_{1}(b_{4}+b_{5})+2c_{2}b_{4}=0.

(7.197)

This can be viewed as an equation giving
f^{\prime}
provided all other parameters are known.

7.9.3
Finding
\tilde{D},\tilde{D}^{*}
that encode Einstein equations mod gauge

We now look for the solution of the system of equations
\tilde{D}^{*}\tilde{D}\sim\Delta
assuming that
\tilde{d}_{2}=d_{2}
and
\tilde{d}_{2}^{*}=d_{2}^{*}
of the Plebański complex. These are the two operators whose composition
d_{2}^{*}d_{2}
encodes the linearised Einstein equations, and we would like to keep this part of
\tilde{D}^{*}\tilde{D}
intact. All other entries in
\tilde{D}^{*}\tilde{D}
are related to gauge, and we are at will to choose them as convenient. Choosing the coefficients
b,b^{\prime}
as they are in the Plebański complex case, and substituting these into (
7.190
), we get

\displaystyle a_{2}=a_{2}^{\prime}=0,\quad a_{1}=-2a_{3},\quad a_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{4a_{3}},\quad a_{3}^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{a_{3}},\quad f^{\prime}=-c_{1}a_{3}.

(7.198)

This gives

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{1}\xi=a_{3}(-2\partial^{\mu}\xi_{\mu},0,2\partial_{\langle\mu}\xi_{\nu\rangle}),

(7.199)

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}\sigma=\frac{1}{4a_{3}}\partial_{\mu}h-\frac{1}{a_{3}}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}.

We can choose
a_{3}
in such a way that
\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}
is the adjoint of
\tilde{d}_{1}
with respect to the inner products (
7.129
). We have

\displaystyle(\tilde{d}_{1}\xi,\sigma)=\int-\frac{a_{3}}{2}(\partial^{\mu}\xi_{\mu})h+2a_{3}\tilde{h}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}=\int(\frac{a_{3}}{2}\partial_{\mu}h-2a_{3}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})\xi^{\mu}.

(7.200)

So, the adjoint of
\tilde{d}_{1}
matches
\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}
if

\displaystyle\frac{a_{3}}{2}=\frac{1}{4a_{3}},\qquad-2a_{3}=-\frac{1}{a_{3}}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad a_{3}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}.

(7.201)

Thus, finally

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{1}\xi=\sqrt{2}(-\partial^{\mu}\xi_{\mu},0,\partial_{\langle\mu}\xi_{\nu\rangle}),

(7.202)

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}\sigma=\sqrt{2}(\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\mu}h-\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}).

As a check we compute

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{1}\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}\sigma=2(-\frac{1}{4}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h+\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu},0,\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\langle\mu}\partial_{\nu\rangle}h-\partial_{\langle\mu}\partial^{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\nu\rangle\alpha}).

(7.203)

Recalling (
7.133
) we have

\displaystyle(d_{2}^{*}d_{2}+\tilde{d}_{1}\tilde{d}_{1}^{*})\sigma=(\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h,0,-\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}),

(7.204)

which is as desired. Note that the composition
d_{2}\tilde{d}_{1}
does not vanish, so these operators do not form a complex. However, as we will now see, the tilded operators can be rewritten in terms of those of Plebański complex.

7.9.4
Rewriting

We would like to rewrite the operators
\tilde{d}_{1},\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}
in terms of those appearing in the Plebański complex. For this, we introduce two linear operators

\displaystyle\Phi:E\otimes\Lambda^{1}\to\Lambda^{1},\qquad\Phi(a)_{\mu}=\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}a^{i}_{\alpha},

(7.205)

and

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}:\Lambda^{1}\to E\otimes\Lambda^{1},\qquad\Phi^{*}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\xi_{\alpha}

(7.206)

such that

\displaystyle\langle\xi,\Phi(a)\rangle=\langle a^{i},\Phi^{*}(\xi)^{i}\rangle

(7.207)

where
\Phi^{*}
is the adjoint of
\Phi
.
Note that for the case of Plebański
\gamma_{1}=0,\gamma_{2}=1
so

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}(\xi)^{i}_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\xi_{\alpha}.

(7.208)

We then compute

\displaystyle\Phi(d_{2}\sigma)=\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h+2\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}-\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\sigma})=

(7.209)

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{4}\partial_{\mu}h+2\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}-(\delta_{\mu}{}^{\rho}g^{\alpha\sigma}-\delta_{\mu}{}^{\sigma}g^{\alpha\rho})\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\sigma}=-\frac{3}{4}\partial_{\mu}h+\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}+2\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}.

It is now clear that there is a linear combination of
d_{1}^{*}\sigma
and
\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}(d_{2}\sigma)_{\alpha}^{i}
in which the
h^{i}
term cancels

\displaystyle(d_{1}^{*}\sigma)_{\mu}-\Phi(d_{2}\sigma)_{\mu}=-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\mu}h-\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{3}{4}\partial_{\mu}h-\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}h-2\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}=\sqrt{2}\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}\sigma.

(7.210)

Notably, we were able to rewrite the operator
\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}
that we deduced is necessary for
\tilde{D}^{*}\tilde{D}\sim\Delta
in terms of the original Plebański operators. In fact, the inner products (
7.129
) were chosen as they are so that this becomes possible.

We also have

\displaystyle 2d_{2}^{*}(\Phi^{*}(\xi))=(-2\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\alpha},\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\alpha},\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu|}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\partial_{|\nu\rangle}\xi_{\beta}-\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu|}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{|\nu\rangle}{}^{\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta})

(7.211)

\displaystyle=(6\partial^{\mu}\xi_{\mu},\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu},-2\partial_{\langle\mu}\xi_{\nu\rangle}).

This means we have

\displaystyle d_{1}\xi-d_{2}^{*}(\Phi^{*}(\xi))=(-2\partial^{\mu}\xi_{\mu},0,2\partial_{\langle\mu}\xi_{\nu\rangle})=\sqrt{2}\tilde{d}_{1}\xi.

(7.212)

Again, we were able to rewrite the tilded operator in terms of the original Plebański complex untilded ones. This means that the operators
\tilde{D},\tilde{D}^{*}
start to take a more concrete form

\displaystyle\tilde{D}(\sigma,\chi)=(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(d_{1}^{*}\sigma-\Phi(d_{2}\sigma))+\tilde{d}_{4}\chi,d_{2}\sigma+\tilde{d}_{3}^{*}\chi),

(7.213)

\displaystyle\tilde{D}^{*}(\xi,a)=(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(d_{1}\xi-d_{2}^{*}(\Phi^{*}(\xi)))+d_{2}^{*}a,\tilde{d}_{3}a+\tilde{d}_{4}^{*}\xi).

It remains to choose the operators
\tilde{d}_{3},\tilde{d}_{4}
and
\tilde{d}_{3}^{*},\tilde{d}_{4}^{*}
in the most convenient way.

7.9.5
Requiring the adjoints

We have imposed the condition that
\tilde{d}_{1},\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}
are adjoints of each other. Let us demand that the same holds true for the operators
\tilde{d}_{3},\tilde{d}_{4}
and
\tilde{d}_{3}^{*},\tilde{d}_{4}^{*}
. The general operator
\tilde{d}_{3}
is parameterised

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{3}a=c_{1}\partial^{\mu}a_{\mu}^{i}+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a_{\nu}^{k}.

(7.214)

In the inner products (
7.129
) the coefficients of its adjoint are
c_{1}^{\prime}=c_{2}-c_{1}/2,c_{2}^{\prime}=c_{1}/2
, so that

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{3}^{*}\chi=(c_{2}-\frac{c_{1}}{2})\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+\frac{c_{1}}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{k}.

(7.215)

For the operators
\tilde{d}_{4},\tilde{d}_{4}^{*}
we have

\displaystyle(\xi,\tilde{d}_{4}\chi)=\int f\xi^{\mu}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{i}=(\tilde{d}_{4}^{*}\xi,\chi),

(7.216)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{4}^{*}\xi=f\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu},

(7.217)

so that
f^{\prime}=f
.

7.9.6
Additional computations

We have

\displaystyle(\tilde{d}_{4}^{*}\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}+\tilde{d}_{3}d_{2})\sigma=f^{\prime}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\sqrt{2}(\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\nu}h-\partial^{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\nu\alpha})+c_{1}\partial^{\mu}(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h+2\partial_{\mu}h^{i}-\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\beta})

\displaystyle+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+2\partial_{\nu}h^{k}-\Sigma^{k\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma})=-(\sqrt{2}f^{\prime}+c_{1})\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\nu\alpha}+2c_{1}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}.

The absence of the first term demands
f^{\prime}=-c_{1}/\sqrt{2}
.

We also have, for the operator
(\tilde{d}_{1}\tilde{d}_{4}+d_{2}^{*}\tilde{d}_{3}^{*})\chi
, for the trace part

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{2}f\Sigma_{\mu}^{i}{}^{\alpha}\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{i}-2\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{k})=0.

(7.218)

For the vector part

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{m})=\frac{c_{2}^{\prime}}{2}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{i}.

(7.219)

For the tracefree part

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\partial_{\langle\mu}f\Sigma_{\nu\rangle}^{i}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{i}+\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu\rangle}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{i}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}\chi^{k})-\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\nu\rangle}\chi^{i}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu\rangle}{}^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}\chi^{k})=

\displaystyle(\sqrt{2}f+2c_{2}^{\prime})\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\nu\rangle}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{i}.

The vanishing of this requires
f=-\sqrt{2}c_{2}^{\prime}
. Note that this is compatible with
2c_{2}^{\prime}=c_{1}
and
f=f^{\prime}
. This fixes all the operators, apart from the operator
\tilde{d}_{3}a
which can still be chosen arbitrarily. We also need to compute

\displaystyle(\tilde{d}_{4}^{*}\tilde{d}_{4}+\tilde{d}_{3}\tilde{d}_{3}^{*})\chi=f^{\prime}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(f\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{j})+c_{1}\partial^{\mu}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{k})

(7.220)

\displaystyle+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(c_{1}^{\prime}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}+c_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{m})=(-ff^{\prime}+c_{1}c_{1}^{\prime}+2c_{2}c_{2}^{\prime})\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}.

7.9.7
The operator
\tilde{D}^{*}\tilde{D}

With the choices we have made, the operator
\tilde{D}^{*}\tilde{D}
takes the following form

\displaystyle\tilde{D}^{*}\tilde{D}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}h\\
h^{i}\\
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}\\
\chi^{i}\end{array}\right)=-\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-h\\
-\frac{c_{1}}{4}\chi^{i}\\
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}\\
-2c_{1}h^{i}+(c_{1}^{2}-2c_{1}c_{2})\chi^{i}\end{array}\right).

We have parameterised all the appearing coefficients in terms of
c_{1},c_{2}
. We see that the operator is not diagonal in the space
h^{i},\chi^{i}
, but rather a matrix

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-\frac{c_{1}}{4}\\
-2c_{1}&c_{1}^{2}-2c_{1}c_{2}\end{array}\right)

appears. The additional requirement that the eigenvalues of this matrix are
\pm 1
is chosen arbitrarily, which requires the diagonal elements to be both zero, and the product of the off-diagonal elements to be unity. This means

\displaystyle c_{1}=\sqrt{2},\qquad c_{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}.

(7.231)

Note that this means
f=-1,c_{1}^{\prime}=0,c_{2}^{\prime}=1/\sqrt{2}
. With these choices the matrix mixing
h^{i},\chi^{i}
becomes

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\\
-2\sqrt{2}&0\end{array}\right)

7.9.8
Final result for the operator
\tilde{D}

We now note that, with the choices we have made, the twisted operator
\tilde{D}
, written as a matrix acting on
S\oplus E
in terms of the original Plebański maps becomes

\displaystyle\tilde{D}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(d^{*}_{1}-\Phi d_{2})&&-\Phi d^{*}_{3}\\
d_{2}&&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}J_{1}d^{*}_{3}\end{pmatrix}.

(7.234)

This should be contrasted with the “naive” map (
7.179
), which in the same notations is represented by the following matrix

\displaystyle D=\begin{pmatrix}d^{*}_{1}&&0\\
d_{2}&&d^{*}_{3}\end{pmatrix}.

(7.235)

7.9.9
Lagrangian

We can obtain the operators
\tilde{D},\tilde{D}^{*}
as arising from an action

\displaystyle S=\int\xi^{\mu}(\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}\sigma+\tilde{d}_{4}\chi)_{\mu}+\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a^{j}_{\mu}(d_{2}\sigma+\tilde{d}_{3}^{*}\chi)_{\nu}^{k}-\frac{1}{2}(\xi_{\mu})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a^{j}_{\mu}a^{k}_{\nu}.

(7.236)

Explicitly, substituting the operators, we get

\displaystyle S=\int\xi^{\mu}(\sqrt{2}(\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\mu}h-\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{i})

(7.237)

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a^{j}_{\mu}(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+2\partial_{\nu}h^{k}-\Sigma^{k\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\nu\beta}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\epsilon^{klm}\Sigma^{l}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{m})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}(\xi_{\mu})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a^{j}_{\mu}a^{k}_{\nu}.

This is very similar to the Lagrangian that appears as (8.174) with (8.178) in
[
26
]
, apart from the last term in the second line, which would need to be chosen differently in order to get the Lagrangian in
[
26
]
. This is also a specific choice of
c_{1},c_{2}
in
eq.

6.84
such that the equations of motion are related to the non gauge fixed maps through the twisting described here.

Varying this with respect to
\xi,a
we get

\displaystyle\xi=\tilde{d}_{1}^{*}\sigma+\tilde{d}_{4}\chi,\qquad a=d_{2}\sigma+\tilde{d}_{3}^{*}\chi.

(7.238)

Substituting this back into the action we get a second order in derivatives action functional that depends only on
\sigma,\chi

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{2}\int\left(\sqrt{2}(\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\mu}h-\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{i}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+2\partial_{\mu}h^{j}-\Sigma^{j\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\beta}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\epsilon^{jlm}\Sigma^{l}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi^{m})\times

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}h+2\partial_{\nu}h^{k}-\Sigma^{k\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\nu\sigma}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\epsilon^{kpq}\Sigma^{p}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\chi^{q}).

Evaluating this gives

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{2}\int-\frac{1}{4}(\partial_{\mu}h)^{2}-4\sqrt{2}\partial_{\mu}h^{i}\partial^{\mu}\chi^{i}+(\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})^{2}.

(7.239)

This computation of the second order action is just another way to state that the introduced operator
\tilde{D}
has the property
\tilde{D}^{*}\tilde{D}\sim\Delta
.

7.10
Splitting the elliptic operator
\tilde{D}

We now want to understand the arising elliptic operator
\tilde{D}
better.

7.10.1
Changing the parametrisation of the
(\xi,a)
space

Let us introduce new coordinates on the space
E\oplus E\otimes\Lambda^{1}

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mu}^{i}=a_{\mu}^{i}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\xi_{\alpha},\qquad\omega_{\mu}=\xi_{\mu}+\sqrt{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}a_{\alpha}^{i}.

(7.240)

The inverse transformation is

\displaystyle\xi_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\omega_{\mu}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Sigma_{\mu}^{i}{}^{\alpha}\Omega^{i}_{\alpha},\qquad a_{\mu}^{i}=\frac{1}{2}(\Omega_{\mu}^{i}+\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Omega_{\alpha}^{k})-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\omega_{\alpha}.

(7.241)

Using the previously introduced operators
\Phi,\Phi^{*}
we have

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}=a^{i}-\sqrt{2}\Phi^{*}(\xi)^{i},\quad\omega=\xi+\sqrt{2}\Phi(a)

(7.242)

\displaystyle\xi=\frac{1}{2}\omega+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Phi(\Omega),\quad a^{i}=\frac{1}{2}(\Omega^{i}+J_{1}(\Omega)^{i})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Phi^{*}(\omega).

(7.243)

The transformation is selected so that

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}a_{\mu}^{j}a_{\nu}^{k}+(\xi_{\mu})^{2}=(\Omega_{\mu}^{i})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(\omega_{\mu})^{2}.

(7.244)

We have thus found the variables that diagonalise the indefinite quadratic form that appears on the left-hand side of (
7.244
). This makes it clear that it is more interesting to consider the operator
\tilde{D}
composed with the transformation to
\omega,\Omega
variables.

7.10.2
Splitting of
\tilde{D}

Let
T_{2}
be the linear map on
(E,E\otimes\Lambda^{1})
giving
(\omega,\Omega)
from
(\xi,a)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\omega\\
\Omega\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbb{I}&\sqrt{2}\Phi\\
-\sqrt{2}\Phi^{*}&\mathbb{I}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\xi\\
a\end{array}\right).

We then have

\displaystyle T_{2}\tilde{D}=\begin{pmatrix}1&&\sqrt{2}\Phi\\
-\sqrt{2}\Phi^{*}&&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(d_{1}^{*}-\Phi d_{2})&&-\Phi d_{3}^{*}\\
d_{2}&&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}J_{1}d_{3}^{*}\end{pmatrix}=

(7.251)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}d_{1}^{*}+(\sqrt{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}})\Phi d_{2}&&-\Phi d_{3}^{*}+\Phi J_{1}d_{3}^{*}\\
d_{2}-\Phi^{*}(d_{1}^{*}-\Phi d_{2})&&\sqrt{2}\Phi^{*}\Phi d_{3}^{*}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}J_{1}d_{3}^{*}\end{pmatrix}

To simplify this further we will need the following identities

\displaystyle(\Phi J_{1})(a)_{\mu}=\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}a^{k}_{\rho}=2\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}a^{i}_{\nu}=2\Phi(a)_{\mu},

(7.252)

\displaystyle(\Phi^{*}\Phi)(a)^{i}_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}a^{j}_{\rho}=-\frac{1}{2}a^{i}_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}a^{k}_{\nu}=-\frac{1}{2}(1+J_{1})(a)^{i}_{\mu}.

(7.253)

Using the language of operators the above is simply

\displaystyle\Phi J_{1}=2\Phi,\qquad\Phi^{*}\Phi=-\frac{1}{2}(1+J_{1}).

(7.254)

This gives

\displaystyle T_{2}\tilde{D}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(d_{1}^{*}+\Phi d_{2})&&\Phi d_{3}^{*}\\
\frac{1}{2}(1-J_{1})d_{2}-\Phi^{*}d_{1}^{*}&&-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}d_{3}^{*}\end{pmatrix}

(7.255)

The operators appearing in the first column can be further simplified. We have

\displaystyle(d_{1}^{*}+\Phi d_{2})\sigma=-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\mu}h+2\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h^{i}-\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{3}{4}\partial_{\mu}h+\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}+2\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}=

(7.256)

\displaystyle-\partial_{\mu}h+4\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h^{i},

where we took
\Phi d_{2}
from (
7.209
). Note cancellation of the
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}
terms. We also have

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}d_{1}^{*}=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}(-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\alpha}h+2\Sigma^{j}_{\alpha}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h^{j}-\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\nu})=

(7.257)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h-\partial_{\mu}h^{i}-\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\nu},

and

\displaystyle J_{1}d_{2}\sigma=\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{k}_{\alpha}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h+2\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}-\Sigma^{k\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\sigma})=

(7.258)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h+2\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}+\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\beta\mu}-\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\beta},

so that

\displaystyle(1-J_{1})d_{2}\sigma=-\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h+2\partial_{\mu}h^{i}-2\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}-2\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\beta\mu}+\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\beta}.

(7.259)

This gives, finally

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}(1-J_{1})d_{2}-\Phi^{*}d_{1}^{*}\right)\sigma=-\frac{1}{8}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}h+\partial_{\mu}h^{i}-\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}-\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\beta\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h+\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{k}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\nu}=

\displaystyle 2\partial_{\mu}h^{i}+\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\beta}-\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\beta\mu}.

Note the cancellation of the
h
-dependent terms here. We now rewrite the operator
T_{2}\tilde{D}
as one acting on a multiplet of fields
(h,h^{i},\chi^{i},\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})
. Using matrix notation we have

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\omega\\
\Omega\end{array}\right)=T_{2}\tilde{D}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}h\\
h^{i}\\
\chi^{i}\\
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}d&2\sqrt{2}\Phi d&\Phi d&0\\
0&2d&-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}d&\tilde{d}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}h\\
h^{i}\\
\chi^{i}\\
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}\end{array}\right),

where we have defined new operators

\displaystyle d:\Lambda^{0}\to\Lambda^{1},\qquad

\displaystyle(dh)_{\mu}:=\partial_{\mu}h,

(7.272)

\displaystyle d:E\to E\otimes\Lambda^{1},\qquad

\displaystyle(dh^{i})_{\mu}:=\partial_{\mu}h^{i},

\displaystyle\tilde{d}:{\rm Sym}^{2}_{0}(\Lambda^{1})\to E\otimes\Lambda^{1},\qquad

\displaystyle(\tilde{d}\tilde{h})_{\mu}^{i}:=\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\beta}-\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\beta\mu}.

We note that we can rewrite the operator
\tilde{d}
differently using the
\diamond
operator

\displaystyle(\tilde{h}\diamond\Sigma^{i})_{\mu\nu}:=\tilde{h}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha\nu}-\tilde{h}_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\alpha\mu}.

(7.273)

Then

\displaystyle(\tilde{d}\tilde{h})_{\nu}^{i}=-\partial^{\mu}(\tilde{h}\diamond\Sigma^{i})_{\mu\nu}=d^{*}(\tilde{h}\diamond\Sigma^{i})_{\mu}.

(7.274)

Let also write the arising objects
\omega,\Omega
explicitly

\displaystyle\omega_{\mu}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_{\mu}h+\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}(2\sqrt{2}h^{i}+\chi^{i}),

(7.275)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mu}^{i}=\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\beta}-\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\beta}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_{\mu}(2\sqrt{2}h^{i}-\chi^{i}).

We now introduce

\displaystyle h_{\pm}^{i}:=2h^{i}\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\chi^{i}.

(7.276)

Note that these are precisely the variables that diagonalise the mixed term in (
7.239
).

\displaystyle 4\sqrt{2}\partial^{\mu}h^{i}\partial_{\mu}\chi_{i}=((\partial_{\mu}h_{+})^{2}-(\partial_{\mu}h_{-})^{2}).

(7.277)

We also have

\displaystyle 8(h^{i})^{2}+(\chi^{i})^{2}=(h_{+}^{i})^{2}+(h_{-}^{i})^{2},

(7.278)

so that these are also the correct variables to diagonalise the inner product quadratic form. We can then write

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}h\\
h^{i}\\
\chi^{i}\\
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&0&0&0\\
0&\frac{1}{4}&\frac{1}{4}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&0\\
0&0&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}h\\
h^{i}_{+}\\
h^{i}_{-}\\
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}\end{array}\right)=T_{1}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}h\\
h^{i}_{+}\\
h^{i}_{-}\\
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}\end{array}\right).

The result is then

\displaystyle T_{2}\tilde{D}T_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}d&\sqrt{2}\Phi d&0&0\\
0&0&d&\tilde{d}\end{array}\right).

This shows that the operator
T_{2}\tilde{D}T_{1}
splits into the direct sum of two operators.

Let us describe the arising operators more explicitly. We define

\displaystyle D_{4}:\Lambda^{0}\oplus E\to\Lambda^{1},\qquad D_{12}:E\oplus{\rm Sym}^{2}_{0}(\Lambda^{1})\to E\otimes\Lambda^{1},

(7.297)

given by

\displaystyle D_{4}(h,h_{+})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}d&\sqrt{2}\Phi d\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}h\\
h_{+}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle D_{12}(h_{-},\tilde{h})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}d&\tilde{d}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}h_{-}\\
\tilde{h}\end{array}\right).

Explicitly, these operators are given by

\displaystyle D_{4}(h,h^{i}_{+})=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\partial_{\mu}h-2\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}_{+}\right),

(7.304)

\displaystyle D_{12}(h^{i}_{-},\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})=\partial_{\mu}h^{i}_{-}+\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\beta}-\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\beta}.

Each of these two operators times its adjoint gives a multiple of
\Delta
. This is easiest to confirm by performing the action computation. We have

\displaystyle\int(D_{4}(h,h_{+}))^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int(\partial_{\mu}h)^{2}+4(\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}_{+})^{2},

(7.305)

\displaystyle\int(D_{12}(h_{-},\tilde{h}))^{2}=\int(\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}_{-})^{2}+(\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})^{2}.

Here we used

\displaystyle\int(\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\beta}-\Sigma^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\beta})^{2}=\int 3(\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})^{2}-2(\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})^{2}+(\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})^{2}-(\partial^{\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})^{2}=\int(\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})^{2}.

We then have

\displaystyle\int(D_{12}(h_{-},\tilde{h}))^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(D_{4}(h,h_{+}))^{2}=\int(\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu})^{2}+(\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}_{-})^{2}-(\partial_{\alpha}h^{i}_{+})^{2}-\frac{1}{4}(\partial_{\mu}h)^{2}=2S,

(7.306)

where
S
is the action (
7.239
).

Part III
Nonlinear Gravity

Chapter 8
Type D Lorentzian Spacetimes

Type D spacetimes are class of solutions to Einstein’s equations characterised by the structure of their Weyl tensor, where type D means that the self-dual part of the Weyl tensor has two repeated eigenvalues and is of the form
\Psi^{ij}={\rm diag}(2\alpha,-\alpha,-\alpha)
. In general type D regions contain massive objects such as stars or black holes. One of the more famous type D spacetimes is the Kerr metric
[
28
]
. Its original derivation was done by brute force, assuming algebraic speciality and using the null tetrad formalism
[
12
]
. Derivations of the Kerr metric that are found in the literature are either quite involved
[
29
]
or they use a somewhat unexplained trick, for example the Newman-Janis shift
[
96
,
97
]
, and the Kerr-Schild form
[
98
]
. Another reference surrounding the question of simplifying the derivation of the Kerr metric can be found in
[
99
]
.

The Kerr family of solutions is a member of a more general Plebański-Demiański family
[
100
]
. All metrics of this family can be analytically continued to produce Euclidean signature metrics in 4D. Being an analytic continuation of a Lorentzian algebraically special metric of type D, the resulting Euclidean metrics are type D with respect to both halves of their Weyl curvature. A remarkable result in
[
30
]
states that a 4D Ricci-flat metric, with a one-sided type D Weyl curvature, is conformal to a Kähler metric. In particular, there is an integrable almost complex structure. This theorem can be applied independently to both halves of the Weyl curvature, and tells us that the Euclidean analytic continuation of any Lorentzian metric that solves the vacuum Einstein equations and is type D has two distinct integrable almost complex structures. These are of two different orientations, and complex structures in 4D that are of different orientations commute. This implies that for the Euclidean Kerr metric there exists two commuting complex structures, meaning there are two Kähler metrics in the conformal class. By assumption, the Kerr metric also has two commuting Killing vector fields. This is a very rigid setup and the Ricci-flat Euclidean metrics of this type can be described completely. This is the viewpoint taken in
[
51
]
where the Plebański-Demiański family of metrics are derived as examples of ambitoric 4D geometries, i.e. metrics that are toric with respect to two different complex structures. A symplectic toric manifold of dimension
2n
is a symplectic manifold endowed with a hamiltonian action of the torus
{(S^{1})}^{n}
.

This chapter is a presentation of the author’s paper
[
50
]
, which itself is a translation of the paper
[
51
]
into the language of Plebański’s formalism. The aim is to streamline and simplify their argument as much as possible and present an elementary derivation of the Kerr metric. In
section

8.1
we present a review of the works leading to
[
51
]
. Sections
8.2
and
8.3
briefly describe Plebański’s formalism and a characterisation of Killing vectors adapted to this formalism. In
section

8.4
we present an alternative proof to that of Derdziński, that type D metrics are conformal to Kähler. Section
8.5
reviews the Kerr metric as a metric that is conformal to Kähler metrics in two ways and makes explicit the two commuting complex structures. Which motivates the setup for the remainder of the chapter. In
section

8.6
we present our derivation of type D metrics following
[
51
]
, but using Plebański’s formalism to impose Einstein’s equations.

8.1
Historical Remarks

Since the discovery of the Kerr metric
[
28
]
in 1963 much effort has been put towards understanding the geometry and properties that it carries. The Kerr metric is a 2 parameter family of type D solutions to Einstein’s equations. In 1969 Kinnersley parameterised all Lorentzian type D solutions
[
101
]
. This was not given in a compact form and many different metrics were presented. Later, in 1976 Plebański and Demiański found a single expression for the most general black hole solution
[
100
,
102
]
. The Plebański-Demiański (PD) family of metrics are a 7 parameter family of solutions to the Einstein-Maxwell equations. When removing the electric and magnetic charge parameters from the PD metric we are left with a 5 parameter family of solutions to Einstein’s equations. All the type D spacetimes have Euclidean analogues, obtained by taking an analytic continuation through a complex transformation. Doing so to the PD metric results in a double-sided type D Euclidean metric. Performing the analytic continuation to the 4-parameter subfamily with no cosmological constant gives rise to the asymptotically locally Euclidean (ALE) metric. More recently, Chen and Teo generalised the Ricci-flat PD metric to an asymptotically locally flat (ALF) metric
[
103
]
, which becomes ALE in the appropriate limit. However, not all the Chen-Teo metrics contain Lorentzian sections. Recently, Biquard and Gauduchon showed that the ALF, Ricci-flat, Hermitian, toric metrics fall into one of 4 types
[
104
]
. These 4 types are Kerr, Chen-Teo, Taub-NUT and Taub-bolt.

One of the first realisations of complex properties of type D was through the Newman-Janis shift
[
96
]
. The Newman-Janis shift links the Kerr and Schwarzschild solutions of Einstein’s field equations through a complex diffeomorphism transformation. It was noticed by Flaherty
[
105
,
106
]
that this shift was related to the existence of an integrable almost complex structure that all type D spacetimes are equipped with. Type D spacetimes were known to be locally Hermitian because of this. Later, Derdziński proved, in Euclidean signature, that all one-sided type D metrics (with an extra condition implied by Einstein’s equations) are conformal to Kähler
[
30
]
. Soon after Derdziński’s result Przanowski and Baka derived a condition using this locally Hermitian condition to reduce the vanishing of the Ricci tensor for type D to a second order PDE of a single function
[
107
]
. However, the PDE was written using the complex coordinates making solutions difficult to find. This PDE is a transformation of
eq.

8.2
to complex coordinates. In 1991 LeBrun derived a related result, LeBrun was able to obtain a simple form of Kähler metrics with a
U(1)
symmetry
[
31
]
. The metric is parameterised using 2 functions
u=u(x,y,z)
,
w=w(x,y,z)
and is given by

\displaystyle g=e^{u}w(dx^{2}+dy^{2})+wdz^{2}+w^{-1}\alpha^{2},\quad\alpha=dt+\theta,\quad\alpha=J(wdz)

(8.1)

where
\theta\in\Lambda^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
J
is the complex structure. The Killing vector is
\partial_{t}
, which generates the
U(1)
symmetry. Vanishing scalar curvature for LeBrun’s ansatz becomes the
SU(\infty)
Toda-Lattice equation which is another second order PDE for the function
u
,

\displaystyle u_{xx}+u_{yy}+{(e^{u})}_{zz}\ =0

(8.2)

and is Ricci flat when
w=cu_{z}
for some constant
c
. This ansatz covers a large class of Kähler metrics in dimension 4, however, the Toda equation is difficult to solve in general. More recently, Tod
[
108
]
analysed the case of one-sided type D metrics that have an additional commuting Killing vector field. An even more recent related work is
[
109
]
. In this case the
SU(\infty)
Toda equation linearises, as shown by Ward in a different context
[
110
]
. The solutions can then be obtained from axisymmetric solutions of the flat three-dimensional Laplacian. However, even after the linearisation, deriving a general solution from
eq.

8.2
remains difficult. Apostolov, Calderbank and Gauduchon took a different approach in
[
111
,
51
]
building upon the fact that many interesting type D metrics are type D with respect to both halves of the Weyl curvature. This is also the approach we follow in the present work.

This chapter proceeds to derive useful results for Plebański’s formulation involving Killing vectors. We then compute the Kerr example to show that it satisfies the properties we have stated. In particular the two commuting complex structures are presented explicitly. Then we move to a translation of the results in
[
51
]
to a language more familiar to physicists. An explicit calculation is shown rather than a more involved algebraic topology argument

8.2
Euclidean Plebański Formalism

In this section we briefly review the Euclidean version of the Plebański formulation. For an introduction to Plebański’s formulation see
chapter

4
or
[
59
,
68
]
.

The Hodge star splits the space of 2-forms into
\Lambda^{2}=\Lambda^{+}\oplus\Lambda^{-}
, with eigenvalues
\pm 1
respectively. Self-dual 2-forms have an eigenvalue of
+1
and anti-self-dual have
-1
. Each eigenspace has dimension 3 (as
\dim(\Lambda^{2})=6
), meaning we can prescribe bases
\Sigma^{i}
and
\overline{\Sigma}^{i}
for
\Lambda^{+}
and
\Lambda^{-}
. The index
i=1,2,3
is an
\mathfrak{so}(3)=\mathfrak{su}(2)
index, and as such
\Sigma^{i}
are called
SU(2)
structures. The Riemann curvature can be viewed as an endomorphism acting on the space of 2-forms. It can then be decomposed with respect to the decomposition of the space of 2-forms, as seen in
eq.

4.128
. To impose Einstein’s condition only the self-dual projection of the Riemann curvature is needed and in turn the self-dual projection is encoded in the curvature of the self-dual connection. The self-dual connection,
A^{i}\in\mathfrak{su}(2)\otimes\Lambda^{1}
, is defined by

\displaystyle d\Sigma^{i}+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}=0.

(8.3)

Where the 2-forms satisfy the metricity condition

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{j}\sim\delta^{ij}.

(8.4)

Einstein’s equations are then

\displaystyle F^{i}=\left(\Psi^{ij}+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\delta^{ij}\right)\Sigma^{j},\quad{\rm with}\quad F^{i}=dA^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\wedge A^{k}

(8.5)

where
\Psi^{ij}
is the symmetric tracefree matrix encoding the self-dual Weyl tensor and
\Lambda
is the cosmological constant. Given a frame
e^{I}\in\Lambda^{1}
the self-dual and anti-self-dual 2-forms are given by

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}

\displaystyle=e^{0}\wedge e^{i}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}e^{j}\wedge e^{k}

(8.6)

\displaystyle\overline{\Sigma}^{i}

\displaystyle=e^{0}\wedge e^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}e^{j}\wedge e^{k}.

(8.7)

The metric can then be recovered by Urbantke formula

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{12}\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{\epsilon}^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\rho}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu\sigma}\Sigma^{k}_{\alpha\beta}.

(8.8)

The self-dual 2-forms also satisfy the same quaternion algebra,

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\Sigma^{j}_{\rho}{}^{\nu}=-\delta^{ij}\delta_{\mu}^{\nu}+\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{k}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}.

(8.9)

All the fields in this case are real-valued which means we do not need to impose any reality conditions.

8.3
SU(2)
Structures and Killing Vectors

Killing vectors fields are infinitesimal symmetries of the metric
\mathcal{L}_{X}g_{\mu\nu}=0
, where
\mathcal{L}
is the Lie derivative. For our purposes below we need to characterise Killing vectors from the point of view of
SU(2)
structures. Given that the metric (
4.97
) defined by a triple of 2-forms
\Sigma^{i}
is invariant with respect to the
{\rm SO}(3)
action on
\Sigma^{i}
, it is suggestive that in order for a vector field
X
to be infinitesimal isometries at the level of
\Sigma^{i}
, the Lie derivative of
\Sigma^{i}
should be an
{\rm SO}(3)
transformation

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X}\Sigma^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{\theta}^{j}\Sigma^{k}.

(8.10)

We will call a vector field
X
satisfying this property a
\Sigma
-Killing vector field. The following lemma describes an equivalent way of stating the
\Sigma
-Killing condition

Lemma 8.1
.

\Sigma
-Killing vectors
X\in TM
can be equivalently described as those satisfying

\displaystyle d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}\theta^{j}\Sigma^{k},

(8.11)

where
d^{A}
is the exterior covariant derivative with respect to the
\Sigma
-compatible
d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=0
connection
A^{i}
.

Proof.

To see this we use the Cartan’s magic formula

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X}\Sigma^{i}=\iota_{X}d\Sigma^{i}+d\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i}.

(8.12)

We now require this to be a gauge transformation

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X}\Sigma^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{\theta}^{j}\Sigma^{k}.

(8.13)

Using
d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=0
and
eq.

8.12
we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X}\Sigma^{i}=d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i}+\epsilon^{ijk}{(\iota_{X}A)}^{j}\Sigma^{k}=\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{\theta}^{j}\Sigma^{k}\quad\Rightarrow\quad d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}\theta^{j}\Sigma^{k}

(8.14)

where
\theta^{i}=\tilde{\theta}^{i}-\iota_{X}A^{i}
is the shifted
SO(3)
gauge transformation parameter.
∎

Next we link
\Sigma
-Killing vector fields with the usual Killing vector fields for the metric.

Proposition 8.1
.

A
\Sigma
-Killing vector field is a Killing vector field for the metric (
8.8
) defined by the triple of 2-forms
\Sigma^{i}
.

Before we prove this statement, we need to remind the reader some facts about the decomposition of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
-valued 2-forms. This construction is from
[
15
]
and details are given in
section

4.5
, here we will only summarise the main points. Let us denote
E=\mathfrak{su}(2)
, and consider the space of
E
-valued 2-forms. The space
\Lambda^{2}\otimes E
decomposes into the eigenspaces of this operator

\displaystyle\Lambda^{2}\otimes E={(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)}_{1}\oplus{(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)}_{3}\oplus{(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)}_{5}\oplus{(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)}_{9}.

(8.15)

The subscript in
{(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)}_{k}
indicates the dimension of the corresponding space. It can also be shown that
{(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)}_{9}=\Lambda^{-}\otimes E
. Here
\Lambda^{-}
is the space of anti-self dual 2-forms, with
\Lambda^{2}=\Lambda^{+}\oplus\Lambda^{-}
. We also have the following explicit projections to each of the eigenspaces

\displaystyle B^{(i}_{\alpha\beta}\Sigma^{j)\alpha\beta}\in S^{2}(E)=(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)_{1}\oplus(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)_{5},

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}B^{j}_{\alpha\beta}\Sigma^{k\alpha\beta}\in E=(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)_{3},

(8.16)

\displaystyle B^{i}_{(\mu|\alpha|}\Sigma^{i\alpha}{}_{\nu)}\in S^{2}(T^{*}M)=(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)_{1}\oplus(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)_{9}.

The first and last maps share a common trace and therefore contain the same
(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)_{1}
component. The tracefree part of the first map gives the
5
dimensional components as a symmetric tracefree internal matrix. The second map provides the
3
components as an internal vector. Lastly, the final map returns the
9
components as a symmetric tracefree spacetime tensor. We see that the different irreducible components can be packaged into other objects which will be useful for the rest of this chapter.

Proof.

We now prove the
proposition

8.1
. To this end, we consider the
E
-valued 2-form
d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i}
, for an arbitrary vector field
X
, and compute its irreducible parts. We have

\displaystyle(d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i})_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{[\mu}(X^{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{|\rho|\nu]})+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}_{[\mu}X^{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{|\rho|\nu]}.

(8.17)

We can replace the partial derivative in the first term by the covariant derivative with respect to the metric, because it is applied to a 1-form, and the result is
\mu\nu
anti-symmetrised. Replacing the derivative we have

\displaystyle(d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i})_{\mu\nu}=\nabla_{[\mu}(X^{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{|\rho|\nu]})+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}_{[\mu}X^{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{|\rho|\nu]}.

(8.18)

Now it is possible to use the fact that the connection 1-forms
A^{i}_{\mu}
are the “intrinsic torsion” components of the
{\rm SU}(2)
-structure, that is we have the following equation

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\nu}+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}_{\mu}\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\nu}=0.

(8.19)

This fact is proven in
[
15
]
, by considering the
E
-valued 2-forms
X^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}
, and showing that only the
(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)_{3}
projection is non-vanishing. This is then parametrised by
X^{\mu}A_{\mu}^{i}
. Contracting (
8.19
) with
X^{\rho}
and
\mu\nu
anti-symmetrising we see that we can rewrite (
8.18
) as

\displaystyle(d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i})_{\mu\nu}=\Sigma^{i}_{[\mu}{}^{\rho}\nabla_{\nu]}X_{\rho}.

(8.20)

We can now use the algebra
eq.

8.9
of
\Sigma
’s to compute the projections of

d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i}
onto the irreducible components. The result is

\displaystyle(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)_{1}

\displaystyle:\quad\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}(d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i})_{\mu\nu}=3\nabla_{\mu}X^{\mu}

\displaystyle(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)_{3}

\displaystyle:\quad\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}(d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{k})_{\mu\nu}=-2\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\nabla_{[\mu}X_{\nu]}

(8.21)

\displaystyle(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)_{5}

\displaystyle:\quad\Sigma^{\langle i|\mu\nu}(d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{|j\rangle})_{\mu\nu}=0

\displaystyle(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)_{9}

\displaystyle:\quad\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu|}{}^{\alpha}(d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i})_{\alpha|\nu\rangle}=-3\nabla_{\langle\mu}X_{\nu\rangle}

Here
\langle\mu\nu\rangle
denotes the symmetric traceless part.
We now assume that the
\Sigma
-Killing equation is satisfied and so
d^{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}\theta^{j}\Sigma^{k}
. The right-hand side here has the only non-vanishing projection

\displaystyle(\Lambda^{2}\otimes E)_{3}:\quad\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}(\epsilon^{klm}\theta^{l}\Sigma^{m})_{\mu\nu}=8\theta^{i}.

(8.22)

It is then clear that the
\Sigma
-Killing equation is equivalent to

\displaystyle\nabla_{(\mu}X_{\nu)}=0,\quad\theta^{i}=-\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\nabla_{[\mu}X_{\nu]}.

(8.23)

The first is the usual Killing equation, proving the proposition. The last relation gives
\theta^{i}
in terms of the vector field
X
. In words,
\theta^{i}
is the vector giving the self-dual projection of
dX^{\flat}
, where
(X^{\flat})_{\mu}=g_{\mu\nu}X^{\nu}
is the one-form corresponding to the vector field
X
using the musical isomorphism
\flat:TM\rightarrow\Lambda^{1}
.
∎

8.4
Type D metrics are conformal to Kähler

The aim of this section is to present an alternative (and new) proof of the theorem by Derdziński
[
30
]
. We use the chiral formalism, which makes the statement almost manifest. We also prove another statement from
[
30
]
, namely that all one-sided type D Einstein manifolds have a Killing vector. Einstein manifolds being smooth manifolds with a metric that satisfies Einstein’s equation.

8.4.1
Derdziński theorem

We start by using the chiral formalism to prove the following theorem

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
(M,g)
be a smooth 4-manifold that is one-sided type D, i.e. with one of the two halves of the Weyl curvature, say SD, having two coinciding eigenvalues. Assume in addition that the SD part of the Weyl curvature is divergence-free
\nabla\cdot W^{+}=0
. Then
g
is conformal to a Kähler metric.

This was first proved by Derdziński in
[
30
]
. Note that there is no assumption here that the metric is Einstein, one only assumes that the SD Weyl tensor is divergence-free. This is true for Einstein manifolds (as we will also see later), but is a weaker condition. The proof we present here is an elementary consequence of the chiral Plebański formalism. In fact, one can motivate the usefulness of the chiral formalism by the fact that this, reasonably non-trivial statement of Riemannian geometry, becomes apparent in this formalism.

We start by translating the divergence-free condition as a condition on objects in the Plebański formalism. As is clear from
eq.

8.5
, the self-dual part of the Weyl curvature is directly related to the matrix
\Psi^{ij}
, and we have
W^{+}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=\Psi^{ij}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{j}_{\rho\sigma}
.
The condition that the self-dual Weyl curvature is divergence-free translates to

\displaystyle 0=(\nabla\cdot W^{+})_{\nu\rho\sigma}=\nabla^{\mu}W^{+}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=\nabla^{A\mu}\left(\Psi^{ij}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{j}_{\rho\sigma}\right)=\nabla^{A\mu}\Psi^{ij}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\Sigma^{j}_{\rho\sigma},

(8.24)

where
\nabla^{A}_{\mu}
is the total gauge covariant derivative
\nabla^{A}_{\mu}\theta^{i}_{\nu}=\nabla_{\mu}\theta^{i}_{\nu}+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}_{\mu}\theta^{k}_{\nu}
and
A^{i}
is the self-dual connection defined by
d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=0
. As we have already remarked in (
8.19
), by construction, this covariant derivative has the property
\nabla_{\mu}^{A}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=0
, which justifies the last equality.

The object on the right-hand side of (
8.24
) is self-dual with respect to the index pair
\rho\sigma
. It can therefore be converted to an
E
-valued one-form

\displaystyle(\nabla\cdot W^{+})_{\nu\rho\sigma}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}=4\nabla^{A\mu}(\Psi^{ij}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu})=4\nabla^{A\mu}(\Psi^{ij})\Sigma^{j}_{\mu\nu}.

(8.25)

This can be rewritten in form notations. Considering the 3-form
(\nabla^{A}\Psi^{ij})\Sigma^{j}
, and taking the Hodge star we obtain a multiple of (
8.25
). Thus, we see that the following two statements are equivalent

\displaystyle(\nabla\cdot W^{+})=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad d^{A}(\Psi^{ij}\Sigma^{j})=0.

(8.26)

We now have the following simple proposition of the Plebański formalism

Proposition 8.2
.

Every Einstein 4-Manifold has divergence free self-dual Weyl curvature,
\nabla\cdot W^{+}=0
.

Proof of
proposition

8.2
.

The proof follows from the Bianchi identity
d^{A}F^{i}=0
. In Plebański’s formalism, the Einstein equation takes the following form

\displaystyle F^{i}=\left(\Psi^{ij}+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\delta^{ij}\right)\Sigma^{j}.

(8.27)

Applying the exterior covariant derivative on the left, and using the Bianchi identity, we have

\displaystyle 0=d^{A}F^{i}=d^{A}\left(\Psi^{ij}\Sigma^{j}+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\Sigma^{i}\right)=d^{A}(\Psi^{ij}\Sigma^{j})=0

(8.28)

where we have used
d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=0
and that
\Lambda
is constant.
Using
eq.

8.26
we see that Einstein 4-manifolds have divergence free self-dual Weyl curvature.
∎

Proof of
theorem

8.1
.

Let us denote the eigenvalues of
\Psi^{ij}
as
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
. We can assume, without loss of generality that
\alpha\geq\beta\geq\gamma
.
The traceless condition on
\Psi^{ij}
imposes
\alpha+\beta+\gamma=0
.
This means that
\alpha>0
and
\gamma<0
.
There are then two possibilities for two eigenvalues to coincide. One is when the middle eigenvalue coincides with the negative one
\beta=\gamma
. The other is when the two positive eigenvalues coincide. Thus, the matrix
\Psi^{ij}
is of the two possible forms

\displaystyle\Psi^{ij}=\begin{pmatrix}2\alpha&0&0\\
0&-\alpha&0\\
0&0&-\alpha\end{pmatrix},\qquad\Psi^{ij}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&0&0\\
0&\alpha&0\\
0&0&-2\alpha\end{pmatrix}

(8.29)

with
\alpha
being a positive real function. The two cases differ by the sign of
{\rm det}(\Psi)
. See section 2 of
[
112
]
for a related discussion. We consider the first case in details. The second case is treated analogously, by observing that it can be mapped to the first by allowing
\alpha
to be negative and relabelling the basis vectors. In the proof below, we never need to assume that
\alpha>0
, and so the proof covers both of the cases.

We conclude that for every one-sided type D Riemannian metric, one can always pick a basis
\Sigma^{i}
in the space of SD 2-forms, so that
\Psi^{ij}
is diagonal and of the form of the first matrix in
eq.

8.29
. With this form of
\Psi^{ij}
the equations (
8.26
) become

\displaystyle d\left(2\alpha\Sigma^{1}\right)+A^{2}\left(-\alpha\Sigma^{3}\right)-A^{3}\left(-\alpha\Sigma^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle d\left(-\alpha\Sigma^{2}\right)+A^{3}\left(2\alpha\Sigma^{1}\right)-A^{1}\left(-\alpha\Sigma^{3}\right)

\displaystyle=0

(8.30)

\displaystyle d\left(-\alpha\Sigma^{3}\right)+A^{1}\left(-\alpha\Sigma^{2}\right)-A^{2}\left(2\alpha\Sigma^{1}\right)

\displaystyle=0.

Using also
d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=0
, these equations can be rewritten as follows

\displaystyle 2d\alpha\Sigma^{1}+3\alpha d\Sigma^{1}=0

(8.31)

\displaystyle d\alpha\Sigma^{2}+3\alpha(d\Sigma^{2}-A^{1}\Sigma^{3})=0,\quad d\alpha\Sigma^{3}+3\alpha(d\Sigma^{3}+A^{1}\Sigma^{2})=0.

(8.32)

The first equation in the above can be written as

\displaystyle d\omega=0,\quad\omega:=\alpha^{\frac{2}{3}}\Sigma^{1}.

(8.33)

We now introduce a scaled complex linear combinations of the remaining 2-forms

\displaystyle\Omega:=\alpha^{\frac{2}{3}}(\Sigma^{2}+i\Sigma^{3})\quad(resp.\quad\bar{\Omega}:=\alpha^{\frac{2}{3}}(\Sigma^{2}-i\Sigma^{3})),

(8.34)

where the scaling factor in front is the same as for
\omega
. The last two equations in
eq.

8.30
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle d\alpha(\Sigma^{2}+i\Sigma^{3})+3\alpha d(\Sigma^{2}+i\Sigma^{3})+3i\alpha A^{1}(\Sigma^{2}+i\Sigma^{3})=0.

(8.35)

For later purposes, we note that using
d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=0
we can also rewrite the last two equations as

\displaystyle d\alpha\Sigma^{2}-3\alpha A^{3}\Sigma^{1}=0,\qquad d\alpha\Sigma^{3}+3\alpha A^{2}\Sigma^{1}=0,

(8.36)

and thus their complex linear combination gives

\displaystyle d\alpha(\Sigma^{2}+i\Sigma^{3})+3i\alpha(A^{2}+iA^{3})\Sigma^{1}=0.

(8.37)

This will be useful later.

We now use (
8.35
) to get an equation for
\Omega

\displaystyle d\Omega+\left(iA^{1}-\frac{d\alpha}{3\alpha}\right)\Omega=0.

(8.38)

We will rewrite this equation in a more suggestive way below.
Finally, the algebraic equations
\Sigma^{i}\Sigma^{j}\sim\delta^{ij}
imply the following equations

\displaystyle\omega\Omega=0,\quad\Omega\Omega=0,\quad\omega^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\Omega\bar{\Omega}.

(8.39)

We can now understand where the conformal to Kähler comes from. The second equation in (
8.39
) implies that the complex 2-form
\Omega
is decomposable. Its two 1-form factors can be declared to be the
(1,0)
forms of an almost complex structure, which defines this almost complex structure. The equation in (
8.38
) can then be rewritten in a better way. It is clear that it states

\displaystyle d\Omega+\left(iA^{1}-\frac{1}{3\alpha}d\alpha\Big|_{(0,1)}\right)\Omega=0,

(8.40)

because only the projection of
d\alpha
to the space
\Lambda^{0,1}
survives the wedge product with
\Omega
. We can then add another term that is valued in
\Lambda^{1,0}
and rewrite this equation as

\displaystyle d\Omega+ia\Omega=0,\qquad a=A^{1}+\frac{1}{3i\alpha}(d\alpha\Big|_{(1,0)}-d\alpha\Big|_{(0,1)}).

(8.41)

The connection 1-form
a
is now real-valued. We note that it can also be written as

\displaystyle\quad a_{\mu}=A^{1}_{\mu}+\omega_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\ln\left(\alpha^{\frac{1}{3}}\right).

(8.42)

The first equation (
8.41
) implies that this complex structure is integrable. Indeed, it implies that
d\Omega
has only a
\Lambda^{2,1}
part, which is equivalent to integrability
[
113
]
. The first of the equations in (
8.39
) implies that
\omega
is in
\Lambda^{1,1}
with respect to this complex structure. It can thus be declared to be the Kähler form. The last equation in (
8.39
) is the correct normalisation condition linking the volume form as defined by the Kähler form
\omega
and the volume form defined by
\Omega
. The equation (
8.33
) states that the Kähler form is closed. Altogether, we deduce that the metric defined by
\omega,\Omega
is Kähler, with Ricci form
\rho=da
. It is also clear that the original metric, defined by the
\Sigma^{i}
’s, is conformal to this Kähler metric

\displaystyle g_{K}=\alpha^{\frac{2}{3}}g_{\Sigma}

(8.43)

This proves
theorem

8.1
.
∎

8.4.2
Killing Vectors in Einstein one-sided type D spaces

Another statement proven in
[
30
]
is about the existence of Killing vectors in every Einstein manifold satisfying the assumptions of
theorem

8.1
.

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
(M,\Sigma^{i})
be an Einstein manifold with type D self-dual Weyl tensor. Let
\Sigma^{i}
be a basis for the triple of self-dual 2-forms chosen so that the SD part of the Weyl curvature is diagonal, and
\Sigma^{1}
is in the direction of the special (non-repeated) eigenvalue. Then
X^{\mu}=\Sigma^{1\mu\nu}\nabla_{\nu}\alpha^{-\frac{1}{3}}
is a Killing vector field. Here
\alpha
with
\alpha^{2}=\frac{1}{6}\Tr{\Psi^{2}}
is the repeated eigenvalue of the self-dual Weyl tensor.

Proof.

Inserting
X
into
\Sigma^{1}
we find

\displaystyle(\iota_{X}\Sigma^{1})_{\mu}=X^{\nu}\Sigma^{1}_{\nu\mu}=\Sigma^{1\nu\rho}\nabla_{\rho}\alpha^{-\frac{1}{3}}\Sigma^{1}_{\nu\mu}=\nabla_{\mu}\alpha^{-\frac{1}{3}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\iota_{X}\Sigma^{1}=d\alpha^{-\frac{1}{3}}

(8.44)

where we have used the quaternion algebra of the 2-forms. The other two 1-forms are computed as

\displaystyle(\iota_{X}\Sigma^{2})_{\mu}=\Sigma^{1\sigma\rho}\nabla_{\rho}\alpha^{-\frac{1}{3}}\Sigma^{2}_{\sigma\mu}=\Sigma^{1}_{\mu}{}^{\sigma}\Sigma^{2}_{\sigma}{}^{\rho}\nabla_{\rho}\alpha^{-\frac{1}{3}},

(8.45)

where we have used the algebra of
\Sigma
’s to anti-commute
\Sigma^{1,2}
. We similarly have

\displaystyle(\iota_{X}\Sigma^{3})_{\mu}=\Sigma^{1}_{\mu}{}^{\sigma}\Sigma^{3}_{\sigma}{}^{\rho}\nabla_{\rho}\alpha^{-\frac{1}{3}}.

(8.46)

Introducing
\Sigma^{+}=\Sigma^{2}+i\Sigma^{3}
we have

\displaystyle(\iota_{X}\Sigma^{+})_{\mu}=\Sigma^{1}_{\mu}{}^{\sigma}\Sigma^{+}_{\sigma}{}^{\rho}\nabla_{\rho}\alpha^{-\frac{1}{3}}.

(8.47)

We now use (
8.37
), which we rewrite as

\displaystyle d\alpha^{-1/3}\Sigma^{+}=i\alpha^{-1/3}A^{+}\Sigma^{1},

(8.48)

where we introduced
A^{+}=A^{2}+iA^{3}
. Taking the Hodge dual we get

\displaystyle\Sigma^{+}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\nabla_{\rho}\alpha^{-1/3}=i\alpha^{-1/3}\Sigma^{1}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}A^{+}_{\rho}.

(8.49)

Using this in (
8.47
) we get

\displaystyle(\iota_{X}\Sigma^{+})_{\mu}=i\alpha^{-1/3}\Sigma^{1}_{\mu}{}^{\sigma}\Sigma^{1}_{\sigma}{}^{\rho}A^{+}_{\rho}=-i\alpha^{-1/3}A^{+}_{\mu}.

(8.50)

We now summarise the above results

\displaystyle\iota_{X}\Sigma^{1}=d\alpha^{-1/3},\qquad\iota_{X}\Sigma^{+}=-i\alpha^{-1/3}A^{+},\qquad\qquad\iota_{X}\Sigma^{-}=i\alpha^{-1/3}A^{-}.

(8.51)

Having computed the components of
\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i}
, we can compute its exterior covariant derivative and use the characterisation of the
\Sigma
-Killing vectors to show that
X
is a Killing vector field. It will be convenient to rewrite the formulas for the exterior covariant derivative of
\iota_{X}\Sigma^{i}
in terms of the introduced objects
\Sigma^{\pm},A^{\pm}
. We have

\displaystyle d_{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{1}=d\iota_{X}\Sigma^{1}+\frac{1}{2i}(A^{-}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{+}-A^{+}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{-}),

(8.52)

\displaystyle d_{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{+}=d\iota_{X}\Sigma^{+}-iA^{+}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{1}+iA^{1}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{+}.

Substituting (
8.51
) into the first line we see it vanishes
d_{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{1}=0
. For the first term this is manifest from
d^{2}=0
. In the second term there is a cancellation of the two
A^{+}A^{-}
terms. On the second line we have

\displaystyle d_{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{+}=-id(\alpha^{-1/3}A^{+})-iA^{+}d\alpha^{-1/3}+A^{1}\alpha^{-1/3}A^{+}=-i\alpha^{-1/3}(dA^{+}+iA^{1}A^{+}).

(8.53)

What arises here is the curvature

\displaystyle F^{+}=F^{2}+iF^{3}=dA^{+}+iA^{1}A^{+}.

(8.54)

Using the Einstein equations in the form (
8.27
) we have

\displaystyle F^{+}=(-\alpha+\frac{\Lambda}{3})\Sigma^{+}.

(8.55)

This means that all in all

\displaystyle d_{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{1}=0,\qquad d_{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{+}=-i\alpha^{-1/3}(-\alpha+\frac{\Lambda}{3})\Sigma^{+}.

(8.56)

On the other hand, the
\Sigma
-Killing condition with the vector
\theta^{i}=(\theta,0,0)
becomes

\displaystyle d_{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{1}=0,\qquad d_{A}\iota_{X}\Sigma^{+}=i\theta\Sigma^{+}.

(8.57)

We see that this is indeed satisfied, with

\displaystyle\theta=\alpha^{-1/3}(\alpha-\frac{\Lambda}{3}).

(8.58)

This finishes the proof.
∎

8.5
Two complex structures of Euclidean Kerr

The aim of this section is to exhibit the structures described in the previous section for the example of the Euclidean Kerr metric. This section serves only to motivate the considerations that follow.

8.5.1
Metric and Chiral objects

The Euclidean Kerr metric is given by

\displaystyle g=

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{2Mr}{\rho^{2}}\right)dt^{2}+\frac{\rho^{2}}{\Delta}dr^{2}+\rho^{2}d\theta^{2}+\left(r^{2}-a^{2}-\frac{2Mra^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\sin(\theta)^{2}\right)\sin(\theta)^{2}d\phi^{2}

(8.59)

\displaystyle+\frac{4Mra\sin(\theta)^{2}}{\rho^{2}}dtd\phi,

where

\displaystyle\rho^{2}=r^{2}-a^{2}\cos(\theta)^{2},\qquad\Delta=r^{2}-2Mr-a^{2}.

(8.60)

The constants
a
and
M
are the Euclidean versions of the angular momentum and mass of the black hole
[
114
]
. This is obtained from the usual Lorentzian Kerr metric in Boyer-Lindquist coordinates through a transformation
t\rightarrow it
,
a\rightarrow ia
.
A useful tetrad basis is given by

\displaystyle e^{0}=\frac{\sqrt{\Delta}}{\rho}(dt-a\sin(\theta)^{2}d\phi),\quad e^{1}=\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{\Delta}}dr,\quad e^{2}=\rho d\theta,\quad e^{3}=\frac{\sin(\theta)}{\rho}(adt+(r^{2}-a^{2})d\phi).

(8.61)

8.5.2
Self-dual objects

The self-dual 2-forms are readily calculated from the
eq.

8.6
using the tetrad basis. We obtain

\displaystyle\Sigma^{1}

\displaystyle=(dt-a\sin(\theta)^{2}d\phi)\wedge dr-\sin(\theta)d\theta\wedge(adt+(r^{2}-a^{2})d\phi)

(8.62)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\Delta}(dt-a\sin(\theta)^{2}d\phi)\wedge d\theta-\frac{\sin(\theta)}{\sqrt{\Delta}}(adt+(r^{2}-a^{2})d\phi)\wedge dr

(8.63)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{3}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\Delta}\sin(\theta)dt\wedge d\phi-\frac{\rho^{2}}{\sqrt{\Delta}}dr\wedge d\theta.

(8.64)

The self-dual connections obtained by solving
d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=d\Sigma^{i}+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}=0
are the following

\displaystyle A^{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{M}{z_{+}^{2}}(dt-a\sin(\theta)^{2}d\phi)+\frac{r\cos(\theta)-a}{z_{+}}d\phi

(8.65)

\displaystyle A^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sqrt{\Delta}\sin(\theta)}{z_{+}}d\phi

(8.66)

\displaystyle A^{3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{a\sin(\theta)}{\sqrt{\Delta}z_{+}}dr+\frac{\sqrt{\Delta}}{z_{+}}d\theta

(8.67)

where
z_{+}=r-a\cos(\theta)
.
The self-dual curvatures can be computed (by hand!) and are given by

\displaystyle F^{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2M}{z_{+}^{3}}\Sigma^{1},\quad F^{2}=-\frac{M}{z_{+}^{3}}\Sigma^{2},\quad F^{3}=-\frac{M}{z_{+}^{3}}\Sigma^{3}.

(8.68)

This verifies that Kerr is Ricci-flat, and also gives the components of the self-dual Weyl tensor
F^{i}=\Psi^{ij}\Sigma^{j}

\displaystyle\Psi^{ij}_{+}=\begin{pmatrix}2\alpha_{+}&0&0\\
0&-\alpha_{+}&0\\
0&0&-\alpha_{+}\end{pmatrix},\quad\alpha_{+}=\frac{M}{z_{+}^{3}}.

(8.69)

The matrix
\Psi^{ij}
of the self-dual Weyl is tracefree as expected. Note that we have just used Plebański formalism to verify that the Kerr metric is Ricci flat. It is worth noting that this computation is easy to do, even by hand. This once again demonstrates the usefulness and power of the Plebański formalism.

8.5.3
Anti-self-dual objects

The anti-self-dual 2-forms can also be calculated using
eq.

8.7
to find

\displaystyle\bar{\Sigma}^{1}

\displaystyle=(dt-a\sin(\theta)^{2}d\phi)\wedge dr+\sin(\theta)d\theta\wedge(adt+(r^{2}-a^{2})d\phi)

(8.70)

\displaystyle\bar{\Sigma}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\Delta}(dt-a\sin(\theta)^{2}d\phi)\wedge d\theta+\frac{\sin(\theta)}{\sqrt{\Delta}}(adt+(r^{2}-a^{2})d\phi)\wedge dr

(8.71)

\displaystyle\bar{\Sigma}^{3}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\Delta}\sin(\theta)dt\wedge d\phi+\frac{\rho^{2}}{\sqrt{\Delta}}dr\wedge d\theta.

(8.72)

The corresponding anti-self-dual connections are

\displaystyle\bar{A}^{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{M}{z_{-}^{2}}(dt-a\sin(\theta)^{2}d\phi)+\frac{r\cos(\theta)+a}{z_{-}}d\phi

(8.73)

\displaystyle\bar{A}^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sqrt{\Delta}\sin(\theta)}{z_{-}}d\phi

(8.74)

\displaystyle\bar{A}^{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{a\sin(\theta)}{\sqrt{\Delta}z}dr+\frac{\sqrt{\Delta}}{z_{-}}d\theta.

(8.75)

Here
z_{-}=r+a\cos(\theta)
.
The anti-self-dual part of the curvature is

\displaystyle\bar{F}^{1}=-\frac{2M}{z_{-}^{3}}\bar{\Sigma}^{1},\quad\bar{F}^{2}=\frac{M}{z_{-}^{3}}\bar{\Sigma}^{2},\quad\bar{F}^{3}=\frac{M}{z_{-}^{3}}\bar{\Sigma}^{3}.

(8.76)

Clearly the anti-self-dual Weyl tensor is

\displaystyle\Psi_{-}^{ij}=\begin{pmatrix}2\alpha_{-}&0&0\\
0&-\alpha_{-}&0\\
0&0&-\alpha_{-}\end{pmatrix},\quad\alpha_{-}=-\frac{M}{z_{-}^{3}}

(8.77)

Note that the anti-self-dual and self-dual objects are related by the transformation
t\rightarrow-t
,
a\rightarrow-a
, which is the Euclidean analogue of the complex conjugation in the Lorentzian signature.

8.5.4
Conformal to Kähler

Looking at both halves of the Weyl curvature we can see that Euclidean Kerr is double-sided type D. Then by Derdziński’s theorem we know Euclidean Kerr is conformal to two different Kähler metrics. The two conformal factors are

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pm}=\frac{1}{z_{\pm}}\simeq\alpha_{\pm}^{\frac{1}{3}}

(8.78)

the factor of
M
can be ignored as this is a trivial conformal transformation. Conformally transforming the 2-forms
\Sigma^{1}
and
\bar{\Sigma}^{1}
we find a pair of closed 2-forms

\displaystyle\omega_{+}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{z_{+}^{2}}\Sigma^{1}=-dt\wedge d\left(\frac{1}{r-a\cos(\theta)}\right)+d\phi\wedge d\left(\frac{a-r\cos(\theta)}{r-a\cos(\theta)}\right)

(8.79)

\displaystyle\omega_{-}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{z_{-}^{2}}\bar{\Sigma}^{1}=dt\wedge d\left(\frac{1}{r+a\cos(\theta)}\right)-d\phi\wedge d\left(\frac{a+r\cos(\theta)}{r+a\cos(\theta)}\right).

(8.80)

The 2-forms
\omega_{\pm}
are the Kähler forms of the two Kähler metrics.

8.5.5
Complex Structures

We can extract the complex structures from the metric and the corresponding Kähler forms.
It is useful to introduce a basis for
TM
dual to the tetrad basis in
eq.

8.61
, these are given by

\displaystyle e_{0}=\frac{r^{2}-a^{2}}{\rho\sqrt{\Delta}}\partial_{t}-\frac{a}{\rho\sqrt{\Delta}}\partial_{\phi},\quad e_{1}=\frac{\sqrt{\Delta}}{\rho}\partial_{r},\quad e_{2}=\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_{\theta},\quad e_{3}=\frac{1}{\rho}\left(a\sin(\theta)\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}\partial_{\phi}\right).

(8.81)

These are dual to the tetrads in the sense that
e_{I}(e^{J})=e^{\mu}_{I}e^{J}_{\mu}=\delta_{I}^{J}
.
The complex structures can be calculated at the level of the Einstein metric as they are conformally invariant.
Hence, we look for operators
J_{\pm}
that satisfy
g(\cdot,J_{\pm}\cdot)=\lambda^{-2}_{\pm}\omega_{\pm}
.
Written in terms of tetrads and their duals this is simply

\displaystyle J_{\pm}

\displaystyle=e^{1}\otimes e_{0}-e^{0}\otimes e_{1}\mp e^{3}\otimes e_{2}\pm e^{2}\otimes e_{3}.

(8.82)

It is not difficult to see that
J^{2}_{\pm}=-\mathbb{I}
.
Substituting the expression in Boyer-Lindquist coordinates we find

\displaystyle J_{\pm}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Delta}dr\otimes\left((r^{2}-a^{2})\partial_{t}-a\partial_{\phi}\right)-\frac{\Delta}{\rho^{2}}(dt-a\sin(\theta)^{2}d\phi)\otimes\partial_{r}

(8.83)

\displaystyle\mp\frac{\sin(\theta)}{\rho^{2}}(adt+(r^{2}-a^{2})d\phi)\otimes\partial_{\theta}\pm d\theta\otimes\left(a\sin(\theta)\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}\partial_{\phi}\right).

(8.84)

We have explicitly checked that these complex structures are integrable (by computing and checking that their Nijenhuis tensors, defined in
eq.

8.94
, vanishes) and that they commute.

8.5.6
Killing Vectors

The Euclidean Kerr metric is conformal to two different Kähler metrics with opposite orientations. The general statements of the previous section imply that there should be two Killing vector fields, one coming from each of the two orientations. These are given by

\displaystyle X_{\pm}^{\mu}=J_{\pm}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(d\lambda_{\pm}^{-1})^{\nu}

(8.85)

Plugging in the conformal factors,
eq.

8.78
, we calculate the intermediate objects

\displaystyle d\lambda_{\pm}^{-1}=dr\pm a\cos(\theta)d\theta.

(8.86)

The vector fields dual to the 1-forms are given by

\displaystyle(d\lambda_{\pm}^{-1})^{\sharp}=\frac{\Delta}{\rho^{2}}\partial_{r}\pm\frac{a\sin(\theta)}{\rho^{2}}\partial_{\theta}

(8.87)

We get the Killing vectors by applying the complex structures

\displaystyle X_{\pm}=J_{\pm}(d\lambda_{\pm}^{-1})^{\sharp}=-\partial_{t}.

(8.88)

Thus, we find that the two Killing vectors coming from the two orientations coincide in the case of Kerr. Thus, the Euclidean Kerr is covered by the case (iii) of Proposition 11 from
[
51
]
. We note that the property that these two Killing vector fields coincide is related to the fact that the Kerr Laplace operator is separable. Indeed, this property holds only when one of the parameters of the Plebański-Demiański solution is zero
\epsilon=0
, where
\epsilon
is the constant appearing in the solutions
eq.

8.148
. This is also when the Laplace operator is separable. For a discussion related to the separability and Killing forms see e.g.
[
115
]
.

Even though we see that for the Kerr metric there is just one Killing vector field coming from the general construction of Proposition
8.3
, there is still another Killing vector field
\partial_{\phi}
unrelated to the conformal structure. The two Killing vector fields
\xi_{1}=\partial_{t},\xi_{2}=\partial_{\phi}
commute, and they also span an isotropic subspace
\omega_{\pm}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=0
with respect to either of the Kähler forms.
It is also obvious that each of these two Killing vector fields is
J_{\pm}
-holomorphic, in the sense that
{\mathcal{L}}_{\xi_{1,2}}J_{\pm}=0
. This is manifest in the case of the Kerr metric, because all the geometric objects constructed from it are
t,\phi
independent. We will take these properties of
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
as the starting point of the derivation of the Kerr metric in the next section.

For completeness, let us also compute the result of the action of the complex structures
J_{\pm}
on
\xi_{1,2}
. We have

\displaystyle J_{\pm}(\partial_{t})=\frac{\Delta}{\rho^{2}}\partial_{r}\pm\frac{a\sin(\theta)}{\rho^{2}}\partial_{\theta}

(8.89)

\displaystyle J_{\pm}(\partial_{\phi})=-\frac{a\sin(\theta)^{2}\Delta}{\rho^{2}}\partial_{r}\pm\frac{(r^{2}-a^{2})\sin(\theta)}{\rho^{2}}\partial_{\theta}

(8.90)

It is clear that
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},J(\xi_{1}),J(\xi_{2})
span all of
TM
, for each of the two complex structures. It can also be checked that all four of these vector fields are mutually commuting, for either
J
. The resulting frame will play an important role in the derivation of the next section.

8.6
Toric double-sided type D metrics

We now proceed with a derivation of the Euclidean Kerr metric. In fact, our discussion will be more general, and include the more general Plebański-Demiański family of metrics.
The Plebański-Demiański (PD) spacetimes
[
100
,
102
]
are Lorentzian algebraically special (type D) spacetimes that generalise many
important GR solutions. In general the PD spacetimes are solutions to the Einstein-Maxwell equations, however, here we will only consider the subclass of metrics that satisfy the vacuum Einstein equations. Our aim is to provide a “simple” derivation of this class of metrics by building on the conformal to Kähler ideas described in the previous section.
Trying to set up Einstein’s equation and solve them by brute force is doable but is far from the best strategy, see
[
29
,
99
,
116
]
for examples of such methods. Instead, we follow the approach of
[
111
,
51
]
that is based on the availability of two different commuting complex structures in these spaces.

8.6.1
Properties of PD Spacetimes

We begin by detailing properties of the spacetimes we aim to derive. We are interested in Lorentzian signature spacetimes that are algebraically special (type D), although we will solve for their Euclidean analytic continuations.
For example, to obtain a Euclidean metric from the Kerr metric one sends
t\rightarrow it
and
a\rightarrow ia
, where
t
and
a
are the time coordinate and the angular momentum parameter respectively. The self-dual (SD) and anti-self-dual (ASD) Weyl tensor in Lorentzian spacetimes are complex conjugates of each other, i.e.
W^{+}=(W^{-})^{*}
.
This is a consequence of the fact that SD 2-forms are complex-valued objects and the ASD 2-forms are complex conjugates of their SD counterparts.
Unlike the Euclidean signature case, there are no one-sided type D metrics in Lorentzian signature. Thus, analytically continuing a type D Lorentzian metric to Euclidean signature we get a Riemannian metric that is type D with respect to both
W^{\pm}
.

We are looking for Einstein metrics, for which we know both
W^{\pm}
are divergence free. This lets us use Derdziński’s theorem, see
theorem

8.1
, which in this case implies that the metric is conformal to two Kähler metrics, in general not coinciding. We denote these two Kähler metrics by
g_{\pm}
, so that
g=\lambda^{2}_{\pm}g_{\pm}
where
g
is the Einstein metric of interest. Being conformal to two Kähler metrics implies the existence of two integrable almost complex structures
J_{\pm}
. Each complex structure is associated with the SD and ASD orientations, hence the complex structures have different orientations and are not coinciding. Moreover, in 4D, two complex structures of different orientations commute. Having two commuting integrable complex structures is an extremely strong property.

By the discussion in the previous section, one-sided type D Einstein metrics are equipped with a Killing vector, see
proposition

8.3
. In our situation of double-sided type D metrics we have two Killing vector fields of this type. However, they may coincide, which, as we have seen in the previous section, is indeed the situation in Kerr. However, the metric we want to reproduce has two commuting Killing vector fields, one related to its stationary property, the other related to it being axisymmetric. So, we will assume that there are two commuting Killing vectors
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
. In the Euclidean setting both of these Killing vectors have compact orbits. This means that we have the two-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}
acting on our space by isometries. Such spaces are called toric. Moreover, in our setup with two different Kähler metrics
g_{\pm}
, we have the action of
\mathbb{T}^{2}
on both
g_{\pm}
. This is the reason why these spaces are called ambitoric in
[
51
]
.

The final condition we impose is that the Killing vectors
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
are
J
-holomorphic with respect to both
J_{\pm}
, that is
{\mathcal{L}}_{\xi_{1,2}}J_{\pm}=0
, and that
\omega_{\pm}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=0
. We have seen that this is true in the case of Kerr, and so this is a geometrically well-motivated assumption. Having motivated the geometry of the problem, we are ready to convert the assumptions into a concrete metric ansatz.

8.6.2
A frame of commuting vector fields

Let us consider just one of the two complex structures for the moment. The first statement that we would like to prove is that the collection of vector fields
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},J(\xi_{1}),J(\xi_{2})
spans
TM
and mutually commutes. The first half of the statement follows from the fact that
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
span an isotropic subspace
\omega(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=0
. This means that
J(\xi_{1}),J(\xi_{2})
is the complementary subspace.

To prove commutativity we need to use both J-holomorphicity, and the integrability of
J
. Let us start by giving an equivalent way of stating J-holomorphicity. This is a standard statement in complex geometry.

Lemma 8.2
.

A vector field
X
is
J
-holomorphic,
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}J=0
, if and only if

\displaystyle[X,JY]=J[X,Y],\forall Y\in TM.

(8.91)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{X}JY=({\mathcal{L}}_{X}J)(Y)+J({\mathcal{L}}_{X}Y)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad({\mathcal{L}}_{X}J)(Y)=[X,JY]-J[X,Y].

(8.92)

This shows that
({\mathcal{L}}_{X}J)=0
if and only if
[X,JY]-J[X,Y]=0
for any
Y
.
∎

We can now use this to show that most of the vector fields in
