\xi_{1},\xi_{2},J(\xi_{1}),J(\xi_{2})
commute.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]=0
and
\xi_{1,2}
be
J
-holomorphic. Then

\displaystyle[\xi_{1},J(\xi_{1})]=0,\quad[\xi_{1},J(\xi_{2})]=0\quad[\xi_{2},J(\xi_{1})]=0\quad[\xi_{2},J(\xi_{2})]=0.

(8.93)

Proof.

This is obvious from the
J
-holomorphicity of
\xi_{1,2}
and the previous lemma.
∎

To prove that
J(\xi_{1}),J(\xi_{2})
commute, we need to show that the integrability of
J
implies that they are also
J
-holomorphic.

Lemma 8.4
.

If
J
is integrable, and
X
is a
J
-holomorphic vector field, then
J(X)
is also
J
-holomorphic.

Proof.

J
is integrable if and only if the Nijenhuis tensor

\displaystyle N_{J}(X,Y)=[JX,JY]-J[X,JY]-J[JX,Y]-[X,Y]

(8.94)

vanishes. Let us assume that
X
here is
J
-holomorphic. Then the second and the fourth terms annihilate each other, and the vanishing of
N_{J}
implies

\displaystyle[JX,JY]=J[JX,Y],

(8.95)

which is precisely the statement that
JX
is
J
-holomorphic.
∎

Now, with both
J(\xi_{1}),J(\xi_{2})
being
J
-holomorphic, it is clear that
[J(\xi_{1}),J(\xi_{2})]=J^{2}[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]=0
. We have established that
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},J(\xi_{1}),J(\xi_{2})
are all mutually commuting. We end this subsection with another simple lemma.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
\xi_{1,2,3,4}
span
TM
and all commute. Let
\theta_{1,2,3,4}
be the dual 1-forms. Then all these 1-forms are closed.

Proof.

This follows from the standard formula

\displaystyle d\theta(X,Y)=X(\theta(Y))-Y(\theta(X))-\theta([X,Y]),\quad\theta\in\Lambda^{1},\quad X,Y\in TM.

(8.96)

Indeed, evaluating
d\theta
for any of the 1-forms, on any pair of the vector fields
\xi_{1,2,3,4}
, we see that all the terms on the right-hand side are zero.
∎

8.6.3
Construction of the frame

This subsection contains a central part of the construction for the derivation of the Kerr metric. Here we use the availability of the two commuting complex structures to find a convenient parametrisation of the 1-forms dual to
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},J(\xi_{1}),J(\xi_{2})
. After such a parametrisation is obtained, one can characterise the two commuting complex structures explicitly. Here we follow
[
51
]
rather closely, and even keep some notations of these authors. However, unlike this reference, we try to be as elementary as possible, which means without using any algebraic geometry.

First, we note that we can always choose the frame dual to
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},J(\xi_{1}),J(\xi_{2})
to be of the form
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},J(\theta_{1}),J(\theta_{2})
. Since the original vector fields commute, these 1-forms are all closed. This means that there are local coordinates such that

\displaystyle\theta_{1}=d\tau,\qquad\theta_{2}=d\varphi.

(8.97)

The condition that the other two 1-forms are closed becomes

\displaystyle dJd\tau=0,\qquad dJd\varphi=0.

(8.98)

We now come to the main statement of this subsection.

Proposition 8.4
.

There exists a choice of coordinates
x,y
, and of two functions
F=F(x),G=G(y)
, such that the two commuting complex structures
J_{\pm}
are realised as follows

\displaystyle J_{\pm}d\varphi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{F}dx\pm\frac{1}{G}dy,

\displaystyle J_{\pm}d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{x}{F}dx\pm\frac{y}{G}dy,

\displaystyle J_{\pm}dx

\displaystyle=-\frac{F}{y-x}(d\tau-yd\varphi),

\displaystyle J_{\pm}dy

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{G}{y-x}(d\tau-xd\varphi).

(8.99)

Proof.

Since
dJ_{\pm}d\tau=0,dJ_{\pm}d\varphi=0
, there exist coordinates
\xi,\eta
such that

\displaystyle d\xi=\frac{1}{2}(J_{+}+J_{-})d\varphi,\quad d\eta=\frac{1}{2}(J_{+}-J_{-})d\varphi.

\displaystyle J_{\pm}d\varphi=d\xi\pm d\eta

(8.100)

The two coordinates introduced
(\xi,\eta)
form a good coordinate system together with
\tau,\varphi
because both complex structures map
(d\tau,d\varphi)
to a complementary subspace, which we have now parametrised by
(d\xi,d\eta)
. The action of the complex structures on
(d\xi,d\eta)
gives a linear combination of
(d\tau,d\varphi)
, and can be parametrised as follows.

\displaystyle J_{\pm}d\xi=\alpha_{\pm}d\tau+\beta_{\pm}d\varphi,\quad J_{\pm}d\eta=\chi_{\pm}d\tau+\delta_{\pm}d\varphi.

(8.101)

Here
\alpha_{\pm},\beta_{\pm},\chi_{\pm},\delta_{\pm}
are eight at this stage arbitrary functions. However, with
\partial_{\tau},\partial_{\varphi}
being Killing vectors, we can assume that these functions depend on
\xi,\eta
only. Next we impose that
J_{\pm}^{2}=-1
on
d\varphi

\displaystyle J_{\pm}^{2}d\varphi=J_{\pm}d\xi\pm J_{\pm}d\eta=(\alpha_{\pm}\pm\chi_{\pm})d\tau+(\beta_{\pm}\pm\delta_{\pm})d\varphi=-d\varphi

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\alpha_{\pm}\pm\chi_{\pm}=0,\quad\beta_{\pm}\pm\delta_{\pm}=-1.

(8.102)

Next we enforce that
J_{+}
and
J_{-}
commute. We have

\displaystyle J_{-}J_{+}d\varphi=J_{-}d\xi+J_{-}d\eta=(\alpha_{-}+\chi_{-})d\tau+(\beta_{-}+\delta_{-})d\varphi,

\displaystyle J_{+}J_{-}d\varphi=J_{+}d\xi-J_{+}d\eta=(\alpha_{+}-\chi_{+})d\tau+(\beta_{+}-\delta_{+})d\varphi.

(8.103)

The commutativity
[J_{+},J_{-}]=0
, together with the conditions in
8.102
, implies that the action of
J_{\pm}
on
d\xi,d\eta
can be parametrised by only two functions

\displaystyle J_{\pm}d\xi=-\chi d\tau-(1+\delta)d\varphi,\qquad J_{\pm}d\eta=\pm(\chi d\tau+\delta d\varphi).

(8.104)

Lastly, we look at the action on
d\tau
, in general it will have the form

\displaystyle J_{\pm}d\tau=a_{\pm}d\xi+b_{\pm}d\eta

with 4 arbitrary functions
a_{\pm},b_{\pm}
. Again we check

\displaystyle J_{\pm}^{2}d\tau=a_{\pm}J_{\pm}d\xi+b_{\pm}J_{\pm}d\eta=\left(-a_{\pm}\pm b_{\pm}\right)\chi d\tau+\left(-a_{\pm}(1+\delta)\pm b_{\pm}\delta\right)d\varphi=-d\tau

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad a_{\pm}=-\frac{\delta}{\chi},\quad b_{\pm}=\mp\frac{1+\delta}{\chi}

(8.105)

Hence the action on
d\tau
is

\displaystyle J_{\pm}d\tau=-\frac{\delta}{\chi}d\xi\mp\frac{1+\delta}{\chi}d\eta.

(8.106)

We have already imposed the closure
dJ_{\pm}d\varphi=0
. It remains to impose
dJ_{\pm}d\tau=0
. For this we find

\displaystyle 0=d(J_{\pm}d\tau)=\left[\left(\frac{\delta}{\chi}\right)_{\eta}\mp\left(\frac{1+\delta}{\chi}\right)_{\xi}\right]d\xi\wedge d\eta

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\left(\frac{\delta}{\chi}\right)_{\eta}=0,\quad\left(\frac{1+\delta}{\chi}\right)_{\xi}=0

(8.107)

These equations are easily solved by introducing two functions
a=a(\xi)
,
b=b(\eta)
such that
\delta=-\frac{a}{a-b},\chi=\frac{1}{a-b}
.
Summarising the results so far we have

\displaystyle J_{\pm}d\varphi=

\displaystyle d\xi\pm d\eta

\displaystyle J_{\pm}d\tau=

\displaystyle ad\xi\pm bd\eta

\displaystyle J_{\pm}d\xi=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{a-b}\left(-d\tau+bd\varphi\right)

\displaystyle J_{\pm}d\eta=

\displaystyle\pm\frac{1}{a-b}\left(d\tau-ad\varphi\right)

(8.108)

From here we make a coordinate transformation
x=a(\xi),y=b(\eta)
. Writing
dx=a^{\prime}d\xi\equiv Fd\xi,dy=b^{\prime}d\eta=Gd\eta
,
we rewrite the action of the complex structures as

\displaystyle J_{\pm}d\varphi=

\displaystyle\frac{dx}{F}\pm\frac{dy}{G}

\displaystyle J_{\pm}d\tau=

\displaystyle\frac{xdx}{F}\pm\frac{ydy}{G}

\displaystyle J_{\pm}dx=

\displaystyle-\frac{F}{x-y}\left(d\tau-yd\varphi\right)

\displaystyle J_{\pm}dy=

\displaystyle\pm\frac{G}{x-y}\left(d\tau-xd\varphi\right),

(8.109)

where
F=F(x),G=G(y)
. Therefore, proving the original statement.
∎

8.6.4
Product structure

We now consider the operator
j:=-J_{+}J_{-}
. This squares to the identity
j^{2}=\mathbb{I}
. It is also clear that, because both
J_{\pm}
are orthogonal (i.e. metric-compatible) complex structures, the operator
j
is also metric-compatible. Such an operator
j:TM\to TM:j^{2}=+\mathbb{I}
is known as an orthogonal product structure. The reason for this terminology is that it decomposes the (tangent and) cotangent space into eigenspaces of eigenvalue
\pm 1
. It is easy to see that the subspaces of opposite eigenvalue are metric orthogonal. Indeed, let
\eta_{+},\eta_{-}\in\Lambda^{1}
be arbitrary eigenforms of
j
belonging to different eigenspaces

\displaystyle j\eta_{+}=+\eta_{+},\quad j\eta_{-}=-\eta_{-}.

(8.110)

Such that

\displaystyle(\eta_{-},\eta_{+})=0,

(8.111)

where the bracket denotes the metric pairing. Now, using one of the complex structures, say
J_{+}
, we can introduce two more 1-forms

\displaystyle J_{+}\eta_{-},J_{+}\eta_{+}\in\Lambda^{1},\quad s.t.\quad j(J_{+}\eta_{-})=-J_{+}\eta_{-},\quad j(J_{+}\eta_{+})=+J_{+}\eta_{+}.

(8.112)

It is easy to see that
(J_{+}\eta_{-},\eta_{-})=0,(J_{+}\eta_{+},\eta_{+})=0
and so the basis of 1-forms

\displaystyle\eta_{+},\quad J_{+}\eta_{+},\quad\eta_{-},\quad J_{+}\eta_{-}

(8.113)

is metric-orthogonal.

8.6.5
Metric ansatz

Using the complex structures defined in
eq.

8.99
, it is now an easy exercise to check that

\displaystyle j(dx)=dx,\qquad j(dy)=-dy.

(8.114)

This means that the 1-forms

\displaystyle dx,dy,(d\tau-yd\varphi),(d\tau-xd\varphi),

(8.115)

where the last two 1-forms are obtained by applying e.g.
J_{+}
to
dx,dy
, form a metric orthogonal basis. This means that the metric we are considering is of t
he form

\displaystyle g=A(d\tau-yd\varphi)^{2}+Bdx^{2}+Cdy^{2}+D(d\tau-xd\varphi)^{2}

(8.116)

where
A,B,C,D
are functions of
x,y
only. However, metrics of this form are not always compatible with the 2 complex structures.
To require this we impose that
g(\cdot,J_{\pm}\cdot)\in\Lambda^{2}
.
Computing the tensor
g(\cdot,J_{\pm}\cdot)
we find

\displaystyle g(\cdot,J_{\pm}\cdot)=\frac{A(x-y)}{F}dx\otimes(dt-yd\varphi)-BF\frac{(dt-yd\varphi)}{x-y}\otimes dx

(8.117)

\displaystyle\mp\frac{D(x-y)}{G}dy\otimes(dt-xd\varphi)\pm CG\frac{(dt-xd\varphi)}{x-y}\otimes dy.

Imposing that this is a 2-form results in the following restrictions to the components of
g
,

\displaystyle\frac{A(x-y)}{F}=\frac{BF}{x-y},\quad\frac{D(x-y)}{G}=\frac{CG}{x-y}.

(8.118)

Which are most conveniently solved by introducing two new functions
U=U(x,y)
and
V=V(x,y)
so that the solutions become

\displaystyle A=\frac{FU}{(x-y)^{2}},\quad B=\frac{U}{F},\quad C=\frac{V}{G},\quad D=\frac{GV}{(x-y)^{2}}.

(8.119)

This leads us directly to the following proposition.

Proposition 8.5
.

A 4-dimensional Riemannian metric, and its corresponding compatible 2-forms, with two distinct commuting Killing vectors that are J-holomorphic with two complex structures are given locally by

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=FU\left(\frac{d\tau-yd\varphi}{x-y}\right)^{2}+\frac{U}{F}dx^{2}+\frac{V}{G}dy^{2}+GV\left(\frac{d\tau-xd\varphi}{x-y}\right)^{2}

(8.120)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{1}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\frac{U}{x-y}dx\wedge(dt-yd\varphi)\mp\frac{V}{x-y}dy\wedge(d\tau-xd\varphi).

(8.121)

It should be appreciated how close this metric ansatz is to the Euclidean Kerr metric. We emphasise that the only information that went into the construction of this ansatz is that there are two commuting Killing vector fields that are J-holomorphic with the two commuting integrable complex structures. No field equations have yet been imposed.

8.6.6
Conformal to two Kähler metrics

We now want to impose the requirement that
g
is conformal to two different Kähler metrics. That is, we demand that
\Sigma^{1}_{\pm}
are conformal to closed 2-forms

\displaystyle d(\lambda^{2}_{\pm}\Sigma^{1}_{\pm})=0.

(8.122)

Where
\lambda_{\pm}=\lambda_{\pm}(x,y)
are the conformal factors for each Kähler metric.
The Lee forms of
\Sigma^{1}_{\pm}
are 1-forms,
\theta_{\pm}
, defined by

\displaystyle d\Sigma^{1}_{\pm}=\theta_{\pm}\wedge\Sigma^{1}_{\pm}.

(8.123)

When
\Sigma^{1}_{\pm}
are conformal to closed 2-forms the Lee forms themselves are closed.
In terms of the conformal factors we find that
\theta_{\pm}=-d\ln(\lambda^{2}_{\pm})
, and hence closed.
Using the 2-forms in
eq.

8.121
and solving for
\theta_{\pm}
we find them to be

\displaystyle\theta_{\pm}=\left(\frac{V_{x}}{V}\pm\frac{U}{V(x-y)}\right)dx+\left(\frac{U_{y}}{U}\mp\frac{V}{U(x-y)}\right)dy,

(8.124)

where we write
V_{x}=\partial_{x}V
and similarly for
y
. We want to impose the condition that both of these are closed 1-forms.
It is more efficient to take linear combinations. Firstly, we demand

\displaystyle d(\theta_{+}+\theta_{-})=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\ln(U)_{xy}-\ln(V)_{xy}=0

(8.125)

which can be solved by reparameterising by new functions
A=A(x),\ B=B(y)
and
H=H(x,y)
, such that

\displaystyle U=\frac{x-y}{H^{2}A},\quad V=\frac{x-y}{H^{2}B}.

(8.126)

The
A,B
here are unrelated to those used to parametrise the metric earlier. The introduction of
x-y
factor here is to help simplify later formulae.
The other linear combination is

\displaystyle d(\theta_{+}-\theta_{-})=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad-(B^{2})_{y}(x-y)-2B^{2}=(A^{2})_{x}(x-y)-2A^{2}.

(8.127)

Taking derivatives twice with respect to
x
we get
(A^{2})_{xxx}=0
. Similarly, taking the derivative with respect to
y
twice we get
(B^{2})_{yyy}=0
. This means that
these equations can be solved by
A^{2}
and
B^{2}
being quadratic polynomials in their respective variables, with arbitrary coefficients. Further,
substituting the quadratic ansatz for
A^{2},B^{2}
back into
eq.

8.127
we find that their coefficients coincide, that is

\displaystyle A^{2}=R(x),\quad B^{2}=R(y),\quad R(z)=r_{0}+r_{1}z+r_{2}z^{2}.

(8.128)

We thus find that, given the two functions
F(x),G(y)
and the 3 constants
r_{0},r_{1},r_{2}
defining
A(x),B(y)
, the metric

\displaystyle g=\frac{1}{H^{2}}\left[\frac{F}{A(x-y)}(d\tau-yd\varphi)^{2}+\frac{x-y}{FA}dx^{2}+\frac{x-y}{BG}dy^{2}+\frac{G}{B(x-y)}(d\tau-xd\varphi)^{2}\right]

(8.129)

is an ambitoric metric that is conformal to two Kähler metrics with opposite orientations. This ansatz for the metric is half-way to determining the Kerr metric.

8.6.7
Solving for the conformal factor

We are interested in a metric that is Einstein, so we now impose Einstein’s equations.
The easiest way to do this is using Plebański’s formalism, the details of which are in
chapter

4
. The derivation we present here is different from that in
[
51
]
, and, we hope, more straightforward. From
eq.

8.129
we choose a convenient basis for the frame

\displaystyle e^{0}=\frac{1}{H}\sqrt{\frac{F}{A(x-y)}}(d\tau-yd\varphi),

\displaystyle e^{1}=\frac{1}{H}\sqrt{\frac{x-y}{FA}}dx,

(8.130)

\displaystyle e^{2}=\frac{1}{H}\sqrt{\frac{x-y}{BG}}dy,

\displaystyle e^{3}=\frac{1}{H}\sqrt{\frac{G}{B(x-y)}}(d\tau-xd\varphi).

Using
eq.

8.6
we can build the SD 2-forms

\displaystyle\Sigma^{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{H^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{A}(d\tau-yd\varphi)\wedge dx-\frac{1}{B}dy\wedge(d\tau-xd\varphi)\right)

(8.131)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{H^{2}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{F}{ABG}}(d\tau-yd\varphi)\wedge dy-\sqrt{\frac{G}{ABF}}(d\tau-xd\varphi)\wedge dx\right)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{H^{2}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{FG}{AB}}d\varphi\wedge d\tau-\frac{x-y}{\sqrt{ABFG}}dx\wedge dy\right).

The self-dual connection, obtained by solving
d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=d\Sigma^{i}+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}=0
, in this basis becomes

\displaystyle A^{1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{BF}{A}-\frac{F^{2}}{AH^{2}}\left(\frac{(x-y)AH^{2}}{F}\right)_{x}\right)\frac{d\tau-yd\varphi}{2(x-y)^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt-\left(\frac{AG}{B}+\frac{G^{2}}{BH^{2}}\left(\frac{(x-y)BH^{2}}{G}\right)_{y}\right)\frac{d\tau-xd\varphi}{2(x-y)^{2}}

\displaystyle A^{2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{FG}{AB}}\left[\frac{AH_{x}-BH_{y}}{H(x-y)}d\tau+\frac{H(A-B)+2yBH_{y}-2xAH_{x}}{H(x-y)}d\varphi\right]

(8.132)

\displaystyle A^{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{AB}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{F}{G}}\frac{H(B-A)+2B(x-y)H_{y}}{2H(x-y)}dx+\sqrt{\frac{G}{F}}\frac{H(A-B)-2A(x-y)H_{x}}{2H(x-y)}dy\right].

The curvatures are given by rather long expressions. This is the only place where we needed to resort to algebraic manipulation software. We state the curvature 2-forms by decomposing them into their self-dual and anti-self dual components. We write

\displaystyle F^{i}=M^{ij}\Sigma^{j}+R^{ij}\bar{\Sigma}^{j}.

(8.133)

The non-zero components of
M^{ij}
are

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} M^{11}=&\frac{AH^{2}}{24}\left(\frac{F\log(A)_{x}}{x-y}\right)_{x}-\frac{AH^{3}}{12(x-y)}\left(F\left(\frac{1}{H}\right)_{x}\right)_{x}-\frac{AH^{2}}{24}\left(\frac{F}{x-y}\right)_{xx}\\
&+\frac{BH^{2}}{24}\left(\frac{G\log(B)_{x}}{x-y}\right)_{y}-\frac{BH^{3}}{12(x-y)}\left(G\left(\frac{1}{H}\right)_{y}\right)_{y}\\
&-\frac{BH^{2}}{24}\left(\frac{G}{x-y}\right)_{yy}-\frac{BGH^{2}}{48(x-y)^{3}}-\frac{AFH^{2}}{48(x-y)^{3}}\\
&+\frac{ABH^{2}}{12}\left[\left(\frac{G}{B(x-y)^{2}}\right)_{y}-\left(\frac{F}{A(x-y)^{2}}\right)_{x}\right]-\frac{H^{2}}{16(x-y)^{3}}\left(\frac{A^{3}G+B^{3}F}{AB}\right)\end{aligned}

(8.134)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} M^{22}=M^{33}=&\frac{ABH^{2}}{24}\left(\frac{F}{A(x-y)^{2}}\right)_{x}-\frac{ABH^{2}}{24}\left(\frac{G}{B(x-y)^{2}}\right)_{y}\\
&-\frac{AH^{3}}{12(x-y)}\left(F\left(\frac{1}{H}\right)_{x}\right)_{x}-\frac{AFH^{2}}{48(x-y)^{3}}-\frac{AH^{2}}{24}\left(\frac{F}{(x-y)^{2}}\right)_{x}\\
&-\frac{BH^{3}}{12(x-y)}\left(G\left(\frac{1}{H}\right)_{y}\right)_{y}-\frac{BGH^{2}}{48(x-y)^{3}}+\frac{BH^{2}}{24}\left(\frac{G}{(x-y)^{2}}\right)_{y}\\
&+\frac{H^{2}}{48(x-y)^{3}}\left(\frac{A^{3}G+B^{3}F}{AB}\right)\end{aligned}

(8.135)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} M^{12}=M^{21}=&-\sqrt{\frac{FG}{AB}}\frac{H^{2}}{24(x-y)^{3}}\left(\frac{x-y}{2}(A^{2})_{x}-A^{2}+\frac{x-y}{2}(B^{2})_{y}+B^{2}\right)\end{aligned}

(8.136)

We remind that one of the Einstein equations states that the trace of the matrix
M^{ij}
coincides with the cosmological constant
M^{11}+M^{22}+M^{33}=\Lambda
. The components of
R^{ij}
are

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R^{11}=&\frac{AFH}{12}\left(\frac{H}{x-y}\right)_{xx}-\frac{AH^{2}}{24}\left(\frac{F}{x-y}\right)_{xx}+\frac{AHF_{x}H_{x}}{12(x-y)}+\frac{AH^{2}}{24}\left(\frac{F\log(A)_{x}}{x-y}\right)_{x}\\
&-\frac{7AFH^{2}}{48(x-y)^{3}}-\frac{BGH}{12}\left(\frac{H}{x-y}\right)_{yy}-\frac{BH^{2}}{24}\left(\frac{G}{x-y}\right)_{yy}+\frac{BHG_{y}H_{y}}{12(x-y)}\\
&+\frac{BH^{2}}{24}\left(\frac{G\log(B)_{y}}{x-y}\right)_{y}-\frac{7BGH^{2}}{48(x-y)^{3}}+\frac{H^{2}}{16(x-y)^{3}}\left(\frac{B^{3}F-A^{3}G}{AB}\right)\end{aligned}

(8.137)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R^{22}=&-\frac{FH(AH_{x})_{x}}{24(x-y)}+\frac{FH^{2}A_{x}}{24(x-y)^{2}}-\frac{AFH^{2}}{48(x-y)^{3}}+\frac{GH(BH_{y})_{y}}{24(x-y)}+\frac{GH^{2}B_{y}}{24(x-y)^{2}}\\
&-\frac{BGH^{2}}{48(x-y)^{3}}+\frac{H^{2}}{48(x-y)^{3}}\left(\frac{B^{3}F-A^{3}G}{AB}\right)\end{aligned}

(8.138)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R^{33}=&-\frac{FH(AH_{x})_{x}}{24(x-y)}+\frac{FH^{2}A_{x}}{24(x-y)^{2}}-\frac{AFH^{2}}{48(x-y)^{3}}-\frac{GH(BH_{y})_{y}}{24(x-y)}-\frac{GH^{2}B_{y}}{24(x-y)^{2}}\\
&+\frac{BGH^{2}}{48(x-y)^{3}}-\frac{H^{2}}{48(x-y)^{3}}\left(\frac{B^{3}F-A^{3}G}{AB}\right)\end{aligned}

(8.139)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R^{12}=&\sqrt{\frac{FG}{AB}}\frac{H}{12(x-y)^{2}}\left[2AB\left(2(x-y)H_{xy}+H_{x}-H_{y}\right)\right.\\
&\quad+\left.\left(2B^{2}H_{y}-\frac{H}{2}(B^{2})_{y}-2A^{2}H_{x}+\frac{H}{2}(A^{2})_{x}\right)\right]\end{aligned}

(8.140)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R^{21}=&\sqrt{\frac{FG}{AB}}\frac{H}{12(x-y)^{2}}\left[2AB\left(2(x-y)H_{xy}+H_{x}-H_{y}\right)\right.\\
&\quad-\left.\left(2B^{2}H_{y}-\frac{H}{2}(B^{2})_{y}-2A^{2}H_{x}+\frac{H}{2}(A^{2})_{x}\right)\right]\end{aligned}

(8.141)

We find it useful to first analyse the equations imposed by
R^{12}=0,R^{21}=0
. Taking the sum and difference of these equations we get

\displaystyle 2(x-y)H_{xy}+H_{x}-H_{y}=0

(8.142)

\displaystyle 2A^{2}H_{x}-\frac{H}{2}(A^{2})_{x}-2B^{2}H_{y}+\frac{H}{2}(B^{2})_{y}=0.

(8.143)

To solve the first equation we introduce a coordinate substitution
t,r=(x+y,x-y)
such that is becomes

\displaystyle H_{tt}=r\left(\frac{1}{r}H_{r}\right)_{r}.

(8.144)

Looking for separable solutions of the form
H=T(t)+R(r)
we obtain the following class of solutions

\displaystyle H(t,r)=c_{0}r^{2}\ln(r)+c_{3}r^{2}+c_{0}t^{2}+c_{1}t+c_{2}.

(8.145)

Another class of solutions is obtained by taking
H=H(z)
and
z=\sqrt{t^{2}-r^{2}}
the equation reduces to

\displaystyle H_{zz}=0

(8.146)

which has a solution of the form

\displaystyle H(t,r)=c_{4}\sqrt{t^{2}-r^{2}}+const.

(8.147)

As the original PDE is linear in
H
, we can add these solutions.
We note that other separable solutions exist in terms of Bessel functions, but these will not be relevant.
Substituting the solution into
eq.

8.143
we can restrict the allowed values of the constants
c_{i}
and
r_{i}
, see
eq.

8.128
for the definition of the latter. In doing so we find 3 different solutions for
H(x,y)
and
R(z)

\displaystyle 1)\quad H(x,y)

\displaystyle=1+\varepsilon\sqrt{xy},\quad

\displaystyle R=z

\displaystyle 2)\quad H(x,y)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{xy},\quad

\displaystyle R=z^{2}+2\varepsilon z

(8.148)

\displaystyle 3)\quad H(x,y)

\displaystyle=1+\varepsilon(x+y),\quad

\displaystyle R=1

where we have renamed some constants in such a way that
\varepsilon
is the only constant left. In the main text we will focus on case
1)
as this gives the family that contains the PD metrics. The other cases are described in
[
51
]
and briefly in the appendix of
[
50
]
. It happens that the physically relevant case is
1)
, we have that the solutions for
H,A,B
are

\displaystyle H=1+\varepsilon\sqrt{xy},\quad A(x)=\sqrt{x},\quad B(y)=\sqrt{y}.

(8.149)

We now perform another change of variables to remove the square roots. We take
x=r^{2},\ y=q^{2}
, taking the positive branch of the square root.
The metric in these variables becomes

\displaystyle g=\frac{1}{(1+\varepsilon rq)^{2}}\left(\frac{C}{r^{2}-q^{2}}(d\tau-q^{2}d\varphi)^{2}+\frac{r^{2}-q^{2}}{C}dr^{2}+\frac{r^{2}-q^{2}}{D}dq^{2}+\frac{D}{r^{2}-q^{2}}(d\tau-r^{2}d\varphi)^{2}\right)

(8.150)

where we have introduced
C(r)=F/r
,
D(q)=G/q
.

8.6.8
Plebański-Demiański family of solutions

Having chosen a solution for the conformal factor function
H^{2}
, we now calculate the Ricci scalar condition
M^{11}+M^{22}+M^{33}=\Lambda
. The components of the matrix
M^{ij}
for the metric
eq.

8.150
are as follows

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} M^{11}=&\frac{1}{6}\frac{(1+\varepsilon rq)^{2}(r+q)^{3}}{q^{2}-r^{2}}\left[\left(\frac{C}{(r+q)^{3}}\right)_{rr}\hskip-10.0pt+\left(\frac{D}{(r+q)^{3}}\right)_{qq}\right]\\
+&\frac{(1+\varepsilon rq)^{5}}{12(q^{2}-r^{2})}\left[\left(\frac{C}{(1+\varepsilon rq)^{3}}\right)_{rr}\hskip-10.0pt+\left(\frac{D}{(1+\varepsilon rq)^{3}}\right)_{qq}\right],\end{aligned}

(8.151)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} M^{22}=M^{33}=&-\frac{1}{12}\frac{(1+\varepsilon rq)^{2}(r+q)^{3}}{q^{2}-r^{2}}\left[\left(\frac{C}{(r+q)^{3}}\right)_{rr}\hskip-10.0pt+\left(\frac{D}{(r+q)^{3}}\right)_{qq}\right]\\
+&\frac{(1+\varepsilon rq)^{5}}{12(q^{2}-r^{2})}\left[\left(\frac{C}{(1+\varepsilon rq)^{3}}\right)_{rr}\hskip-10.0pt+\left(\frac{D}{(1+\varepsilon rq)^{3}}\right)_{qq}\right].\end{aligned}

(8.152)

Taking the trace results in the following equation

\displaystyle 12\varepsilon^{2}(q^{2}C+r^{2}D)-6\varepsilon(1+\varepsilon rq)(qC_{r}+rD_{q})+(1+\varepsilon rq)^{2}(C_{rr}+D_{qq})=4(q^{2}-r^{2})\Lambda.

(8.153)

By taking derivatives of both sides 3 times with respect to
r
and
q
gives

\displaystyle C_{rrrrr}=0,\quad D_{qqqqq}=0.

(8.154)

This shows that
C
and
D
are now 4th order polynomials of their respective variables.
The only remaining nonzero component of the traceless Ricci tensor is then

\displaystyle R^{11}=

\displaystyle(r^{2}-q^{2})(1+\varepsilon rq)D_{qq}+(4q+6\varepsilon rq^{2}-2\varepsilon r^{3})D_{q}-4(1+3\varepsilon rq)D

\displaystyle-(r^{2}-q^{2})(1+\varepsilon rq)C_{rr}+(4r+6\varepsilon r^{2}q-2\varepsilon q^{3})C_{r}-4(1+3\varepsilon rq)C.

This acts to further restrict
C,D
. We substitute a generic quartic polynomial ansatz for C, D into
M^{11}+M^{22}+M^{33}=\Lambda
and
R^{11}=0
.
This gives the following solution

\displaystyle C=-b+2mr+r^{2}+2n\varepsilon r^{3}-(\varepsilon^{2}b+\Lambda/3)r^{4}

(8.155)

\displaystyle D=b+2nq-q^{2}+2m\varepsilon q^{3}+(\varepsilon^{2}b+\Lambda/3)q^{4}.

We have used the freedom of rescaling
r,q\rightarrow\lambda r,\lambda q
to set the coefficients in front of
r^{2},-q^{2}
to unity.

We have thus derived the Euclidean version of the Plebański-Demiański metrics with their 5 parameters,
b,m,n,\varepsilon,\Lambda

[
100
,
102
]
. This is the same result that was obtained in
[
51
]
, however the approach here was, we believe, more elementary, without any need for algebraic geometry considerations.

To check that the metric we obtained is indeed double-sided type D we can calculate both parts of the Weyl curvature. In doing so we find

\displaystyle\Psi=\begin{pmatrix}2\alpha&0&0\\
0&-\alpha&0\\
0&0&-\alpha\end{pmatrix},\quad\alpha=\frac{n-m}{6}\left(\frac{1+\varepsilon rq}{r+q}\right)^{3},

(8.156)

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}=\begin{pmatrix}2\bar{\alpha}&0&0\\
0&-\bar{\alpha}&0\\
0&0&-\bar{\alpha}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bar{\alpha}=\frac{n+m}{6}\left(\frac{1+\varepsilon rq}{r-q}\right)^{3}.

Clearly both halves of the Weyl tensor are of type D.
When
m=\pm n
one of the sides of the Weyl curvature vanishes, in this case the spacetimes are called self-dual Plebański-Demiański
[
117
]
.

8.6.9
Kerr Metric

To obtain the Kerr metric from the more general Plebański-Demiański family of metrics we set
\Lambda=0
. We want the metric to be asymptotically flat, requiring that
g_{rr}\rightarrow 1
as
r\rightarrow\infty
, where
g_{rr}=g_{\mu\nu}(\partial_{r})^{\mu}(\partial_{r})^{\nu}
is the radial component of the metric. For this to be the case the polynomial
C(r)
must be at most quadratic, which necessitates
\varepsilon=0
.
Now both
C,D
are simple quadratic functions

\displaystyle C(r)=r^{2}-2Mr-a^{2},\qquad D(q)=a^{2}-q^{2},

(8.157)

where we renamed the constant terms suggestively, and also set the NUT charge (vanishing in Kerr)
n=0
. Now
C(r)=\Delta
of the Kerr metric, and setting
q=a\cos\theta
makes
D=a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta
. These choices can be motivated using the requirement that the metric is an appropriate analytic continuation of a Lorentzian signature metric, and that the Lorentzian metric is axisymmetric, but we will not attempt this here. The main claim we are making is that the Euclidean Kerr metric is a member of a large family of metrics whose determination reduces to an exercise in linear algebra, determining the metric ansatz, following by a straightforward computation of curvatures using the Plebański formalism.

With these choices the metric
eq.

8.150
becomes

\displaystyle g=\frac{\Delta}{\rho^{2}}(d\tau-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta d\varphi)^{2}+\frac{\rho^{2}}{\Delta}dr^{2}+\rho^{2}d\theta^{2}+\frac{\sin^{2}\theta}{\rho^{2}}(ad\tau-ar^{2}d\varphi)^{2}.

(8.158)

Now further changing the coordinates

\displaystyle d\varphi=-\frac{1}{a}d\phi,\qquad d\tau=dt-ad\phi

(8.159)

gives the usual form of the Kerr metric in Boyer-Lindquist coordinates, with the frame given by
eq.

8.61
. The
\Lambda\not=0
Kerr metric is another member of the Plebański-Demiański family, but we have not attempted to exhibit it explicitly.

8.7
Discussion

We have presented an alternative derivation of the (Euclidean) Kerr metric, one that proceeds by deriving the more general Plebański-Demiański family. The derivation we described is “elementary” in the sense that the most non-trivial step of the construction, which is establishing the ansatz (
8.121
), proceeds by a calculation in linear algebra. Thus, one gets rather far without imposing any differential equations whatsoever. Some differential equations are imposed at the next step, which requires that the metric is conformal to two different Kähler metrics. This leads to the metric ansatz (
8.129
). The rest of the analysis consists in imposing Einstein equations. This is seen to reduce the arbitrariness to two quartic polynomials, each of one variable. One can then recognise the Euclidean Kerr metric in the obtained family of solutions without difficulty. It corresponds to the case of quadratic polynomials.

Our main reason to be interested in the geometry we described is the fact that the (Euclidean) Kerr metric exhibits the beautiful and rich geometry if viewed as a complex 4D manifold. This is of course also one of the motivations of
[
51
]
. It can also be seen from the derivation we presented that the Plebański chiral formalism is best-suited for viewing 4D Euclidean geometry via the prism of complex geometry. Most of what we described is not new for mathematicians, apart from our new characterisation of Killing vector fields in
section

8.3
and the new proof of Derdziński theorem in
section

8.4
. Some reasoning in section 6, in particular in the part where we impose Einstein equations, is also different from
[
51
]
. But our main motivation in describing these results is our hope that the adopted here point of view of the Plebański formalism will make these beautiful ideas more digestible for the gravitational physicists.

Chapter 9
Nonlinear Gauge Fixing

Plebański’s formulation of gravity places Einstein’s equations (EEs) in a form that resembles a gauge theory. Locally, two different groups act on this theory, the first is the diffeomorphism group which contains all coordinate transformations on the manifold. The second group is particular to the Plebański formulation and is the
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
group of rotations of the frame. To solve Einstein’s equations one has to fix a representative in both of these groups, this is known as gauge fixing. How exactly one should gauge fix is arbitrary, but there are some gauges with more desirable properties. In this Thesis we are ultimately interested in obtaining stable numerical schemes for Einstein’s equations, as such we consider first-order hyperbolic gauge fixings. See
chapter

10
for definitions of stable, well-posed and hyperbolic. Only the highest order in derivatives term contribute to the hyperbolicity, and therefore we can ignore the lower order terms when checking hyperbolicity. We develop the required modifications to Plebański’s equations of motion that produce the second-order wave operators for fundamental fields.

In
chapter

6
we considered the linearised version of this problem, and developed a first-order hyperbolic gauge fixing for Plebański’s formulation of gravity. There we had to introduce extra fields to control the diffeomorphism and rotation gauge, they arose as a 1-form and internal vector, that is
\xi\in\Lambda^{1}
and
\chi^{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
. The linearisation of the self-dual 2-forms
\Sigma^{i}
could be parametrised by an internal vector and symmetric tensor
h^{i},h_{\mu\nu}
, and its self-dual connection was
a^{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}
. Using a modified de Donder and Lorenz gauge it was possible to create a system whose second-order dynamics was the wave equation. Furthermore, these equations conformally separated into spaces of dimension
12
and
4
, each evolving independently. Here we present the nonlinear version of the same gauge fixing and produce as much as possible from the conformal separation. The linearised parametrisation of the self-dual 2-forms is no longer possible as there is no background with which to expand around, we instead must use the full 2-form.

We begin with a short overview of the Einstein’s equations in Plebański’s formulation. Then in
section

9.2
we compute the highest order structure of these equations to show that they are indeed not hyperbolic in the sense that the second-order wave operator structure is spoilt by the presence of other terms. After this, in
sections

9.3
and
9.4
, we develop the minimum gauge fixing required to obtain the wave equation for all the present fields. This, however, does not fix the
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
gauge which is done by introducing a modification to the de Donder and Lorenz gauge. The form of this modification is derived in
section

9.5
and the resulting system is presented in
section

9.6
. Some results pertaining to the conformal separation are available at the nonlinear level, these are explored in
section

9.7
and an evolution system based on the conformal separation is given in
section

9.8
.

9.1
First-Order Plebański Equations of Motion

We very briefly recap Einstein’s equations as imposed using the Plebański formulation. The main fields are the self-dual 2-forms and the connections,

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}\in\Lambda^{+},\quad A^{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\Lambda^{1}.

(9.1)

The 2-forms satisfy the metric and reality conditions
eqs.

4.3
and
4.5
. Einstein’s equation, in a vacuum, are then neatly encoded into the following first order system

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}=0,\quad d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=0.

(9.2)

where
F^{i}=dA^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\wedge A^{k}
is the curvature 2-form. The first equation in the above is Ricci flat condition on the self-dual curvature and the second defines
A^{i}
as the self-dual connection for
\Sigma^{i}
. This way of writing the equations is not at its most useful for the gauge fixing we consider here. Instead, we solve the second equation in the above for the self-dual connection. First by moving to components and taking the Hodge star of the second equation in
eq.

9.2
we find

\displaystyle 2(\star d^{A}\Sigma)^{i}_{\mu}=\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\nu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+\epsilon^{ijk}\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}A^{j}_{\nu}\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}=0.

(9.3)

Then by using the self-duality we can write this as

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\nu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}-2i\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}A^{k}_{\nu}=0.

(9.4)

Using the previously introduced operator
J_{1}:E\times\Lambda^{1}\rightarrow E\times\Lambda^{1}
we can write this as

\displaystyle\star d\Sigma^{i}=iJ_{1}(A)^{i}.

(9.5)

Then by applying
-iJ_{1}^{-1}
to both sides

\displaystyle A^{i}=-iJ_{1}^{-1}(\star d\Sigma)^{i}

(9.6)

expanding into components results in

\displaystyle A^{i}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{4}(\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}-\delta^{ik}\delta_{\mu}{}^{\nu})\epsilon_{\nu}{}^{\alpha\rho\sigma}\partial_{\alpha}\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}

(9.7)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{4}\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\nu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\Sigma^{k}_{\mu\sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}

(9.8)

We see that this is defined in a very similar way to the Christoffel symbols in that it is a linear combination of the partial derivatives of the metric variable, in this case the metric-like variable is
\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}
. The first-order system that imposes Einstein’s equations can then be written as

\displaystyle A^{i}=-iJ_{1}^{-1}(\star d\Sigma)^{i},\quad\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}=0.

(9.9)

From here we can start considering gauge fixings. Before that we compute the highest order symbol of the second-order dynamical equations implied by this system.

9.2
Hyperbolicity of Einstein’s Equations

We can now take our original Plebański system and compute the highest order terms arising to check that the equations are not hyperbolic already. The notion of hyperbolicity here refers to the second order equation containing a leading order term that resembles the wave equation. Similarly to the linear story we will see there is a tensor, arising as a nonlinear version of the de Donder 4-vector, that destroys the hyperbolicity.

To begin we compute the components of the curvature using
eq.

9.8
and keeping only the highest order terms

\displaystyle F^{i}_{\mu\nu}=2\partial_{[\mu}A^{i}_{\nu]}=\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho[\mu}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu]\sigma}-\frac{i}{2}\epsilon_{[\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\nu]\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}.

(9.10)

Not that we have introduced the notation
\partial_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}=\partial_{(\mu}\partial_{\nu)}
and later
\partial^{2}=\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
. The reason being is that at the level of the leading order terms,
\partial_{\mu\nu}
behaves as a single derivative and satisfies the same product rule

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu\nu}(fg)=g\partial_{\mu\nu}f+f\partial_{\mu\nu}g+{\rm lower\ order\ terms}.

(9.11)

The metric can then be used to raise and lower either index as one would for the single derivative. Substituting the curvature into the Einstein condition we find

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}=

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}\left(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial^{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\sigma\alpha}\right)

(9.12)

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu\sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\nu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}\right)

(9.13)

\displaystyle+\partial_{\nu}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}\right)

(9.14)

\displaystyle-\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu)\rho}-\Sigma^{i}_{\rho(\mu}\partial^{\rho\sigma}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu)\sigma}.

(9.15)

In this form it is difficult to extract anything meaningful. The result is symmetric in
\mu\nu
as the antisymmetric part is equal to
\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}\wedge F^{k}
and this vanishes whenever
A^{i}
is coming from its definition in
eq.

9.9
. However, there are a collection of identities derived in
appendix

A
which can be used to simplify the above. The identities are derived by considering only the highest order structure, here we will state the identities without derivation. To begin we use the following

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial^{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=6\partial^{2}\ln(\sqrt{-g})

(9.16)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\sigma\alpha}=-\partial^{\rho\sigma}g_{\rho\sigma}-\partial^{2}\ln(\sqrt{-g})

(9.17)

which allows us to simplify the terms in brackets on the first line

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial^{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}{}^{\alpha}\Sigma^{i}_{\sigma\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\partial^{2}\ln(\sqrt{-g})-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}\ln(\sqrt{-g})-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{\rho\sigma}g_{\rho\sigma}

(9.18)

\displaystyle=\partial^{2}\ln(\sqrt{-g})+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\rho\sigma}g^{\rho\sigma}.

(9.19)

For the second and third lines we can use

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\sigma}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\ln(\sqrt{-g})-g_{\mu\rho}\partial_{\sigma}g^{\rho\sigma}

(9.20)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\partial_{\mu}\ln(\sqrt{-g})

(9.21)

to find

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\Sigma^{i\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\ln(\sqrt{-g})-g_{\mu\rho}\partial_{\sigma}g^{\rho\sigma}.

(9.22)

Using these in Einstein’s equations gives

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}=

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}\left(\partial^{2}\ln(\sqrt{-g})+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\rho\sigma}g^{\rho\sigma}\right)

(9.23)

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu}\ln(\sqrt{-g})-g_{\nu\rho}\partial_{\sigma}g^{\rho\sigma}\right)

(9.24)

\displaystyle+\partial_{\nu}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\ln(\sqrt{-g})-g_{\mu\rho}\partial_{\sigma}g^{\rho\sigma}\right)

(9.25)

\displaystyle-\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu)\rho}-\Sigma^{i}_{\rho(\mu}\partial^{\rho\sigma}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu)\sigma}.

(9.26)

The final line in the above can be simplified using the following identities

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu)\rho}

\displaystyle=\partial^{2}g_{\mu\nu}+g_{\mu\nu}\partial^{2}\ln(\sqrt{-g})

(9.27)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\rho(\mu}\partial^{\rho\sigma}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu)\sigma}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}(g_{\nu\rho}\partial_{\sigma}g^{\rho\sigma})-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu}(g_{\mu\rho}\partial_{\sigma}g^{\rho\sigma})+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho\sigma}g^{\rho\sigma}-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}g_{\mu\nu}.

(9.28)

Substituting these into the original equation, and after many cancellations, the result greatly simplifies to

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}g_{\mu\nu}+\partial_{(\mu}\Gamma_{\nu)},\quad\Gamma_{\mu}=-\partial_{\mu}\ln(\sqrt{-g})-g_{\mu\sigma}\partial_{\rho}g^{\rho\sigma}=-\underaccent{\tilde}{g}_{\mu\rho}\partial_{\sigma}\tilde{g}^{\rho\sigma}.

(9.29)

Where
\Gamma^{\mu}=\Gamma^{\mu}_{\rho\sigma}g^{\rho\sigma}
and
\Gamma^{\mu}=\Box x^{\mu}=0
is the Harmonic condition. The Harmonic condition is the nonlinear version of the de Donder gauge. We have also introduced the notation
\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}=\sqrt{-g}g^{\mu\nu}
and
\underaccent{\tilde}{g}_{\mu\nu}=g_{\mu\nu}/\sqrt{-g}
. The 2-forms
\Sigma^{i}
contain the metric plus a frame, it is clear that Einstein’s equation are then equations on the metric part of this 2-form. Using the fact that, in this notation,
(\delta g)_{\mu\nu}=2\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}hg_{\mu\nu}
and linearising the Harmonic condition we see that it reproduces the linear gauge fixing
eq.

6.44
. From this we see that the highest order structure of the equations is closely related to the linearisation, which is perhaps unsurprising as they are very similar calculations. We can now suggest nonlinear equations of motion to remove the unwanted terms to the highest order, keeping in mind that the linearisations should match those in the previous section. In fact, due to the similarities, the nonlinear gauge can almost be read directly from the linear gauges.

9.3
Highest Order Harmonic Gauge

From
eq.

9.29
we can see that
\Gamma^{\mu}=0
would make the equations hyperbolic. To impose this, as in the linear case, we introduce a first-order field to impose the condition dynamically. Let
\xi\in\Lambda^{1}
be a connection 1-form, we can modify the equations of motion such that

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}+\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}=0.

(9.30)

When supplemented with

\displaystyle\xi_{\mu}=-\Gamma_{\mu}

(9.31)

and the original equation for
A^{i}
we obtain a hyperbolic second order partial differential equation for
g_{\mu\nu}
to highest order

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}+\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}g_{\mu\nu}.

(9.32)

When
\xi_{\mu}=0
we recover the Harmonic conditions and the modifications to the Ricci flat condition are removed. Hence, use the gauge fixed Einstein’s equations along with
\xi=-\Gamma
, as long as
\xi=0
is imposed somehow. This provides a hyperbolic second-order equation for the metric components in
\Sigma^{i}
, however, for the
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
frame we require a different gauge fixing.

9.4
Highest Order Modified Lorenz Gauge

Taking inspiration from the linear case we can fix the
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
freedom by imposing the Lorenz condition non-linearly

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}A^{i}_{\mu}=g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A^{i}_{\nu}=0

(9.33)

Using our nonlinear definition for
A^{i}
,
eq.

9.8
, we can compute the Lorenz condition to leading order and find

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}A^{i}_{\mu}=-\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial^{2}\Sigma^{k}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\rho\sigma}\partial^{\alpha}{}_{\rho}\Sigma^{k}_{\sigma\alpha}.

(9.34)

The last term in the above can be manipulated to reveal

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\rho\sigma}\partial^{\alpha}{}_{\rho}\Sigma^{k}_{\sigma\alpha}=

\displaystyle-\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\left(\partial_{\nu}\ln(\sqrt{-g})+g_{\nu\rho}\partial_{\sigma}g^{\rho\sigma}\right)-\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial^{2}\Sigma^{k}_{\mu\nu}

(9.35)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Gamma_{\nu}-\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial^{2}\Sigma^{k}_{\mu\nu}.

(9.36)

In which case the Lorenz condition becomes

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}A^{i}_{\mu}=-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Gamma_{\nu}-\frac{1}{8}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial^{2}\Sigma^{k}_{\mu\nu}.

(9.37)

This suggests a modification to the gauge choice, whose linear structure matches that gauge fixing found in
chapter

6
, which we call the modified Lorenz gauge

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}A^{i}_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}=0.

(9.38)

Then clearly with
\xi_{\mu}=-\Gamma_{\mu}
we have

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}A^{i}_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}=-\frac{1}{8}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial^{2}\Sigma^{k}_{\mu\nu}.

(9.39)

Which has the wave operator as its leading order term. Computing the linearisation of the right-hand side also confirms that this is the wave equation on the perturbation of the
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
frame. This condition combined with the Harmonic gauge converts the leading order symbol for Einstein’s equations to be that of the wave operator acting on all the components in the 2-form
\Sigma^{i}
.

9.5
Modified Harmonic and Connections

As in the linear case we can count the same number of components in the frame and connection sector to find
13
in
\Sigma^{i}
and
16
in the connections
A^{i}
and
\xi
. Since the 16 connections are defined by 13 frame variables we will find that in the numerical scheme that some equations become constraints instead of evolution equations. Specifically in this case we will find 3 Gauss constraints related to the
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
gauge. The desired numerical scheme will evolve the constraints instead of imposing them as additional conditions. To achieve this at the covariant level we introduce an internal vector
\chi^{i}
which we interpret as being part of the frame. This matches the number of components in the frame and connection sectors, at the linear level this allowed for the construction of the hyperbolic Dirac operator. We will see that a similar construction is available at the nonlinear level, and modifications can be made to the previously described gauges that give
\chi^{i}
a hyperbolic second-order equations of motion.

The first modification is to the definition of the self-dual connection, we add all the possible terms that contains a derivative of
\chi^{i}
to find

\displaystyle A^{i}

\displaystyle=-iJ_{1}^{-1}(\star d\Sigma)^{i}+c_{1}d\chi^{i}+c_{2}J_{1}(d\chi)^{i}

(9.40)

which in components is

\displaystyle A^{i}_{\mu}=\frac{i}{4}\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\nu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\Sigma^{k}_{\mu\sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}+c_{1}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}.

(9.41)

The intrinsic torsion of the self-dual 2-form has changed due to this gauge fixing, performing a computation similar to that in the proof
section

4.6
we find that the intrinsic torsion is now

\displaystyle-T^{i}=A^{i}-c_{1}d\chi^{i}-c_{2}J_{1}(d\chi)^{i}.

(9.42)

This means that the metric covariant derivative of the self-dual 2-forms is then

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=-\epsilon^{ijk}\left(A^{j}_{\mu}-c_{1}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{j}-c_{2}J_{1}(d\chi)^{j}_{\mu}\right)\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}.

(9.43)

Or that the total covariant derivative is

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}^{A}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=\epsilon^{ijk}(c_{1}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{j}+c_{2}J_{1}(d\chi)^{j}_{\mu})\Sigma^{k}_{\rho\sigma}.

(9.44)

The only possible modification to the connection 1-form is

\displaystyle\xi_{\mu}=-\Gamma_{\mu}+c_{3}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}.

(9.45)

Here,
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\in\mathbb{C}
are constants. This is the simplest nonlinear modification that reproduces the linear modification. The structure is exactly the same as the linear case, however, the
\Sigma^{i}
here are assumed to be dynamical instead of the flat Minkowski 2-forms. Contributions from
\partial\Sigma^{i}
terms have already been computed, and the highest order terms are linear in their arguments. Therefore, we can consider

\displaystyle A^{i}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=c_{1}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\chi^{k}

(9.46)

\displaystyle\xi_{\mu}

\displaystyle=c_{3}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}.

(9.47)

We are able to separate the solutions this way as the highest order terms are linear in their arguments. Substituting the above into the gauge fixed Einstein’s equations,
eq.

9.30
, we find each term to be

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}

\displaystyle=c_{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\partial^{2}\chi^{i}+2c_{2}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu)\rho}\chi^{i}

(9.48)

\displaystyle\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}

\displaystyle=c_{3}\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu)\rho}\chi^{i}.

(9.49)

Combining we find

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}+\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}=c_{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}\partial^{2}\chi^{i}+(c_{3}+2c_{2})\Sigma^{i}_{(\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\nu)\rho}\chi^{i}.

(9.50)

The correct choice of constants is then
c_{3}=-2c_{2}
, which again matches the linear story. The modified Lorenz condition in this case remains hyperbolic

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}A^{i}_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}=(c_{2}-c_{1})\partial^{2}\chi^{i}.

(9.51)

9.6
Gauge Fixed Plebański System

Collecting all the gauge fixing results together we find our first-order hyperbolic equations of motion for Plebański’s formulation of gravity are

\displaystyle A^{i}=-iJ_{1}^{-1}(\star d\Sigma)^{i}+c_{1}d\chi^{i}+c_{2}J_{1}(d\chi)^{i},\quad\xi_{\mu}=-\Gamma_{\mu}-2c_{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}

(9.52)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}+\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}=0,\quad\partial^{\mu}A^{i}_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}=0.

(9.53)

The dynamical fields are then
\Sigma^{i},\chi^{i}
which we call the frame variables, along with the connection variables
A^{i},\xi
. The connections are defined as linear combinations of the derivatives of the frame fields. Einstein’s equations on these connections are then first-order differential equations. The second-order evolution equations for the frame, implied by the above system, have the wave operator as their leading order symbols. The fields
\chi^{i}
and
\xi
are 3 and 4 components that are introduced to dynamically control the
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
and diffeomorphism gauges. The first equation is a modified definition for the self-dual connection, the second is a modification to the Harmonic condition involving
\chi^{i}
. Einstein’s equations is adjusted with derivatives of
\xi
and the final equation is a modified Lorenz condition on the self-dual connection. This kind of modification to Einstein’s equations has appeared in the literature before and has been used to prove that Einstein’s equations are well-posed in
[
118
,
119
]
and in other numerical works, see
[
120
,
121
]
. As for the Lorenz condition this appears in the context of Yang-Mills theories where it is well known that it produces wave operator, e.g. see chapter 8 of
[
68
]
. In numerical relativity the Lorenz condition has appeared in implementations of the tetrad formalism
[
122
]
.

It is clear that in order to recover the original system we must also impose
\xi=0=\chi^{i}
such that the changes to the original equations of motion are undone. At the level of the Hamiltonian these new fields arise as conjugate momentum for the Lagrange multipliers, therefore, the primary constraints of the theory require them to vanish. It is also the reason why we can add these terms to the equations of motion without any worry, as the modifications keep the first-class structure of the primary and secondary constraints. The reason we have not already set the new fields to zero is because as an evolution system removing
\xi
or
\chi^{i}
would destroy the hyperbolicity and therefore cause the system to be ill-posed. Instead, we notice that since every field satisfies the wave equation we can impose
\partial_{t}\xi=\xi=0=\chi^{i}=\partial_{t}\chi^{i}
for the initial data, and analytically it will remain true for all times. This is exactly the same mechanism that is used in the chiral Maxwell gauge fixing. This setup does require that the initial data be a solution to the original Einstein equations, and this is not always possible to find for every physical setup. Instead, as we will discuss in more detail in
chapter

10
, it may be useful to add damping terms to achieve this. Damping terms can be added to this gauge fixing as they occur at a lower order and can be added such that Einstein’s equations are recovered as the additional fields tend to zero. In practice, we can add any terms containing
\xi
and
\chi^{i}
as long it can be shown that numerically these quantities tend to zero, or in some cases to a constant, sufficiently quickly such that true solutions to Einstein’s equations are recovered in a reasonable time interval.

One can also notice that this system no longer transforms covariantly under diffeomorphism transformations. This is most notably a problem in spherical polar coordinates as some objects have angular dependence that clearly breaks the spherical symmetry. In some cases the lack of covariance will introduce equations of motion that destroy the system and force the metric to be degenerate. We briefly describe how each field transforms under diffeomorphisms, to see where the problem occurs. Under diffeomorphism transformations we know that the frame variables all transform as tensors by definition, the self-dual connection also transforms covariantly. However, the Harmonic 1-form
\Gamma_{\mu}
is not a tensor under diffeomorphisms as it is constructed from the Christoffel symbols which are themselves not tensors. While this is not necessarily an issue as this term exists to fix the diffeomorphism gauge, as mentioned in spherical symmetry this condition is not well-defined. To resolve this and restore covariance we follow
[
123
,
124
]
and introduce a non-dynamical reference metric
\bar{g}_{\mu\nu}
. This reference metric is fixed to be the Minkowski metric in whichever coordinate system is being used. We can then compute its Christoffel symbols
\bar{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\rho\sigma}
which are in general not zero since they have different values for different choices of coordinates, and there may exist a coordinate system where they are not zero. This can then be used to construct the tensor,

\displaystyle\Delta\Gamma_{\mu}=\Gamma_{\mu}-g^{\rho\sigma}g_{\mu\nu}\bar{\Gamma}^{\nu}_{\rho\sigma}.

(9.54)

Then whenever
\Gamma_{\mu}
appears we can replace it with
\Delta\Gamma_{\mu}
without changing the physical dynamics. In the usual Cartesian coordinates,
x^{\mu}=(t,x,y,z)
, it is easy to see that
\bar{\Gamma}=0
and so the original Harmonic condition is recovered. We call this the covariant Harmonic 1-form.

The other noncovariant term is in the modification to the Einstein condition,
\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}
, the use of a partial derivative destroys the covariance of this term. It is possible to promote this to covariant derivatives, this can be recognised as the Z4 equations
[
120
]
which have been widely successful in numerical relativity. However, as we will see in later chapters, promoting the derivative in this way can destroy the polynomial structure that occurs in the 3+1 decomposition of this system. We argue that as this term should vanish for suitable schemes then it should not produce a problem during the numerical evolution. Therefore, we choose to leave this term as partial derivative. The other noncovariant equation is the Lorenz gauge where partial derivatives are employed instead of metric covariant derivatives. For the same reason we leave this term as a partial derivative as the structure of the evolution system is simpler in this choice. A brief look into the numerical performance of these choices will be presented in
chapter

11
.

9.7
Nonlinear Gauge Conformal Separation

In
chapter

6
we presented a splitting on the field equations into first-order sectors of size 4 and 12. In this section we show here that a slightly weaker result is obtainable with minimal modification to
eq.

9.53
. It is possible to obtain a first order system of 12 frame and connection variables that is separate from the remaining 4 component sector. However, the 4 sector still depends on 12, this is due to the fact that the full metric is a mix of the 12 and 4 component of the full frame. It was seen that the conformal transformations were stored in the 4 component part and the rest remained in the 12 component sector. Using this knowledge we develop a conformal transformation for the nonlinear fields that separates the gauge fixed equations of motion.

9.7.1
Connection Definition Conformal Transformation

We begin by performing a conformal decomposition of the frame variables. We denote the metric related to the 2-forms
\Sigma^{i}
by
g
, then by introducing a scalar,
\lambda=1/\sqrt{\sqrt{-\det(g)}}
, we can define a new metric with unit determinant,

\displaystyle g_{H}=\lambda g

(9.55)

such that
\det(g_{H})=-1
. The 2-forms that correspond to this conformal metric are

\displaystyle H^{i}=\lambda\Sigma^{i}\in\Lambda^{+}\subset\Lambda^{2}

(9.56)

and
g_{H}
is recovered from
H^{i}
using the Urbantke metric. Due to the conformal invariance of the Hodge star on middle degree forms,
H^{i}
and
\Sigma^{i}
are self-dual under both metrics. We note that the spacetime indices on
H^{i}_{\mu\nu}
are raised and lowered using
g_{H}
, such that it satisfies a quaternion algebra with this metric

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\mu\rho}H^{j\rho}{}_{\nu}=-\delta^{ij}g_{H}{}_{\mu\nu}+\epsilon^{ijk}H^{k}_{\mu\nu}.

(9.57)

All other identities follow from the replacement of
(\Sigma^{i},g)\rightarrow(H^{i},g_{H})
.

Next we can look at the effect of this conformal transformation on the definitions of the connections in
eq.

9.52
. We note that
\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}=H^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}
as they are conformally invariant, therefore
J_{1}
is also unchanged by any conformal transformation. The modified self-dual connection becomes

\displaystyle A^{i}

\displaystyle=-iJ_{1}^{-1}\left(\star d\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}H^{i}\right)\right)+c_{1}d\chi^{i}+c_{2}J_{1}(d\chi)^{i}

(9.58)

\displaystyle=iJ_{1}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\star\left(d\log(\lambda)\wedge H^{i}\right)\right)-iJ_{1}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\star dH^{i}\right)+c_{1}d\chi^{i}+c_{2}J_{1}(d\chi)^{i}.

(9.59)

Where
\star
is the Hodge star for
g
. Using the conformal transformation we can relate the conformal Hodge star
\star_{H}
to usual Hodge star
\star
. Indeed, when
\star
is acting on a 3-form we find

\displaystyle(\star T)_{\mu}=\epsilon{}_{\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}T_{\nu\rho\sigma}=g{}_{\mu\alpha}\epsilon^{\alpha\nu\rho\sigma}T_{\nu\rho\sigma}=\frac{1}{\lambda}g_{H}{}_{\mu\nu}\sqrt{-g}\epsilon^{\alpha\nu\rho\sigma}_{H}T_{\nu\rho\sigma}=\lambda\epsilon_{H}{}_{\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}T_{\nu\rho\sigma}=\lambda(\star_{H}T)_{\mu}

(9.60)

where we have used the definition of
\lambda
. The self-dual connection simplifies slightly to become

\displaystyle A^{i}

\displaystyle=iJ_{1}^{-1}\left(\star_{H}\left(d\log(\lambda)\wedge H^{i}\right)\right)-iJ_{1}^{-1}\left(\star_{H}dH^{i}\right)+c_{1}d\chi^{i}+c_{2}J_{1}(d\chi)^{i}.

(9.61)

For the first term we find the following

\displaystyle\star_{H}(d\log(\lambda)\wedge H^{i})_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{H\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\nu}\log(\lambda)H^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=iH^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\log(\lambda)=i(H^{i}\cdot d\log(\lambda))_{\mu}

(9.62)

In the above we have used the notation
(H^{i}\cdot\theta)_{\mu}=H^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\theta_{\nu}
, and later we will use the same notation with
\Sigma^{i}
. Next, we recall that
J_{1}^{-1}=\frac{1}{2}(J_{1}-1)
and
J_{1}(H^{i}\cdot X)=2H^{i}\cdot X
, which can be checked using the quaternion algebra and that
\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}=H^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}
, to show that
J_{1}^{-1}(H^{i}\cdot X)=\frac{1}{2}H^{i}\cdot X
. The conformal decomposition of the self-dual connection is then

\displaystyle A^{i}=-\frac{1}{2}H^{i}\cdot d\log(\lambda)-iJ_{1}^{-1}(\star_{H}dH^{i})+c_{1}d\chi^{i}+c_{2}J_{1}(d\chi)^{i}.

(9.63)

The same conformal transformation can be applied to the connection 1-form,
\xi
. Using the standard formula for the conformal transformation of the Christoffel symbols reveals

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}=g^{\rho\sigma}g_{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\nu}{}_{\rho\sigma}=g_{H}^{\rho\sigma}g_{H\mu\nu}\Gamma_{H}^{\nu}{}_{\rho\sigma}+\partial_{\mu}\log(\lambda)=\Gamma_{H\mu}+\partial_{\mu}\log(\lambda).

(9.64)

Applying this to the connection 1-form becomes

\displaystyle\xi=-\Gamma_{H}-d\log(\lambda)-2c_{2}H^{i}\cdot d\chi^{i}.

(9.65)

As in the linear case we introduce two special combinations of the self-dual and 1-form connections,

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}=A^{i}-\frac{1}{2}H^{i}\cdot\xi,\quad\omega=\xi+2H^{i}\cdot A^{i}.

(9.66)

Recall that
H^{i}\cdot=\Sigma^{i}\cdot
as they are conformally invariant, so this is equivalent to the definitions in
chapter

6
.
These can be inverted to find

\displaystyle A^{i}=\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{i}+\frac{1}{2}J_{1}(\Omega)^{i}-\frac{1}{4}H^{i}\cdot\omega,\quad\xi=-\frac{1}{2}\omega+H^{i}\cdot\Omega^{i}

(9.67)

which gives a way to convert between the two connections. Computing the first connection in
eq.

9.66
using the conformal transformation we find

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}H^{i}\cdot d\log(\lambda)-iJ_{1}^{-1}(\star_{H}dH^{i})+c_{1}d\chi^{i}+c_{2}J_{1}(d\chi)^{i}

(9.68)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\left(-H^{i}\cdot\Gamma_{H}-H^{i}\cdot d\log(\lambda)+2c_{2}d\chi^{i}+2c_{2}J_{1}(d\chi)^{i}\right)

\displaystyle=-iJ_{1}^{-1}(\star_{H}dH^{i})+\frac{1}{2}H^{i}\cdot\Gamma_{H}+(c_{1}-c_{2})d\chi^{i}.

(9.69)

The second of the new connections can be derived using
H^{i}\cdot J_{1}^{-1}(A)^{i}=\frac{1}{2}H^{i}\cdot A^{i}
to give

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=-\Gamma_{H}-d\log(\lambda)-2c_{2}H^{i}\cdot d\chi^{i}+2\left(\frac{3}{2}d\log(\lambda)-\frac{i}{2}H^{i}\cdot\star_{H}dH^{i}+(c_{1}+2c_{2})H^{i}\cdot d\chi^{i}\right)

\displaystyle=-\Gamma_{H}+2H^{i}\cdot d\log(\lambda)-iH^{i}\cdot\star_{H}dH^{i}+2(c_{1}+c_{2})H^{i}\cdot d\chi^{i}

(9.70)

9.7.2
Conformal Connection

To summarise, the conformal connections are defined using the conformal frame variables
H^{i},\lambda,\chi^{i}
in the following way

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}

\displaystyle=-iJ_{1}^{-1}(\star_{H}dH^{i})+\frac{1}{2}H^{i}\cdot\Gamma_{H}+(c_{1}-c_{2})d\chi^{i}

(9.71)

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=-\Gamma_{H}+2d\log(\lambda)-iH^{i}\cdot\star_{H}dH^{i}+2(c_{1}+c_{2})H^{i}\cdot d\chi^{i}

(9.72)

One specific choice of
c_{1},c_{2}
deserves some more attention. Similarly to the linear case we see that when
c_{2}=c_{1}=c
then the 12 connection,
\Omega^{i}
, moreover, as we require
c\neq 0
such that not all the gauge fixing is removed, we can scale
\chi^{i}
such that
c=1
can always be chosen and the connection equations become

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}

\displaystyle=-iJ_{1}^{-1}(\star_{H}dH^{i})+\frac{1}{2}H^{i}\cdot\Gamma_{H}

(9.73)

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=-\Gamma_{H}+2d\log(\lambda)-iH^{i}\cdot\star_{H}dH^{i}+4H^{i}\cdot d\chi^{i}.

(9.74)

Counting the number of components in
H^{i}
we find it contains a metric with determinant 1 and the
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
frame which constitutes
10-1+3=12
components The 4 remaining components are accounted for by
\lambda,\chi^{i}
. This suggests that the 12,4 split is also available at the nonlinear level, albeit a weaker version where the 12 components are independent of the 4 components, but the reverse is not true.

9.7.3
Connection Evolution Conformal Transformation

The conformal separation of the new connection variables is expected to be preserved when rewriting Einstein’s equations and the Lorenz gauge with the new connections. Here, we will show that, by changing the lower order terms in
eq.

9.53
and slightly modifying the higher order terms, the separation of the 12 dimensional sector is achievable at the nonlinear level.

We start by making an educated guess as to the equations of motion for
\Omega^{i},\omega
we then show that this is indeed a gauge fixing of Einstein’s equations. Looking at
eq.

6.80
we define the equations of motion for the conformal connection to be

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\rho}F(\Omega)^{i}_{\nu\rangle\rho}=0,\quad g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}=0

(9.75)

\displaystyle H^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}=0,\quad g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}=0.

(9.76)

Where
F(\Omega)^{i}=d\Omega^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Omega^{j}\wedge\Omega^{k}
, however we expect to modify these equations of motion with potential terms later. All that needs to be checked is that these equations are equivalent to Einstein’s equations plus terms involving the primary constraints
\xi,\chi^{i}
, then this system is equivalent to the original equations of motion on the constraint surface.

9.7.4
\Omega^{i}
Connection Equations

Beginning with the first equation in
eq.

9.75
we use
eq.

9.66
to expand the curvature,

\displaystyle F(\Omega)^{i}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=F(A)^{i}_{\mu\nu}+\partial^{A}_{[\mu}\left(\Sigma^{i}_{\nu]}{}^{\rho}\xi_{\rho}\right)+\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{[\mu}{}^{\rho}\Sigma^{k}_{\nu]}{}^{\sigma}\xi_{\rho}\xi_{\sigma}

(9.77)

\displaystyle=F(A)^{i}_{\mu\nu}+\partial^{A}_{[\mu}\left(\Sigma^{i}_{\nu]}{}^{\rho}\xi_{\rho}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{[\mu}{}^{\rho}\xi_{\nu]}\xi_{\rho}

(9.78)

which when substituting into the equation of motion results in

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\rho}F(\Omega)^{i}_{\nu\rangle\rho}

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\rho}F(\Omega)^{i}_{\nu\rangle\rho}=\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\rho}F(A)^{i}_{\nu\rangle\rho}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{A}_{\nu\rangle}\left(\Sigma^{i}_{\rho}{}^{\sigma}\xi_{\sigma}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\rho}\partial^{A}_{\rho}\left(\Sigma^{i}_{\nu\rangle}{}^{\sigma}\xi_{\sigma}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\xi_{\langle\mu}\xi_{\nu\rangle}

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\rho}F(A)^{i}_{\nu\rangle\rho}+\partial_{\langle\mu}\xi_{\nu\rangle}+\frac{1}{2}\xi_{\langle\mu}\xi_{\nu\rangle}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu|}{}^{\rho}\xi_{\sigma}\partial^{A}_{|\nu\rangle}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho}{}^{\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\langle\mu|}{}^{\rho}\xi_{\sigma}\partial^{A}_{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{|\nu\rangle}{}^{\sigma}.

(9.79)

Performing a similar expansion for the second equation in
eq.

9.75
gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}=g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}=g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A^{i}_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\left(\Sigma^{i}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}\xi_{\rho}\right)=g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A^{i}_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\xi_{\rho}\partial_{\mu}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}=0.

(9.80)

We see that in both cases we obtain the previously derived gauge condition with an extra term proportional to
\xi_{\mu}
. This means that when on the constraint surface, that is
\xi_{\mu}=0
, these additional terms vanish and Einstein’s equations are recovered. The presence of the derivatives of
\Sigma^{i}
do not change the hyperbolicity as these will only enter at the first-order level in the second-order equations of motion for the frame variables. For the new Lorenz condition the same is structure is present and upon removing the constraint terms we are left with the usual Lorenz gauge.

In conclusion for the
\Omega^{i}
variable we have seen that the proposed evolution equations are indeed modifications to the Einstein’s equations (and Lorenz condition) that return Einstein solutions when the constraints are satisfied.

9.7.5
\omega
Connection Equations

We now turn to the 4 dimensional part of the connection. Starting with the equation of motion
g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}=0
, we show that it is a modification of part of Einstein’s equations.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}

\displaystyle=g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}=g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+2g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\left(\Sigma^{i}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}A^{i}_{\rho}\right)

\displaystyle=g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+2\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A^{i}_{\nu}+2A^{i}_{\rho}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Sigma^{i}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}.

(9.81)

Using the previous result for the last term we can write this as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}

\displaystyle=g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+2\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A^{i}_{\nu}-2A^{i}_{\mu}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\Omega^{k}_{\nu}

(9.82)

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}F(A)^{i}_{\mu\nu}+g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}-\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}A^{j}_{\mu}A^{k}_{\nu}-2A^{i}_{\mu}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\Omega^{k}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}F(A)^{i}_{\mu\nu}+g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\xi_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+g^{\mu\nu}\left(\frac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}+\Omega^{i}_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}\right)-\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\omega_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}.

(9.83)

Going from the second to the third line in the above we have used the definitions of the new connections. The potential part contain terms of the form
A^{i}A^{j}
, these will change the dynamics from being Einstein. To remove them we simply use the last line in the above to change the equation of motion in the connection variables to be

\displaystyle g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}-g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\left(\frac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}+\Omega^{i}_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}\right)+H^{i\mu\nu}\omega_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}=0.

(9.84)

Hence, the unwanted potential terms are no longer there at the cost of a slightly more complicated equation of motion. This involves both of the 12 and 4 component connections, this mixing of sectors is not unsurprising as the same occurs for the definition of the connections. Lastly, the final evolution equation for the connections is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}H^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}=\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+2\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\left(\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}A^{j}_{\rho}\right)=\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+2\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial^{A}_{\mu}\left(\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}A^{j}_{\rho}\right)

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}-2g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A^{i}_{\nu}-2\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\partial^{A}_{\mu}A^{k}_{\nu}+2A^{j}_{\rho}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial^{A}_{\mu}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}-2g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A^{i}_{\nu}-\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}F^{k}_{\mu\nu}-\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\epsilon^{klm}A^{l}_{\mu}A^{m}_{\nu}+2A^{j}_{\rho}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial^{A}_{\mu}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}.

(9.85)

Here we can derive another useful identity, using
eqs.

9.44
and
9.52
, to find

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial^{A}_{\mu}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\nabla^{A}_{\mu}\Sigma^{j}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}+\delta^{ij}g^{\mu\sigma}\Gamma_{\mu\sigma}^{\rho}

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\epsilon^{jlm}\left(c\partial_{\mu}\chi^{l}+cJ_{1}(d\chi)^{l}_{\mu}\right)\Sigma^{m}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}+\delta^{ij}\Gamma^{\rho}

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\epsilon^{jlm}\left(c\partial_{\mu}\chi^{l}+cJ_{1}(d\chi)^{l}_{\mu}\right)\Sigma^{m}_{\nu}{}^{\rho}-\delta^{ij}(\xi^{\rho}+2c\Sigma^{k\rho\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k})

\displaystyle=-\delta^{ij}\xi^{\rho}

(9.86)

Which means that the last term in the equation of motion contains
\xi_{\mu}
and as such does not contribute on the constraint surface. Another way of seeing the same result is to note that
\nabla^{A}_{\mu}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=0
on the constraint surface as all the equations return to their unmodified form. We can then safely ignore that term and write the connection squared terms as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}H^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}

\displaystyle\simeq-2\left(g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A^{i}_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}\right)-\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}F^{k}_{\mu\nu}-\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\epsilon^{klm}A^{l}_{\mu}A^{m}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=-2g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}-\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}F^{k}_{\mu\nu}-\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\epsilon^{klm}A^{l}_{\mu}A^{m}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=-\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}F^{k}_{\mu\nu}-g^{\mu\nu}\omega_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{k\mu\nu}\omega_{\mu}\Omega^{j}_{\nu}+\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}\Omega^{i}_{\mu}\Omega^{j}_{\nu}.

(9.87)

The first term in the last line is proportional to
d^{A}d^{A}\Sigma^{i}
, and as
d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=...d\chi^{i}
where the right-hand side contains
\chi^{i}
which vanishes on the constraint surface. Knowing this implies that
\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j\mu\nu}F^{k}_{\mu\nu}
also vanishes on the constraint surface. Therefore, the equation of motion becomes

\displaystyle H^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}+g^{\mu\nu}\omega_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}H^{k\mu\nu}\omega_{\mu}\Omega^{j}_{\nu}-H^{j\mu\nu}\Omega^{i}_{\mu}\Omega^{j}_{\nu}=0

(9.88)

which is proportional to the algebraic constraint
d^{A}d^{A}\Sigma^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}F^{j}\wedge\Sigma^{k}=0
plus additional terms proportional to the constraints
\xi_{\mu},\chi^{i}
.

Summarising the connection equations of motion we have

\displaystyle g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}=g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\left(\frac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}+\Omega^{i}_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}\right)-H^{i\mu\nu}\omega_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}

(9.89)

\displaystyle H^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}+g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\omega_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}H^{k\mu\nu}\omega_{\mu}\Omega^{j}_{\nu}-H^{j\mu\nu}\Omega^{i}_{\mu}\Omega^{j}_{\nu}

(9.90)

\displaystyle g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}=0,\quad H^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\rho}F(\Omega)^{i}_{\nu\rangle\rho}=0.

(9.91)

By comparing these equations with the linearisations in
chapter

6
we see that the highest order terms match, the only difference is the addition of some connection squared terms in the 4 dimensional sector.

9.8
Conformal System

Here we gather the equations of motion for the conformal Plebański system. The basic variables for the frame are the triple of 2-forms
H^{i}\in\Lambda^{+}\subset\Lambda^{2}
, a scalar function
\lambda
and an internal vector
\chi^{i}
. The triple of 2-forms satisfies
H^{i}\wedge H^{j}=2i\delta^{ij}d^{4}x
such that the metric they describe has constant determinant. The physical metric is then
g=\frac{1}{\lambda}g_{H}
where
g_{H}
is the metric defined by
H^{i}
. By taking derivatives of the frame variables we are able to define the conformal connections,

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}

\displaystyle=-iJ_{1}^{-1}(\star_{H}dH^{i})+\frac{1}{2}H^{i}\cdot\Gamma_{H}

(9.92)

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=-\Gamma_{H}+2d\log(\lambda)-iH^{i}\cdot\star_{H}dH^{i}+4cH^{i}\cdot d\chi^{i}.

Where
(\Gamma_{H})_{\mu}=g_{H\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}g_{H}^{\rho\nu}
is the conformal Harmonic 1-form. These connections are related to the self-dual connection of the physical spacetime via
eq.

9.67
. The gauge fixed Einstein’s equations in terms of the conformal connections
\Omega^{i}
and
\omega
are then imposed through

\begin{gathered}g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}=g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\left(\frac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}+\Omega^{i}_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}\right)-H^{i\mu\nu}\omega_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}\\
H^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}+g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\omega_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}H^{k\mu\nu}\omega_{\mu}\Omega^{j}_{\nu}-H^{j\mu\nu}\Omega^{i}_{\mu}\Omega^{j}_{\nu}\\
g_{H}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Omega^{i}_{\nu}=0,\quad H^{i}_{\langle\mu}{}^{\rho}F(\Omega)^{i}_{\nu\rangle\rho}=0.\end{gathered}

(9.93)

We have seen that the gauge fixing produces a surprisingly simply system of equations for the connection. Moreover, the 12 dimensional conformally flat sector
(H^{i},\Omega^{i})
can be evolved completely independently of the remaining
4
dimensional sector
(\lambda,\chi^{i},\omega)
. Unlike the linear version the
4
dimensional sector still depends on the
12
sector to evolve, this is a slightly weaker result, but it is still remarkable that it occurs. The
12
dimensional sector has
9\mathbb{R}+3\mathbb{C}+12\mathbb{C}=39\mathbb{R}
components that one needs to evolve, this is smaller than the full system which has
39+3\mathbb{C}+1\mathbb{R}+4\mathbb{C}=54\mathbb{R}
components. The linearisation of this system produces two decoupled Dirac equations, which are known to be strongly hyperbolic (as their characteristic matrices are Hermitian), therefore the full system is said to be hyperbolic. This property along with it being first-order make it a useful starting point for considering numerical relativity.

9.8.1
Weyl Curvature Extraction

For strongly gravitating systems one often expects gravitational waves to be produced during the evolution. These are contained in the Weyl curvature of this system. We briefly detail two ways how one can extract the Weyl curvature components from these new connection variables. First one can reconstruct the original connection variable

\displaystyle A^{i}_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{i}_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}H^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\Omega^{k}_{\nu}-\frac{1}{4}H^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\omega_{\nu}.

(9.94)

From this one can compute its curvature
F(A)^{i}=dA^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}\wedge A^{k}
. The self-dual Weyl tensor is then given by the
3\times 3
symmetric tracefree matrix

\displaystyle\Psi^{ij}=\frac{1}{\lambda}H^{\langle i|\mu\nu|}F(A)^{j\rangle}_{\mu\nu}.

(9.95)

The 5 Weyl-NP scalars are then packaged into
\Psi^{ij}
. The exact linear combinations depend on the basis chosen when computing
H^{i}
. Assuming that during the evolution the primary constraints
\xi
vanish, then we find that
A^{i}=\Omega^{i}
and in this case the Weyl curvature can be computed as

\displaystyle\Psi^{ij}=\frac{1}{\lambda}H^{\langle i|\mu\nu}F(\Omega)^{j\rangle}_{\mu\nu}.

(9.96)

We see that both the 12 and the 4 sectors are still needed and the Weyl curvature cannot be computed with only the 12.

9.9
Discussion

We have seen that the full nonlinear Einstein’s equations, when written using Plebański’s formulation, can be gauge fixed into a 2 parameter family of first-order hyperbolic equations. The fundamental fields are the self-dual 2-forms and connection,
\Sigma^{i}\in\Lambda^{+},A^{i}\in\Lambda^{1}
, we also introduce a connection 1-form and internal vector
\xi\in\Lambda^{1},\chi^{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
. The additional fields are used to impose the diffeomorphism and
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
gauge freedom in the form of dynamical equations of motion rather than fixing components. The resulting family of equations of motion shown in
eqs.

9.52
and
9.53
. It is seen that
\xi
encodes the Harmonic 1-form and is itself used to modify Einstein’s equations on the self-dual connection. The definition of both the self-dual and Harmonic connections are modified by the introduction of derivatives of
\chi^{i}
in such a way that it satisfies a second-order wave equation to leading order. The resulting system has
16+16=32
equations of motion for the
16+16=32
fields. The nature of this gauge fixing is to promote every field to be dynamical and as such there is no gauge freedom in choosing a fixed value for a field, only its initial and boundary conditions need to be specified.
