Counting the remaining components gives
13\mathbb{C}=10\mathbb{C}+3\mathbb{C}
, enough to form a complex-valued metric and an
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
frame. We see that the metricity condition removes the unwanted components that do not belong in the usual theory of gravity.

11.1.2
Decomposing the Reality Conditions

To restrict ourselves to a real-valued metric we need to impose the reality conditions,

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}\wedge\overline{\Sigma}^{j}=0,\quad{\rm Re}(\Sigma^{i}\wedge\Sigma^{i})=0.

(11.21)

The latter of the above we see imposes that

\displaystyle\underaccent{\tilde}{X}\det(\tilde{E})\sim iV

(11.22)

where
V
is some real-valued function which we will determine later. The first reality condition requires that we define the anti-self-dual 2-forms, as we are in Lorentzian signature they are related to the complex conjugates of the self-dual 2-forms and their decomposition can be adopted from the self-dual 2-forms as

\displaystyle\bar{\tilde{E}}^{i\mu\nu}=\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{X}}\epsilon^{ijk}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\mu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{k\nu}-2\bar{N}^{[\mu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{i\nu]}.

(11.23)

Here the bar above a term denotes the negative complex conjugate, i.e.
\bar{X}=-X^{*}
where
∗
denotes complex conjugation. Inserting this into the first reality condition and expanding we find

\displaystyle\underaccent{\tilde}{X}\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{X}}\epsilon^{ijk}\epsilon^{jmn}\tilde{E}^{k\mu}\tilde{E}^{l\nu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{m\rho}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{n\sigma}\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}-2\underaccent{\tilde}{X}\epsilon^{ikl}\tilde{E}^{k\mu}\tilde{E}^{l\nu}\bar{N}^{\rho}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\sigma}\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}

(11.24)

\displaystyle+2\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{X}}\epsilon^{jmn}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{m\rho}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{n\sigma}N^{\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}-4N^{\mu}\bar{N}^{\rho}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\sigma}\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=0.

To simplify this we note that the complex conjugates
\bar{\tilde{E}}^{i\mu}
are still purely spatial with respect to
dt
, therefore they span the same space as
\tilde{E}^{i\mu}
, the first term is the antisymmetric product of 4 3-vectors which will contain repeats and is therefore zero due to symmetry. As for the middle two terms we introduce
\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{\mu}
as the inverse of the triad
\tilde{E}^{i\mu}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{j}_{\mu}=\delta^{ij},\quad\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{\nu}=\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}-N^{\mu}(dt)_{\nu}.

(11.25)

Which implies that its components are
\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{\mu}=(-N^{b}\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{b},\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{a})
. Which we use to simplify the second term in appearing in the first line of
eq.

11.24
, it can be shown that

\displaystyle\underaccent{\tilde}{X}\epsilon^{ikl}\tilde{E}^{k\mu}\tilde{E}^{l\nu}\bar{N}^{\rho}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\sigma}\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=-2\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{\mu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\mu}\underaccent{\tilde}{X}\det(\tilde{E}).

(11.26)

By separating both sides into 3+1 and using the component definitions we see that both sides match. By taking the complex conjugate of this and using the definition of the barred variables we obtain

\displaystyle\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{X}}\epsilon^{ikl}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{k\mu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{l\nu}N^{\rho}\tilde{E}^{j\sigma}\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=2\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{E}}^{i}_{\mu}\tilde{E}^{j\mu}\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{X}}\det(\bar{\tilde{E}})

(11.27)

\displaystyle=-2\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{E}}^{i}_{\mu}\tilde{E}^{j\mu}\underaccent{\tilde}{X}\det(\tilde{E}).

(11.28)

Where in the last equality we have used
eq.

11.22
to reveal that
\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{X}}\det(\bar{\tilde{E}})=-\underaccent{\tilde}{X}\det(\tilde{E})
. The reality condition then becomes

\displaystyle\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{\mu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\mu}\underaccent{\tilde}{X}\det(\tilde{E})-\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{E}}^{j}_{\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\underaccent{\tilde}{X}\det(\tilde{E})-N^{\mu}\bar{N}^{\rho}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\sigma}\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=0.

(11.29)

To compute the last term we split the vector
N^{\mu}
into
N^{\mu}=t^{\mu}-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{i}\tilde{E}^{i\mu}
where
t^{\mu}=(1,0)
is purely timelike. A similar split is available for
\bar{N}^{\mu}=-t^{\mu}+\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}^{i}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{i\mu}
where the bar operator is defined the same way with respect to the unbarred fields. The only terms that survive in the last term of the equation above are

\displaystyle N^{\mu}\bar{N}^{\rho}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\sigma}\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{k}\tilde{E}^{k\mu}\bar{N}^{\rho}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\sigma}\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}+\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}^{k}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{k\mu}N^{\rho}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\sigma}\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}.

(11.30)

Using the previous results this becomes

\displaystyle N^{\mu}\bar{N}^{\rho}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\sigma}\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=-\epsilon^{ikl}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{k}\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{l}_{\mu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\mu}\det(\tilde{E})-\epsilon^{jkl}\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}^{k}\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{E}}^{l}_{\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\det(\bar{\tilde{E}}).

(11.31)

Collecting all the results the reality condition becomes

\displaystyle\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{\mu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\mu}\underaccent{\tilde}{X}\det(\tilde{E})-\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{E}}^{j}_{\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\underaccent{\tilde}{X}\det(\tilde{E})+\epsilon^{ikl}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{k}\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{l}_{\mu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\mu}\det(\tilde{E})+\epsilon^{jkl}\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}^{k}\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{E}}^{l}_{\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\det(\bar{\tilde{E}})=0.

(11.32)

To understand this condition we introduce the matrix
H^{ij}=\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{\mu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\mu}
along with the vector
H^{i}=\frac{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{i}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{X}}
such that it becomes

\displaystyle H^{ij}+\bar{H}^{ji}+\epsilon^{ikl}H^{k}H^{lj}-\epsilon^{jkl}\bar{H}^{k}\bar{H}^{li}=0

(11.33)

where we have made use of
eq.

11.22
and removed the factors of
\underaccent{\tilde}{X}\det(\tilde{E})
. From here we can notice that
-\bar{H}^{ij}
is the inverse of
H^{ij}
, indeed by computing

\displaystyle-H^{ik}\bar{H}^{kj}=\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{\mu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{k\mu}\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{E}}^{k}_{\nu}\tilde{E}^{j\nu}=\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{\mu}(\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}-N^{\mu}(dt)_{\nu})\tilde{E}^{j\nu}=\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{\mu}\tilde{E}^{j\mu}=\delta^{ij}

(11.34)

it is clear that they are indeed inverses of each other. We can then multiply the reality condition with the appropriate factor of
-\bar{H}
to find

\displaystyle\delta^{ij}-\bar{H}^{ki}\bar{H}^{kj}+\epsilon^{ijk}H^{k}+\epsilon^{kml}\bar{H}^{l}\bar{H}^{mi}\bar{H}^{kj}=0.

(11.35)

The first two terms in the above are symmetric in the
ij
indices and the last two are antisymmetric and as such we can treat these terms separately. The first two terms then imply that
\bar{H}^{T}=\bar{H}^{-1}
, or equivalently

\displaystyle H^{ij}=-\bar{H}^{ji}

(11.36)

which by multiplying by
\tilde{E}^{i\mu}
and
\bar{\tilde{E}}^{j\nu}
suggests that

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}=\bar{\tilde{E}}^{i\mu}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{i\nu}.

(11.37)

Meaning that the tensor

\displaystyle\tilde{\tilde{\gamma}}^{\mu\nu}=\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}

(11.38)

is real-valued. An immediate consequence of this is that
\det(\tilde{E})=\pm\det(\bar{\tilde{E}})
and therefore
\det(\bar{H})=\pm 1
. Without loss of generality we choose the branch
\det(\tilde{E})=-\det(\bar{\tilde{E}})
which implies that
\det(\tilde{E})=\det(\tilde{E}^{*})
,
\det(\bar{H})=1
and
\underaccent{\tilde}{X}=\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{X}}
. This allows us to identify
\bar{H}\in SO(3,\mathbb{C})
and make use of the identity

\displaystyle\epsilon^{kml}\bar{H}^{mi}\bar{H}^{kj}=\epsilon^{jik}\bar{H}^{lk}

(11.39)

such that the antisymmetric terms in the reality condition become

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}H^{k}+\epsilon^{kml}\bar{H}^{l}\bar{H}^{mi}\bar{H}^{kj}=\epsilon^{ijk}H^{k}-\epsilon^{ijk}\bar{H}^{lk}\bar{H}^{l}=0.

(11.40)

It is clear then at the only independent condition is

\displaystyle H^{i}-\bar{H}^{ji}\bar{H}^{j}=0.

(11.41)

Substituting in the definitions of the H fields we find

\displaystyle\frac{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{i}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{X}}-\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{E}}^{j}_{\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\frac{\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}^{j}}{\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{X}}}=0.

(11.42)

Now we can make use of
\underaccent{\tilde}{X}=\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{X}}
and multiply by
\tilde{E}^{i\mu}
to find

\displaystyle\bar{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}^{i}\bar{\tilde{E}}^{i\mu}-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{i}\tilde{E}^{i\mu}=0.

(11.43)

Finally, using the definition of
N^{\mu}
and
\bar{N}^{\mu}
this is equivalent to

\displaystyle N^{\mu}+\bar{N}^{\mu}=0,\quad\textrm{ or }\quad N^{\mu}=(N^{\mu})^{*}.

(11.44)

Collecting all the results of the reality conditions we find

\displaystyle\underaccent{\tilde}{X}=\pm i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\in i\mathbb{R},\quad N^{\mu}\in\mathbb{R},\quad\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}\in\mathbb{R}.

(11.45)

Since
N^{\mu}
is real this also means that the 3-vector
N^{a}
is real-valued. The decomposition of the bivector that satisfies the reality and metricity conditions is then

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}=\pm i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{j\mu}\tilde{E}^{k\nu}+2N^{[\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\nu]}

(11.46)

Counting the number of components we see that there are
1\mathbb{R}+3\mathbb{R}+6\mathbb{R}+3\mathbb{C}=10\mathbb{R}+3\mathbb{C}
which make up the real metric and choice of
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
frame.

11.1.3
Recovering the Metric

Now that we have the split of the variables for
\Sigma^{i}
, we can use this and the Urbantke formula for the metric to compare the Plebański story with the usual ADM metric language. To do so we make use of the inverse Urbantke formula in
eq.

4.22
and fix the conformal factor such that it is related to the inverse of the physical metric,

\displaystyle\sqrt{-g}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{12}\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{i\mu\rho}\tilde{E}^{j\alpha\beta}\tilde{E}^{k\sigma\nu}\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}.

(11.47)

Here
g_{\mu\nu}
is the physical metric,
\sqrt{-g}=\sqrt{-\det(g)}
and
\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}=\sqrt{-g}g^{\mu\nu}
. Substituting
eq.

11.46
into this, expanding all the terms, and cancelling expressions due to symmetry we find

\displaystyle\sqrt{-g}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{12}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{l\mu}\tilde{E}^{l\nu}-\frac{1}{6}N^{\mu}N^{\nu}\right)\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}N^{\alpha}\tilde{E}^{i\beta}\tilde{E}^{j\rho}\tilde{E}^{k\rho}

(11.48)

\displaystyle+\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\tilde{E}^{j\nu}\left(\frac{1}{3}\epsilon^{jkl}\tilde{E}^{j\beta}-\frac{1}{12}\epsilon^{ikl}\tilde{E}^{i\beta}\right)\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}N^{\alpha}\tilde{E}^{k\rho}\tilde{E}^{l\sigma}.

(11.49)

Making use of
eq.

11.18
and expanding the product of two epsilons we find

\displaystyle\sqrt{-g}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}=\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\det(\tilde{E})\left(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}-\frac{N^{\mu}N^{\nu}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}\right).

(11.50)

We note that the sign of in front of the first term in
eq.

11.46
does not change the definition of the metric. To remove the factors of the determinant of the metric we compute the determinant of
\sqrt{-g}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}
to find

\displaystyle\det(\sqrt{-g}\tilde{g})=\det(g)^{3}=-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{6}\det(\tilde{E})^{6},\quad\Rightarrow\sqrt{-g}=\pm\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\det(\tilde{E}).

(11.51)

Here we can fix the sign by requiring that
\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\det(\tilde{E})>0
such that the square root of the determinant of the metric is

\displaystyle\sqrt{-g}=\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\det(\tilde{E}).

(11.52)

Therefore, the inverse densitised metric is

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}=\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}-\frac{N^{\mu}N^{\nu}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}},

(11.53)

and for the inverse metric we find

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}=\frac{\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}}{\det(\tilde{E})}-\frac{N^{\mu}N^{\mu}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\det(\tilde{E})}.

(11.54)

Computing the lapse we see that,

\displaystyle N=(-(dt)_{\mu}(dt)_{\nu}g^{\mu\nu})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\sqrt{\det(\tilde{E})}.

(11.55)

The shift vector,
N^{a}
, is identified with the 3-vector inside
N^{\mu}=(1,-N^{a})
. The inverse spatial metric is

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu\nu}=\frac{\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}}{\det(\tilde{E})}.

(11.56)

This gives a dictionary between the metric and Plebański components.

11.1.4
Enforcing Self-Duality

The final step in this decomposition is to fix the sign in
eq.

11.46
; we check that these bivectors are self-dual. To do so we compute

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tilde{g}^{\mu\rho}\tilde{g}^{\nu\sigma}\underaccent{\tilde}{\epsilon}_{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}\tilde{E}^{i\alpha\beta}=\mp i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\det(\tilde{E})\left(\pm i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{j\mu}\tilde{E}^{k\nu}+2N^{[\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\nu]}\right)=\mp i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\det(\tilde{E})\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}

(11.57)

where the
\pm
relative to the
\pm
in
eq.

11.46
. We have used the decomposition of
\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}
and
\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}
into
\tilde{E}^{i\mu},N^{\mu},\underaccent{\tilde}{N}
and then used the previous identities to obtain the right-hand side. It is a lengthy calculation so only the result is stated. We see that the self-dual bivectors are then given by

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}=-i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{j\mu}\tilde{E}^{k\nu}+2N^{[\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\nu]}.

(11.58)

11.1.5
3+1 Coordinate Decomposition

We have computed the decomposition of the bivector into
\tilde{E}^{i\mu},N^{\mu}
and
\underaccent{\tilde}{N}
. It will be useful later to have these objects separated into temporal and spatial coordinates
x^{\mu}=(t,x^{a})
. In doing so we find

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{i\mu}=(0,\tilde{E}^{ia}),\quad N^{\mu}=(1,-N^{a})

(11.59)

as such the basic variables are the triad
\tilde{E}^{ia}
, the shift vector
N^{a}
and the inverse densitised lapse
\underaccent{\tilde}{N}
. The decomposition of the bivector is then

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{i0a}=\tilde{E}^{ia},\quad\tilde{E}^{iab}=-i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}-N^{a}\tilde{E}^{ib}+N^{b}\tilde{E}^{ia}.

(11.60)

The metric in 3+1 is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{00}=-\frac{1}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}},\quad\tilde{g}^{0a}=\frac{N^{a}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}},\quad\tilde{g}^{ab}=\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib}-\frac{N^{a}N^{b}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}

(11.61)

and the inverse metric is given by

\displaystyle\underaccent{\tilde}{g}_{00}=-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}+\underaccent{\tilde}{g}_{ab}N^{a}N^{b},\quad\underaccent{\tilde}{g}_{0a}=\underaccent{\tilde}{g}_{ab}N^{b},\quad\underaccent{\tilde}{g}_{ab}=\frac{1}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{a}\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{b}.

(11.62)

For later purposes it will be useful to compute the decomposition of the following endomorphisms

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}=-i\underaccent{\tilde}{g}_{\mu\rho}\tilde{E}^{i\rho\nu},

(11.63)

which we find gives

\begin{gathered}\Sigma^{i}_{0}{}^{0}=i\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{a}\frac{N^{a}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}},\quad\Sigma^{i}_{a}{}^{0}=\frac{i}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{a},\quad\Sigma^{i}_{0}{}^{a}=i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}-i\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{b}\frac{N^{a}N^{b}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}-\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{j}_{b}N^{b}\tilde{E}^{ka}\\
\Sigma^{i}_{a}{}^{b}=-i\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{a}\frac{N^{a}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}-\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{j}_{a}\tilde{E}^{kb}\end{gathered}

(11.64)

This completes the decomposition of the metric variables into temporal and spatial components. For the connection variables the story is much simpler, we simply introduce

\displaystyle A^{i}_{\mu}dx^{\mu}=A^{i}_{0}dt+A^{i}_{a}dx^{a}

(11.65)

and the curvatures are

\displaystyle F^{i}_{0a}=\partial_{t}A^{i}_{a}-\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{0},\quad F^{i}_{ab}=\partial_{a}A^{i}_{b}-\partial_{b}A^{i}_{a}+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}_{a}A^{k}_{b}

(11.66)

where
\partial^{A}_{a}\xi^{i}_{b}=\partial_{a}\xi^{i}_{b}+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}_{a}\xi^{k}_{b}
is the spatial gauge covariant derivative. We call
A^{i}_{0}
the temporal connection and
A^{i}_{a}
the spatial connection and no extra conditions need to be satisfied by them. This completes the decomposition of the frame and connection fields in the Plebański formulation of gravity. Now we can start to apply it to the equations of motion.

11.2
Ashtekar’s Formalism

Ashtekar’s formalism was first introduced in
1985
by Abhay Ashtekar
[
17
]
, where they introduced a canonical pair of position and momentum variables for gravity. The idea was to use densitised inverse triads as the main position variables,
\tilde{E}^{ia}=\det(e)e^{ia}
where
e^{I}_{\mu}
is the tetrad and
e^{ia}
is the inverse of the spatial part. When the action for gravity is modified by the addition of the purely imaginary Holst term then the canonical momentum variables appear as a complex linear combination of the spin connection and extrinsic curvature
A^{i}_{a}=\epsilon^{ijk}\omega^{jk}_{a}+ie^{ib}K_{ab}
. When written in these variables the Hamiltonian becomes polynomial in the variables, moreover there are no second-class constraints as compared to the frame formalism. This is ideal for numericals as the constraints satisfy a closed set of evolution equations, as previously discussed this allows for modification to the equations of motion that are attractive towards the constraint surface. We show how the action for Plebański becomes polynomial and write down the equations of motion and constraints that define the theory.

11.2.1
Ashtekar Lagrangian

We now write the Plebański Lagrangian in terms of the new fundamental variables,
\tilde{E}^{ia}
,
N^{a}
,
\underaccent{\tilde}{N}
,
A^{i}_{0}
and
A^{i}_{a}
. We start with the Plebański Lagrangian and move to components to find

\displaystyle L=\Sigma^{i}\wedge F^{i}/d^{4}x=\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\epsilon}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu\nu}F^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}F^{i}_{\mu\nu}.

(11.67)

Then by separating the coordinates into time and space,
x^{\mu}=(t,x^{a})
, and using the 3+1 split in
section

11.1.5
we find

\displaystyle L=\tilde{E}^{ia}\left(\partial_{t}A^{i}_{a}-\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{0}\right)-\frac{i}{2}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}F^{i}_{ab}+\tilde{E}^{ia}N^{b}F^{i}_{ab}.

(11.68)

This is the Lagrangian for gravity in Ashtekar variables. It is now easy to see that
\tilde{E}^{ia}
is conjugate to
A^{i}_{a}
and as the time derivative only appears between them these are the only dynamical variables. The variation with respect to these two fields gives

\displaystyle\frac{\delta L}{\delta\tilde{E}^{ia}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}A^{i}_{a}-\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{0}-i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{jb}F^{k}_{ab}+N^{b}F^{i}_{ab}=0

(11.69)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta L}{\delta A^{i}_{a}}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{t}\tilde{E}^{ia}-\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}_{0}\tilde{E}^{ka}-i\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{b}^{A}\left(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}\right)+2\partial_{b}^{A}\left(\tilde{E}^{i[a}N^{b]}\right)=0.

(11.70)

Where we have made use of the variational formula
\delta F^{i}_{ab}=2\partial^{A}_{[a}\delta A^{i}_{b]}
. We see that from their variations we obtain two evolution equations for the triad and spatial connection,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}A^{i}_{a}

\displaystyle=\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{0}+i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{jb}F^{k}_{ab}+N^{b}F^{i}_{ba}

(11.71)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\tilde{E}^{ia}

\displaystyle=-\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}_{0}\tilde{E}^{ka}-i\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{b}^{A}\left(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}\right)+2\partial_{b}^{A}\left(\tilde{E}^{i[a}N^{b]}\right).

(11.72)

Varying with the remaining fields we obtain the following constraints

\displaystyle\frac{\delta L}{\delta\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}F^{i}_{ab}=0

(11.73)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta L}{\delta N^{a}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{E}^{ib}F^{i}_{ba}=0

(11.74)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta L}{\delta A^{i}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{a}^{A}\tilde{E}^{ia}=0.

(11.75)

Variation with respect to the lapse and shift give the Hamiltonian and momentum constraints

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}F^{i}_{ab}=0,

(11.76)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{a}

\displaystyle=\tilde{E}^{ib}F^{i}_{ba}=0.

(11.77)

These generate the time and space diffeomorphism gauge transformations. The Ashtekar formalism comes with an extra constraint known as the Gauss constraint

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{i}=\partial^{A}_{a}\tilde{E}^{ia}

(11.78)

these generate the
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
gauge transformations. Due to the extra
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
gauge symmetry in Ashtekar and Plebański’s formulations, as compared to the metric formulation, we obtain the additional gauss constraint. It can be shown that the constraints are first-class and as such form a closed system under the time derivative
[
152
]
. Moreover, every equation of motion and constraint is polynomial in the fields or derivatives of them, this is surprisingly simple for GR and the highest order degree polynomial appearing is degree
4
in the equations of motion (or degree
5
at the level of the Lagrangian). The polynomial property can have important consequences for numerical simulations, for certain initial conditions some fields may pass through degenerate points and in the standard formalisms one would have to divide by zero, this causes equations to diverge and codes to crash, see section 6.6 of
[
33
]
. In this formulation as no division is required this problem does not occur.

It has been shown that the Ashtekar system is weakly hyperbolic
[
153
]
. It was then shown in
[
154
]
that by adding the appropriate constraint to the evolution equations Ashtekar’s evolution system becomes a symmetric hyperbolic system. This property was taken further in a series of papers by Shinkai and Yoneda
[
153
,
126
,
155
,
136
,
156
]
where they explored the numerical properties of Ashtekar’s variables. They applied the adjusted and
\lambda
-systems to the evolution and constraint equations to form a strongly hyperbolic evolution system. It was shown that modifying the equations of motion with the constraints worked as expected and the constraint growth was tamed. Interestingly they found no striking difference between the weakly, strongly and symmetric hyperbolic systems and found that the constraint was under control for all systems
[
156
]
. This is in agreement with
[
157
]
where a similar result was found for a different system of equations for gravity. It was suggested that the requirement on the constraint propagation eigenvalues is more important than the hyperbolicity, that is constraint damping is more important than hyperbolicity. With this result in mind we require that the system by strongly hyperbolic and have as many eigenvalues of the constraint amplification matrix be non-positive as possible.

11.2.2
Wave Equation Analogy

The difference between the ADM and Ashtekar formalism can be nicely summed in an analogy with the wave equation. In dimension 4 the hyperbolic wave equation is

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{t}u=\partial^{i}\partial^{i}u

(11.79)

where
u(t,x^{i})
is a real function on a Minkowski manifold. The solution
u
represents Einstein’s equation on the metric, in the Harmonic gauge it is a collect of wave like equations on
g_{\mu\nu}
. The goal of the ADM and Ashtekar systems is then put Einstein’s equation in a form where the method of lines can be applied. To do this for the wave equation we can introduce a first-order variable for the time and spatial derivatives of
u

\displaystyle v=\partial_{t}u,\quad a^{i}=\partial^{i}u

(11.80)

Then the wave equation becomes the following first order in time system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u\\
v\\
a^{i}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&\delta^{jk}\\
0&\delta^{ij}&0\end{pmatrix}\partial^{j}\begin{pmatrix}u\\
v\\
a^{k}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}v\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}

(11.81)

This is the usual way of producing a first-order system from a second-order system. It is hyperbolic as this is a first-order reduction of the wave equation, furthermore, in this representation it is symmetric hyperbolic. The first row and column in the above matrix do not contribute the characteristic matrix as there are no spatial differential operators involved. This is effectively the ADM way of reducing the Einstein equations, where the extrinsic curvature is the time derivative,
v
, of the metric and the Levi-Civita connections are the spatial derivatives,
a^{i}
.

Another way of producing a first-order system for the wave equation is to use the technology of spinors and the gamma matrices. The gamma matrices in 4d are a set of four matrices
\gamma^{\mu}
such that
\gamma^{(\mu}\gamma^{\nu)}=g^{\mu\nu}
. More formally the gamma matrices,
\gamma^{\mu}
, generate the matrix representation of the Clifford algebra
Cl_{1,3}(\mathbb{R})
. The smallest real representation of the gamma matrices is the
4\times 4
Majorana representation, for which we can take the solution vector,
U
, to be a real 4 component vector. The first-order reduction of the wave equation is then

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}U=0.

(11.82)

To write this in a similar form to the ADM version we split the matrices into
\gamma^{\mu}=\gamma^{0},\gamma^{i}
and using that
-\gamma^{0}
is the inverse of
\gamma^{0}
we find

\displaystyle\partial_{t}U=\Gamma^{i}\partial^{i}U

(11.83)

where
\Gamma^{i}=\gamma^{0}\gamma^{i}
. This first-order system is strongly hyperbolic as the gamma matrices are diagonalisable in any representation and
\Gamma^{(i}\Gamma^{j)}=\delta^{ij}
, so the eigenvalues are all real. Moreover, in dimension 4 there exists a basis such that
\gamma^{0}
is also anti-Hermitian and
\gamma^{i}
are Hermitian, in this basis the wave equation becomes symmetric hyperbolic and real-valued. The spinor version of the first-order decomposition is analogous to the Ashtekar variable formalism, where the variables
(\tilde{E}^{ia},A^{i}_{a})
are equivalent to the spinor
U
. For the wave equation in the ADM form, only the original variable
u
satisfies the wave equation to second order, whereas the functions
a^{i}
do not. This differs from the spinor formulation where all the fields in the 4 component vector
U
satisfy the wave equation.

As for other systems, like gravity, the spinor formulation usually involves complex variables as spinors are naturally complex-valued. In the wave equation example, the existence of a Majorana basis allows the spinor to be real-valued, whereas for gravity the issue of restricting to a real-valued field is done through imposing the reality conditions. The aim of the rest of this chapter is then to further explore the spinor-like first-order equations of motion for gravity, and in particular develop an evolution system ready for use with the numerical approaches mentioned previously.

11.3
Gauge Fixed 3+1 Decomposition

Now we have seen how the space-time decomposition works for the usual Plebański formalism and Lagrangian we can apply the same techniques to our gauge fixed system. As there is no Lagrangian for the gauge fixing found in
chapter

9
we must decompose the equations of motion directly into 3+1. We repeat the system here for the reader

\displaystyle A^{i}=-iJ_{1}^{-1}(\star d\Sigma)^{i}+c_{1}d\chi^{i}+c_{2}J_{1}(d\chi)^{i},\quad\xi_{\mu}=-\Gamma_{\mu}-2c_{2}\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}

(11.84)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{i}_{\mu}{}^{\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}+\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}=0,\quad\partial^{\mu}A^{i}_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}=0.

(11.85)

Before performing the 3+1 decomposition we need to manipulate these equations into a form that best suits this process.

11.3.1
Component Formulas

The basic metric variables in the Plebański formalism are efficiently encoded into the bivector
\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}
, hence, we will try to place
\Sigma^{i}
in this form where possible. As shown in the previous section the inverse densitised metric
\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}
naturally decomposes into the components of
\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}
, and manipulating terms such that they involve
\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}
will therefore be useful for the decomposition. Starting with the definition for
A^{i}
we apply
J_{1}
to both sides and use
J^{2}_{1}=J_{1}+2\mathbb{I}
to find

\displaystyle J_{1}(A)^{i}=-i\star d\Sigma^{i}+(c_{1}+c_{2})J_{1}(d\chi)^{i}+2c_{2}d\chi^{i}

(11.86)

which in components reads

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}A^{k}_{\nu}=-\frac{i}{2}\epsilon_{\mu}{}^{\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\Sigma^{j}_{\mu}{}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}+2c_{2}\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}.

(11.87)

The raising the index with the densitised inverse metric,
\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}
, we find

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{\Sigma}^{j\mu\nu}A^{k}_{\nu}=-\frac{i}{2}\tilde{\epsilon}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}+(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{\Sigma}^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}+2c_{2}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}.

(11.88)

Where we have used that
\tilde{\Sigma}^{i\mu\nu}=\sqrt{-g}\Sigma^{i\mu\nu}
. From here we can use that
\tilde{\epsilon}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=\pm 1
to commute it with the partial derivative and then use the self-duality
\tilde{\epsilon}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=2i\tilde{\Sigma}^{i\mu\nu}
. After which the equation becomes

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{\Sigma}^{j\mu\nu}A^{k}_{\nu}=\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\Sigma}^{i\mu\nu}+(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{\Sigma}^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}+2c_{2}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}.

(11.89)

The term involving
A^{i}
can be packaged into the gauge covariant derivative, i.e.
\partial^{A}_{\mu}\chi^{i}=\partial_{\mu}\chi^{i}+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}_{\mu}\chi^{k}
, such that

\displaystyle\partial^{A}_{\nu}\tilde{\Sigma}^{i\mu\nu}+(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{\Sigma}^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}+2c_{2}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}=0.

(11.90)

Using the definition
\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\epsilon}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\Sigma^{i}_{\rho\sigma}=i\tilde{\Sigma}^{i\mu\nu}
we rewrite the above equation as

\displaystyle\partial^{A}_{\nu}\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}+(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}+2ic_{2}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}=0.

(11.91)

This form of the first equation will be easier to decompose into 3+1 components.

Next we tackle the equation defining the new connection 1-form,
\xi_{\mu}
. The Harmonic 1-form takes a simple form when using the inverse densitiesed metric,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}=\Gamma_{\mu\rho\sigma}g^{\rho\sigma}=g^{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}g_{\mu\sigma}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}g_{\rho\sigma}=-\underaccent{\tilde}{g}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}\tilde{g}^{\nu\rho}.

(11.92)

Similarly to before we raise the index with
\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}
and using this form of the Harmonic 1-form we have

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}\xi_{\nu}=\partial_{\nu}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}-2c_{2}\tilde{\Sigma}^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}.

(11.93)

Converting to
\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}
reveals

\displaystyle\partial_{\nu}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}+2ic_{2}\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}-\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}\xi_{\nu}=0.

(11.94)

It remains to express the two equations on the connections using these variables, for the modified Einstein condition we simply raise the
\mu
index, densitise and convert to
\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}
resulting in

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{i\mu\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}+i\tilde{g}^{\mu\rho}\partial_{(\nu}\xi_{\rho)}=0.

(11.95)

The Lorenz condition we can multiply by
\sqrt{-g}
to give the appropriate density weight,

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A^{i}_{\nu}+\frac{i}{2}\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}=0.

(11.96)

Collecting the new equations of motion we find

\displaystyle\partial^{A}_{\nu}\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}+(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{j\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{k}+2ic_{2}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}

\displaystyle=0

(11.97)

\displaystyle\partial_{\nu}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}+2ic_{2}\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi^{i}-\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}\xi_{\nu}

\displaystyle=0

(11.98)

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{i\mu\rho}F^{i}_{\nu\rho}+i\tilde{g}^{\mu\rho}\partial_{(\nu}\xi_{\rho)}

\displaystyle=0

(11.99)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A^{i}_{\nu}+\frac{i}{2}\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}

\displaystyle=0.

(11.100)

When written this way the metric variables only appear in
\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}
or
\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}
and as such they can be decomposed using the technology from the previous section. The connection variables also appear with their indices in fixed positions, therefore, the 3+1 decomposition is simple to perform.

11.3.2
3+1 Frame Evolution Equations

Recalling the space-time split from the previous section we defined a time coordinate
t
and an induced 1-form
dt
. Using these we found the following decomposition for our main variables

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{j\mu}\tilde{E}^{k\nu}+N^{\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}-N^{\nu}\tilde{E}^{i\mu}

(11.101)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{i\mu}\tilde{E}^{i\nu}-\frac{N^{\mu}N^{\nu}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}

(11.102)

where

\displaystyle(dt)_{\nu}\tilde{E}^{i\nu\mu}=\tilde{E}^{i\mu},\quad\textrm{and}\quad(dt)_{\nu}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}=-\frac{N^{\mu}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}.

(11.103)

In the adapted spacetime coordinates,
x^{\mu}=(t,x^{a})
, the above decomposition becomes

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{i0a}

\displaystyle=\tilde{E}^{ia},\quad\tilde{E}^{iab}=-i\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}-N^{a}\tilde{E}^{ib}+N^{b}\tilde{E}^{ia}

(11.104)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{00}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}},\quad\tilde{g}^{0a}=\frac{N^{a}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}},\quad\tilde{g}^{ab}=\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib}-\frac{N^{a}N^{b}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}.

(11.105)

With this mind we can start decomposing
eqs.

11.97
,
11.98
,
11.99
and
11.100
into 3+1 components.
First we tackle
eq.

11.97
and look at the
\mu=0
component,

\displaystyle\partial_{a}^{A}\tilde{E}^{i0a}+(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{j0a}\partial_{a}\chi^{i}+2ic_{2}\tilde{g}^{00}\partial_{t}\chi^{i}+2ic_{2}\tilde{g}^{0a}\partial_{a}\chi^{i}=0

(11.106)

then using the decomposition we find

\displaystyle\partial_{a}^{A}\tilde{E}^{ia}+(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{ja}\partial_{a}\chi^{i}-2ic_{2}\frac{1}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\chi^{i}=0.

(11.107)

Solving for
(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\chi^{i}
gives

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\chi^{i}=\frac{1}{2ic_{2}}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\partial^{A}_{a}\tilde{E}^{ia}+\frac{1}{2ic_{2}}(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\partial_{a}\chi^{k}.

(11.108)

Which is our equation of motion for
\chi^{i}
. Next we take the
\mu=a
component of the same equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{A}\tilde{E}^{ia0}+\partial_{b}^{A}\tilde{E}^{iab}+(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{ja0}\partial_{t}\chi^{k}+(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{jab}\partial_{b}\chi^{k}

\displaystyle+2ic_{2}\tilde{g}^{a0}\partial_{t}\chi^{i}+2ic_{2}\tilde{g}^{ab}\partial_{b}\chi^{i}=0.

(11.109)

Which becomes
Applying the decomposition expands to

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}^{A}\tilde{E}^{ia}+\partial^{A}_{b}\left(-i\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}+\tilde{E}^{ia}N^{b}-\tilde{E}^{ib}N^{a}\right)-(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{ja}\partial_{t}\chi^{k}

\displaystyle+(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}(-i\epsilon^{jlm}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{la}\tilde{E}^{mb}+\tilde{E}^{ja}N^{b}-\tilde{E}^{jb}N^{a})\partial_{b}\chi^{k}

\displaystyle+2ic_{2}\frac{N^{a}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})\chi^{i}+2ic_{2}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{jb}\partial_{b}\chi^{i}=0.

(11.110)

After some rearranging we can factor this into

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{A}-N^{b}\partial_{b})\tilde{E}^{ia}=-i\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{kb}\partial^{A}_{b}\left(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\right)-i\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\partial^{A}_{b}\tilde{E}^{kb}-\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}N^{a}+\tilde{E}^{ia}\partial^{A}_{b}N^{b}

\displaystyle-(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{ja}(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})\chi^{k}-i(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\epsilon^{jlm}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{la}\tilde{E}^{mb}\partial_{b}\chi^{k}

\displaystyle+2ic_{2}\frac{N^{a}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}\left((\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})\chi^{i}-\frac{1}{2ic_{2}}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\partial^{A}_{b}\tilde{E}^{ib}-\frac{1}{2ic_{2}}(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{jb}\partial_{b}\chi^{k}\right)

\displaystyle+2ic_{2}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{jb}\partial_{b}\chi^{i}

(11.111)

Substituting the evolution equation for
\chi^{i}
where it appears simplifies this to be

\displaystyle(\partial^{A}_{t}-N^{a}\partial^{A}_{a})\tilde{E}^{ia}=-i\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{kb}\partial_{b}^{A}\left(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\right)-\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}N^{a}+\tilde{E}^{ia}\partial_{b}N^{b}+i(c_{1}+c_{2})\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{jb}\partial_{b}\chi^{j}

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{2}-c_{1}}{2ic_{2}}\left(\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\partial_{b}^{A}\tilde{E}^{kb}+(c_{1}+c_{2})\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}\chi^{j}+(3c_{2}+c_{1})\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{jb}\partial_{b}\chi^{i}\right).

(11.112)

Revealing an evolution equation for the triad. Moving to the next equation we look at the
\mu=0
component of
eq.

11.98
,

\displaystyle\partial_{0}\tilde{g}^{00}+\partial_{a}\tilde{g}^{0a}+2ic_{2}\tilde{E}^{i0a}\partial_{a}\chi^{i}-\tilde{g}^{00}\xi_{0}-\tilde{g}^{0a}\xi_{a}=0

(11.113)

which decomposes to be

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}}\partial_{t}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}+\partial_{a}\left(\frac{N^{a}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}\right)+2ic_{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\partial_{a}\chi^{i}+\frac{1}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}(\xi_{0}-N^{a}\xi_{a})=0.

(11.114)

Solving for
(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\underaccent{\tilde}{N}
gives

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\underaccent{\tilde}{N}=-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\partial_{a}N^{a}+2ic_{2}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\partial_{a}\chi^{i}-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}(\xi_{0}-N^{a}\xi_{a})

(11.115)

which is an evolution equation for the densitiesed lapse. Taking the
\mu=a
component of the same equation gives us

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\tilde{g}^{a0}+\partial_{b}\tilde{g}^{ab}+2ic_{2}\tilde{E}^{ia0}\partial_{t}\chi^{i}+2ic_{2}\tilde{E}^{iab}\partial_{b}\chi^{i}-\tilde{g}^{a0}\xi_{0}-\tilde{g}^{ab}\xi_{b}=0.

(11.116)

Which becomes

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\frac{N^{a}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}\right)+\partial_{b}\left(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib}-\frac{N^{a}N^{b}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}\right)-2ic_{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\partial_{t}\chi^{i}

\displaystyle+2ic_{2}\left(-i\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}+\tilde{E}^{ia}N^{b}-\tilde{E}^{ib}N^{a}\right)\partial_{b}\chi^{i}-\frac{N^{a}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}\left(\xi_{0}-N^{b}\xi_{b}\right)-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib}\xi_{b}=0.

(11.117)

Making use of the following property for covariant derivatives

\displaystyle\partial_{b}(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib})=\partial_{b}^{A}(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib})=\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\partial_{b}^{A}\tilde{E}^{ib}+\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}^{A}(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia})

(11.118)

and collecting terms suggestively we obtain

\displaystyle-\frac{N^{a}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}}\left((\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})\underaccent{\tilde}{N}+\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\partial_{b}N^{b}+2ic_{2}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}\chi^{i}+\underaccent{\tilde}{N}(\xi_{0}-N^{b}\xi_{b})\right)+\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}^{A}(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia})

\displaystyle-2ic_{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\left((\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})\chi^{i}+\frac{i}{2c_{2}}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\partial_{b}^{A}\tilde{E}^{ib}\right)+\frac{1}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})N^{a}

\displaystyle+2c_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}\partial_{b}\chi^{i}-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib}\xi_{b}=0.

(11.119)

Into which we can use the evolution equation for
\underaccent{\tilde}{N}
to kill the first and the evolution for
\chi^{i}
to replace the first term on the second line. The result is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})N^{a}+\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}^{A}(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia})+(c_{2}-c_{1})\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}\partial_{b}\chi^{i}-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib}\xi_{b}=0.

(11.120)

Solving for
(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})N^{a}
we have another equation of motion

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})N^{a}=-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}^{A}(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia})+(c_{1}-c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}\partial_{b}\chi^{i}+\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib}\xi_{b}

(11.121)

this time for the shift,
N^{a}
. Combining these results we find an evolution system for the frame variables.

The evolution equations for the frame variables are

\displaystyle(\partial^{A}_{t}-N^{a}\partial^{A}_{a})\tilde{E}^{ia}=

\displaystyle-i\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{kb}\partial_{b}^{A}\left(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\right)-\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}N^{a}+\tilde{E}^{ia}\partial_{b}N^{b}+i(c_{1}+c_{2})\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{jb}\partial_{b}\chi^{j}

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{2}-c_{1}}{2ic_{2}}\left(\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\partial_{b}^{A}\tilde{E}^{kb}+(c_{1}+c_{2})\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}\chi^{j}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 150.0pt+(3c_{2}+c_{1})\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{jb}\partial_{b}\chi^{i}\right).

(11.122)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})N^{a}=

\displaystyle-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}^{A}(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia})+(c_{1}-c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}\partial_{b}\chi^{i}+\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib}\xi_{b}

(11.123)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\underaccent{\tilde}{N}=

\displaystyle-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\partial_{a}N^{a}+2ic_{2}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\partial_{a}\chi^{i}-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}(\xi_{0}-N^{a}\xi_{a})

(11.124)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\chi^{i}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2ic_{2}}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\partial^{A}_{a}\tilde{E}^{ia}+\frac{1}{2ic_{2}}(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\partial_{a}\chi^{k}.

(11.125)

We now discuss the structure of the above system of evolution equations for the frame as compared to the original Ashtekar system
eq.

11.72
. It is still wished for the gauge fixed system to describe the original evolution system as such we require that the Gauss constraint,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{i}=\partial^{A}_{a}\tilde{E}^{ia}\simeq 0,

(11.126)

to vanish during evolution. As in the Maxwell example
section

6.1
we also require the new gauge variables
\chi^{i}=0
to be or close to zero during evolution. This constraint is due to the fact that
\chi^{i}
plays the role of the conjugate momentum for
A^{i}_{0}
, which is a Lagrange multiplier for the Gauss constraint, therefore, its conjugate momentum should vanish or be constant as it is a primary constraint (it being zero means the contribution to the Lagrangian,
\chi^{i}\partial_{t}A^{i}_{0}
, vanishes). Requiring this means that the modifications to the evolution equation for the triad vanishes and the result is the original equation of motion from Ashtekar’s formalism. As for the evolution equations for the lapse and shift, these components are pure gauge variables and as such their equations of motion can be freely specified. The above choice is a modification of Harmonic slicing which has no physical meaning and is simply a change in description. Finally, the evolution of the internal gauge vector
\chi^{i}
evolves proportional to the Gauss constraint and derivatives of itself. Therefore, if the Gauss constraint and
\chi^{i}
are zero initially, then the
\partial_{t}\chi^{i}|_{t=0}
is true also and locally
\chi^{i}
will remain constant. For system where the constraint cannot be imposed initially then one needs to check the constraint amplification matrix to make sure that the violating modes are damped. An analysis of this will be performed later once all the equations of motion have been found. Next we consider the equations of motion for the constraints.

11.3.3
3+1 Connection Evolution Equations

Having derived the evolution system for the frame sector fields, we can now perform the same for the connection sector. We begin with
\mu,\nu=0,a
component of
eq.

11.99
to find

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{i0b}F^{i}_{ab}+\frac{i}{2}\tilde{g}^{00}\left(\partial_{t}\xi_{a}+\partial_{a}\xi_{0}\right)+\frac{i}{2}\tilde{g}^{0b}\left(\partial_{a}\xi_{b}+\partial_{b}\xi_{a}\right)=0.

(11.127)

Using the decomposition and solving for
(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})\xi_{a}
we find

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})\xi_{a}=-\partial_{a}\xi_{0}+N^{b}\partial_{a}\xi_{b}-2i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}F^{i}_{ab}.

(11.128)

Next we can look at the
\mu,\nu=a,b
components of the same equation,

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{ia0}F^{i}_{b0}+\tilde{E}^{iac}F^{i}_{bc}+\frac{i}{2}\tilde{g}^{a0}\left(\partial_{b}\xi_{0}+\partial_{t}\xi_{a}\right)+\frac{i}{2}\tilde{g}^{ac}\left(\partial_{b}\xi_{c}+\partial_{c}\xi_{b}\right)=0.

(11.129)

Using the decomposition gives

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{ia}F^{i}_{0b}+\left(-i\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kc}+\tilde{E}^{ia}N^{c}-\tilde{E}^{ic}N^{a}\right)F^{i}_{bc}

\displaystyle+\frac{i}{2}N^{a}(\partial_{b}\xi_{0}+\partial_{t}\xi_{b})+\frac{i}{2}(\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ic}-N^{a}N^{c})(\partial_{b}\xi_{c}+\partial_{c}\xi_{b})=0.

(11.130)

Collecting into useful factors we have

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{ia}(F^{i}_{0b}+N^{c}F^{i}_{bc}-i\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{jc}F^{k}_{bc}+i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ic}\partial_{(b}\xi_{c)})

\displaystyle+\frac{i}{2}N^{a}\left((\partial_{t}-N^{c}\partial_{c})\xi_{b}+\partial_{b}\xi_{0}-N^{c}\partial_{b}\xi_{c}-2i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ic}F^{i}_{bc}\right)=0.

(11.131)

The term in the brackets on the second line is zero via the evolution equation for
\xi_{a}
, and multiplying by
\underaccent{\tilde}{E}^{i}_{a}/\underaccent{\tilde}{N}
we find

\displaystyle F^{i}_{0a}=-N^{b}F^{i}_{ab}+i\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{jb}F^{k}_{ab}-i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{(a}\xi_{b)}.

(11.132)

To put this in the form of an evolution equation we decompose the first two curvatures appearing using
F^{i}_{0a}=\partial_{t}A^{i}_{a}-\partial_{a}A^{i}_{0}+\epsilon^{ijk}A^{j}_{0}A^{k}_{a}=\partial_{t}A^{i}_{a}-\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{0}
and similarly
F^{i}_{ab}=\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{b}-\partial_{b}A^{i}_{a}
such that

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})A^{i}_{a}=\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{0}-N^{b}\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{b}+i\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{jb}F^{k}_{ab}-i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{(a}\xi_{b)}.

(11.133)

The only component remaining is the
\mu,\nu=0,0
components, for which we find

\displaystyle\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{i0a}F^{i}_{0a}+i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{g}^{00}\partial_{t}\xi_{0}+\frac{i}{2}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{g}^{0a}(\partial_{t}\xi_{a}+\partial_{a}\xi_{0})=0

(11.134)

and with the decomposition becomes

\displaystyle\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}F^{i}_{0a}-i(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\xi_{0}+\frac{i}{2}N^{a}(\partial_{t}\xi_{a}-\partial_{a}\xi_{0})=0.

(11.135)

Rearranging this for
(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\xi_{0}
reveals

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\xi_{0}=-N^{a}\partial_{a}\xi_{0}-(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib}-N^{a}N^{b})\partial_{a}\xi_{b}

\displaystyle-2i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}N^{a}\tilde{E}^{ib}F^{i}_{ab}+\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{jb}F^{k}_{ab}.

(11.136)

The final evolution equation is in the Lorenz condition,
eq.

11.100
. Expanding this into 3+1 components gives

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{00}\partial_{t}A^{i}_{0}+\tilde{g}^{0a}(\partial_{t}A^{i}_{a}+\partial_{a}A^{i}_{0})+\tilde{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}A^{i}_{b}+\frac{i}{2}\tilde{E}^{i0a}(\partial_{t}\xi_{a}-\partial_{a}\xi_{0})+\frac{i}{2}\tilde{E}^{iab}\partial_{a}\xi_{b}=0.

(11.137)

Using the decomposition and collecting terms we find

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}A^{i}_{0}+N^{a}(\partial_{t}A^{i}_{a}+\partial_{a}A^{i}_{0})+\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{jb}\partial_{a}A^{i}_{b}+\frac{i}{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}(\partial_{t}\xi_{a}-\partial_{a}\xi_{0})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}\partial_{a}\xi_{b}+\frac{i}{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}N^{b}\partial_{a}\xi_{b}+\frac{i}{2}\tilde{E}^{ib}N^{a}\partial_{b}\xi_{a}=0.

(11.138)

Substituting the previous evolution equations for
A^{i}_{a}
and
\xi_{a}
and solving for
(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})A^{i}_{0}
gives our final connection evolution equation

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})A^{i}_{0}=

\displaystyle\ (\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{jb}-N^{a}N^{b})\partial_{a}A^{i}_{b}+N^{a}\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{0}-i\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}N^{b}F^{k}_{ab}

\displaystyle\ +\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{jb}F^{j}_{ab}-i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\partial_{a}\xi_{0}-iN^{a}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{(a}\xi_{b)}

\displaystyle\ +iN^{a}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}\xi_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}\partial_{a}\xi_{b}

(11.139)

The result of this decomposition is an evolution equation for the self-dual connection and the connection 1-form

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})A^{i}_{a}

\displaystyle=\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{0}-N^{b}\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{b}+i\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{jb}F^{k}_{ab}-i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{(a}\xi_{b)}

(11.140)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})A^{i}_{0}

\displaystyle=\ (\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{jb}-N^{a}N^{b})\partial_{a}A^{i}_{b}+N^{a}\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{0}-i\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}N^{b}F^{k}_{ab}

\displaystyle+\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{jb}F^{j}_{ab}-i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\partial_{a}\xi_{0}-iN^{a}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{(a}\xi_{b)}

\displaystyle+iN^{a}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}\xi_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}\partial_{a}\xi_{b}

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\xi_{0}

\displaystyle=-N^{a}\partial_{a}\xi_{0}-(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib}-N^{a}N^{b})\partial_{a}\xi_{b}

\displaystyle-2i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}N^{a}\tilde{E}^{ib}F^{i}_{ab}+\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{jb}F^{k}_{ab}

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})\xi_{a}

\displaystyle=2i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}F^{i}_{ba}-\partial_{a}\xi_{0}+N^{b}\partial_{a}\xi_{b}

As with the evolution of the frame we want for this system to describe original Ashtekar formulation of Einstein’s equations, but now the focus is on the evolution equation for the connection. The system before gauge fixing had 4 constraints which were found to be the Hamiltonian and Momentum constraints

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{jb}F^{k}_{ab},\quad\mathcal{M}_{a}=\tilde{E}^{ib}F^{i}_{ba}.

(11.141)

The components of the 1-form,
\xi_{0}
and
\xi_{a}
, appear as conjugate momentum for the lapse and shift Lagrange multipliers and can thus be taken as primary constraints that should vanish. The equation of motion for the spatial connection is only modified by the derivative of the primary constraints
\xi_{a}
and as such it recovers the original dynamics on the constraint surface. The equation of motion for
A^{i}_{0}
is pure gauge, due to
A^{i}_{0}
being a Lagrange multiplier for the Gauss constraint, and the evolution simply changes the gauge orbit of the full connection
A^{i}_{\mu}
without changing the physical solution. Finally, for
\xi_{0}
and
\xi_{a}
, they evolve proportional to constraints and their derivatives. The same argument that was applied to the frame evolution system can be applied here, since the modification to the equations of motion are either pure gauge or are proportional to constraints the dynamics are unchanged on the constraint surface. If one prepares the system with all the constraints satisfied initially we see that their time derivatives are also satisfied and so should remain zero throughout evolution. It remains to be checked that this system is attractive towards the constraint surface during evolution.

11.4
Gauge Fixed Evolution System

Collecting both the frame and connection evolution equations we find our full evolution system that corresponds to the hyperbolic gauge fixing

\displaystyle(\partial^{A}_{t}-N^{a}\partial^{A}_{a})\tilde{E}^{ia}

\displaystyle=-i\epsilon^{ijk}\tilde{E}^{kb}\partial_{b}^{A}\left(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\right)-\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}N^{a}+\tilde{E}^{ia}\partial_{b}N^{b}+i(c_{1}+c_{2})\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{jb}\partial_{b}\chi^{j}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{c_{2}-c_{1}}{2ic_{2}}\left(\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\partial_{b}^{A}\tilde{E}^{kb}+(c_{1}+c_{2})\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}\chi^{j}+(3c_{2}+c_{1})\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{jb}\partial_{b}\chi^{i}\right).

(11.142)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})N^{a}

\displaystyle=-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}^{A}(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia})+(c_{1}-c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}\partial_{b}\chi^{i}+\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib}\xi_{b}

(11.143)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\underaccent{\tilde}{N}

\displaystyle=-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\partial_{a}N^{a}+2ic_{2}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\partial_{a}\chi^{i}-\underaccent{\tilde}{N}(\xi_{0}-N^{a}\xi_{a})

(11.144)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\chi^{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2ic_{2}}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\partial^{A}_{a}\tilde{E}^{ia}+\frac{1}{2ic_{2}}(c_{1}+c_{2})\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\partial_{a}\chi^{k}.

(11.145)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\xi_{0}

\displaystyle=-N^{a}\partial_{a}\xi_{0}-(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib}-N^{a}N^{b})\partial_{a}\xi_{b}

\displaystyle\ -2i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}N^{a}\tilde{E}^{ib}F^{i}_{ab}+\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{jb}F^{k}_{ab}

(11.146)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})\xi_{a}

\displaystyle=2i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}F^{i}_{ba}-\partial_{a}\xi_{0}+N^{b}\partial_{a}\xi_{b}

(11.147)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})A^{i}_{a}

\displaystyle=\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{0}-N^{b}\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{b}+i\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{jb}F^{k}_{ab}-i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{(a}\xi_{b)}

(11.148)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})A^{i}_{0}

\displaystyle=\ (\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{jb}-N^{a}N^{b})\partial_{a}A^{i}_{b}+N^{a}\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{0}-i\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}N^{b}F^{k}_{ab}

\displaystyle\ +\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{jb}F^{j}_{ab}-i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\partial_{a}\xi_{0}-iN^{a}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{(a}\xi_{b)}

\displaystyle\ +iN^{a}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}\partial_{b}\xi_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ja}\tilde{E}^{kb}\partial_{a}\xi_{b}.

(11.149)

To check that this system is strongly hyperbolic one has to diagonalise the characteristic matrix and check that the eigenvalues are real. As this is a lengthy calculation that does not reveal any insight to the equations, we simply state that the characteristic matrix is strongly hyperbolic. The eigenvalues all take values in
-N^{a}k_{a}\pm\sqrt{k^{2}}
where
k^{2}=k_{a}k^{a}
and
k_{a}
is the arbitrary wave vector used to construct
P=M^{a}k_{a}
. This means that none of the fields have zero propagation speed modes, in
[
158
]
this was argued to be a useful property for avoiding instabilities. The strong hyperbolicity also means that we can trust our evolution system to be well-posed, at least locally. The strong hyperbolicity of this system is somewhat expected, and as we have seen in
chapter

9
this system obeys the wave equation at second order which is strongly hyperbolic.

11.4.1
Constraint Amplification Analysis

Now we check that our evolution system is indeed attractive towards the constraint surface, this is so that any constraint violation is quickly damped and removed rather than growing. To check we expand around the Minkowski background and perform a Fourier transform, as shown in
section

10.2
, the eigenvalues of the amplification matrix will indicate whether the constraint will grow or shrink. On the Minkowski background the fields have components
\underaccent{\tilde}{N}=1,N^{a}=0,\tilde{E}^{ia}=\delta^{ia},A^{i}_{\mu}=0,\xi_{\mu}=0
and
\chi^{i}=0
. Around this background the equation of motion for
\tilde{E}^{ia}
becomes

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\tilde{E}^{ia}=-i\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{k}\tilde{E}^{ja}-i\epsilon^{iak}\partial^{k}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}-\partial^{i}N^{a}+\delta^{ia}\partial_{b}N^{b}+i(c_{1}+c_{2})\delta^{ia}\partial^{j}\chi^{j}+

\displaystyle\frac{c_{2}-c_{1}}{2ic_{2}}\left(\epsilon^{iak}\mathcal{G}^{k}+(c_{1}+c_{2})\partial^{i}\chi^{a}+(3c_{2}+c_{1})\partial^{a}\chi^{i}\right)

(11.150)

where we have used
\tilde{E}^{ia}=\delta^{ia}
on the background to convert indices between internal and spatial. With this we can compute the time derivative of the linearised Gauss constraint

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathcal{G}^{i}=\frac{c_{1}+c_{2}}{2ic_{2}}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{k}\mathcal{G}^{j}-\frac{(c_{1}+c_{2})^{2}}{2ic_{2}}\partial^{i}\partial^{j}\chi^{j}+\frac{(c_{2}-c_{1})(3c_{2}+c_{1})}{2ic_{2}}\partial^{j}\partial^{j}\chi^{i}.

(11.151)

The linearised equation for the internal vector,
\chi^{i}
, is then

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\chi^{i}=\frac{1}{2ic_{2}}\mathcal{G}^{i}-\frac{c_{1}+c_{2}}{2ic_{2}}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{k}\chi^{j}.

(11.152)

We see that
(\chi^{i},\mathcal{G}^{i})
form a closed system. Taking the Fourier transform of these equations and writing them in matrix form gives us

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\binom{\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{i}}{\hat{\chi}^{i}}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{c_{1}+c_{2}}{2ic_{2}}\epsilon^{ijk}k^{k}&&\frac{(c_{1}+c_{2})^{2}}{2ic_{2}}k^{i}k^{j}-\frac{(c_{2}-c_{1})(3c_{2}+c_{1})}{2ic_{2}}k^{2}\delta^{ij}\\
\frac{1}{2ic_{2}}\delta^{ij}&&-\frac{c_{1}+c_{2}}{2c_{2}}\epsilon^{ijk}k^{k}\end{pmatrix}\binom{\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{i}}{\hat{\chi}^{i}}.

(11.153)

Where
k^{i}
is the wave vector for the Fourier transformation and
k^{2}=k^{i}k^{i}
. Computing the eigenvalues for the characteristic matrix we find

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pm}=\pm\sqrt{\frac{k^{2}}{2}}\sqrt{1-2\frac{c_{1}}{c_{2}}-\left(\frac{c_{1}}{c_{2}}\right)^{2}}

(11.154)

with multiplicity 3 for each sign. The condition for stability is that all the eigenvalues should have a nonpositive real part for any
k^{i}
. For this to be the case we must require that
c_{1}=\alpha c_{2}
where
\alpha\leq-1-\sqrt{2}
or
\alpha\geq-1+\sqrt{2}
. In which case

\displaystyle 1-2\alpha-\alpha^{2}\leq 0

(11.155)

and hence the eigenvalues are purely imaginary. On the connection side of the evolution system we can compute the equations of motion for the Hamiltonian and Momentum constraints, skipping the details, to find

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle=2i\partial^{i}\mathcal{M}^{i},

(11.156)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathcal{M}^{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\partial^{i}\mathcal{H}+\frac{i}{2}\partial^{i}\partial^{j}\xi^{j}-\frac{i}{2}\partial^{j}\partial^{j}\xi^{i}.

(11.157)

The linearised equations of motion for
\xi_{0}
and
\xi^{i}
are

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\xi_{0}=\mathcal{H}-\partial^{i}\xi^{i}

(11.158)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\xi^{i}=2i\mathcal{M}^{i}-\partial^{i}\xi_{0}.

(11.159)

Then by taking the Fourier transform and taking the solution vector to be
u=(\hat{\xi_{0}},\hat{\xi^{i}},\hat{\mathcal{H}},\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{i})
the amplification matrix is

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&-ik^{j}&1&0\\
-ik^{i}&0&0&2i\delta^{ij}\\
0&0&0&-2k^{j}\\
0&\frac{i}{2}k^{2}\delta^{ij}-\frac{i}{2}k^{i}k^{j}&-\frac{k^{i}}{2}&0\end{pmatrix}.

(11.160)

The eigenvalues of the amplification matrix are

\displaystyle\pm\sqrt{k^{2}},\quad\pm i\sqrt{k^{2}}

(11.161)

with multiplicity
2
for the first and
4
for the second. Only the real part of one of the eigenvalues is positive, while this means that there exists a constraint mode that will grow during evolution it says nothing about the rate of growth and the simulation could still converge before the constraint grows to overthrow the true solution. To check this one would need to perform numerical simulations.

11.4.2
Constraint Damping

So far we have seen that the eigenvalues for the constraints are purely imaginary, this implies that the constraints are propagated instead of growing or damped. To induce damping we can add linear terms involving the momentum that are conjugate to the Lagrange multipliers, i.e.
\chi^{i},\xi_{0}
and
\xi_{i}
, to their respective equations of motion. One of the simpler modifications that can introduce damping is

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\chi^{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2ic_{2}}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\partial_{a}^{A}\tilde{E}^{ia}+\frac{c_{1}+c_{2}}{2ic_{2}}\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ja}\partial_{a}\chi^{k}-\beta\chi^{i}

(11.162)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\xi_{0}=

\displaystyle 2i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}N^{a}\tilde{E}^{ib}F^{i}_{ba}+\epsilon^{ijk}\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{jb}F^{k}_{ab}-(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib}-N^{a}N^{b})\partial_{a}\xi_{b}

\displaystyle-N^{a}\partial_{a}\xi_{0}-\rho\xi_{0}

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})\xi_{a}=

\displaystyle 2i\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ib}F^{i}_{ba}-\partial_{a}\xi_{0}+N^{b}\partial_{a}\xi_{b}-\sigma\xi_{a}

where
\beta,\rho,\sigma
are damping coefficients. This modification is allowed as
\chi^{i},\xi_{0}
and
\xi_{a}
are all weakly zero and during the evolution they should converge to
0
. The eigenvalues for the
(\chi^{i},\mathcal{G}^{i})
amplification matrix becomes

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pm}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\beta}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\beta^{2}+2(1-2\alpha-\alpha^{2})k^{2}}

(11.163)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pm}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\beta}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\beta^{2}+2i\beta(\alpha+1)\sqrt{k^{2}}+2(1-2\alpha-\alpha^{2})k^{2}}

(11.164)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pm}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\beta}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\beta^{2}-2i\beta(\alpha+1)\sqrt{k^{2}}+2(1-2\alpha-\alpha^{2})k^{2}}

(11.165)

For the real part of these eigenvalues to be nonpositive we notice that when
\alpha=1
they reduce to

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pm}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\beta}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\beta^{2}-4k^{2}}

(11.166)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pm}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\beta}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}(\beta+2i\sqrt{k^{2}})

(11.167)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pm}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\beta}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}(\beta-2i\sqrt{k^{2}}).

(11.168)

Then choosing
\beta\geq 0
we find that 4 of the eigenvalues have negative real parts and 2 have zero. Doing the same calculation for the connection amplification matrix we find the eigenvalues to be

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pm}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\rho+\sigma}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(\rho-\sigma)^{2}-4k^{2}}

(11.169)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\pm\sqrt{k^{2}}

(11.170)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pm}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-4k^{2}}.

(11.171)

The last eigenvalue requires
\sigma\geq 0
for no growth to occur, and for the first eigenvalue we need
\rho>0
to ensure the real part is
\leq 0
. However, the damping has not remedied the middle eigenvalue for which the positive sign still produces a growing eigenvalue. When
\beta,\rho,\sigma
are all greater than zero then some constraint modes should be damped, all but one of the modes of the constraint will be damped. The presence of this positive eigenvalue suggests that there is perhaps a modification to the gauge fixing that will remove this positive eigenvalue and perform better numerically. Since the search space for such a gauge fixing is effectively infinite we do not consider such modifications. It is still possible that the system is well-behaved numerically as it could be that the corresponding components are never active. To check this more thoroughly one would need to perform numerical test.

The choice
\alpha=1
implies that
c_{1}=c_{2}=c
for some non-zero constant
c
, we will see in the next section that this choice also leads to the development of a new evolution system with simplified algebraic properties and that makes use of the conformal separation property that was noted at the linear level.

11.5
Conformal Evolution System

In the linear analysis of the hyperbolic gauge fixing,
chapter

6
, we saw that the fields separated conformally into sectors of dimension 12 and 4. This surprising property allowed one to ignore the
4
component sector as the physical content was contained in the
12
dimensional part. A similar story occurred for the nonlinear gauge fixing in
chapter

9
where the 12 dimensional system could be defined independently of the 4, however, the 4 dimensional system was still dependent on the 12. As the 12 contains the physical degrees of freedom this is not a problem and one can still ignore the
4
dimensional system. The equations of motion for the 12 component connection involved combinations of the original connections and frame variables, this mixing of sectors introduces more complicated dynamics for the constraint propagation, for this reason we also consider a partially conformal system where only the frame variables are separated into their conformal parts. The partially conformal system offers some economy over the system in the previous section which we explore.

11.5.1
Conformal Frame Evolution System

We begin by finding the nonlinear analogues of the linearised separated variables. The metric linearises to
(\delta g)_{\mu\nu}=2h_{\mu\nu}=2\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}hg_{\mu\nu}
, where
(\delta e)^{I}{}_{\mu}=h^{I}{}_{\mu}
. From this we wish to separate
\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}
and
h
, that is the tracefree and trace parts. For the trace part we can make use of a formula from linear algebra

\displaystyle\delta\sqrt{-g}=\sqrt{-g}g^{\mu\nu}h_{\mu\nu}=h

(11.172)

where we have used that
\sqrt{-g}=1
on the background. It shows that the nonlinear version of
h
is then
\sqrt{-g}
, in practice we can use any function of
\sqrt{-g}
without disrupting the splitting. The tracefree part can then be found by multiplying the densitiesed inverse metric (we choose the densitised inverse metric as it is more conveniently written using the components of
\tilde{E}^{i\mu\nu}
) by the appropriate power of
\sqrt{-g}
, that is

\displaystyle\delta(\sqrt{-g}^{n}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu})=\delta(\sqrt{-g}^{n+1}g^{\mu\nu})=(n+1)g^{\mu\nu}h-2h^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\quad=(n+1)g^{\mu\nu}h-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}h-\hat{h}^{\mu\nu}=\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)g^{\mu\nu}h-\hat{h}^{\mu\nu}.

(11.173)

We can see that to remove
h
and leave
\hat{h}^{\mu\nu}
as the only irreducible component we can pick
n=-\frac{1}{2}
. In which case the nonlinear analogue of
\hat{h}^{\mu\nu}
is
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sqrt{-g}}}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}
which matches the conformal metric appearing in
eq.

9.55
. Moving to the 3+1 components we recall that
\sqrt{-g}=\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\det(\tilde{E})
, and we can see that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sqrt{-g}}}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\det(\tilde{E})}}\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}&&\frac{N^{a}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}\\
\frac{N^{b}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}&&\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib}-\frac{N^{a}N^{b}}{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{3}\det(\tilde{E})}}\begin{pmatrix}-1&&N^{a}\\
N^{b}&&\underaccent{\tilde}{N}^{2}\tilde{E}^{ia}\tilde{E}^{ib}-N^{a}N^{b}\end{pmatrix}.

(11.174)

If we introduce a new conformal triad
H^{ia}=\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\tilde{E}^{ia}
then the above simplifies slightly

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sqrt{-g}}}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det(H)}}\begin{pmatrix}-1&&N^{a}\\
N^{b}&&H^{ia}H^{ib}-N^{a}N^{b}\end{pmatrix}.

(11.175)

Which shows that the conformal metric is a function of
\hat{\gamma}^{ab}=H^{ia}H^{ib}
and
N^{a}
. These are
6+3=9
components which matches the number of components
\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}
. As for the
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
frame perturbation,
h^{i}
, this only possible to write down in relation to another frame, as the nonlinear version of
h^{i}
would be an
SO(3,\mathbb{C})
transformation. Instead, we know that it will exist in the choice of frame
H^{ia}
as this contains
6+3=9
components where the
6
count the components of the spatial metric and the remaining
3
belong to the Euler angles needed to rotate to the chosen frame. Therefore, we can say that (
H^{ia},N^{a}
) contains the nonlinear fields that correspond to the linear components (
h^{i},\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}
) which parametrises the 12 components sector. As for the remaining
4
components we know that they will be functions of all the fields, as
h=\delta\sqrt{-g}=\delta(\underaccent{\tilde}{N}\det(\tilde{E}))
and
\hat{\chi}^{i}=h^{i}-(c_{1}+c_{2})\chi^{i}
, so instead of creating the nonlinear combination we find the resulting equations simpler when leaving
\underaccent{\tilde}{N}
and
\chi^{i}
separate. We can now write the frame sector,
eqs.

11.122
,
11.123
,
11.124
and
11.125
, using the conformal triad
H^{ia}
. We start with evolution of the conformal triad and find that it is

\displaystyle(\partial^{A}_{t}-N^{b}\partial^{A}_{b})H^{ia}=

\displaystyle-i\epsilon^{ijk}H^{kb}\partial_{b}^{A}H^{ja}-H^{ib}\partial_{b}N^{a}-H^{ia}(\xi_{0}-N^{b}\xi_{b})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{i}{2c_{2}}(c_{1}-c_{2})\left(\epsilon^{ijk}H^{ja}\partial^{A}_{b}H^{kb}-\epsilon^{ijk}H^{ja}H^{kb}\partial_{b}n+(c_{1}+c_{2})H^{ja}H^{ib}\partial_{b}\chi^{j}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 100.0pt+(3c_{2}+c_{1})H^{ja}H^{jb}\partial_{b}\chi^{i}+2c_{2}H^{ia}H^{jb}\partial_{b}\chi^{j}\right).

(11.176)

Here we have introduced a new variable
n=\log(\underaccent{\tilde}{N})
with which it will be useful to write some equations of motion. The equation for
H^{ia}
also contains
\chi^{i}
components, and from the analysis in
chapter

6
we know that the linearised
12
sector is parameterised by (
\hat{h}_{\mu\nu},\hat{h}^{i}
), with,
\hat{h}^{i}=h^{i}-(c_{1}-c_{2})\chi^{i}
. This means that, in general, we will have to form a nonlinear combination out of
\chi^{i}
,
H^{ia}
and
N^{a}
in order to find the splitting. Instead of constructing more complicated combinations of these fields we choose an easier tactic, by fixing
c_{2}=c_{1}=c
we see that
\hat{h}^{i}=h^{i}
and the nonlinear evolution equation for
H^{ia}
loses all of its terms containing
\chi^{i}
. This choice of
c_{1},c_{2}
implies that
\alpha=1
which is within the correct range for the constraints to not exponentially grow as per
eq.

11.155
, this value also agrees with the damped eigenvalue analysis. The evolution equation for the conformal triad is then

\displaystyle(\partial^{A}_{t}-N^{b}\partial^{A}_{b})H^{ia}=-i\epsilon^{ijk}H^{kb}\partial^{A}_{b}H^{ja}-H^{ib}\partial_{b}N^{a}-H^{ia}(\xi_{0}-N^{b}\xi_{b}).

(11.177)

As for the shift evolution equation, when
c_{2}=c_{1}=c
we find

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})N^{a}=-H^{ib}\partial_{b}^{A}H^{ia}+H^{ia}H^{ib}\xi_{b}.

(11.178)

It is clear then that the only frame variables required for the evolution of (
H^{ia},N^{a}
) are themselves. The other
4
equations of motion, when using
H^{ia}
become

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})n

\displaystyle=-\partial_{a}N^{a}-2icH^{ia}\partial_{a}\chi^{i}-\xi_{0}+N^{a}\xi_{a}

(11.179)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\chi^{i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{2c}\partial^{A}_{a}H^{ia}+\frac{i}{2c}H^{ia}\partial_{a}n-i\epsilon^{ijk}H^{ja}\partial_{a}\chi^{k}.

(11.180)

At the nonlinear level we do not see a separation of the two sectors entirely, the
4
component sector depends on the
12
but the
12
is still independent.

Conformal Frame Evolution System

With the conformal frame,
H^{ia}
, we find that the system simplifies greatly if supported with the correct choice of
c_{1},c_{2}
. We call the choice of
c_{1}=c_{2}=c
our conformal evolution system, for which the evolution of the frame variables is.

\displaystyle(\partial^{A}_{t}-N^{b}\partial^{A}_{b})H^{ia}

\displaystyle=-i\epsilon^{ijk}H^{kb}\partial^{A}_{b}H^{ja}-H^{ib}\partial_{b}N^{a}-H^{ia}(\xi_{0}-N^{b}\xi_{b})

(11.181)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})N^{a}

\displaystyle=-H^{ib}\partial_{b}^{A}H^{ia}+H^{ia}H^{ib}\xi_{b}

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})n

\displaystyle=-\partial_{a}N^{a}-2icH^{ia}\partial_{a}\chi^{i}-\xi_{0}+N^{a}\xi_{a}

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\chi^{i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{2c}\partial^{A}_{a}H^{ia}+\frac{i}{2c}H^{ia}\partial_{a}n-i\epsilon^{ijk}H^{ja}\partial_{a}\chi^{k}.

Some discussion of the hyperbolicity of this system, as compared to the original system, is needed. We see that
c
is now a rescaling parameter for
\chi^{i}
, fixing it to a specific value does not change the hyperbolicity. Choosing
c=\frac{i}{2}
we can write the characteristic matrix in block matrix form, choosing the solution vector to be
((H^{ia},N^{a}),(\chi^{i},n))
we find

\displaystyle P(n)=\begin{pmatrix}A(n)^{I}{}_{J}\otimes\delta^{a}_{b}&&0\\
-\delta^{I}_{J}n_{b}&&-A(n)^{I}{}_{J}\end{pmatrix}

(11.182)

where
A^{I}{}_{J}(n)
acts on objects of the type
(X^{i},X)
with
4
components. Checking the structure of
A^{I}{}_{J}
we see that is Hermitian,

\displaystyle A^{I}{}_{J}=\begin{pmatrix}-i\epsilon^{ijk}n^{k}&&-n^{i}\\
-n^{j}&&0\end{pmatrix}

(11.183)

where
n^{i}=H^{ia}n_{a}
. This means that
A^{I}{}_{J}
has a full set of eigenvectors
v_{A}
, and corresponding eigenvalues
\lambda_{A}=\pm\sqrt{n^{2}}
, that span the solution space. Due to the reality conditions the eigenvalues are real-valued and as such
A
is strongly hyperbolic. We can then try to construct eigenvectors for
P(n)
, which amounts to solving

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}A(n)^{I}{}_{J}\otimes\delta^{a}_{b}&&0\\
-\delta^{I}_{J}n_{b}&&-A(n)^{I}{}_{J}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u^{Jb}\\
v^{J}\end{pmatrix}=\lambda\begin{pmatrix}u^{Jb}\\
v^{J}\end{pmatrix}

(11.184)

or as a system of equations

\displaystyle A^{I}{}_{J}u^{Ja}=\lambda u^{Ia}

(11.185)

\displaystyle-n_{a}u^{Ia}-A^{I}{}_{J}v^{J}=\lambda v^{I}.

(11.186)

If
u^{Ia}=0
then we find there are 4 eigenvectors inherited from
A
,

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0\\
v_{A}^{I}\end{pmatrix}

(11.187)

with eigenvalues
\lambda_{A}
. If
u^{Ia}=0
then we see that
u^{Ja}=v_{A}^{J}u^{a}
and
\lambda=\lambda_{A}
for some spatial 1-form
u_{a}
. The second eigenvalue equations will become

\displaystyle-n_{a}u^{a}v_{A}^{I}-A^{I}{}_{J}v^{J}=\lambda_{A}v^{I}

(11.188)

To solve this we introduce an ansatz
v^{I}=\alpha v_{A}^{I}
and see that this equation becomes

\displaystyle-n_{a}u^{a}-\alpha\lambda_{A}=\alpha\lambda_{A},\quad\Rightarrow\alpha=-\frac{n_{a}u^{a}}{2\lambda_{A}}.

(11.189)

Which gives the remaining 3 eigenvectors

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}u^{a}v_{A}^{I}\\
-\frac{n_{a}u^{a}}{2\lambda_{A}}\end{pmatrix}

(11.190)

with eigenvalues
\lambda_{A}
. We see that
P(n)
has a full set of eigenvectors and real values and is hence strongly hyperbolic. It is also worth mentioning that the matrix,
A^{I}{}_{J}
, also appears in the context of Chiral Yang-Mills. In fact, it is the characteristic matrix there as well. We see that gravity is two copies of Maxwell with some mixing terms, this has the flavour of the double copy structure (e.g.
section

5.3
) but the link in its current form is tenuous at best.

11.5.2
Conformal Connection Evolution System

For the connection evolution equations we explore two options, one related to the original system and where the constraint analysis directly carries across which we call the partially conformal system. The first system is simply the connection evolution equations using the conformal frame. However, the second system completely decouples the 12 components using the modification to the nonlinear gauge fixing presented in
chapter

9
.

Partially Conformal System

For the partially conformal system we simply replace the frame variables in the equations of motion for the frame with the conformally transformed variables. Interestingly, looking at
eqs.

11.146
,
11.147
,
11.148
and
11.149
we find that the variables
H^{ia}
appear naturally and one can immediately write this system with only
H^{ia}
and
N^{a}
as the frame variables,

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{a}\partial_{a})\xi_{0}

\displaystyle=2iN^{a}H^{ib}F^{i}_{ab}-\epsilon^{ijk}H^{ia}H^{jb}F^{k}_{ab}-(H^{ia}H^{ib}-N^{a}N^{b})\partial_{a}\xi_{b}

(11.191)

\displaystyle-N^{a}\partial_{a}\xi_{0}

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})\xi_{a}

\displaystyle=2iH^{ib}F^{i}_{ab}-\partial_{a}\xi_{0}+N^{b}\partial_{a}\xi_{b}

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-N^{b}\partial_{b})A^{i}_{a}

\displaystyle=\partial^{A}_{a}A^{i}_{0}-N^{b}\partial_{a}^{A}A^{i}_{b}+i\epsilon^{ijk}H^{jb}F^{k}_{ab}-iH^{ib}\partial_{(a}\xi_{b)}
